Commentaria in Canticum canticorum
by Wolbero of St Pantaleon

## Commentaries on the Canticle of Canticles

who work in me shall not sin, and who make me plain
shall have life everlasting [n: (Eccli. XXIV)].


## NUNCUPATORY EPISTLE

[1001]To the most reverend Father in Christ, the venerable Lord Henry Spichernagell, abbot of St Pantaleon and [1002] most deserving principal president of the holy Union of Barsfeld, and also to the very reverend and most ample [1003A] Lords Henry Libler, John Munch, Paul Vrechen, Henry Goermann and Gabel Scaffenius, most worthy abbots of the monasteries of the order of Saint Benedict of the same observance at Saint Martin, Brawiler, Tuitium, Gladbach and Graffscafft, his lords and prelates to be revered.

Your very reverend and most ample Lordships will wonder, and not without cause, why this commentator on the *Canticles of Canticles of Solomon* has lain so long under a bushel, and only now for the first time looks upon the light: to which, in all likelihood, I [answer] with that Jew Mordecai pressing his beloved Esther, and who knows whether you have come to the kingdom for this, that this Wolbero might be made ready at such a time? Wherefore I did not judge it enough for one soul alone, if such an author, [1003B] written, should only lie hidden in the house of St Pantaleon, unless, given over to the anvil, he should come forth into the light, opportune upon some new occasion, to profit the use and the spiritual taste of very many.

And what, you will say, is this occasion? It is now the third year since, at the time of the annual chapter celebrated in the monastery of Saint Pantaleon on the Kalends of September, sacred to blessed Aegidius the abbot, your consecrated heads too were newly adorned with the pontifical infula. By no words or writings am I able to comprehend how much joy grew from this for your dearest confreres, and with how great gladness I too was filled by it, in my own name as much as in the public one, and how heartily I desired to congratulate you, after I had understood that from our most holy lord [1003C] Urban VIII this episcopal authority had been superadded to your very reverend Lordships, and that pontifical mitres had been placed upon your heads in solemn order by the reverend lord bishop of Cyrene. For why should I not congratulate your Lordships on a glory so holy, so honourable and so new? a glory, I say, with which, beyond all ambition, as I am persuaded, you have been gifted and endowed by the sole merit of your most illustrious virtues (inasmuch as you are numbered among the chief pillars of our sacred Union of Bursfeld, and establish it by unswervingly supporting it)?

What was I to do? Filial love [1003D] demanded that I go down to acclaim this new honour of the reverend lord my prelate; gratitude required that I go likewise to meet [you], to venerate the tiaras of your very reverend Lordships; the examples of others provoked [me]. But I did not wish to appear empty in the sight of the highest priests, nor was there even a gift at hand that I might offer. When suddenly, God so inspiring, I remembered this commentator Wolbero, your co-abbot, and was delighted; and because very many of the religious, enticed heretofore [sic: *antebac*] by the reading of his most sweet interpretations, grieved exceedingly with me that commentaries so pious and profound lay hidden among the other furniture of our library, and that such a treasure was not communicated to more, I set about it with confidence, to testify my [1004A] readiness toward your very reverend Lordships and my zeal to deserve well, which I had long had among my wishes; and I not only reviewed the *Commentaries* of our Wolbero, but also, reviewed, arranged them into a better order, and I began to move every stone that, so arranged and renewed, and to be inscribed with the names of your very reverend Lordships, they might be put under the type. For the rest, since, as Solomon himself attests, of making many books there is no end, and so new things are wont to smile upon the wits of this age more than old ones, there was perhaps something I feared: lest either the antiquity of this commentator, or, more truly, the smallness of my own person, from which the book, not a little renewed, might not go forth, should be an obstacle, whereby it should pass less happily into the hands of many. [1004B] But, animated by the authority of your very reverend Lordships, I thought that I should easily be freed from a like fear, nay, from the detraction of all the Zoiluses, who are not wont to be wanting to good endeavours: both because into the Lord's storehouse and barn not always the hundredfold or sixtyfold, but more often the thirtyfold fruit is brought in; and because, to be protected by your Aaronic horns, it returns from its prelate at the same time, the course of the year having run, as that in which your abbatial almuces were changed into pontifical mitres.

Wherefore, most reverend lord president, my most revered prelate, receive thou this work, such as it is, of thy least client, superadded to my pastoral cares and hitherto drawn out [sic: *protacta*] by the various incursions of soldiers standing in the way; and because, according to the saying [1004C] of the Wise Man: *A man who loveth wisdom maketh his father glad [n: (Prov. XXIX)]*: gladden thy spirit, through this endeavour of mine, in the wisdom and spiritual canticle of this book, and refresh thy infirm and aged limbs, weakened by many labours endured for the monastery and our Union of Bursfeld, but not yet broken. Receive you also, venerable prelates, this small token of my regard toward you, Wolbero your co-abbot, humbly dedicated by me to your very reverend Lordships; and let him be a sharer in your new honour, who stood forth as the eminent narrator of the ancestral, and equally monastic, religion. That the same, under you as prelates, may grow and be propagated in the begun increase of spiritual discipline, [1004D] I will unceasingly entreat God, best, greatest, and our most holy lawgiver Benedict, and the patrons of the same order. May he also deign, by his most patient kindness, to keep your very reverend and most ample Lordships, with all committed to them, surviving and unharmed for as very long and as very happily as may be. So he cherishes and prays, from the pastorate of Laugel, on the very [day] of blessed Aegidius the abbot, in the year 1629.

Of your very reverend and most ample Lordships the humble servant and brother, Henry ERSMERS of Grave, a Pantaleonite, of the order of St Benedict.

## EPIGRAM TO THE AUTHOR AND THE READER OF THESE COMMENTARIES.

[1005A] That thou mayest outlast the times of the Pylian, most learned writer,
And that thy fame may be known in every place,
To commit this excellent work to the press has seemed
Fitting to many, and not without praise of thee.
Go forth then secure, Wolbero; it befits not
That the so pious writings of thy genius lie hid.
Whom the sweet love of composing canticles now touches,
[1006A]Lo, he has Wolbero, under whose leading he may compose.
Not such canticles did the Dircaean bard play,
When at the touch of the lyre the boar leapt up.
Wolbero, thou deservest; he sings a chaste life,
He tells the mystic words of the Bridegroom with the bride.
Go now, whoever thou art that lovest the nuptials of the soul and of Christ:
Whom thou mayest win to thyself, lo, the paranymph is here.


## BROTHER HENRY GRAVIUS TO THE KIND READER.

*The Commentaries of the venerable lord abbot Wolbero, although they were composed and inscribed by the author himself for devout virgins consecrated to God, who once so requested, and he wished them to lie hidden with the nuns alone, and not to come forth into the common light unless, corrected by someone, they had turned out a finished and masculine work: I would not have thee persuaded, kind reader, that thou hast obtained this very thing by my labour. For this is the virtue of those who truly think humbly of themselves: to do or to write things great and splendid, and nonetheless to think meanly and moderately of themselves and of what is theirs. In this kind of virtue our abbot Wolbero shines forth in no common way. in whose commentaries, most ancient indeed, but gathered by the assiduous study of nine years, I am so far from having met with anything shapeless, or womanish, or wanting a more correcting file (although in the dedication addressed to his religious sisters, and in other prefaces of this work, he seems to hint at this very thing), that this little work ought deservedly to seem conceived by a divine rather than a human spirit, and traced by the pen. Wherefore I would not have it hidden from thee, whoever thou art that shalt be the reader of this work, that I have added or mixed little of my own into these Commentaries, save where ambiguity of sense, looseness of periods and disjunction of verses had seemed to want some such thing. These perhaps were the things that might have offended a delicate reader in the ancient Wolbero. Since these have been sufficiently relieved by my labour and effort, [and] through I know not what carelessness or haste of printer or corrector some errors have crept in here and there, which it is permitted to see corrected at the end of this book, take this labour of mine, such as it is, in good part; and while thou enjoyest it, receive it to thy profit, and receive kindly this perpetual monument of this author. Farewell*.



## DEDICATORY EPISTLE OF ABBOT WOLBERO.

[1005B]To his most beloved sisters on the Island of the Rhine, serving as soldiers to God and to blessed Mary, WOLBERO, a sinner.

While I was often treating with myself, and frequently turning over with a doubtful mind, what sweet thing, what pleasant, what useful thing I might sing to the inward ears of your holy charity, O most holy sisters, by whose sweetness and incitement your mind would be raised to a greater love of the heavenly life, there suddenly occurred to my mind that singable epithalamium of the bridegroom and the bride, which for its excellence is called the *Canticle of Canticles.* This kind of song, indeed, greatly befits the dignity of your profession, because this surpasses the order of all professions, as that surpasses every kind of canticles.

[1006B]For there are three kinds of professions: one of the married, another of widows, the third of virgins. Now we read that there is *a canticle of praise, a canticle of gladness, a new canticle.* Let us assign therefore, as by certain steps, the canticle of praise to the married, the canticle of gladness to widows, the new canticle to virgins, which *no man* can *say, save they who follow the Lamb whithersoever he shall go [n: (Apoc. XIV)]* [var: Vulg. Apoc. 14:3 *nemo poterat dicere canticum, nisi illa centum quadraginta quatuor millia*, "no man could say the canticle but those hundred forty-four thousand"; *Hi sequuntur Agnum* is 14:4]. For the *canticle* of *praise* the married sing, who have received some beginning of religion and of good conversation, but have not yet escaped the tribulation of the flesh; while the *canticle* of *gladness* widows sing, who have attained something higher, excelling in greater religion, and, having now experienced the tribulation of the flesh, are set free from it, and therefore can [1007A] sing both praise and gladness. But virgins sing the *new canticle,* who hold the highest step, never having experienced the aforesaid tribulation of the flesh, and therefore able to sing both praise, and gladness, and a newness singular and unutterable to the rest.

But great heed must be taken, in those who are called virgins, of which part they are; for the Lord divided this profession into two parts, that is, into wise and foolish virgins [n: (Matth. XXV)]; because this new canticle they cannot say, save those who are of the part of the wise: and the wise are those who are virgins both in body and in mind; but the foolish, those who, although they are virgins in body, yet live corrupted in mind. Such, assuredly, cannot sing [1007B] the *new canticle,* nor even the canticle of the last profession; who do not even attain to its merit, because they have no oil in the vessel; nay rather, for this negligence of theirs, they do not enter the bridegroom's chamber.

Wherefore your charity, you who are virgins in body, ought greatly to take heed and be solicitous that you be so in mind also; because virginity of the flesh confers nothing on you, if you have already married in mind. If, then, you hold the right rule of the virginal profession, you sing a singular canticle, and, so to speak, not simply a canticle, as married women and widows do, but the *Canticle of Canticles*, which it is possible to sing only for those *who are before the throne of God without spot, because they have not defiled their garments [n: (Apoc. XIV)]* [var: Vulg. Apoc. 14:5 *sine macula enim sunt ante thronum Dei*; the clause *qui non inquinaverunt vestimenta sua* is Apoc. 3:4], that is, their [1007C] bodies.

Authorities for these three professions are indeed found in Holy Scripture, because conjugal chastity is proclaimed in Elizabeth, widowly holiness in Anna, virginal perfection in the blessed Mother of God, Mary. And the merit of each the Lord himself shows in the Gospel, where the *seed* is said to have fallen *into good ground and* to have made *fruit, some thirtyfold, some sixtyfold, and some a hundredfold [n: (Luc. VIII)]* [var: Vulg. Luc. 8:8 *fecit fructum centuplum*, "made fruit a hundredfold", with no thirty or sixty; these stand in Matt. 13:8 and Mark 4:20].

For the thirtyfold fruit is that of the married living chastely, whom the reckoning of the fingers shows by this number, the forefinger being laid over the thumb; it indicates the weight of the tribulation and depression of the widows themselves, destitute of the support and consolation of a man. [1007D] But the hundredfold number, which is put for perfection, shows the perfection of holy virgins; which, carried over from the left hand to the right, the tip of the forefinger being joined to the tip of the thumb, expresses the crown of virginity.

Weigh therefore the excellence of this profession, whose reward is the hundredfold fruit, whose merit is to say the *new canticle,* to whose dignity, by reason of the special divine conjunction of grace, these *Canticles of Canticles* which we intend to sing to you according to our small measure are most fitting. Not even with you, whichever of you are truly virgins, can I, I say not sing these canticles, but not even whisper them, I who differ far [1008A] from your holiness; but as someone who can convey the knowledge of some art by word only, but cannot express it in act, so I too, although I cannot sing these things with you, my fault standing in the way, will nevertheless try with God's help to say something from which you may in some measure know the excellence and singular dignity of these Canticles. Therefore, as the gentile poet says: *I will serve as a whetstone, which knows how to render*
*iron sharp, itself without share in cutting:*
[n: (HORAT., Ars poet., 304.)] and since it is not permitted to sing, let it at least be permitted to give counsel for singing. For holy Moses also, with whom God spoke face to face, heard and did the counsel of Jethro his kinsman, a gentile man, although he himself was full [1008B] of wisdom and holiness, while the other stood very far distant from him in gentilehood and in the quality of his life. But since God has given to will this, may he give also to be able. I know indeed that it will be unworthy in the eyes of many, who receive only the writings of the ancients, *And admire nothing save what Libitina has hallowed.*
[n: (HOR., Ep. II, I, 49.)] that after the sayings of the elders, which charity has confirmed, I have shamelessly laid bare my own follies as well; but your love will excuse me before them, whose desire alone I have set myself to satisfy. And if they reproach the speech as too simple for the majesty of the cause, let them remember that in the construction of the tabernacle not only gold and silver, byssus [1008C] and purple, but also goats' hair were offered, [n: (Exod. XXV)], and the two small mites of the widow were more praised by the Lord than the immense gifts of the rich [n: (Luc. XXI)]. And if they cavil at the arrangement of the words, let them nevertheless venerate the cause of the matter, and let both the dignity of the matter and the obedient charity of devout supplication excuse the thinness of the speech or composition. For we have in the Church very many books composed by metrical art, on the Gospels and the deeds of the saints, in a humble style and simple speech, which nevertheless, on account of the dignity of their matter, have already obtained authority. Whence Arator, when about to write the Acts of the Apostles, says: *In fasting speech indeed, but deeds that are rich*
*Have we written, and a drop flows with the weight of the sea*.
[1008D] Let no one therefore despise our simplicity, because *discourse with the simple* is ours [n: (Prov. III)]; nay rather, let him indulge us sinners the more, in that it pleased [us] more to write something, however simply, who in empty leisure, torpor, since we even wish this work to go forth nowhere, but to lie hidden only with the holy sisters alone to whom it has been sent; unless perhaps it shall please someone, out of a regard of kindness, to take this up for correcting, and out of a shapeless and soft composition to work out some beautiful and masculine work.

Receive therefore this work of my littleness, in which it has not irked me to labour for the sake of your [1009A] love, for your profit indeed, but for my own perpetual monument; so that when you look upon these things [1010A] and read them, you may be mindful of me, and, whether I am living or dead, entreat our common God for me.



## CENSURE OF THE ABBOT OF GERODHEN On the Commentaries of Abbot Wolbero on the Canticle of Canticles.

[1009]

[1009A]To the venerable and inwardly lovable lord WOLBERO, abbot of Saint Pantaleon, brother, called abbot of the monastery of GERODHEN: [may you,] led not only into the cellar but also into the chamber of the bridegroom, be inebriated with consummate charity *from the plenty of the house of God, and with the torrent of the pleasure* of him be given to drink for ever [n: (Psal. XXXV)].

For you write, and with a humility familiar to all and to those who use the wisdom of the spirit, you admonish by asking and [1009B] ask by admonishing, that I should certify you in writing concerning your work on the *Canticles of Canticles,* considered with more diligence, every courting of your good will being wholly put away, what nevertheless it may seem to me, namely whether I think it should be brought forth into the light, or understand that it should be hidden and laid aside; because you would trust no one better than me, in that both kinship binds [me] to good will and the wisdom of correction would not deny a verdict. And indeed, as every man of understanding recognizes that all judgments are contained in nothing but these two, prudence and good will, so, since I reflect not without grief that good will is surely present and prudence absent, following him who prayed, saying: *That I may know what is wanting to me [n: (Psal. XXXVIII)]*, not [1009C] being sufficient to bear the burden that you lay on me, I not undeservedly shrink from it.

But since this is the excuse even of all who are torpid in leisure, and your charity, trusting me and hoping that this can be done by me, admonishes me to do it, not as a judge, but as a friend of the bridegroom by desire: while I hear the voice [1010A] of him ministering his doctrine to your heart in the liberty of the spirit, at the voice of the bridegroom using you as an organ I rejoice together in charity rejoicing with the truth, and I judge that this work is not to be abolished, nay, to the glory of God, for it is glory to reveal the word [n: (Tob. XII)], and for the profit of the Church of God and the instruction of those for whom it is written, it is by all means to be made manifest. Truly both catholic in faith, and, for morals [1010B] to be instructed, most apt, every Christian disposing himself to live well in the house of God will judge it.

I have learned that you took it somewhat ill that I did not at once send back the codex, finished; but some things were an impediment: first, because no thing can be at once hurried and examined, which I learned from you also, since you pressed on almost into the new year; wherefore what was written with diligence [should], as it is read, not be judged with negligence. To this was added that our brothers, against your will indeed, but with me willing and rejoicing over it, eagerly snatched it and read it sedulously and diligently, and by the study of reading made it commendable; yet so that they withdrew from me, even unwilling, some of the reading of [1010C] it. But now, read through, and not without diligence, I send it back, to be communicated to all of you in Christ for reading; it remains that you not deny a share in the fruit to him whom you judged fit to share the labour. Farewell.



## GENERAL PREFACE OF WOLBERO ON THE CANTICLE OF CANTICLES OF SOLOMON.

[1009]

[1009D]In Holy Scripture many canticles are spoken of, which, differing by a diverse kind of causes, have been sung now generally, now specially, according to place and time; place, because either in the fatherland or in a foreign land; time, because either in peace or in captivity. For there is spoken of the *Canticle of Moses, the Canticle of Anna, the Canticle of Habacuc the prophet,* or others of this kind; but all these are simply canticles. Here, however, they are called *Canticles of Canticles,* because they surpass all kinds of canticles by the majesty of mystical understanding.

And the other canticles, indeed, have a cause why they were [1010D] sung, as Moses sang the *canticle of praise* for the victory celebrated, when Pharaoh was drowned with his chariots and horsemen in the Red Sea. Anna too sang a canticle to the Lord for fruitfulness bestowed, the reproach of barrenness being taken away. Ezechias too sang a canticle for health restored to him. Therefore the several canticles were, for each one, a thanksgiving for a benefit conferred on him by God. But this canticle represents the harmony of that highest and perfect charity, by which, all the cares [sic: *curi*] of worldly things being lulled to sleep, every faithful soul [1011A] is joined to her Creator as a bride to the bridegroom with the ineffable sweetness of love; in whose embraces she dwells with such sweetness of peace and quiet that there is nothing she desires besides him, nothing she would set before so magnificent a grace; because certainly there is nothing that resists the spirit which, the war of the flesh trodden down, with the flesh subjugated to it, keeps sabbath in Christ in the same happy concord. For a great part, and a Sabbath to be desired, that is, rest, is when the flesh does not lust against the spirit, but, the tumult of the vices being stilled, the mind enjoys such sweetness of inward tranquillity that, desiring nothing else than the Lord, it wills and is able to say the *Canticle of Canticles*, that is, to have true and perfect charity. For charity towers above all the virtues, as the *Canticle of Canticles* [1011B] goes beyond all canticles. But ascents are to be disposed in the heart, so that, as by certain steps, one may arrive at singing the *Canticles of Canticles;* that just as he who enters the holy place still has need of many things to be able to enter the Holy of holies; and he who celebrates Sabbaths needs far higher things to be able to arrive at the Sabbaths of sabbaths: so he who sings canticles ought ever to advance to greater things, to be able to sing the *Canticles of Canticles.*

For seven canticles are found in the divine page, the causes of whose singing mystically indicate to us, as by certain steps, the progresses of the soul, which are ruled through the grace of the sevenfold Spirit. [1011C] For we must go out of the Egypt of this world and cross the Red Sea through baptism, or through the tears of penitence, that, the attendant of the vices being drowned, we may first sing the canticle with Moses: *Let us sing to the Lord, for he is gloriously honoured; the horse and the rider he hath thrown forth into the sea [n: (Exod. XV)]* [var: Vulg. *magnificatus est … dejecit*, "is magnified … hath cast down"]. Then, the tumult of worldly business being left behind, the spiritual solitude is to be walked through until we find the well which the fathers dug, by whose doctrines and examples being informed in the manner of living rightly, we may sing together the second canticle, which the children of Israel sang for the well they had found. *Let it ascend,* they sang together, *the well which the princes dug, and the leaders of the multitude prepared, in the giver of the law and with their staves [n: (Num. XXI)]*. And when we have come to the neighbourhood of the land of promise, [1011D] preparing ourselves to possess that blessed land, and inviting with us the spiritual and the carnal, let us sing with Moses the third canticle: *Attend, O heaven, and I will speak; and let the earth hear the words from my mouth [n: (Deut. XXXII)]* [var: Vulg. *Audite, caeli, quae loquor: audiat terra verba oris mei*, "Hear, O heavens, the things I speak: let the earth hear the words of my mouth"]. But that we may inhabit this land spiritually, the enemies of the soul must be fought down, against whom we must war with our whole intention, that, these being put to flight, we may sing together the fourth canticle with Debbora: *The remnants of the people are saved, the Lord hath fought among the mighty [n: (Jud. V)]*. The enemies then being overcome, let us sing to the Lord the fifth canticle with David, for our escape and deliverance, that canticle namely which David sang when he was delivered from the hand of all his enemies and from the hand of Saul, and said: *The Lord is my rock [1012A] and my strength and my saviour [n: (II Reg. XXII)]*, and the rest. After, namely, the motions of spiritual adversities having been repressed and all the cares of worldly things set aside, we shall at last have been placed in the longed-for quiet of mind, we must rise and hasten with the progresses of the soul to merit the embraces of that heavenly Bridegroom; the canticle of which intention we shall sing in the sixth place with Isaias: *I will sing to the beloved the canticle of my cousin, of his vineyard [n: (Isai. V)]*. When, namely, by the progress of this angelic conversation, we shall have flown up to such a height on the wings of holy contemplation that, dwelling now in the embraces of the Bridegroom himself, we can say with the Apostle: *But our conversation is in heaven [n: (Philip. III)]*. Then at last we shall sing the seventh canticle, [1012B] which is called not only a canticle but the *Canticle of Canticles*, when, resting from every worldly work, we shall have begun to keep sabbath with the bridegroom himself in continual quiet.

Now three things are to be considered in this book: the matter, the intention, the final cause. The matter is the espousals of Christ and the Church, or of each faithful soul, which consist in true and perfect love of him; which love, namely, is shown by the mortification of the vices and the delight of the virtues and the observance of his commandments. The intention is to exhort those who read and hear so to study to be united to Christ through charity that they may be worthy to be called by the name of bride. The final cause is thence to attain the highest good, which is God, of which good it is written: *That which neither [1012C] eye hath seen, nor ear heard, nor into the heart of man* have those things ascended *which God hath prepared for them that love him [n: (I Cor. II)]*.

It is to be heeded, namely, that in this book nothing is to be understood carnally according to the letter, but the whole is to be referred to the spiritual sense, because *the letter killeth, but the spirit quickeneth [n: (I Cor. III)]*. For because God, speaking by himself to the human heart, could not be grasped, he mercifully moves and recalls it through enigmas, and through known things secretly insinuates to it the love of unknown things, so that, since it had to be addressed allegorically, through the exterior things which it knew it might understand the interior things, which it ought to love and keep. For here words as it were of bodily love are set down, where kisses, breasts, cheeks, thighs are named; that the soul, shaken out of its torpor [1012D] by accustomed speech, may grow warm again, and through the words of the love which is below may be stirred to the love which is above. In which the sacred description is not to be made void nor despised, but the mercy of God is to be considered, and in this the ampler, because, to kindle us to the embraces of sacred love, he inclines himself even to the words of our infirmity. But whence he humbles himself in speech, thence he exalts us in understanding, because from the speech of this love we learn how we should serve in the love of the Godhead. Greatly, or skilfully, must we look to it, lest, when we hear exterior things, we remain in those exterior things by our perceiving, and the machine which is set to lift should itself rather press down and weigh down. We ought therefore in the exterior words to seek whatever is within, and, speaking of the body, to become outside the body; [1013A] we ought to the nuptials of the bridegroom and bride with the understanding of charity [nt: (to come with the wedding garment, lest, if we lack it, into the outer darkness (Matth. XXII) )] we be driven back into the blindness of ignorance.

It is to be known besides that Solomon put forth three books, the first of *Proverbs,* the second *Ecclesiastes,* the third the *Canticles of Canticles,* which are placed, each under each, beneath the three parts of philosophy, namely Physics, Ethics, Logic, that is, natural, moral, rational science. For in *Ecclesiastes* he disputes of the natural state of things; in *Proverbs* he discourses of the honesty of morals; in the *Canticles of Canticles* he sings of the rational disposition of divine mysteries. For by these three modes, as by certain steps, the progress of the mind is led, that is, by naturality, [1013B] morality, rationality. Naturality is the visible variation of things created in order; morality, the informing of an honest life; rationality, the desirable attainment of spiritual and divine knowledge.

Indeed, from the consideration of naturality, while it is heeded of how great beauty, of how great utility, how becomingly, how rationally, and how orderly the created things have been disposed, how unchangeably it keeps its appointed turns in place and time, it can be sufficiently weighed of what kind and how great, how much more beautiful, how much more sublime, how much more powerful, and therefore how justly to be worshipped, how worthily to be adored, is God, the founder of this creature. Whence the Apostle: *The invisible things,* he says, *of him from the creation of the world through the things which are made, being understood, are clearly seen, his eternal power also and divinity, [1013C] so that they are inexcusable [n: (Rom. I)]*. Therefore man will not be able to be excused, because by consideration of this kind he is led to the knowledge of the Creator, and to the faith which believes to be true the things which God promises to the just, threatens to the unjust; and from this he is pricked, first by fear, afterwards by love.

For first by fear he rises to the honesty of morality, until, by the progress of conversation, morality itself is taken up through charity, which *casteth out fear [n: (I Joan. IV)]*. For since man is more excellent than every earthly creature, and the creature itself is, as we have said, of such great beauty, surely he will be able to weigh how much more beautiful he will be, if through the comeliness of mortality he cleaves to his Creator. For to the image [1013D] and likeness of God man was made [n: (Gen. II)], who is compounded of two substances, that is, of soul and body: he has the likeness of God in this, that he lives and among the other creatures is called very good, raised up originally by the very bearing of his body for receiving heavenly things and trampling earthly ones. But because, just as God himself neither grows with growing creatures nor decreases, although he yet is in all; so neither is the soul diminished when the members are diminished, nor increased when they are increased, and yet it is in all.

But he has the image of God from the better portion, namely the soul, which thrives in wit, reason, understanding, in which the image of God is indeed shown, by which he is conformed to him through the honesty of morality, so that, *as* he bore *the image of the earthly* by loving earthly things, *he may bear [1014A] also the image of the heavenly [n: (I Cor. XV)]*, by ardently seeking out and exercising heavenly things. For it is not to be thought childishly that God, who is incorporeal and incomprehensible, can be comprehended by any bodily lineaments, so that he should have an image to which man was made; but his image is the reason implanted in the human mind, by which man himself differs from irrational things, and by which he discerns the just from the unjust, the honest from the dishonest, the useful from the useless, by which finally he represses the bestial motions of the mind, lest they corrupt the integrity of the mind; and if, some frailty impelling, they have corrupted it, he may reform it anew, the same reason leading, through the study of holy morality.

By which study, man being morally instructed, the grace of rationality worthily advances him, by which he may receive knowledge of himself [1014B] and of God: of himself indeed, by which he will weigh what he is, and whence the gift which he receives, that he is saved not by his own merit but by the grace of God, [and] has been taken up *in the liberty of the glory of the sons of God [n: (Rom. VIII)]*: but of God, by which he may understand that nothing is better, nothing more powerful, nothing more sublime than God, whom alone he ought to know, fear, venerate, love and worship with all the virtue of his mind. And this is *the wisdom which will not enter into a malicious soul, nor dwell in a body subject to sins, because the Holy Spirit of discipline will flee the deceitful, and will withdraw himself from thoughts that are without understanding, and will be rebuked by iniquity coming upon it [n: (Sap. I)]*.

Therefore the virtue of morality takes up the grace of rationality, because the mind which draws near to God through the honesty of discipline considers the more the subtlety of his knowledge [1014C], according to the voice of James, who exhorts his hearers, saying: *Draw nigh to God and he will draw nigh to you [n: (Jacob. IV)]*.



## GENERAL PREFACE OF WOLBERO ON THE CANTICLE OF CANTICLES OF SOLOMON. (cont.)

This threefold progress of the soul the Psalmist prays for, saying: *Goodness and discipline and knowledge teach me [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*: for first he puts goodness, then discipline, in the third place knowledge. For God, being naturally good, created man naturally good, since he made him to his own image and likeness, reason being given to him by which he could discern good and evil; but he himself, understanding that he can [do] the one of these, the good, not from himself but from God, asks to be taught goodness, that through the goodness of naturality, by the affection of holy morality, discipline, [1014D] through which he may be able to advance to that incomprehensible and divine knowledge of rationality; and, ever demanding the order of this progress with the whole desire of his mind, he cries out: *goodness,* he says, *and discipline and knowledge teach me.* As if he said in other words: Since thou, O Lord God, hast created me naturally good; how that very good of nature may not be corrupted by my fault, but propagated by thy grace, teach [me], so that in this way I may will and be able by thy help to live rightly, and, living rightly, to penetrate the secrets of thy mysteries with the citadel of the mind.

To this third part, then, which we have called rationality, this book is subscribed, on account of the excellence of the sacraments which are sung therein in Christ and the Church, or in any whatever, beloved of God, of the mind; which [1015A] is also deservedly inscribed with this name which is the *Canticles of Canticles,* because, marked as by a certain privilege of spiritual harmony, more perfectly and more sublimely than all canticles, it intones mystical things. For if you consider more subtly, you will understand that almost all the sacraments of Christ and the Church are sung in it. For, to speak by way of example, in the very beginning of the canticle, when it is said in the person of the Church: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth,* the coming of Christ through the flesh is longed for, as if the Church said in other words: Thou who once spokest to me through the mouths of thy prophets and of the other Fathers, now at length, born according to the flesh, offer thyself in presence, and with thine own mouth hold out to me the decrees of thy commandments. The passion also of Christ and the resurrection it denotes in that place where the beloved [1015B] speaks: *A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me, he shall abide between my breasts,* and presently after, *a cluster of Cyprus is my beloved to me [n: (Cant. I)]*, where by the myrrh the bitterness of the passion, by the cluster the sweetness of the resurrection is expressed. And elsewhere: *Flee,* she says, *my beloved, and be like to a roe or to a young hart upon the mountains of spices [n: (Cant. VIII)]* [var: Vulg. *capreae, hinnuloque cervorum*, "to a roe and to a young hart"]; by the roe and the young hart suggesting the power of the Lord ascending into heaven. In the flock of shorn ones coming up from the washing [n: (Cant. IV, VI)], the people of the baptized laying down the burdens of sins is understood; in the twin offspring, the twin things of charity; and in the eyes, the teeth, the cheeks, the hair, and the other arrangements of members which are named there, the spiritual adornment and arrangement of holy Church, or of any holy soul, is described. There the depravity of heretics, [1015C] the sweetness of doctors is denoted; and, to comprehend the whole, whatever in holy Church is done, generally or specially, or is celebrated as done, is sung with most beautiful allusions, so that deservedly this book is called by such a name, which is the *Canticles of Canticles*, for its excellence.

Our purpose, therefore, in this work is to investigate, as we are able, the rationality of these sacraments; but chiefly, for your edification, holy sisters, to look into morality and to stir up your mind in the love of your bridegroom, the Lord Jesus Christ.

We have divided the whole book into four tomes, on account of the four subdistinctions of the recapitulations of the periochae, [1015D] which are marked by this chapter thrice repeated: *I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the goats, that you stir not up nor make the beloved to awake, till she herself will.* [var: Vulg. Cant. 2:7 *filiae Jerusalem, per capreas cervosque camporum*, "daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields"]

Of these periochae, then, the first is from where it says: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth,* up to: *I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem,* the second from where it says: *The voice of my beloved; behold he cometh, leaping upon the mountains, skipping over the hills,* up to, likewise: *I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem.* The third, longer, which begins thus: *Who is she that goeth up by the desert,* up to the third adjuration: *I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem.* The fourth. *Who is she that goeth up from the desert, flowing with delights,* up to the end.

The singings of these four parts we have distinguished into four [1016A] little books: either on account of the evangelical chariot of Aminadab, or the moral one, which consists of the four cardinal virtues, which the bride, troubled, dreaded; or that the mind studious of morality may be admonished that it ought to be squared by the virtues, so that, as by squareness it stands on whatever [face] it uses, so he is neither lifted up by prosperity nor cast down by adversity, but, like a squared stone placed in the building of the Lord, is conformed to the passion of Christ by the mortification of the carnal life, the bearer of his cross, *that* he may be able *to comprehend with all the saints what is the length, breadth, sublimity and depth [n: (Ephes. III)]* [var: Vulg. *latitudo, et longitudo*, "breadth, and length", the reverse of the order the exposition follows]. The virtue of which comprehension belongs to the perfect alone, who, *rooted and founded in charity [n: (ibid.)]*, so cleave to their Creator that by the amplitude of charity they continually sing the *Canticle of Canticles*; in whom [1016B] this squareness of charity consists. For charity in the saints is long, because it is not for an hour, but persevering and continual; broad, because it extends even to enemies; high, because it is had for the sake of the hope and reward of eternal life; deep, because it penetrates even the secrets of God; *no man*, however, *hath known the things which are of God, save the Spirit of God [n: (I Cor. II)]*, through whom charity itself is poured forth in the hearts of the saints [n: (Rom. V)].

In the head himself also, our true exemplar, the Lord, that is, Christ, the beauty of this squareness shines forth delightfully, figured in the manner of his passion, namely in the form of the cross. For from the top of the cross down to the bottom, the length of his charity is expressed, of whom John says that *having loved [1016C] his own who were in the world, he loved them unto the end [n: (Joan. XIII)]*: and the breadth, stretched from the right to the left, is extended to the love of enemies, for whom, supplicating, *Father,* he says, *pardon them, because they know not what they do [n: (Luc. XXIII)]*; the sublimity is regarded in the inscription of the title: for scarcely can the height of his kindness, by which he rules, cherishes and guards us, consider; the depth, through the part fixed in the earth, which sustains the rest, hides the abyss of the judgments of God, because, though God has done and suffered so much for the salvation of men, why he takes up this one, repels that one, is inscrutable and unsearchable.

You therefore, O beloved sisters, crucified to the world with the Lord, learn to comprehend with all [1016D] the saints the length, breadth, sublimity and depth of charity, so that, led into the chamber of our Lord, your bridegroom, with lighted lamps, that is, good works, you may be able to sing continually before the throne of God that new canticle to be sung by virgins alone, truly the Canticle of Canticles.

But now let us come to the course of the book itself, forewarning this: that if it moves anyone that the discourse is stretched beyond measure, and the speech overflows the mode of explanation, let him know that we have undertaken this work not only for the sake of explaining; but rather with this intention, that thence we might overcome the matter for ourselves, whence we might satisfy, at least by writings, the religious desire of the holy sisters, whom we could not by words.

## THE LORD ABBOT WOLBERO, OF THE ORDER OF ST BENEDICT, ON THE CANTICLE OF CANTICLES OF SOLOMON.

[1017]

## BOOK ONE.



## CHAPTER ONE.

[1017]

* 2* VERS. 1.-- *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*

If the Son of God is the Word of God, of whom John says: *In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was [1017B] with God, and the Word was God [n: (Joan. I)]*, assuredly the same Son of God is also amply understood as the mouth of God, because a word is brought forth by the mouth. Not unfittingly can we take the kiss to be the Holy Spirit, because the peace and charity which are signified by a kiss are given through the Holy Spirit, in whom the remission of sins is made, through whom we are reconciled to God, through whom is given the distribution of gifts by which we are joined to God [n: (Rom. V)]. But as the substance of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit is undivided, so too is their operation undivided; and in the whole Trinity, although there is distinction in the persons, yet the will and the operation are one and the same. The one kissing, then, and the mouth, and the kiss, the three work one thing, that is, charity in the hearts [1017C] of those who share in this kiss; because, as we have said, the operation of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit is one; and the kiss of the mouth, that is, of the Word, which is the Son, is also the kiss of the one kissing, that is, of the Father, because the Holy Spirit is of the Father and of the Son; and the kiss is the grace and peace and charity of this undivided Trinity. Through the kiss of the mouth of the one kissing, then, that is, through the Holy Spirit, of the Father and of the Son, whose substance is one, one majesty, one virtue, one power, one will, one operation: first indeed is given the remission of sins, by the kiss of peace, the reconciling kiss; then the distribution of gifts, by the kiss of charity, the contemplative kiss.

This kiss, then, of a twofold grace, holy Church universally, or [1017D] any holy soul particularly, seeks in its own order from the Lord her Creator, pondering and considering that the creating Trinity, which is everywhere present through the power of the divinity, is not present through the grace of indwelling, which is not everywhere save through love, through which the indwelling of the divinity is known, and conversely love through the indwelling of the divinity: and therefore she asks that the kiss be held out to her [sic: *sibipo rrigi*], first, as we have said, the reconciling kiss through the remission of sins, so that she may then be able to merit the contemplative kiss through the grace of gifts. But since the proof of love is the showing of the work, assuredly through the showing of the work the grace of the indwelling divinity is recognized; because love working and working loving is the kiss of the mouth of the one kissing, that is, the grace of the Trinity dwelling wholly in the mind [1018] *3,* Whence the Lord says in the Gospel: *If anyone love me he will keep my word, and my Father will love him, and we will come to him and will make our abode [1018B] with him [n: (Joan. XIV)]*.

She says, then: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.* As if she should say in other words: May my Creator himself, by his grace, so kindle this heart of mine that I may love him, that loving I may please him by working, that pleasing him I may be made worthy of his indwelling.

Or, that we may refer the mystery of his incarnation to the person of the Son alone: the Church longs that she may hear him speaking in his own person, whom until then she had heard through the mouths of the Fathers. For the Word that was to be incarnate first promised the secrets of his precepts or sacraments in the law and the prophets; which, coming afterwards, he himself fulfilled, and sealed as things to be observed unchangeably, as it were by the setting of his hand to them: whence he says through the prophet: *Therefore my people shall know [1018C] my name in that day, because I who spoke, behold, I am here [var: Vulg. Is 52:6 *ego ipse qui loquebar*, "I myself who spoke"] [n: (Isa. LII)]*.

That speech indeed, by which he spoke through the law and the prophets as through an instrument, was obscure and indeterminable; but when he himself came and spoke in his own person, it was made determinable and plain; because, the truth being present, the shadow had to cease. But because the preceding speech, whether through an angel, or through a man, or through some other creature, was made in many ways, that is, either by voice, or by sign, or by operation, or by vision, or by the touch of the heart alone; the following one through the very mediator of God and men; the blessed Apostle says *that in many ways and in many manners God, speaking of old to the fathers in the prophets, last of all [1018D] in these days hath spoken to us in the Son [n: (Hebr I)]*; assuredly, as a craftsman is far better than his instrument, so this last speech, since the Son of God brought it forth himself in his own person, is far worthier and more to be desired than that speech which he used in the Fathers as in instruments. It is last for this reason, because after it nothing could be, nor will be able to be, said or written more firmly or more certainly.

The sweetness of this speech, then, as being the true truth of truth, to be revealed to her understanding, holy Church, burning with the whole ardour of her soul, cries out for: *let him kiss me,* she says, *with the kiss of his mouth.* Now a kiss is the sign of peace or of love: and of this truly peaceable Lord Jesus Christ it is written that *coming he preached peace to those who were far off and to those [1019A] who were near [n: (Ephes. II)]*. But what peace? That, surely, which dissolves the enmities between God and man, which the holy prophets did indeed announce, but could not give. But he, *the Angel of great counsel* [var: Vulg. Is 9:6 *Admirabilis, Consiliarius*, "Wonderful, Counsellor"] and *Prince of peace [n: (Isa. IX)]*, both announced it and gave it. Wherefore holy Church, sighing for his presence, his address and his affability, rightly desires with all her prayers to enjoy to the full his converse, as the kiss of a peaceable mouth sowing and giving true and whole peace, saying: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of thy mouth,* [var: Vulg. Ct 1:1 *oris sui*, "of his mouth"] as if she should say: May he himself show forth to me in his own person what the holy Fathers *4* of old confirmed by sayings, writings and signs as something to be shown forth.

And why she desires this rather, she adds the cause: *for thy breasts are better,* she says, *than wine,* that is, the nourishments [1019B] of evangelical gentleness provide better and more quickly for my salvation than the austerity of the legal sentence, or the swelling and empty loquacity of the philosophers; of which it will be treated more fully under the second verse.

Morally, however, the soul beloved of God, having now attained that noble and incomparable celibacy, and having been brought into the Holy of holies of her bridegroom, her Creator and her Redeemer, secret and inaccessible to the human mind, rejoicing; *let him kiss me,* she says, *with the kiss of his mouth.* But whence comes this proclamation, so great, of such a kind, so confidently and sublimely presuming [sic: *praesumes*]? surely *from a pure heart and a good conscience and faith unfeigned [n: (I Tim. V)]*. For this is the method of attaining perfection: to know what, in what manner, why, that is, the matter, the intention, the usefulness of working. For it must be [1019C] greatly considered what thou doest, with what intention, with what fruit. For every action is conceived in the heart, nourished by intention, perfected by faith. For indeed if thou beginnest anything good, the intention of that good itself will be its judge, because *if thy eye be single, thy whole body will be lightsome; but if evil, it will be darksome [n: (Luc. XI)]*. Moreover faith either destroys or advances the work itself. For a man either believes or does not believe God, who promises his worshipper those things *which neither eye hath seen, nor ear heard, nor into the heart of man* have they ascended [n: (I Cor. II)]. For he who is unfaithful acts unfaithfully, and he who is faithful acts faithfully.

For faith can be divided in three ways, so that it is called faith dead, feigned, proved. *Dead faith* may be called that which, [1019D] according to the Apostle, *is without works [n: (Jac. I)]*, which, insensible, as though having no soul, that is, charity, is quickened or moved to no good work. Feigned faith is that which, not rooted in charity, according to the Lord's word, *believeth in time, and in time of temptation falleth away* [var: Vulg. Lc 8:13 *ad tempus credunt*, "believe for a while"] [n: (Luc. VIII)]. But proved faith is that which fails under no tribulations, under no adversities, because it is firmly rooted in charity.

Let the faithful and prudent soul, then, be zealous with *faith unfeigned* to keep charity *from a pure heart and a good conscience [n: (I Tim. I)]*, which is the perfection of the interior habit, which habit *charity* alone produces, that charity which *being perfect casteth out fear [n: (I Joan. IV)]*; which these three approve: a pure heart, a good conscience, [1020A] faith unfeigned, which nevertheless proceed from charity itself. For who *keepeth his heart with all watchfulness [n: (Prov. IV)]*, that he may have a good conscience, save from charity? And who keeps faith firm and unshaken, save from charity? These three, then, are probative of charity, and true charity itself is effective of them.

These are, in the Gospel, the oil, the vessels, the lamps, adorned with which the virgins, not foolish but wise, await the coming of their Bridegroom, our Lord *5* Jesus Christ, that they may enter with him to the wedding of weddings. For what is the oil, save a good work? And what the vessels, save the consciences, which, namely, carry those same works? And what the lamps, save the fervour and splendour of those same works? For if holy men are fervent in charity, they are splendid in the showing of it; so that to those who see [1020B] they may be for an example, to those who work, for a light. They carry oil in their vessels with their lamps, then, who cover a good work in a glad and secure conscience, with this intention, that they both may not seek [sic: *quaerat*] human praise, and may afford their neighbours an example of working well.

*From a heart,* then, *pure, and a good conscience and faith unfeigned [n: (I Tim. I)]*, let the soul beloved of God set up a ladder for herself, on which she may dispose ascents, by which, *from virtue to virtue [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*, she may make her way through charity [sic: *charita em*] to that highest good which is God, taking her form from her Bridegroom himself, Jesus Christ, who, *the Lord high above all nations [n: (Psal. CXII)]*, came humble among the nations, that he might teach us to climb his humility thither whence our first parents had descended through pride. For humility leads [1020C] her through charity to that vision of the Deity, as it were *to the third heaven,* to which the Apostle asserts that he was *caught up,* *and* that he heard ineffable *words, which it is not lawful for a man to speak [n: (II Cor. XII)]*.

Let us posit that, as it were, the first heaven is humility, the second charity, the third the sublimity of contemplation. When, then, the mind, lifted up, rises to the virtue of humility as to the first heaven, all the pleasures of the world being now despised and trodden underfoot, through *charity, which many waters* cannot *quench; for love is strong as death [n: (Cant. VIII)]*; it ascends to the second heaven; and when it has advanced so far that it thinks of nothing else, desires nothing else but God, it is lifted up through contemplation to the third heaven, [1020D] where, set above itself, and marvelling at the hidden things of the divine wisdom, it says: *My secret to myself, my secret to myself [n: (Isa. XXIX)]*. In which secret, now inebriated by the torrent of that ineffable delight, her face washed and her lips purged *with desolating coals [n: (Psal. CXIX)]*, she lingers in the longed-for embraces of her Bridegroom, saying: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*

But what is this confidence, that she so suddenly seeks the kiss of her Lord's mouth, when, as it were, reason does not permit one to approach, without order and some degree, a thing so eminent and supreme?



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Whence it is to be considered that certain lower kisses also went before that one: first indeed the kiss of redemption, then the kiss of reconciliation, and at the summit the kiss of love. And the kiss [1021A] of redemption is as it were the kiss of the Lord's feet; the kiss of reconciliation is as it were the kiss of the hands; but the kiss of love is as it were the kiss of the mouth. For by the feet, with which we are moved from place to place, the humanity of the Saviour is designated, the dispensation of which humanity, namely *6* that he was crowned with thorns, suffered, died, and was buried, and rose again, lay in movement and passage: for *Christ,* *rising from the dead, dieth now no more, death shall no more have dominion over him [n: (Rom. VI)]*; because through this [man] went out from the captivity of the devil into the liberty of Christ, and according to the prophet there rotted *the yoke before the face of the oil [n: (Isa. X)]*. For since in Greek ἔλεος is called in Latin *mercy,* and it is the nature of God [cj: *Dei*; read *olei*, "of oil"] to be borne above all liquids, and *the mercy* moreover *of the Lord is above all [1021B] his works [var: Vulg. Ps 144:9 *miserationes ejus*, "his tender mercies"] [n: (Psal. CXLIV)]*; when God looked upon the world in mercy, the yoke of its captivity rotted [sic: *computrui*] at the coming of the Saviour.

Now we kiss the feet of our Redeemer when we embrace the mysteries of his humanity with faith and devotion, and imitate, as far as we can, the footsteps of his humility, patience and obedience. But, the captivity being taken away, did man forthwith merit to be joined to God? For by his own fault, through free will, he had come into the captivity of the devil; because, though he had been created good by nature, he slipped into evil by his own vice. Because, then, he had rebelled against God in will and in deed, and by this had withdrawn far from his grace, he had need of reconciliation.

The ministers of God, our holy Fathers, went before, [1021C] pleading the cause of this reconciliation by preaching, exhorting, teaching and frequently announcing his precepts; and they could indeed free him from error, but could not bestow perfect liberty on him. His Creator himself came after, taking on his mortality, that he might give him his own immortality; and *having destroyed the middle wall, dissolving the enmities in his flesh through his blood [var: Vulg. Eph 2:14 *medium parietem maceriae solvens, inimicitias in carne sua*, without *destructo* or *per sanguinem*] [n: (Ephes. II)]*, he presented him with liberty by the kiss of Redemption, and then presently joined the same man to himself by the kiss of reconciliation: he who, bending himself downward from that height of his divinity, having torn up the old handwriting of sin, wrote on the earth the new testament of this peace and reconciliation through the sacrament of baptism, in which, as though the grace of manumission [1021D] were given, he restored perfect liberty; and adopting him as a son, whom before he had not even deigned to have as a servant because of the voluntary yoke of sin, he gave him the deed of such an inheritance as *neither eye hath seen, nor ear heard, nor hath it ascended into the heart of man [n: (I Cor. II)]*. And this testament or deed holy Church holds now and will hold, or any faithful soul, so that, if she does not cease to know, to have and to love God as her true Father, she may always be secure of such an inheritance.

We hold out a kiss to the hands of the Lord, then, when we observe the covenant or decree of this reconciliation in faith and work, and, consenting even in the way of this life with our adversary, that is, the word of God, we have perpetual [1022A] peace with him, by living justly and piously.

*7* But after reconciliation, as though security had been received, man began to draw near to the divinity more freely and more joyfully; and according to what James says: *Draw nigh to God and he will draw nigh to you [n: (Jac. IV)]*, the more he drew near to God through zeal for good work, the nearer he made God to himself through contemplation, so that, having set aside all the business of the world and trodden down earthly desires, he says, ascending the mountain with Moses: *Lord, show me thy face [n: (Exod. XXXIII)]*; and, cleaving delightedly in the vision of him, he advances so far in the ardour of divine charity that he seeks the kiss of his mouth, saying: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*

Not suddenly, therefore, but little by little, and as it were by certain [1022B] steps, he comes to the ineffable sweetness of that supreme charity, as to the kiss of the Lord's mouth: so that the first step is the redeeming kiss, the second the reconciling kiss, the third the contemplative kiss. For this is also the third heaven, to which the Apostle, asserting that he was caught up, received the grace of a special knowledge from that principal kiss of the Lord's mouth, and came to all the churches throughout the world, preaching the sweetness of that same kiss and exhorting them to the perfection of so great a holiness.

The grace of this threefold kiss that woman in the Gospel prefigured by her dutiful service, when first, falling devoutly at the feet of the Lord, kissing them, watering them with tears, wiping them with her hair, she obtained pardon of her sins, as though the redeeming kiss [1022C] had been given her, when she heard: *Thy sins are forgiven thee [n: (Luc. VII)]*. And presently, having received as it were from the hand of the Lord the written tablets of liberty, she is presented with the reconciling kiss, when, reconciled to God through the remission of sins, it is said to her: *Go in peace [n: (ibid.)]*. Thereafter, by the progress of a good way of life, she cleaved to the Lord with such ardour of charity, as though by the kiss of his mouth, the contemplative kiss, that she was praised by the proclamation of the Lord himself, saying: *Mary hath chosen the best part, which shall not be taken away from her [n: (Luc. X)]*.

We can also, not absurdly, take this threefold kiss as faith, hope, charity, so that faith is as it were the kiss of the Lord's feet, hope of his hands, charity of his mouth; for by faith we are saved, by hope we are lifted up, [1022D] by charity we are glorified. By faith we embrace the feet of the Lord, that is, the sacraments of his humanity, which pertains to salvation; by hope we reach the hands of the Lord, by which we are led to hope for the heavenly promises, which pertains to patience; for *if we hope for the things we see not, we wait for them through patience [n: (Rom. VIII)]*; but by charity we hold out a kiss as it were to the mouth of the Lord, when we are presented by him with eternal beatitude, which pertains to reward.

*8* Let the whole Church, then, say generally, or any holy soul specially: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth;* with the kiss, she says, of the mouth, not as before of the prophets or of any other teachers whatever, but *his;* because he himself by his teaching and example has established for me a form of living, through which, by his own help, all the obstacles of this world being repulsed [1023A] and cast away, and perfect liberty now attained, they may cleave to him continually by the kiss of love, until, immortality and incorruption of body and soul having at length been taken on, I may be joined to him unchangeably and inseparably, and may say truly, *let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth:* that what *now* I see *through a glass in a dark manner, then* I may see *face to face [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*, *when God shall be all in all [n: (I Cor. XV)]*. These things, therefore, I desire, this I seek, this I long for with all my prayers. Why?

VERS. 2.-- *For thy breasts are better than wine, fragrant with the best ointments.*

What are these breasts of thine? The remission of sins, the distribution of gifts. These console me when I weep, these nourish me when I am little and bring me forward into manly strength: [1023B] these call me, draw me, soothe me; because they cry to me through thy Gospel: *Do penance, for the kingdom of heaven will draw near [var: Vulg. Mt 3:2 *appropinquavit*, "is at hand"] [n: (Matth. III)]*. But the wine of the biting law terrifies me by its authority, turns me away and exasperates me; because it cries: *Eye for eye, tooth for tooth, and whoever does this or that, let him die the death [n: (Exod. XXI)]*. These breasts of thine, I say, greatly invite me, animate me, and are strengthened, because of the exceeding abundance of thy mercy and the prerogative of thy gifts: but since from one of these lashes [cj: *verberum*; read *uberum*, "breasts"] of thine flows the remission of sins, from the other the distribution of gifts, the order is that the remission of sins be given first, then the distribution of gifts. But the greatness of his mercy sometimes makes that which is later, that [1023C] is, the distribution of gifts, and that which is earlier, that is, the remission of sins, run before in certain persons, for this reason, namely, that they may be the more roused and may labour with thy grace which calls them to receive the remission of sins.

Which, for example's sake, may be considered in Cornelius the centurion, in whom the gift of thy grace in kinds of tongues went before; so that, the more animated by it and as it were assured, he might come more readily to receive the sacrament of faith unto the remission of sins. In Paul the apostle also the same gift of thy grace ran before, in the gift of understanding and discernment, to whom, when Ananias had been sent and had come: *Saul,* he said, *brother; the Lord Jesus hath sent me, who appeared to thee in the way by which thou camest, that thou mayest see and be filled [1023D] with the Holy Spirit [n: (Act. II)]*. Did this seeing of his pertain only to the illumination of his bodily eyes, and not rather to the illumination of the mind, by which he might see and know *9* the truth which he was attacking? Was not this also to be filled with the Holy Spirit. For to this end thou gavest the Holy Spirit to him while not yet baptized, that he might know how to discern between the shadow of the law, which he was defending, and the truth of the Gospel, which he was attacking; and, having left the shadow, might come the more eagerly and fervently, by the help of the same Holy Spirit, to receive the truth, unto the remission of sins. These evangelical breasts of thine, then, which draw to salvation because of their gentleness, are better than those legal institutions which frighten away from salvation because of their austerity.

[1024A]But that I may consider something more deeply: what also, O beloved, *are* these *breasts of thine,* save the ineffable sweetness and glory of those heavenly secrets of thine, of which the Psalmist says: *I shall be satisfied while thy glory shall appear? [var: Vulg. Ps 16:15 *cum*, "when"] [n: (Psal. XVI.)]* Into this wondrous secret chamber of thine, then, after I shall have ascended through contemplation by the gift of thy grace, I shall be able to be torn from thy embraces neither by any blandishments of alluring prosperities nor by any scourges of frightening adversities, *for thy breasts are better than wine;* better, nay rather incomparable, are that paradise of thy delight and the manifestation of thy glory than the pleasures and passing honours of this world. And of what kind are these breasts of thine? *Fragrant with the best ointments.*

For these ointments are the signs not of any breasts whatever, [1024B] but of thine, redolent assuredly with such sweetness that by their very odour it becomes known what they are and whose they are. But what are these ointments? Surely those which that most excellent perfumer or spice-maker of thine, the Holy Spirit, has compounded and compounds. And what are the spices of which these ointments are made? Assuredly those which the Apostle, enumerating them in a certain place, calls spiritual fruits: *the fruits,* he says, *of the spirit are: peace, patience, goodness, kindness, faith, mildness, obedience, continence, chastity [var: Vulg. Gal 5:22–23 *caritas, gaudium, pax, patientia, benignitas, bonitas, longanimitas, mansuetudo, fides, modestia, continentia, castitas*; no *obedientia*] [n: (Gal. V)]*.

How good and useful a tree in the garden of the Lord, producing such kinds of fruits, and, rejoicing for its fruitfulness, proclaiming: *But I, as a fruitful olive tree [1024C] in the house of the Lord [var: Vulg. Ps 51:10 *in domo Dei*, "in the house of God"] [n: (Psal. LI)]*. The cultivator of this tree is assuredly the Holy Spirit, who takes such fruits to himself for compounding the ointments with which the beloved's breasts are fragrant, which are conferred on those who shall have been anointed with these ointments and can say with the Apostle: *We are the good odour of Christ unto God in every place [var: Vulg. 2Cor 2:15 *in iis qui salvi fiunt*, "in them that are saved"] [n: (II Cor. II)]*; because to such is given the remission of sins and the distribution of graces.

But to the sweetness of ointments of this kind, seasoned from the aforesaid kinds of virtues, no human reason makes progress without the aid of grace; because man, although capable of reason, begins or perfects nothing holy, nothing reasonable, unless that compounder of those same ointments, the Holy Spirit, works it by his interior unction, as John says of him to his hearers: [1024D] And *the unction* *10* *of him will teach you concerning all things [var: Vulg. 1Jo 2:27 *docet*, "teacheth"] [n: (I Joan. II)]*.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Such an unction, assuredly, is the true medicine of the inner man and the strongest antidote against the diseases of the soul, by which it heals the soul wounded by the wound of sin and quickens her, enlightens the mind, rouses her to merit the gifts of divine grace, and lastly pervades her with such vigour of this sweetness that, forgetting the things behind and stretching forth to those before, she raises herself even above herself, saying with the Apostle: *But our conversation is in heaven [n: (Philip. III)]*, where, liberty from all misery having been attained, she may be satisfied *from the breasts of the consolation of God,* [var: Vulg. Is 66:11 *ab ubere consolationis ejus*, "from the breast of her consolation"] and may feast from the entrance of his glory [n: (Isa. LXVI)].

The breasts of this beloved can also, not unfittingly, be taken as the teaching and the working of our Lord [1025A] Jesus Christ, by which, in his humanity, he made himself known to the world with the best ointments of the virtues, fragrant now as well: then indeed nourishing the first-fruits of the new faith with the milk of teaching and confirming them by the power of working, but now strengthening the fullness of that same faith as with the solid food of teaching, and forming it by the example of working to the perfection of an honourable life.

These *breasts,* assuredly, fragrant with the ointment of so great a mercy and grace, *are better than wine* by far, that is, than the austerity of the royal ceremonies, or of things done: as truth is better than the shadow, the quickening spirit than the killing letter: *For the law,* as the Apostle says, *added nothing to perfection* [var: Vulg. Hbr 7:19 *adduxit*, "brought"]; but *all things happened to them in figure; and they are written for our correction, upon whom the ends of the ages are come [n: (I Cor. X)]*. Or [1025B] they are better, or rather incomparable, than wine, that is, than the pleasure of worldly doctrines, which, when they infect their hearers with the ointment of carnal delight, drag them to the death of the soul; and thus, from the opposite side, as much as the soul advances toward thy grace by the consolation of thy breasts, O beloved, so much does she fall away from that grace of thine by the deadly concoction of those others.

*Thy name is oil poured out: therefore the young maidens have loved thee.*

They who behold me wonder, O beloved, that I praise thee so, that I proclaim so great a grace of thy mercy and sweetness, that I desire thy countenance, thy embraces, with such avidity; and they long to know of what name thou art, or of what dignity, whom I esteem so highly, judge so highly; to whom, lest they excuse themselves on the ground of ignorance, because [1025C] thy name has not been announced to them, I say that *thy name* is not hidden, is not concealed, but rather manifest, and as it were *oil poured out,* whose nature is that when it is poured out it spreads the more.

How, then, has thy name been poured out and spread abroad? Not as once thou wast *known in Judaea* only, and only *in Israel great is thy name [n: (Psal. LXXV)]*, but as thou sayest through another prophet *11*: *From the rising of the sun even to the going down, great is thy name among the nations [n: (Malach. I)]*. *The name which is above every name, so that in thy name every knee should bow, of those in heaven, on earth and under the earth [n: (Philip. II)]*. *And there is no other name under heaven given to men, through [1025D] which we must be saved [n: (Act. IV)]*.

And that I may draw them up with me to higher things, that they may understand and know why I so greatly desire thy kiss, why I delight in the sweetness of thy breasts, let them hear something further, and let them weigh why I have said, *thy name is oil poured out.* For oil is borne above all liquids. What, then, is understood by oil, save charity, which excels all the virtues? For no virtue can be called a virtue, or be one, without charity; because where charity is not, there is no virtue. For *if I speak with the tongues of men and of angels, and have not charity, I am become as sounding brass or a tinkling cymbal; and if I should have prophecy and should know all mysteries and all knowledge, and if I should have faith so that I could remove mountains, and [1026A] have not charity, it profiteth me nothing; and if I should distribute all my goods to feed the poor, and if I should deliver my body to be burned, and have not charity, I am nothing [var: Vulg. 1Cor 13:2–3 ends v. 2 *nihil sum* and v. 3 *nihil mihi prodest*; v. 2 *omnem fidem*] [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*.

Behold of how great virtue, of how great excellence, do you think, charity is, so that without it neither prophecy, nor any knowledge, nor faith, nor almsgiving, nor martyrdom is anything? And what is this charity? Surely thou art it, O beloved, as that beloved one of thine testifies, cleaving to thy holy kiss, and, reclining on thy breast, drinking in the sweetness of thy breasts; he, I say, the heavenly eagle, flying with the wings of holy contemplation into thy very secrets, and directing the keenness of his mind upon thee, the sun of justice. And what does he say? *God is charity [n: (I Joan. IV)]*. [1026B] Thou, then, art called charity and art charity, O beloved, because charity made thee take on flesh and come into this world, and be humbled for human salvation even unto the death of the cross; and *for his exceeding charity with which God loved us [n: (Ephes. II)]*. Thee, his Son, he sent in the likeness of sinful flesh, that of sin thou mightest condemn sin [n: (Rom. VIII)], made a curse for our sakes, that thou mightest free us from the curse [n: (Gal. II)]. And how has this name of thine, the name of charity, been poured out and spread abroad? *Like the ointment on the head, that ran down upon the beard, the beard of Aaron, that ran down to the skirt of his garment [n: (Psal. CXXXII)]*. Thou art Aaron, that is, the mountain of strength, in whom, namely in our head, the distinction of this charity abounded specially, with which *God, thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of exultation [1026C] above thy fellows [n: (Psal. XLIV; Hebr. I)]*, namely of that exultation with which thou didst exult both to have shown [sic: *ostendi se*] so great an abundance of thy charity and to have fulfilled the order of our redemption.

The beard of this head were the apostles, or apostolic men, into whom the grace of this ointment flowed down so abundantly that they cleaved to thee, as a beard *12* to the head, firmly, with the ardour of holy charity, even unto death.

*That ran down to the skirt of thy garment,* which thy faithful have known, from the first age of the world up to this one in which we now are, we *upon whom the ends of the ages are come [n: (I Cor. X)]*, who are the skirt, that is, the end, of this garment of thine; in all of whom, namely both the former and the present and the future, the strength of this [1026D] ointment, that is, of charity, has laboured and labours [sic: *labora*], and will labour even in the very end of the world under the persecution of Antichrist for thy name. *Thy name,* then, *is oil poured out,* because *thy charity is poured forth in our hearts, through the Holy Spirit who is given to us [n: (Rom. V)]*.

And *Therefore the young maidens have loved thee exceedingly.* [var: Vulg. Ct 1:2 *dilexerunt te*, without *nimis*] Because, she says, so great is the sweetness of thy breasts, so great the abundance of thy charity, that gentleness rather than harshness is found in thee, rather a cherishing madness [cj: *dementia*; read *clementia*, "clemency"] than a striking sentence, *therefore the young maidens have loved thee exceedingly.*

But who are these young maidens, assuredly faithful souls, removed indeed from the milk of infancy, but not yet having attained the solidity of perfection; therefore [1027A] still weak, and needing to be sustained by divine help; and on that account they have greatly loved thee, O beloved, and love thee, because they attend to this in thee, that they in no way flee from it, but rather by it may at length grow strong more and more to the sublimity of perfection.

Now it is customary in Holy Scripture for the growths of the mind to be designated by the growths of bodies; because, as men advance by nature to greater ages, so the faithful advance by grace to greater virtues of the soul; whence Peter exhorts his hearers to infancy, that is, to innocence of life, saying: *As newborn infants, desire the rational milk without guile, that thereby you may grow unto salvation [n: (I Petr. II)]*. In the Gospel also the Saviour, exhorting his disciples to purity [1027B] and simplicity of life: *Unless,* he says, *you be converted, and become as little children, you shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven [n: (Matth. XVIII)]*. And the Apostle: *In malice,* he says, *be children, that in understanding you may be perfect [var: Vulg. 1Cor 14:20 *sensibus autem perfecti estote*, "but in understanding be perfect"] [n: (I Cor. V)]*. The Psalmist too, desiring the youth of his soul to be renewed after the example of the eagle, *shall be renewed,* he says, *like the eagle's thy youth [n: (Psal. CII)]*. So that, stripping off the old man with his deeds, he may put on the new [n: (Colos. III)], who according to God is created in justice and holiness [n: (Ephes. IV)].
