And in the book of Wisdom gravity of character is expressed by old age of mind, when it is said: *Old age is venerable, not that of long time, nor counted by the number of years; for the grey hairs are the understanding of a man, and a spotless life is old age [n: (Sap. IV)]*.

[1027C]Growth, then, can be divided in two ways, so that one speaks of *natural* growth and *affectual* growth. And natural growth is ascribed to every bodily creature which is moved naturally with vital vegetation according to its own property: *13* but affectual growth is granted in the present life to the rational creature alone, according to the measure of the giving grace, by the help of which grace it is promoted, against the vices of the flesh, by the strength of the spirit, to good things, and from good things to better.

But for the most part in Holy Scripture natural growth is significative of affectual, as the Psalmist: *And it shall please,* he says, *God better than a young calf that bringeth forth horns and hoofs [n: (Psal. LXVIII)]*. For if thou understandest it according to the letter, what care has God [1027D] for an irrational beast naturally bringing forth horns and hoofs? but if thou considerest the young calf more deeply according to the mystical sense, thou canst take it as the mind newly converted to the Lord, and budding with the flower of good action, which the Apostle calls the new man, saying: *Put on the new man, him who is renewed unto knowledge, according to the image of him that created him [n: (Col. III)]*. Enumerating within the constitutive parts of which man: *Put on,* he says, *as the elect of God, the bowels of mercy, benignity, humility, modesty, patience, bearing with one another and forgiving one another [n: (ibid.)]*.

The horns of this young calf, that is, of the new man, can, not absurdly, as I judge, be taken as constancy of mind and fortitude of spirit, or the spirit of fortitude, [1028A] resisting vices and sins and winnowing the adversaries of this new man

Further, as for its hoofs, with which it stirs the earth, nothing prevents thee from taking them as contempt of the world and love of God, by which, casting away all earthly things, it may bring its body into servitude, subjecting it to the spirit, so that it may co-operate and labour together with it by consent in the discipline of good works; so that in the future renewal, as it was a sharer of the labour, so it may become a sharer also of the reward; which is for the new man himself to be presented with a double stole, namely with the felicity of body and of soul.

Or understand the horns and hoofs of this calf as the sublime and special transports of the theoretic life; because, as horns and hoofs proceed indeed from the flesh but go beyond the flesh, so the theoric man, though he is in [1028B] the flesh, yet transcends carnal affections in mind, saying with the Apostle: *For walking in the flesh, we do not walk according to the flesh [var: Vulg. 2Cor 10:3 *militamus*, "we war"] [n: (II Cor. X)]*. And again: *But our conversation is in heaven [n: (Philip. III)]*; and elsewhere: *Whether we be sober, or whether we fall out of our mind, it is to God [var: Vulg. 2Cor 5:13 *sive enim mente excedimus Deo: sive sobrii sumus, vobis*, "whether we be transported in mind, it is to God; or whether we be sober, it is for you"] [n: (II Cor. V)]*.

But this calf, still not yet having horns and hoofs, but bringing them forth, is young, and has not yet advanced to the strength of a bull; because the believing [sic: *credentes*] soul of each one, already seizing the rudiments of holy newness, but not yet having attained the perfection of the holy Fathers, is called not *adolescens*, a youth, but by diminution *adolescentula*, a young maiden, because on account of her weakness she cannot rise to the sublimity of perfection, but brings forth the first little flowers of good working, [1028C] as far as she is able, until, growing up little by little, she shows the fruits of the virtues now full and ripe.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Let the bride say, then: *Therefore the young maidens* *14* *have loved thee exceedingly,* because the minds of believers, still advancing daily in faith through grace, greatly love the sweetness of thy mercy, O beloved, since they can attain it for its sweetness by thy gift: by thy gift, I say, because without thee we can do nothing; and therefore I cry aloud, and invite all who love thee to cry aloud with me: *Draw me: after thee we will run,* etc.

VERS. 3.-- *Draw me: after thee we will run to the odour of thy ointments. The king hath brought me into his storerooms: we will exult and rejoice in thee, mindful of thy breasts [1028D] above wine: the righteous love thee.*

But whence comes such a proclamation, or petition, of the believing soul that sighs toward God? Surely from the consideration of her own infirmity, by which she weighs that without the aid of grace she can in no way suffice for herself; because, according to the Apostle, *it is not of him that willeth, nor of him that runneth, but of God that showeth mercy [n: (Rom. IX)]*. By which sentence free will is not destroyed, but rather it is shown that the help of divine grace is altogether necessary to free will, without which every will to work well will always lie ineffectual. For indeed, from free will anyone has the willing to come to God; not indeed that the willing itself is from himself, since *not even to think,* which is less than to will, *anything of ourselves, as of ourselves, are we sufficient [n: (II Cor. III)]*. [1029A] These are the words of the Apostle, who attributes everything that can be of good, likewise to think, *to will and to accomplish according to his good will [n: (Philip. II)]*, to God, not to his own choice. If, then, God, as to these three things, that is, to think good, *to will, to accomplish, works in* us, he does the first assuredly without us, the second with us, the third through us. For by sending in a good thought he goes before us; by imitating [cj: *imitando*; read *immutando*, "changing"] also the evil will, he joins it to himself through voluntary consent; by supplying to the consent the faculty or facility, the inward craftsman becomes known outwardly through our covered [cj: *opertum*; read *apertum*, "open"] work. Therefore *it is not of him that willeth, nor of him that runneth, but of God that showeth mercy [n: (Rom. IX)]*, when by the free gift of grace working within we have the willing; but we cannot be moved to the course without that same assisting grace; and when we shall be in the course, it will again [1029B] be of pitying grace that we may be able to arrive.

This drawing, then, which the bride asks for, is not the compulsion of a soul unwilling to follow God, but rather the leading of a willing one, which the Prophet prays for in the Psalm: *Lead me,* he says, *in the path of thy commandments, for I have willed it [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*: as if he should say in other words: because by the gift of thy grace I have [sic: *nabeo*] the will to work well, lead me by the help of the same grace into action, that I may be able to arrive at the promise.

Such a drawing, indeed, is made in four ways, that is, through preaching, through infirmity or adversity, through most evident miracles, through persecution or striking; the kind Father intending this, that he may make [1029C] them willing *15* and not save them unwilling; so that, while he changes from evil into good, he may transfer the will, not take away the will. For no one is saved unwilling; for what is read in the Gospel: *No man cometh to me, unless my Father draw him [var: Vulg. Jo 6:44 *nemo potest venire ad me, nisi Pater, qui misit me*, "no man can come to me, unless the Father, who sent me"] [n: (Joan. VI)]*; likewise in another place: *Compel them to come in [n: (Luc. XIV)]*, is no obstacle; because assuredly, however many the loving Father, *who wills all to be saved [n: (I Tim. II)]*, may seem to draw or compel to salvation, yet he declares none worthy of salvation whom he has not first proved willing.

With this prerogative of divine dignity, indeed, the Maker singularly distinguished the rational creature: that, just as he himself was of his own right, and it was of his own will, not of necessity, that he was good, so also she too might exist in a certain manner of her own right in [1029D] this respect, so that only by her own will she might either become evil and be justly damned, or remain good and deservedly be saved; not that her own will could suffice her for salvation, but that she would in no way attain it without her own will.

The soul tending toward God, then, desires and prays to be drawn spiritually, not as someone unwilling is drawn, but as a blind or weary man, when he is drawn, is not grieved, but rather gives thanks to the one drawing him, as to one lifting him up and helping him; so she too, giving thanks to God, asks that he who gave the willing may give also the being able, to arrive by his help at that toward which she tends, saying: *Draw me after thee: we will run to the odour of thy ointments.*

Whether thou pronounce it thus: *Draw me after thee,* and, making the division there, [1030A] bring in *we will run to the odour of thy ointments;* or thus: *draw me,* so that thou make the division there, and then add, *after thee we will run to the odour of thy ointments,* it has the same force of sense, namely that to be drawn by Christ or to run after him is to follow his footsteps by believing and working.

*We will run,* she says. Who, or of what kind, is this course, as it ought to be? Assuredly not of the body, but of the mind; not a progress of the feet, but of conduct; of which progress, namely, the Psalmist was speaking, when he spoke of the blessed dwelling in the house of the Lord, who in this vale of tears dispose ascents in their hearts. *They shall go,* he says, *from virtue to virtue [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*. And what is the fruit of this course or progress? *The God of gods shall be seen in Sion [n: (ibid.)]*. For what [1030B] is the reward of this course, save true and eternal beatitude? And what is true and eternal beatitude, save the contemplation of that supreme and eternal and consubstantial Trinity? I will not run alone, then, but with thee, O Lord Jesus, holding my right hand, drawing and leading me, and as it were running with me, we will run.

By what way? Through thyself, assuredly, who hast said: *I am the way, the truth and the life [n: (Joan. XIV)]*; for through thee I will run to thee for thy sake. Through thee, who art the way, I will run ready; to thee, who art the truth, I will run secure; for thy sake, who art the life, I will run glad. For I will run ready because I have thee as helper; secure, because I do not doubt of the reward; glad, because I acknowledge [1030C] and believe that to know thee and to have thee is this reward of mine.

*16* So then I will run *not as at an uncertainty [n: (I Cor. IX)]*, but that I may lay hold. Of inestimable price is the gift, the things which thou hast promised to those who run in the stadium of this life through thee, and to thee, and for thy sake; which, namely, thou criest through thy prophet to be of such a kind that neither *eye hath seen, nor ear heard, nor hath it ascended into the heart of man [n: (Isai. LXIV)]*.

*We will run,* then, she says; but how? *In the odour of thy ointments.* [var: Vulg. Ct 1:3 *in odorem*, "to the odour"] Since the perfumes of the bridegroom's ointments are very many and diverse, and therefore not fragrant in one and the same manner, why is it that she says *in the odour* in the singular number, then *of ointments* in the plural, when she could rather have said, in the odours [1030D] of thy ointments? She attends to something, as I think, because she has considered diligently how and by whom those perfumes are spread. For what is that special and singular odour save charity? And every virtue, if it is without charity, is now no virtue, but rather a damnable vice; because all virtues ought to come forth from the root of charity, as the Lord shows in the Gospel, where he has admonished how the virtue of abstinence is to be held, saying: *But thou, when thou fastest, anoint thy head and wash thy face, that thou appear not to men to fast, but to thy Father who is in secret, and thy Father who seeth in secret will repay thee [n: (Matth. VI)]*. What is it to anoint the head, save to cherish the ruling part of the mind in good work through the sweetness of charity? And what to wash the face, save to show forth the habit of the inner [1031A] man, festive and glad from the joy of good works, through the sweetness of charity? Therefore neither abstinence, nor chastity, nor humility, nor obedience, nor any virtue is anything, or can be, without charity; because, although it may put forward the appearance of virtue, unless it proceeds from love of God or of neighbour, it is not only not a virtue, but even a detestable vice before God; and by this, while anyone takes from it human praise only, he deprives [himself] once for all of the reward of the divine gift. But the manifestation of this doubtful virtue will be made at the last judgment, when, according to the voice of Truth, *in* the *night* of that judgment, that is, in tribulation, *two shall be in one bed, two women grinding together, two in the field,* of which two, *one being taken* to life, *the other shall be left* to death [1031B] [n: (Matth. XXIV; Luc. XVII)].



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

By these names of things, indeed, that is, of the bed, the mill, the field, three kinds of profession are denoted; because there are some who keep sabbath with the beloved in rest of mind, while many are occupied with the labours of the active life, and some cultivate the hearts of their hearers with the word of life; but in each profession an outcome of differing intention often appears. For in one bed, that is, in the rest of the contemplative life, which belongs chiefly to those who, separated from the cares of the world, give themselves to God alone in the pursuit of prayer or of sacred meditation, this twofold intention is sometimes found, while there are some who show this for human favour alone *17* or fear, but others practise it from the love and fear of God alone. So too in the active life either the favour of the world or the love of God compels certain men to labour [1031C]; and either the vice of boasting or the love of eternal life leads many to apply themselves to the word of preaching. Whence blessed Augustine, when he was speaking of charity *which dealeth not perversely [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*, which is the judge of this twofold intention: « See, he says, how great works pride does, how like it makes them, and as it were equal to charity. Charity feeds the hungry, pride feeds him too; charity that God may be praised, pride that itself may be praised: charity clothes the naked, pride clothes him too; charity fasts, pride fasts too; charity buries the dead, pride buries him too. All the good works which charity wills to do and does, pride drives against them, and as it were leads its horses [1031D]; but charity is more interior, it takes away the place of pride ill driven. » And after a little: « Do not, he says, attend to what flowers outside, but which root is within rooted is cupidity: there can be an appearance of good deeds, truly good works there cannot be. Charity is rooted, be secure, nothing evil can come forth. » In every good work, then, the mind of each one ought to burn with the fire of charity, so that, the virtues being kindled from it as it were like spices, it may send forth a most sweet smoke of fragrance. With such a fire of charity the Psalmist was burning when he said: *My heart grew hot within me, and in my meditation a fire shall flame out [n: (Psal. XVIII)]*. Whence also in the law it is commanded *that fire burn continually on the altar [n: (Lev. VI)]*. For what is the altar save our heart, in which we offer to God by intention whatever [1032A] good we do, or in which we immolate ourselves by the mortification of vices and the exercise of virtues. On this altar, then, fire ought to burn continually, because every good work, unless it proceeds from the ardour of charity, has no vigour. In another place also it is commanded *that oil be always in the house of the Lord for trimming the lamps [n: (Exod. XXXV; Levit. XXIV)]*. For by oil, which excels all liquids, charity is understood, which stands out above all the virtues. And by the lamps good works are understood, as the Lord says in the Gospel: *And burning lamps in our hands [var: Vulg. Lc 12:35 *in manibus vestris*, "in your hands"] [n: (Luc. XII)]*. And the house of the Lord, or temple, is the body or the mind of any saint, of which, the one and the other, the Psalmist: *Holy is,* he says, *thy temple, wonderful in equity [n: (Psal. LXIV)]*. And [1032B] elsewhere: *Holiness becometh thy house [n: (Psal. XCII)]*. The Apostle too, to the believers: *The temple,* he says, *of God is holy, which you are [n: (I Cor. III)]*. And elsewhere: *Know you not that your bodies are the temple of the Holy Spirit? [var: Vulg. 1Cor 6:19 *membra vestra*, "your members"] [n: (I Cor. VI.)]*

In the house of the Lord, then, there ought to be oil for trimming the lamps, because each faithful man who is the house or temple of God ought to have charity as the matter by which he may be kindled to the pursuits of piety.

Fittingly, then, the beloved speaks to her Bridegroom, saying: *We will run to the odour of thy ointments;* because assuredly anyone labours in vain in the course of good works if he is not at all fragrant with the odour of charity.

But in a course of this kind four things are to be considered, [1032C] namely the manner, the quality, the intention, the usefulness. *18* Now this course is nothing other than the life of each saint. And the perfect manner of running, that is, of living, ought to be such for each one that there be nothing above, nothing below, but all things be paved with charity in the midst [n: (Cant. III)], according to the footsteps of the preceding Fathers, as Scripture commands: *pass not beyond,* it says, *the bounds which thy fathers have set [n: (Prov. XXII)]*, of whom the Psalmist says that *the Lord knoweth the way of the just [n: (Psal. I)]*; as if he should say: Since the way of those is known to God and pleases him, who, doing nothing outside discretion, hold their life in the even balance [sic: *liberamine*] of justice. For to those who will or do anything above and beyond what is just the Apostle commands *not to be more wise than it behoveth to be wise, but to be wise [1032D] unto sobriety [n: (Rom. XII)]*. But of those who fall short below or grow lukewarm it is written: *Cursed is everyone that doeth the work of the Lord deceitfully [n: (Jerem. XLVIII)]*. And those have their pursuits paved with charity in the midst who, like those four living creatures full of eyes before and behind, whose feet, straight feet, with two wings [n: (Ezech. I)] of the twofold charity, of God, namely, and of neighbour, are both lifted up and diminish their bodies, that is, their works, so that they neither withdraw from love of neighbour through the contemplative life, nor fall away from the charity of God through the active. On both sides they hold so discreet a manner of living that the perfect rule of justice does not admit whatever may be above or below. Whence a certain wise man of this world says: [1033A] *There is a measure in things; there are, in short, fixed bounds,*
*Beyond and short of which the right cannot stand.*
[n: (HORAT., Satir. I, I, 107.)]

Because, then, every weight greater and less displeases God, each one's life is to be weighed with such evenness that he neither collapse under too great a weight, nor appear negligent and slothful under a very slight one.

And the quality of this course, or of the runner, is to be attended to, namely that it be such as the Apostle describes, *in all things,* he says, *let us show ourselves as the ministers of God, in much patience, in tribulations, in necessities, in distresses, in stripes, in prisons, in seditions, in labours, in watchings, in fastings, in chastity, in knowledge, in long-suffering, in sweetness, in the Holy Spirit, in charity [1033B] unfeigned, in the word of truth, in the power of God, by the armour of justice on the right hand and on the left, by honour and dishonour, by evil report and good report, as deceivers and yet true, as unknown and yet known, as dying and behold we live, as chastised and not killed, as sorrowful always, yet rejoicing [var: Vulg. 2Cor 6:10 *quasi tristes, semper autem gaudentes*, "as sorrowful, yet always rejoicing"], as needy, yet enriching many, as having nothing and possessing all things [n: (II Cor. VI)]*.

Behold with what motions of the soul this course is expressed, which is accomplished by the labour of both men, that is, of body and of spirit, so that after that course is completed, as in both there was labour and suffering, so in both there may at length be rest and consolation, which is the beatitude of the double stole.

Now the wise men of this world say that there are four [1033C] species of quality, *19* of which the first is habit and disposition; which, while they are one species of quality, differ in this, that disposition is a habit easily noble [cj: *nobilis*; read *mobilis*, "movable"], but habit a disposition remaining for a very long time. The second species of quality is that which comes about according to power and impotence: according to power, that it can easily be bent naturally into any accident whatever; but according to impotence, that it cannot not [cj: *non flecti*; read *flecti*, "be bent"] be bent lightly into anything else whatever. The third species is that which they call passible quality and passion, which two they distinguish within one species thus, saying that passion is that which, having arisen for a time, is easily changed; but passive quality, according to which we are said to be of such a kind, perseveres [1033D] perpetually. This species indeed seems similar to the first, and as it were the same, but it differs in this, that passion is a momentary reception of something; while disposition, which they also call affection, is the enlarged passion of that same accident. Further, habit, since it is perfected by very many things and by a certain order, is called augmented affection; but passible quality is a suddenly inborn and permanent passion. The fourth species they placed in figures and forms, calling figure certain outlines of things, but forms the beauties and comelinesses of those outlines. These species of quality are considered in the course of each one's life according to the diversity of will and purpose, in which is contained the quality of applying the mind in whatever manner to some work

[1034A]For through disposition or affection some pass into habit, when some, from occasion or necessity, turn certain good works they have begun into custom by the progress of their way of life, and by a certain delight in the virtues always gain increase, as is said of the saints: *They shall go from virtue to virtue [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*. Further, according to power and impotence, there is no one who, in this course of a life to be well ordered, does not have the power through the good of the reason granted him, and the grace that assists free will; to which grace he does not have the inability to assent and to labour together. And according to the third species, that is, passion and passible quality, there are many who, when they hear the word concerning the punishment of the wicked and the glory of the good, are pricked for a time [1034B] and grow warm with love of good work; but because this charity is not rooted in them, they grow cold in the time of temptation. But there are some who, when once they hear the word of God, are kindled to the practice of good work, and because they are *rooted in charity* and made firm [n: (Ephes. III)], they do not fall away from the love once conceived, but always direct the step of good work even to the end of life. Or we may say it is passion when the mind for an hour as it were suffers through humility, patience, obedience, by struggling against its own will, not another's, but this very rarely. But passible quality, *20* when by a permanent passion of voluntary mortification, through the aforesaid or any other virtues, it is worn down daily, consenting to no pleasures of this world. But according to the fourth species, which they place [1034C] in figures and forms, this quality of life can be considered, which is established according to the example and form of some life, as James admonishes his disciples, saying: *Take, brethren, the example of labour and patience through the prophets who spoke to you in the name of the Lord [var: Vulg. Jac 5:10 *exitus mali, laboris, et patientiae, prophetas, qui locuti sunt in nomine Domini*, "of suffering evil, of labour and patience, the prophets who spoke in the name of the Lord"] [n: (Jac. V)]*. And Paul: *Be ye followers,* he says, *of me, as I also am of Christ [n: (Phil. III)]*.

That quality, then, by which affection passes into habit, and by which through the grace of God willing and being able are present, and which in good work is not momentary nor of an hour but perpetual, or which takes the form of the preceding Fathers, is to be attended to with the greatest care by everyone who wishes to run well, so that according to it he may become a kind of mirror of honourable conduct and discipline, by which he may gain both the reward of his own labour and the salvation of those who imitate him, and the profit [1034D] of his neighbours.

And the intention of running in this way, that is, of working rightly, will be this: that no desire of vainglory creep into the mind, but that the love of God or the love of eternal life be the cause of labour and watchfulness of this kind.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Now this intention is as it were a certain eye of the mind, of which the Saviour says in the Gospel: *If thy eye be single, thy whole body will be full of light: but if it be evil, thy body also will be dark [n: (Matth. VI)]*. And in this same *Canticle of canticles* the bridegroom speaks to the bride: *Behold, thou art fair, my love, behold, thou art fair, thine eyes are doves' [n: (Cant. V)]*. Sometimes certain works are done which are interpreted in the bad sense by those who [1035A] do not know from what intention they proceed; just as, on the contrary, certain things are regarded in the good sense which are disapproved by the Inspector on high, because they are brought forth with an evil intention; and these are not only not rewarded, but rather are condemned, just as those things too which are judged worthy of condemnation by the ignorant are rather rewarded by the just judge. Whence the Lord also says of the reprobate through the prophet: *This people honoureth me with their lips, but their heart is far from me [n: (Isa. XXIX)]*. All good things, then, are directed according to this eye of intention, so that they are worthy either of reward or of condemnation: of reward, if they are done for God; of condemnation, if for human favour.

But what the profit of this course or labour is, the Apostle sets forth, saying: *The sufferings [1035B] of this time are not worthy to be compared with the glory to come that shall be revealed in us [n: (Rom. V)]*. And the Lord in the Gospel cries out to those who run in such a way, saying: *Come to me, all you that labour and are burdened, and I will* make you to rest [var: Vulg. Mt 11:28 *reficiam vos*, "I will refresh you"] [n: (Matth. XI)]. The Apostle too, understanding that he had not run in vain, after he declares the course of his labour, saying: *I have fought a good fight, I have finished my course, I have kept the faith,* *21* goes on to promise himself confidently the fruit of that very labour: *as for the rest, there is laid up for me a crown of justice which [sic: justitiaequam] the just judge will render to me against that day [n: (II Tim. IV)]*.

The profit, then, great and inestimable, is the very fruit of eternal life, which in the life to come is bestowed eternally, without any failing, on those who labour for God; [1035C] but because it is granted to the Church, or to any faithful soul, to foretaste the fruit of this profit in the present life also, it follows, saying: *The king hath brought me into his storerooms.*

This bringing in has no small dignity, if both the person of the one who brings in and the place and the manner of the bringing in are rightly considered. For it is not just anyone, but a *king*, and not only a king, but the King of kings, who brings in; nor only into the house, but also into the storerooms; nor as it were abruptly, but this bringing in is done in a fitting manner of reason. For there are many who enter the house of this king, that is, the Church, but very few who can have entrance into the storerooms. Some can keep watch before the doors of these storerooms, they are able to look in, but they do not prevail to go in [1035D] unless he brings them in *who hath the key of David, who openeth and no man shutteth; shutteth and no man openeth [n: (Apoc. III)]*.

But it must be considered what these storerooms of so great a king are. All Holy Scripture, which is divided into two Testaments, namely into the law and the Gospel, is the repository, or storehouse, of the riches of this king. And this is divided threefold, that is, into history, allegory and morality. For this King, wise, merciful, and ruling and cherishing with great kindness and with his providence, established such a manner for his riches that there should be in them that which, according to the capacity of diverse understandings, both the strong might seek and the weak might not shun.

For many delight in history, very many [1036A] are fed by allegory, some are instructed by morality. But those whom this king deems worthy of so great a grace of his that he grants them entrance into all these storerooms, so that, namely, from historical knowledge they enter into the allegorical, and from the allegorical into the moral: with what sweetness, thinkest thou, are these fed? with what torrent of the Lord's pleasure are they made drunk? [n: (Psal. XXXV.)] To such indeed the Lord promises through the prophet, saying: *Behold, I will send you corn, wine and oil, and you shall be filled with them [n: (Joel. II)]*.

Desirable are storerooms of this kind, in which the delights of these kinds are contained, so that bread strengthens the heart of man, wine gladdens the heart of man, oil makes the face cheerful [n: (Psal. CIII)]. For we receive corn from the Lord when, in the more obscure sayings, [1036B] the covering of the letter being drawn away, we perceive the inward things through the marrow of the spirit of the law. The Lord supplies us his wine when he inebriates us with the lofty preaching of his Scripture; he bestows his oil on us also when he orders our life with gentle mildness by the plainer precepts.

*22* It is pleasing to attend still more fully, and reverently [sic: *adreverenter*], to why the divine storehouses contain these kinds rather than others. For if thou considerest that one grain of wheat which, having died in the earth, brought forth much fruit [n: (Joan. XII)], and attendest to the glorious greatness of the numerous harvest springing forth from it, thou wilt see how magnificent the examples of distinguished works that shine out from the historical storehouses, how sweet the marrows of the mysteries [1036C] that breathe their fragrance from the allegorical storerooms, how useful the teachings of the precepts that shine forth from the moral repositories.

A most beautiful comparison of Christ in the grain of wheat: for in the grain the marrow is covered by the husk: what then is figured by the husk but the flesh of Christ? what by the marrow but his divinity? In the grain, then, the marrow is covered by the husk; in Christ the divinity is hidden by the flesh: the husk and the marrow make one grain, the flesh and the divinity make one person, Christ, God and man. When therefore thou hast looked into all the things that were done concerning this grain of wheat, that is, Christ, God and man: namely, how this grain, descending from the heavenly granary, fell into the earth and died, then, rising again [1036D] and ascending into heaven, being multiplied in the apostles and the other believers, filled the whole world with the corn of faith and justice; how, with the wine of preaching overflowing, he restrained offenders with the severity of correction, and soothed penitents with the oil of charity and mercy; for in this manner he who had fallen among robbers was healed by that Samaritan of the Gospel [n: (Luc. X)]. When, therefore, thou hast read and understood all these things, some historically, some allegorically, and some morally, now separately, now together, in this same threefold manner, and hast been able by the grace of God to minister the same to others in the same manner, wilt thou not be able, for so great a benefit conferred upon thee, to say gratefully: *The king hath brought me into his storerooms.*

But what ensigns of the virtues have shone more greatly and more perfectly [1037A] than in this grain, that is, in this Lordly man? Surely, whatever the saints have of the bowels of mercy, whatever of meekness of spirit, whatever of the heaven of uprightness, whatever of the keeping of humility, whatever of the fervour of charity, this they have drawn from that very fountain of mercy, from that very root of meekness, from that very power of justice, that is, from the very mediator of God and men, the Lord God.

More, then, and as it were principally, these three kinds are held in the Lord's storehouses; and whatever breathes forth from the abundance of the Old and New Testament is either wheat, which strengthens and confirms those perfect in faith and in the exercise of the virtues; or it is wine, which either soothes the saints with the sweetness of the heavenly promises, or *23* deters sinners by the severity of the divine judgment; [1037B] or it is oil, which is either poured out in the hearts of the saints through charity, or consoles penitents through mercy. Whence holy Church, or each faithful soul beloved of God, considering in mind such treasures of her bridegroom, her king and her Lord, better than all the precious furniture of all kings and princes, and understanding what mysteries or sacraments are contained in them, can rightly chant with the joyful voice of the soul: *the king hath brought me into his storerooms.* And such a bringing in is indeed commendable enough from the person and the place; but the manner of this bringing in is this, that it is not done for an hour, but with the right of possessing, and as it were under the drawing up of a dowry, so that, so great a grace [1037C] having been conferred, there may be the assigning of a spiritual and most holy dowry, whereby the coupling of the divine and the human mind may be confirmed.

This admirable bringing in having therefore been celebrated, there follows the fitting giving of thanks for the gift conferred, which the mind gives to God, joined to him and sharing in the goods designated for her by him, with the motion of both the inner and the outer man, *we will exult,* saying, *and we will rejoice in thee.*

Exultation and gladness seem in some manner to differ from each other, so that the description of each is such that gladness is said to be a motion of the soul only, but exultation of body and soul.

The holy soul, then, perceiving in herself the gifts of divine grace, ought to receive and keep them with gladness and exultation, and to exercise in work what she perceives in mind: [1037D] for because « the proof of love is the showing of work (Gregory); » assuredly the mind joined to God through charity, while it is glad inwardly over the grace of understanding conferred on it, ought also to exult outwardly unto the works of the virtues. Whence also, in the likeness of the children playing in the market place, the apostles cry out to those who would do no good works at their preaching: *We have sung to you, and you have not danced [n: (Luc. VII)]*. For they desired their hearers to have the dance of the spirit, in order to show that faith without works is idle [n: (Jac. II)].

This religious dance, then, David the king prefigured, playing and leaping before the ark of the Lord, because in the spirit he foresaw from afar that through Christ, who was to be born of his flesh, that sacrifice of the Lord, not made with hands but [1038A] heavenly, was at some time to be established by faith and works. For this most beautiful play of holiness is the wonderful Prophet bore before him in his own prelude, fittingly and becomingly; for so great a grace of honour to come, which was to be administered and confirmed through Christ, his son according to the flesh, he rejoiced and exulted before that typical sacrifice of the Lord. And because he was the ruler and leader of the people of God, by such a deed of his he taught beforehand the rulers of the Churches, who ought to be over those subject to them by word and example, that before the ark of the Lord, which is his Church, they ought to dance with a spiritual dance, namely with a motion of soul and of body; so that, according to the Apostle, they may present their *bodies* *a living sacrifice, holy,* *24* *pleasing to God [n: (Rom. XII)]*: being a sacrifice indeed by the mortification of vices, [1038B] but living by the showing of good works. And this before the ark of the Lord, *that they may see their good works and glorify their Father who is in heaven [n: (Matth. V)]*.

Since therefore *with the heart one believes unto justice, but with the mouth confession is made unto salvation [n: (Rom. X)]*, which is to be moved in soul and body, it is certain that the one cannot be without the other; whence the prophet exhorts: *Lift up,* he says, *your hearts with your hands to God [var: Vulg. Lam 3:41 *Levemus corda nostra … ad Dominum in caelos*, "let us lift up our hearts … to the Lord in the heavens"] [n: (Thren. III)]*. For to lift up the hearts with the hands is to believe and to work; for he who believes and does not work lifts up the heart indeed, but does not lift up the hands: but he who works, but does not have faith, lifts up the hands indeed, but does not lift up the heart.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Therefore, for that gracious bringing in of the grace of God into the ineffable subtlety of the divine mysteries, [1038C] the holy soul and bride of Christ ought to render to God the thanksgiving that is due, by the jubilation of heart and mouth and by the showing of works, which is to take oil with the lamps and to go out to meet the bridegroom, saying: *We will exult and we will rejoice in thee,* because we bear the gladness of good works in our conscience, and this in thee, when we ascribe every good that we do not to our own strength but to thy grace. But after that bringing in, gloriously celebrated, for which she gives thanks by exulting and rejoicing, she adds the cause of this exultation and gladness, namely the *remembrance* *of the divine breasts, more desirable than wine.* For what would that exultation or gladness of the religious mind be, thinking and working things worthy of God, if it had not [1038D] the remembrance of these breasts, *breasts better than wine,* that is, sweeter than every pleasure of the world? But of what breasts? Surely either of the two Testaments, of which the whole of Holy Scripture consists, or of the two precepts of charity, on which *the whole law dependeth, and the prophets [n: (Matth. XXII)]*. Truly, meditation on the Holy Scriptures begets the remembrance of the divine commandments, according to which the rule of living rightly consists, to whose rightness the life of each is directed: *in thy justifications,* says the Psalmist, *I will meditate; I will not forget thy words [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*.

For how can anyone be or be called just unless he is mindful of the things which God has commanded, and above all of these breasts, namely the two precepts [1039A] of the charity of God and of neighbour, without which, as the Apostle says, neither kinds of tongues, nor prophecy, nor the knowledge of all mysteries, nor faith, nor almsgiving, nor martyrdom [n: (I Cor. XII)], nor any virtue will be able to profit anyone? for *whosoever shall keep the whole law, but shall offend in one precept of charity, is become guilty of all [var: Vulg. Jac 2:10 *offendat autem in uno*, "but offend in one point", without *praecepto charitatis*] [n: (Jac. II)]*.

Wherefore the bride of Christ, wishing to show that the whole rightness of her life consists in a remembrance of this kind, goes on, saying: *The right love thee;* as if she should say: the proof of rightness *25* is love; because if they are right, they love thee; if they do not love, neither are they right. But because they conceive this rightness from the remembrance indeed of thy precepts, but take it up perfectly from the love of thee, let it rightly be said that [1039B] *mindful of thy breasts, better than wine, the right love thee.*

For this middle little part of the sentence, which is *mindful of thy breasts, better than wine,* can, not absurdly, be joined, by a subdistinction, to the parts placed on either side of it; so that it may be uttered either in this way: *We will exult and we will rejoice in thee; mindful of thy breasts, better than wine.* Or in this way: *We will exult and we will rejoice in thee:* and here, a subdistinction having been made, let there be added after, *mindful of thy breasts, better than wine [sic: vinnm]; the right love thee.* For those who have remembrance of the commandments of God, and settle the manner of their life according to them, become right; and none but the right can love, nor are they right unless they love.

Moreover, it delights not a little the eyes of our inner [1039C] man to consider the most beautiful progression of this canticle, from that place where it is said: *Draw me after thee,* up to this little sentence, *the right love thee.* For the order of this progression becomes a kind of sequel to the kiss that was asked for.

For here that kindly sevenfold Spirit, as by certain ascents of the heart, intimates the sevenfold graces of his condescension, by which the holy soul, the bride of Christ, may rise little by little, until she advances to the inestimable bridal chamber of her Lord, her bridegroom and king, to be coupled to him with eternal charity. For first she is drawn by the compassion of God; then, as it were moving her foot to the race, she offers the first-fruits of a good intention; and soon, having applied zeal, she merits to be brought into [1039D] the storerooms of God, and when she has entered, she exults in understanding and rejoices in believing and working.

Further, from this active life she passes to a certain beginning of the contemplative life by meditation and remembrance of the precepts of God, and directing to their line whatever strength is still in her, she attains the perfection of rightness; and so at last, all cares and business set aside, cleaving to God through contemplation, she is so near to him by burning charity that she can say: *Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth.*

VERSE 4.-- *I am black but beautiful, O daughters of Jerusalem, as the tents of Cedar, as the skins of Solomon.*

Universally indeed the whole Church, and particularly the individual Churches, and singly also each soul beloved of God, have endured adversities, and it is endured [1040A] from the contrary side, because the qualities of the good and of the evil have been set against them, who are divided into diverse and contrary pursuits, of which parts the one is called the heavenly city, the other the earthly; the one God's, the other the devil's.

Of these, moreover, *26* two women bore the type: the one free, who was Sara; the other a bondwoman, who was Agar, the mother of Ismael, whose son was Cedar, which is interpreted *darkness,* to which is likened that blackness which the Church, or each holy soul, says she has according to the judgment of those who live evilly, whereas she, by right consideration, judges this very thing to be not blackness but rather beauty. And what is this blackness? Plainly persecution, which is inflicted on the sons of the bondwoman [cj: *filiis ancillae*; read *a filiis ancillae*, "by the sons of the bondwoman"]; which is therefore thought black by the perverse and by those who do not believe the good things [1040B] to come, because it is heavy and troublesome, and because it makes those who suffer it as it were abject and wretched, whereas they rather become glorious, and inwardly, because of the hope of rewards to come, fair and beautiful with spiritual joy; for the sons of that city which is signified by Sara, who was free, are persecuted by the sons of the other city, which is shown by Agar the bondwoman; now indeed by inflicting evils bodily, but now by afflicting the soul spiritually through malice.

Lastly, that play which went on between the son of the free woman and the son of the bondwoman the Apostle calls persecution; as he says: *He that was born according to the flesh persecuted him that was after the spirit; so also now [n: (Gal. IV)]*: because assuredly the sons of the bondwoman persecute the sons of the free woman, [1040C] not only by carnal persecution but also by spiritual, by which they try to deprive them of that true liberty with which Christ has made them free [n: (ibid.)], that they may not serve sin, as these do who are the sons of the bondwoman. Which persecution is surely as much heavier than every bodily persecution as that spiritual portion is better than the carnal, the soul, namely, than the body.

Whence, indignant at such persecution, that free woman and mother of the free says: *Cast out,* she says, *the bondwoman and her son, for the son of the bondwoman shall not be heir with the son of the free woman [n: (Gen. XXI)]*; because surely the servants of sin do not belong to that heavenly inheritance to which the free of justice belong. The Church, then, or any [1040D] holy soul, makes light of persecution of this kind, inasmuch as, being established upon the rock of faith, she is often shaken only by the storms of the persecution [sic: *persecutionem*] of outward men, but is not thrown down, having patience and awaiting glory and honour and incorruption unto eternal life.

Let her say, then: *I am black but beautiful:* black indeed to those who judge of me by reason of temporal affliction, but to you, O daughters of Jerusalem, who know and believe the good things to come, *beautiful,* because the humbler I am through temporal affliction, the more I am exalted by the greater beauty of the virtues. But how am I judged *black*? *As the tents of Cedar.* Cedar was the son of Ismael, of whom the divine voice says: *This man,* it says, *shall be a slave man [var: Vulg. Gn 16:12 *ferus homo*, "a wild man"], and the hand of all against him; over against the country of his brethren he shall pitch his tents [n: (Gen. XVI)]*.

[1041A]But why did she not say *as the tents* of Ismael, but *of Cedar?* Because, namely, Ismael is interpreted *the hearing of God;* but Cedar *darkness,* and *27* this name of darkness agrees better with the opinion of those who think *light darkness, and darkness light [n: (Isa. V)]*, and, according to the likeness of his father, that is, Ismael, who was a slave and pitched his tents not among his brethren but over against his brethren, they judge the abasement of this world and the labour of true philosophy to be a certain wildness and contrariety.

But how *beautiful? as the skins of Solomon; beautiful,* she says, *as the skins;* not of any other very mighty man, but *of Solomon.* For Solomon, as he was wiser and richer than all the men of his time, so also he had his tents more beautiful and [1041B] more adorned, of choice and elaborately wrought skins.

Now skins are of dead animals; but what are the skins of dead animals but the virtues and examples of the Fathers who went before? And from the virtues and examples of the holy Fathers who went before, as from skins shining with varied beauty, I, compacted as it were into one skin, the elaborate of my king, namely of the true Solomon, that is, of the *peaceable* one, our Lord Jesus Christ, making peace by his blood with all things that are in heaven and on earth [n: (Coloss. I)]; therefore the tent; so that it may worthily be said of me by the grace of God; that is, the tabernacle which God pitched, and not man.

[1041C]VERSE 5.-- *Do not consider me that I am brown, because the sun hath discoloured me: the sons of my mother have fought against me, they have set me the keeper in the vineyards; my own vineyard I have not kept.*

Beautiful am I as that which I am, and I am the comeliness of the skin of the true Solomon, which you, O daughters of Jerusalem, know well, who daily proclaim that same beauty of mine to the praise of God, of whose grace that beauty is.

Wherefore, *do not consider me that I am brown, because the sun hath discoloured me:* do not, I say, consider my outward disposition and habit, which I have contracted by the burning of the sun, that is, by the heat of temptation and of worldly affliction, or from the kindling charity of the true sun, of whom it is written: *But unto you that fear my name the sun of justice shall arise* [1041D] [n: (Malach. IV)], who has as it were burnt me with so great a fervour of his love, as with an inseparable accident, that *neither tribulation, nor distress, nor persecution, nor famine, nor nakedness, nor peril, nor the sword can separate me from his charity [n: (Rom. VIII)]*. Such a discolouring, surely, is the great and glorious beauty and adornment of my inner man.

Further. Sometimes a holy man does certain things which those who discern badly interpret in the sinister sense, since they do not know the intention of the one working, which God alone judges. *For there are right ways which seem crooked to men [var: Vulg. Pr 16:25 *Est via quae videtur homini recta*, "there is a way which seemeth to a man right"] [n: (Prov. XVI)]*; just as, on the contrary, crooked ones which seem right; because they do not heed that eye of intention according to which every work is either *28* set upright or made crooked. Let the faithful soul, then, say [1042A] after this manner, *I am black but beautiful;* as if she should say: Black indeed I seem in some act of mine to those who, not having the single eye, take it in the contrary sense, whereas I am rather beautiful because of my single eye, from which my whole body is full of light [n: (Matth. VI)]. As you too judge, O daughters of Jerusalem, who look upon me with a single and dovelike eye, *do not therefore consider me that I am brown, because the sun hath discoloured me:* that is, do not think my body, that is, my work, to be dark; because surely what proceeds from the luminous grace of the true sun is wholly full of light, not having any part of darkness.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Further. Any soul whatever, turned to the Lord through penitence, seems indeed, from the [1042B] austerity of penitence, as it were black, because of her sad and mournful habit: but because *this sorrow is according to God, and worketh penitence unto salvation that is steadfast [n: (II Cor. VII)]*; and: *Blessed are they that mourn, for they shall be comforted [n: (Matth. V)]*, she is beautiful; having it in hope that, according to the prophet, she may receive from the Lord *a crown for ashes, the oil of joy for mourning, a mantle of praise for the spirit of grief [n: (Isa. LXI)]*. And as a certain wise man says: *The heart that knoweth the bitterness of its own soul, in its joy the stranger shall not intermeddle [n: (Prov. XIV)]*. Let such a one say, then: *I am black but beautiful, O daughters of Jerusalem, as the tents of Cedar, as the skins of Solomon;* because from the blackness of penitence I seem at first to have belonged to the sons of the bondwoman, whose works are of darkness, [1042C] but afterwards that very penitence has conferred on me such beauty that I now belong to the sons of the free woman, who, by the mortification of the carnal life, know both how to put together and how to be the tents of the true Solomon [sic: *Salomanis*]. Wherefore *do not consider me that I am brown, because the sun hath discoloured me,* that is, do not despise my penitence, because that true sun made me take it up, who has pierced and enlightened my heart with the rays of his grace and mercy, so that by repenting I might recover that most general good, namely God himself, whom by sinning I had lost.

The chanting of the present Canticle not unfittingly looks to you, most holy sisters, who are and ought to be brides of Christ, in accordance with the quality of your purpose and order, because you display in your outward [1042D] habit a guise that is despised indeed, but preserve in the inward habit of the mind one beautiful to God because, as we believe, acceptable. Whence let the whole body of your holy society proclaim with a concordant and sweet harmony of heart and mouth; *I am black but beautiful, O daughters of Jerusalem, as the tents of Cedar, as the skins of Solomon: do not consider me that I am brown, because the sun hath discoloured me.* Which is to say in other words more expressly, *for we are made a spectacle to this world, and to angels, and to men [n: (I Cor. IV)]*. The sons of this world judge us black and misshapen, *as the tents of Cedar,* that is, as the gentiles who by the blackness of evils are dead both to God and to the world. But we, seeking neither the favour nor the glory *29* of the world, but carrying *oil in vessels* of our own [n: (Matth. XXV)], that is, the gladness of good [1043A] works in our consciences, are beautiful and fair in the eyes of our Bridegroom, *as the skins of Solomon,* that is, as those who, *bearing about the mortification of Jesus in our bodies [n: (II Cor. IV)]*, build in our hearts tabernacles beloved of him, of which he can say: *I will dwell in them and walk among them [n: (II Cor. VI)]*. Do not therefore consider us that we are brown, in that we do not have the passing and misshapen beauty of this world, because that true sun, who has shone upon us by his grace, has brought this about by the strong fervour of his love, so that, all the pomp of this corruptible life being spurned, we choose rather to please him in act and habit than the world. *The sons of my mother have fought against me, they have set me the keeper in the vineyards; my own vineyard I have not kept.*

[1043B]After the Church, or any holy soul, has set forth beforehand her outward discolouring and the cause of the discolouring, for *all* indeed *that will live godly in Christ shall suffer persecution [n: (II Tim. III)]*; she now adds the aggravation of the persecution, that she has suffered it and suffers it not only from strangers but also, which is heavier, from brothers. For those evils which are inflicted by strangers bring less pain, but those torments rage more fiercely which are inflicted by those of whose minds better was presumed.

Now the chanting of this little part seems to demand not a simple sense, because the Synagogue and the Church and the heavenly Jerusalem, the goodness or power of the Creator, can all be called equivocally by the name of Mother. [1043C] The same equivocation, too, embraces under the name of sons the apostles and the successors of the apostles, the Jews, heretics and the adverse powers. The fight of such sons, too, is not uniform, because it is taken both in the good sense and in the bad. And the several parts ought to have their several expositions.

For the primitive Church, which was from the Jews, suffered persecution from those Jews who did not believe; as we read in the Acts of the apostles, that *filled with zeal they laid hands on the apostles, and put them in the public prison;* and in another place: *When they had beaten them,* moreover, *they charged them that they should not speak in the name of Jesus [n: (Act. V)]*; and in that persecution in which Stephen suffered, it is written *that there was raised a great persecution [1043D] in the Church which was at Jerusalem, and they were all dispersed through the regions of Samaria [var: Vulg. Act 8:1 *per regiones Judaeae et Samariae*, "through the regions of Judea and Samaria"], except the apostles [n: (Act. VIII)]*.

Since therefore both parts, that of the believers, namely, and that of the unbelievers, were sons of the Synagogue by nature or by race, but the unbelieving persecuted the faithful on account of the faith of Jesus Christ, that part of the believers, which was at that time the Church of Christ, could plainly say: *The sons of my mother have fought against me, they have set me the keeper in the vineyards, my own vineyard I have not kept* *30.* As if she should say in other words: When the Lord had commanded, saying: *Go ye not into the way of the gentiles, and into the cities of the Samaritans enter ye not, but go ye rather to the lost sheep of Israel [n: (Matth. X)]*, I, desiring to fulfil this precept, preached to the sons *of my mother* the faith which I received from the Lord and through which they [1044A] ought to be saved; but they, making light of my preaching, *fought against me* by words and deeds, and driving me from their borders, *set me the keeper in the vineyards,* that is, in the churches of the gentiles, who received the word of faith with joy. And while I was spending on them the diligence of my labour and keeping, I neglected to keep *my own vineyard,* that is, my own nation, because they despised my keeping and refused to receive the word of salvation: as one of my own said to them, *To you indeed it behoved [var: Vulg. Act 13:46 *Vobis oportebat primum*, "to you it behoved first"] the word of God to be spoken, but because you reject it and judge yourselves unworthy of eternal life [sic: judicati saeternae], behold, we turn to the gentiles [n: (Act. XIII)]*. For as the same says elsewhere: *By their offence, salvation to the gentiles, that they may be emulous of them [n: (Rom. XI)]*: and presently: *I would not have you ignorant, brethren, of this mystery, lest [1044B] you be wise in your own conceits, that blindness in part befalls Israel, until the fulness of the gentiles should come in, and so all Israel should be saved [sic: fierit] [n: (ibid.)]*.

Because of this contempt, then, the prophet foretold that this vineyard was to be left without a keeper: *And the daughter of Sion,* he says, *shall be left as a covert in a vineyard, and as a lodge in a garden of cucumbers, as a city that is laid waste [n: (Isa. I)]*; and these things indeed the primitive Church could say, whose mother was the synagogue, as also of the contrary part, of which she says that the sons of her mother have fought against her.

Further, the universal Church could and can say of the apostles and their successors: *The sons of my mother have fought against me, they have set me the keeper [1044C] in the vineyards, my own vineyard I have not kept.* For the heavenly Jerusalem is the mother of believers, because of the faith, hope and charity which they have towards her; because, while they hope and believe that they are made partakers of her blessedness, of which the saints who went before have been made partakers, they work and labour through the charity by which they love so great a good, so that they may be able to come to that heavenly fatherland: for the sake of which to live holily and justly, what else is it than to be spiritually begotten from her unto her?

Her sons, too, were the apostolic ones: and apostolic men, who, while for the meriting of this most glorious fatherland they were afflicted with many labours and wearied even unto death, surely, by believing, hoping and loving, were themselves being born unto her, [1044D] and no less were being begotten, as with a mother's affection, by her who was the cause of this labour of theirs.

Such sons, then, fought against the Church when they intended to fight her out of her error, just as even now they daily fight every faithful soul out of vices unto virtue by words and examples. Did not *31* one of them fight in this manner against the churches, when he said to one of them: *What will you? shall I come to you with a rod, or in the spirit of meekness? It is absolutely heard that there is fornication among you, and such fornication as is not among the gentiles, so that one should have his father's wife [n: (I Cor. IV)]*. See how he first fought against these, whom, after he had beaten them out of this sin by rebuking, he relieves with a consoling letter: *Although I made you sorrowful,* he says, *by [1045A] the letter, I do not repent: though I did repent, seeing that the same letter (though but for a time) made you sorrowful: now I am glad; not because you were made sorrowful, but because you were made sorrowful unto penance: for you were made sorrowful according to God, that you might suffer damage by us in nothing [n: (II Cor. VII)]*. Writing also to another Church: *O senseless Galatians, who hath bewitched you, that you should not obey the truth? [n: (Gal. III.)]* While he fights against her in this manner, he fights her out of unbelief.

But what is it that these sons appoint as keepers in the vineyards those whom they assail in such a manner and fight out of vices, but that they teach and persuade them to keep the buds and fruits of the virtues? But those do not keep their own vineyard, because by the fighting of these men they have advanced to so great a keeping of the virtues that they now [1045B] neglect to keep the errors of their former custom, nay rather, by the grace of God, they drive them further from themselves by the spirit of fortitude. Whence the prophet says in the person of such: *I will stand upon my watch [n: (Habac. II)]*: he stands upon his watch who, through the skill of discipline, does not succumb to earthly desires but rises above them, so that, while he seeks the ever-standing eternity, everything that passes away may be beneath him.

In yet another way the Church also cries out over the false brothers who rebel against her, heretics and schismatics, who, as long as they were in the Church and shared in the same sacraments, were thought to be sons of that heavenly Jerusalem which is the mother of the faithful, and to belong to the city of God; but after they were seen to blaspheme against the divinity [1045C] and the humanity of Christ, and to rend the holy unity, they were cast out of the inheritance of the sons, and it was proved that they had not truly been sons, but rather bastards; to such the Apostle says: *But if you be without discipline, whereof all sons are made partakers, then are you bastards, and not sons [n: (Hebr. XII)]*. Of these, too, John says: *Because they went out from us, but they were not of us; for if they had been of us, they would no doubt have remained with us [n: (I Joan. II)]*. And the Lord through the prophet: *My sons,* he says, *are gone out from me, and they are not [n: (Jer. X)]*.

At that time, then, when these sons, not legitimate but bastards, assailed those who were of right sonship by the assertion of a different faith, since heretical [1045D] princes favoured the assertors of the perverse faith, holy and catholic men, driven from their sees, suffered losses and exiles and many perils, because they preached the right dogma of faith and would not communicate with the heretics.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Since, therefore, both were distinguished with ecclesiastical offices *32* and held the same sacraments, and for this reason were believed to belong to the bosom [sic: *grem um*] of one Mother, they were divided by the sole difference of faith, and that society of the wicked persecuted the catholic society; tainted by the heat of whose persecution, this society calls herself black, and says that the sun has discoloured her, the sun for whom she was contending, that he should be said to be equal to the Father in divinity, and of one substance, power and will with him, and in humanity should be believed true [1046A] man, which the contrary party blasphemously said was not so.

The catholic party, then, adds the cause of her discolouring, because, she says, *the sons of my mother have fought against me,* and since, under the persecution of such sons, not legitimate but bastards, that is, heretics, the party of sound faith was compelled to move to other sees and to leave its own cities, she adds that *they have set me the keeper in the vineyards, my own vineyard I have not kept;* for many Churches at that time, deprived of their own pastor, were stained by the heretics with perverse dogma; and holy priests, exiled, sowed the word of faith in foreign places, which they were prevented by the necessity of the times from doing in their own.

Otherwise. Since the mother of every creature is the goodness and [1046B] power of the Creator, for it was of his goodness that he made us when we were not; of his power, because he could do all things that he would [n: (Psal. CXIII)]; and since in all creation there are two rational ones, the angel and man, because of their sharing in reason, and since both were made by the same goodness and power, they seem to have as it were a certain fellowship of brotherhood, so that they are called sons of the same mother. But because that part of the angels which fell, seeing and envying the human creature rise by the grace of God to that glory from which it had been borne, persecuted it in every way and laboured to destroy it, let man himself say in the person of the weak: *The sons of my mother have fought against me, they have set me the keeper in the vineyards, my own vineyard I have not [1046C] kept.*

While, he says, these rational spirits, the sons of my mother, fought against me, they compelled my frail mind to love earthly actions and to desert the keeping of my soul. For by vineyards, whose work is under the open sky, the occupations of outward actions also can be designated: for the more the mind is scattered abroad about earthly actions through the outward senses, the more the soul is neglected inwardly in the keeping of itself.

For since the soul is invisible, it is in no way touched by delight in bodily things, except that, cleaving to the body, it has the senses of that same body as certain openings for going out. For sight, hearing, smell, taste and touch are as it were certain ways of the mind, by which it may come abroad and desire those things which [1046D] are outside *33* its substance. For through these senses of the body, as through certain windows, the soul looks upon each outward thing, and looking, desires it: hence Jeremias says: *Death is come up through your windows, it is entered into your houses [var: Vulg. Jr 9:21 *per fenestras nostras … domos nostras*, "through our windows … our houses"] [n: (Jerem. IX)]*. For death comes up through the windows and enters the houses when concupiscence, coming through the senses of the body, enters the dwelling of the mind.

The adverse powers, then, make the mind that is busied with earthly actions careless of itself, while through the slackness of outward works they force it by no means to stand in the keeping of its own soul, so that it can deservedly say: *The sons of my mother have fought against me, they have set me the keeper in the vineyards, my own vineyard I have kept [var: Vulg. Ct 1:5 *vineam meam non custodivi*, "my own vineyard I have not kept"].* But because in all adversities [1047A] or perils brought upon her, not so much by strangers as by brothers, the Church universal or particular, or any faithful soul, is afflicted with a certain heat of doubt, because many hold the faith in name only, and it is not easy to discern who are truly faithful, she turns to the Lord and her bridegroom, and, begging his help for the grace of discernment of this kind, goes on with a prayer of entreaty, saying: *Show me, thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest, where thou liest in the midday.*

VERSE 6.-- *Show me, thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest, where thou liest in the midday, lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions.*

God is said to show in three ways, namely by thing, by understanding, by voice. By thing, indeed, when, the mind being doubtful, [1047B] he makes manifest by a sign of some power of his what is to be chosen or what is to be held; for in this manner, in order to restrain a seditious people from murmuring, when among the twelve rods which he receives from the several tribes, and commanded to be placed in the tabernacle of the covenant, the rod of Aaron alone, in the tribe of Levi, had blossomed and brought forth fruit [n: (Num. XVII)], in presage of the most noble succession to come, from which that singular birth through the Virgin Mary was to be accomplished, he showed by this sign that he had chosen Aaron himself with his offspring for the priesthood, to draw near in a special manner to offer sacrifices to God and to enter into the Holy of holies.

Further, he shows by understanding when, touching the mind of someone [1047C] with a hidden inspiration, he reveals to his understanding what better thing he should choose to do. Whence, when David the king, the name forewarning, was thinking to build a house for the Lord, it did not displease God, but rather pleased him; because, although he forbade him to do this in his own time, because he was *a man of blood* [n: (II Reg. XVI)], yet he did not disapprove the cause, nay rather judged it worthy of a reward so great, and of reverence, that he promised him that he would raise up a Son who should be exalted by the prerogative of so great *34* peace, innocence and wisdom, as to be fit to accomplish such a work. Moreover, God, wishing to reward this good will of his, which was pleasing to him: *Foretelling,* he says, *I foretell to thee that the Lord will build thee a house [n: (I Reg. VII)]*. So then, that God might show that what [1047D] he had shown him through the understanding was pleasing to him, he not only did not forbid what he had thought, but also, that that very house might become of great esteem and power in the whole world, he approached it with his grace.

But God shows by voice, now indeed by himself, now through others: by himself indeed, as he spoke to Abraham and Moses and the other prophets; but through others, as when, first through the prophets themselves, then through the apostles and the other believers, to whom he committed his things to be announced to others, he showed what was of his will, what was of their salvation.

Again, according to these ways, God is said to show in four ways, namely sometimes by thing and understanding, sometimes by thing and voice, very often by understanding and voice; but sometimes all at once, both by thing and by understanding [1048A] and by voice. For he shows by thing and understanding when, namely, he foreshows something by the showing of things, and at once foreshows through the understanding to him to whom he shows it. Hence it is that when the diversities and qualities of the kingdoms to come were shown to Daniel through the diverse likenesses of beasts, some things he did not understand except by the teaching of an angel; but some, after the things were seen, he understood at once by himself, as in a certain place, after the likenesses of things were shown to him, he at once adds: *And I,* he says, *understood,* and in another place: *A word was revealed to Daniel surnamed Balthasar, and a true word, and great strength: and he understood the word [n: (Dan. VIII et X)]*.

But he is said to show by thing and voice when he foretells something [1048B] by a thing and pronounces by voice what it signifies; as when he had given Peter the astonishing catch of fishes, he forthwith opened the cause of this miracle: *Fear not,* he says, *from henceforth thou shalt catch men [n: (Luc. VIII)]*.

Further, he shows by understanding and voice when he gives someone [sic: *alicu*] to understand his will, and proves by an open voice that he has understood this by his inspiration. Hence it is that Jacob, when about to go down into Egypt, having understood that it had not been done without the will and disposition of God that Joseph should be promised, God not having been consulted about this, as the Scripture relates: *He set out with all his house, and came to the well of the oath, and there, having slain victims to the God of his father, he heard him in a vision saying to him: Jacob, Jacob; who [1048C] answered: Here I am; and he said: Go down into Egypt; fear not, and I will make thee there a great nation [n: (Gen. XXXI)]*. Behold, what *35* he had understood to be of the divine will, the divine voice also attested that it had judged this to him through the understanding.

But he seems to judge all at once, both by thing and by understanding and by voice, when he intimates something by some foregoing sign, by the causation of which sign set beneath him the capable sense of the one seeing weighs through the Spirit of understanding, which the author of that cause confirms also by a voice following after. Lastly, when Isaias, fearing to take up the office of preaching, said: *Woe is me, because I have held my peace [sic: tacu], because I am a man of polluted lips, and I dwell in the midst of a people that hath polluted lips [n: (Isa. VI)]*; by a sign given him by God, which he shows, saying: *And one of the seraphim flew to me, [1048D] and in his hand a live coal, which he had taken with the tongs from the altar, and he touched my lips, saying: Behold, I have touched [var: Vulg. Is 6:7 *tetigit os meum … Ecce tetigit hoc labia tua*, "touched my mouth … Behold, this hath touched thy lips"] thy lips with this, and thy iniquity shall be taken away, and thy sin shall be cleansed,* he understood the condescension of the grace calling him; and when the Lord said, *whom shall I send, and who shall go for us?* he reverently attended to the divine will, and presently, offering himself humbly and dutifully, *behold me,* he said, *send me [n: (Isa. VI)]*. And the Lord, what he had foreshown by the thing and had opened through the understanding, proved also by voice, so as to appoint the prophet to the office of preaching: *Go,* he says, *and thou shalt say to this people: Hear, you that hear me, and do not understand, and see the vision, and do not know [n: (ibid.)]*.

In some one, then, of the ways set down above, the Church, or any soul pleasing to God, asks that there be shown [1049A] to her the grace of the needful discernment, where she can and ought to recognize the well-pleasing dwelling of the divine condescension. *Show,* she says, *me, thou whom my soul loveth.* Not at random, she says, not lightly, and as though it should hold but little weight, is this showing of thine to be taken, O Lord; and therefore grant the gift of this grace not to just anyone, but to me, who love thee; because to the perception of this subtlety only the power of contemplative charity avails.

And what wilt thou show? Namely, *where thou feedest, where thou liest in the midday.* Who are those whom God feeds, but these whom he himself leads forth and sets out in the green places of the virtues, where they may gather the diverse kinds of holiness, whose wholesomeness may confer health on the soul, drive away diseases, and make a man effective [1049B] unto every good work. But what these kinds are, of such great vigour that they make a man so strong, so fair, so glorious, is clearer than light: namely, humility, patience, obedience, chastity, cleanness of body and soul, incorruption of mind and mortification of the flesh, contempt of the world and the fear of God, and finally the very charity of God and of neighbour, without which the compounding of all these kinds will be of no efficacy. These things are for a judgment to one desiring to know who and of what kind are those whom God feeds, *36* namely, that they are the sheep of that true Shepherd, of whom he himself says: *My sheep hear my voice, and I* the Lord *know them [n: (Joan. X)]*, so that he proves those to be his sheep who hear his voice, and they are sheep for this reason, because they hear, whose such [1049C] hearing and being is his knowing; just as, on the contrary, those who do not hear are not sheep either, since they are rather *ravening wolves under the clothing of sheep,* of whom he says that *by their fruits you shall know them [n: (Matth. VII)]*, whose not hearing and not being is his not knowing; if, as he himself says to such: *I know you not; depart from me, you cursed, into everlasting fire [n: (Matth. XXV)]*.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

The place, then, where God feeds those whom he feeds is the meadows or green places of the virtues, and also the pastures of spiritual doctrines, where it is found and recognized who are the sheep of the Lord and who are not, because *they that are according to the spirit mind the things that are of the spirit; but the sensual perceiveth not the things that are of the Spirit of God [n: (I Cor. II)]*.

Now these pastures in which God feeds whomsoever he feeds are not uniform, because of a twofold [1049D] manner, namely of living and of teaching, although in each case they are in themselves indeed the same: for the one is called good life, the other good knowledge; yet in the subjects in which they are, and of which they are predicated, they are diverse because of the diverse distributions of graces; because one indeed is predicated humble, another gentle or mild; this one patient, that one obedient; another chaste, and another merciful or generous, or any other thing of this kind that pertains to the manner of living well. But in the other part, which is good knowledge, it is predicated of this subject that he is a good orator or preacher; of that one, that he is a good interpreter or expositor; of another, that he is skilled in the liberal arts, or any other thing that belongs to the manner of teaching well.

[1050A]The things predicated particularly also admit of more and less, while on this side one is more patient than another, one more chaste than another, or anything else that can befall an honest life more and less Further, on the other side, one is more learned than another, one more eloquent or more erudite than another; or anything else that can befall a knowing soul also, more and less.

Although, then, in pastures of this kind, in which God feeds what he feeds, not simply but manifoldly, there is the diversity which we have spoken of, and in that very diversity more and less occurs, yet those who are fed, although by reason of the diversity and measure of gifts they are not fed equally, are all nevertheless the sheep of the Lord and are so called; because, as, though star differs from star in glory, yet all the stars [1050B] are contained under one firmament, so there are indeed many differences of the saints, because of the different virtues that are in them, yet all are known to belong to the one kingdom of God, *37*.
