Whence, when the Lord said through the prophet: *Behold, I myself will seek my sheep and will visit them, as the shepherd visiteth his flock in the day when he shall be in the midst of his sheep that were scattered,* he adds a little after: *in the rivers and in all the habitations of the land and on the mountains of Israel shall be their pastures [n: (Ezech. XXXIV)]*. The rivers come forth from the springs; but the pastures of the land are lowly, those of the mountains are lofty

What then is meant by the rivers but certain deeds and sayings which flow forth from the great Fathers as from springs? [1050C] And what by the pastures of the land but the lighter teachings of the divine precepts? And what by the pastures of the mountains but the lofty sacraments of the divine mysteries? God has therefore set before his sheep, great and small, weak and strong, pastures befitting both, according to the capacity of both, so that both the small and the weak may have that whereby they may be nourished and strengthened unto eternal life, and the great and strong that whereby they may advance and be filled. In such sheep of his, surely, which he feeds, and in which he feeds, in this manner, God prepares rest for himself, so that by the grace of his indignation [cj: *indignationis*; read *dignationis*, "condescension"] he may lie and rest in them, in whom he is then said to lie and rest when he takes delight in their devout acts. Whence through the prophet: *Upon whom,* he says, *shall I rest but upon the humble and the quiet and him that trembleth at [1050D] my words? [var: Vulg. Is 66:2 *ad quem autem respiciam, nisi ad pauperculum, et contritum spiritu*, "to whom shall I look, but to the poor little one and the contrite of spirit"] [n: (Isa. LXVI.)]* On the contrary, he is said to labour in the minds of the reprobate when he is as it were wearied by their depraved acts. Whence, when the prophet said to certain perverse men: *You have made the Lord to labour with your words;* and they answered: *Wherein have we made him labour; in this* he says, *that you have said: Every one that doeth evil is good in the sight of the Lord, and such please him [n: (Malach. II)]*. The Divinity, then, which is driven away from the hearts of the wicked, exasperated and in a manner wearied by their perverse acts: *For the holy Spirit of discipline,* as it is written, *will flee from the deceitful, and will withdraw himself from thoughts that are without understanding, and he shall be rebuked when iniquity cometh in [n: (Sap. I)]*, whose repulsion is their rejection, it assuredly, in the minds of the saints, by whose devout acts [1051A] it is invited and received, sweetly and as it were delightedly lies and may rest. whose reception is their election.

The Church, then, or the holy soul, asks that it be shown her where the Bridegroom feeds and where he lies, and this not at any time whatever, but in the midday. Since in this place midday ought to be taken only according to anagogy, it is pleasing first to consider what, or in what ways, day is said, and how many and what the parts of the day are, so that, these things being known beforehand, we may leave to another's judgment which more ready sense the diverse signification contains.

Now day seems not to be said simply, because of the diverse forms of its signification, so that, because of the community of the name but the diversity of its reason or definition, it holds the force of equivocation.

[1051B] *38* Let us suppose, then, that there are four forms of day, of which the first indeed may be called the natural day, but the following one, established after the form of the former, may not absurdly be named the active day. The third, which consists by the light of reason alone, may not unfittingly be called the intellectual day; further, the last, which never fails and alone lacks time, and stands fixed in its own immutability, may most fittingly be written the eternal day; and the one indeed is the support of human life, the second the pattern for ordering life, the third the mirror for discerning life, and the fourth and last the reward of a life well consummated.

Let us establish that each of these is divided into three parts, namely into morning, midday and evening, excepting only the last, which always was without time, [1051C] and is and will be: for its being and its incomparable beauty is God himself.

And the first indeed, which is the natural, pertains nothing to the present place, except that the other two only, the last indeed, as we have said, being without time, are established after its form.

In the natural day, then, morning is the first rising of the sun, which, increasing little by little as it ascends, makes midday about the middle of the day, which comes about at that place beyond which the sun, not advancing, is fixed as it were at a centre; and thence again, decreasing as it descends, it makes evening about the end of the day, which is the full setting of the sun.

Now after the form of this day is ordered that one which we have called the active, from the beginning of the world up to its end, whose morning was that time before [1051D] the law in which man used the natural law only; its midday under the law and grace, when he was instructed by both the natural and the written law; further, its evening the time of the last judgment, when he will be judged both for the natural and for the written law, whether unto life or unto death.

For before the law, through the natural law, he had morning, that is, a certain beginning of discernment, with reason as guide, by which he could see and recognize how far he excelled the brute animals, when he could discern good and evil; but above all, to consider and understand that no creature can create itself, but that there is a certain uncreated thing which commanded all things that have been made to be made, and to perceive, from the beauty and usefulness of the creature itself, how much more excellent and glorious that is than all created things, [1052A] and for this reason that he would be inexcusable if he should pay to the creature the honour due to the Creator.

But when that natural law had in some been overwhelmed by the custom of sinning, the written law was given, which should forbid and restrain sinners, from which the merit of each might be established, while either the keeping of it was the cause of reward, or the transgression of it the cause of damnation. But in some *39* that natural law itself so flourished that they had no need of the written law, since they were a law to themselves and showed the work of the law written in their hearts, and, being without the law, did the things that are of the law, their conscience bearing them witness in the day when God shall judge the secrets of men [n: (Num. II)]. But where to the natural law [1052B] the written law was added, after the morning there came as it were midday, because those upon whom the sun of justice had now risen, the commandment having been given them, whether of truth or of the new law, were on both sides kindled with so great a fervour of charity towards God that they would rather choose to die than to transgress the honourable law prescribed to them by God.

This one may see in those most fervent zealots of both laws who had the strongest zeal, whether for defending or for binding themselves by oath to the holy law; of whom, for example, one in the old law was Phinees the son of Eleazar, who vindicated the law by slaying those who had become transgressors of the law: Mathathias also and his sons, who defended the law, and the seven brothers, who chose rather to die by keeping the law than to live by transgressing it. What [1052C] then shall we say of those most glorious and most valiant fighters in the new law, namely the apostles and their followers, in whom there was so great a fervour of divine charity, so great a zeal for rightness, that it did not hesitate for the faith, for justice, for the truth to bear all adversities, to undergo every kind of death, and finally to fall in death itself? Of whom the Apostle says: *The saints,* he says, *had trial of mockeries and stripes, moreover also of bands and prisons, they were stoned, they were put to death by the sword [n: (Hebr. XI)]*. Were not all these, under the law or under grace, in the midday, when they bore so great a heat of temptation in body and heart, the great heats, surely, of that midday burning doubly, because both their mind burned with the fervour of holy charity and their body [1052D] was afflicted with the heat of much tribulation.

The evening, too, which is the time of the last judgment, ought to decree for such veteran recruits refreshment and rest for the heat of their labour, to whom, by the heavenly king for whose love they have toiled, there will be given the bounty of eternal reward, namely that most general, unfailing and unchangeable good which is the God of gods himself. For this is God's rewarding: to give himself to his elect.

Further, that intellectual day consists not unsuitably of the same parts which we set down above, because in the understanding of each one who flourishes in the splendour and heat of the virtues, the beginning of a good work is as it were a certain morning; midday, the perfecting of the work well begun through the increase [1053A] and progress of holiness; and evening, the best completion, even to the end, of the work well begun and better perfected. This day in each wise man is the understanding itself, in that he judges and discerns the things that are about him *40* whether in thought or in work, because he is in the light and can see what he ought to do or what to avoid; *he* moreover *that walketh in darkness knoweth not whither* he goeth [n: (Joan. XII; Isa. XXIX, L)]; because the darkness has blinded his eyes, and therefore his works are works of darkness, who, since he has not this day, that is, understanding, is said to differ little from irrational things, because of certain irrational and as it were bestial motions of depraved acts.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

The first beginnings, as it were, of the light of this intellectual day are [1053B] the first works of uprightness, still small and slight. But when they have grown by assiduity, and the soul has conceived a certain heat from them, that heat little by little taking increase, it blazes up into the whole fullness of divine charity, as into the midday of this day, when a clearer light of understanding is opened, so that now nothing in earthly things pleases it, but it glows wholly in the love of God. Whence the Psalmist, to describe the morning and the midday of this day, says: *My heart grew hot within me, and in my meditation a fire blazes out [n: (Psal. XXXVIII)]*; as if he said openly: Long since, through the desire of good work, my mind grew hot; but because by the meditation of this, of the commandments of God, it is daily led by contemplation from the lowest things to the highest, it no [1053C] longer only grows warm, but burns wholly with the fire of charity in the love of its Creator.

But the evening of such a day is the consummation of good work even to the end of life, nor will the preceding parts be able to confer anything. If this last be lacking, *for he that shall persevere unto the end, he shall be saved [n: (Matth. XXIV)]*. Whence the Psalmist: *The lifting up,* he says, *of my hands, an evening sacrifice [n: (Psal. CXL)]*; as if he said in other words: The beginning or the progress of my works is then a sacrifice to God, if I shall have closed my last day in the pursuit of them. And by the precept of the law the tail of the victim is commanded to be offered in sacrifice. For in the tail is the end of the body. And he sacrifices well who brings the sacrifice of good work through to the end of the action that is due. Hence Joseph is described as having had among the rest of his brothers a tunic reaching [1053D] to the ankles; for a tunic reaching to the ankle is good work reaching to consummation.

The morning of the intellectual day, then, is inchoative virtue; midday, perfective virtue; evening, contemplative virtue. And such a day admits more and less according to the threefold quality of temporal increase in man: for in boyhood the understanding is less, in youth greater, in old age greatest. For what remains over fails in many, as in the decrepit, who sometimes seem to become boys again in sense. Now boyhood, or adolescence, is a kind of morning, as it were the rising of the sun; youth a kind of midday, as it were the sun's highest ascent and its hottest time, just as that age too is of the greatest heat and strength; but old age a kind of [1054A] evening, as it were the sun's descent from the high pole and the failing of its heat, just as in that *41* age also a man fails from the heat of youth.

Whether, then, the understanding be distinguished by the stages of the ages, or be distinguished within the understanding itself by the increases of works, it may not absurdly be called the intellectual day, so that this may be what the Psalmist says: *Evening, and morning, and at midday I will tell and declare [n: (Psal. LIV)]*; as if he said: both in boyhood and in youth and in old age, whether of age or of understanding, as in morning, midday, evening, by teaching and by working I will declare the rule of living rightly.

The last form of day, indeed, which we have called the eternal, has of these three parts midday alone. For of itself it has no morning at all, but in those who [1054B] partake of it, some morning: before the resurrection indeed in the soul alone, when by dying happily they are born to it; but in the resurrection both in soul and in body, when by rising most happily they are born to it again; which happiness of body and soul Holy Scripture calls the double robe, but the blessedness of the soul alone, the single.

That, then, which has some beginning of blessedness, even now in the soul, and then in body and soul together, is in those who partake a kind of morning, as has been said; but in its own nature and property, which unchangeably was, and is, and will be, it never had a morning, because blessedness itself is God, in whose unchangeableness those who have been made unchangeable partake.

[1054C]Further, evening neither could nor can be had in any way, whether in itself or in those who partake, because they will stand fixed eternally with him to whose natural eternity they have been joined through grace. But midday it has always had and will have, both in itself and in those who partake: in itself indeed, because of the special excellence of beauty, brightness and rest; but in those who partake, because of the most ardent fervour of divine charity, which in that life is more fervent than that which they can have in this life by so much as it is agreed that that life is far happier than this, and beyond comparison with it. Whence the prophet: *The Lord liveth,* he says, *whose fire is in Sion, and his furnace in Jerusalem [var: Vulg. Is 31:9 *dixit Dominus*, "saith the Lord"] [n: (Isa. XXXVI)]*. It is more to be a furnace than a fire, because a fire can be even small; but in a furnace a vaster flame [1054D] is kindled. Now Sion is called *watching,* but Jerusalem *vision of peace*; for our peace we watch for here meanwhile, that there afterward we may see it fully. From the love of the Lord, then, there is fire in Sion, a furnace in Jerusalem, because here we burn to some extent with the flames of his love, where we contemplate something of him, but there we shall burn fully where we shall see fully him whom we love.

This eternal day, then, has midday alone, far brighter and more fervent than the middays of the formal or natural days described above; which midday nevertheless, according to the merits of those who partake, admits more *42* and less, because, though in itself it is one and the same, yet those who partake partake of it unlike. For *in the house of the Father there are many mansions* [1055A] [n: (Joan. XIV)], because in the one reward the glory of the elect is diverse, as also the misery of the reprobate, according to the merit of each.

In this place, therefore, neither the midday of the first nor of the last day is to be considered, but of those between them, the middle ones, namely the active and the intellectual, because the intention of each one who lives rightly ought to hang upon the pursuit either of the active or of the contemplative life.

Since therefore many seem to be in the fervour of divine charity, whether existing in the active or in the contemplative life, who do not have the single eye, because they do good things not with a right intention, while they make show of these not for God but for empty glory or for earthly gain, whom it is very difficult to distinguish from those who truly [1055B] love God, the Church, or some holy soul, asks her Bridegroom to show her in some manner of the modes set out above, through the spirit of discretion, who they are who love and follow him not feignedly [sic: *simultane*] but truly; in whom he feeds with the sweetness of his commandments, and in whom he lies and rests with the delightful rest of the virtues in the midday, that is, in the fervour of his charity, as he himself says: *If any one love me, he will keep my word, and my Father loves him, and we will come to him and will make our abode with him [n: (ibid.)]*. For he who observes the words of God proves that he loves God; and he who loves God is worthy of God's abode. And why she asks for the gift of this discretion she adds the cause, saying: *Lest I begin to wander [1055C] after the flocks of thy companions.*

Some are companions of Christ, some are thought to be; and because they differ in the quality of their life, they are rightly called not a flock but *flocks*. For those who are are spoken of in one way, those who are thought to be in another: because those who are truly imitate Christ, whether they be prelates teaching and instructing their subjects by word and example, as Christ did his disciples, or whether they be subjects hearing and questioning one another, as it is written of Christ that *they found him in the temple hearing and asking them [n: (Luc. II)]*. But those who are only thought to be, but are not, feigning that they follow Christ, are set over others in name alone, and are set over their subjects neither by word nor by example. And the subjects among themselves, as though they were brothers, are joined by a feigned [1055D] charity, and therefore they are not recognized. Such companions of the bridegroom, then, both are and are thought to be: they are because true, they are thought to be because feigned.

Wherefore she asks for the discretion needful to her, so that, since she had set forth above that even the sons of her mother had fought against her, and it is therefore doubtful who are faithful, who unfaithful, the grace of discretion being given her, she may be able to recognize to whom she ought to join herself, to whose teaching to give credence, from whom to take the form of living rightly, *43* so that [cj: *ut*; read *ne*, "lest"] it be necessary for her to go round as a wanderer and perhaps ignorantly to go off into the errors of companions [sic: *sed alium*] who are feigned.

To her thus asking, the Lord, as if chiding her why in some temptation she distrusts his help, on whom for her great consolation he had conferred a diverse [1056A] gift of his grace, answered: *If thou know not thyself, O fairest, among women?*

VERSE 7.-- *If thou know not thyself, O fairest among women, go forth, and go away after the steps of the flocks, and feed thy kids beside the tents of the shepherds.*

A little above, where we described the fourfold day, intellectual or active, we set it particularly in some wise man: intellectual indeed because of reason and light among the darkness, that is, rightly dividing virtues and vices; but active because of discretion, bringing forth into works of light the things it has rightly perceived. But now nothing prevents our setting universally also in each man an intellectual day only, because of the liveliness of the reason naturally implanted in him.

[1056B]Let us suppose, then, after the likeness of the natural day, which, whole, is divided into day and night, two portions of this intellectual day also in universal man, according to privation and habit, that is, knowledge of self and ignorance, of which the former is reckoned for the day, the latter for the night.

For this is a certain habit of the soul, of justice, when out of full knowledge of itself it takes on a certain form of uprightness, affected by which, or disposed to which, it makes such progress through the grace that helps it that such an affection or disposition passes into a habit, and from this the whole man is declared just. And the privation of this habit is injustice, when the soul itself either never admitted any [1056C] form of piety, or, if it had one, lost it, divine grace betaking itself away; or drew to itself the vice of some deformity, whose affection or disposition in like manner passes into a habit contrary to the former, so that, as by participation of justice one is called just, so by participation of injustice he is called unjust: which is according to habit; but according to privation, either that he himself lacks justice, or that injustice is the privation of justice, according to the description of evil, by which it is said that evil is nothing other than the privation of good; and justice is a species of good, as on the contrary injustice is of evil.

But because this species of good is born from that part which is knowledge of self, and the species of evil from that part which is ignorance of self, it must be said what it is to know and not to know oneself.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

[1056D]For he truly knows himself who weighs with subtle searching how far he excels every visible creature in that he was created after the likeness of God, which likeness is regarded not in the body but in the mind, according to the prerogative of a reason that understands *44* and discerns; how the creature too, serving his use, is subject to him; that his bodily substance is stretched upward as a kind of reminder to the mind, which ought to despise earthly things and raise itself to heavenly ones; that God so washed [cj: *diluerit*; read *dilexerit*, "loved"] his nature that he deigned to shed his blood to redeem it; lastly, that he predestined that same nature to so great a glory that he [1057A] decreed to make him at some time partaker of his immortality, unchangeableness and eternity, and therefore made him capable of receiving all uprightness, virtue and discipline; and so from this knowledge of himself, as by the light too of a certain day [sic: *dici*], he is led [sic: *dicitur*] to desiring and obtaining justice. As, on the contrary, he is drawn to injustice by a certain blindness of mind, as by the darkness of a certain night, when he neither knows nor can consider in himself all those things set down above, which is to be ignorant of oneself. Knowledge of self, then, is as it were a certain good that disposes to justice, as ignorance of self also is a certain evil that deprives of that same justice. The Bridegroom, then, that is, Christ, chiding his bride, the Church or any holy soul you please, who had complained, who suffered persecution not only from strangers but also from her brothers, and that it was therefore very difficult [1057B] to discern among the companions of the bridegroom who are true, who false, chiding her for faint-heartedness of this kind, and because, as if she were forsaken, she did not show the largess of the grace of God in herself: *If thou know not thyself,* he says, *O fairest among women, go forth*; if, he says, thou dost not at all take the consideration of thyself, and knowest not that thou wast made after my likeness, that I made thee capable of reason, so that thou mayest understand what thou oughtest to seek and what to flee, that I redeemed thee with my blood, and among the synagogues of divers doctrines, or the other souls, made thee alone fair without spot and wrinkle, both by the washing of baptism and by the special gift of my sacraments, and also by the distribution of divers graces; that I made thee such in this manner that rightly [1057C] thou oughtest not to be troubled, but rather to be comforted, and to glory in me, since it is made impossible that thou be utterly forsaken, whom I have consecrated to myself by so great a condescension of my grace and mercy; if, he says, thou art ignorant of this and refusest to know it, *go forth,* that is, go out of doors from my company and from the fellowship of my secret, because *every one that is ignorant shall be ignored, and there is no participation of light with darkness [n: (I Cor. XIV; II Cor. VI)]*, *and go away after the steps of the flocks;* that is, imitate the acts of the peoples who are not one flock, nor one fold, because they have not one shepherd, since, cutting themselves off from the unity of the faith, they follow divers masters of manifold errors. *And feed thy kids,* that is, nourish and cherish thy carnal and wanton acquaintances, *or thy kids,* that is, sinners made [1057D] rotten by thy word and example, and to be set at the left hand in the judgment; *45* *beside the tents of the shepherds,* that is, beside the conventicles of erring masters, who have many tents, because they set up divers synagogues on account of the various sects of doctrines, departing from that one tent of mine in which is *one Lord, one faith, one baptism [n: (Ephes. IV)]*, and there is no schism in the catholic unity.

Now this voice of the Bridegroom is not that of one commanding but of one foretelling, because, according to his own voice: *No man can serve two masters [n: (Matth. VI)]*, that is, God and the world, nor love God and the world at once; and by the voice of John it is said: *for every one that loveth the world, the charity of the Father is not in him [n: (I Joan. II)]*. Wherefore, if to love the world is not to love God, but [1058A] to love God is to be just, surely to love the world is to be unjust. Now two contraries cannot be at one and the same time together in one and the same thing, so that anyone could be at once just and unjust. Again, in one and the same thing, yet at different times, it is possible for two contraries to be, so that now someone doing something good is declared just, but at another time, sinning, unjust. Further, no one can be called definitely at once just and unjust at one and the same time, as we have already said; which is taught from the words of the Gospel, where it is said to certain men: *Either make the tree good and its fruit good, or make the tree evil and its fruit evil [n: (Matth. XII)]*; as if he said: Because you cannot be called definitely both good and evil at once at one and the same time, it is necessary that [1058B] one of these, existing in you, destroy the other, that is, either goodness malice, or malice goodness. This indeed may be said according to the state of the inward man, who is always set either in good or in evil; but if you consider the whole man, who consists of soul and body, this rule of the dialecticians fails, because two contraries can be together at one and the same time in one and the same thing. For good and evil are contraries, but man and sinner are two things. And man indeed, according to substance, is a certain good, *for all things that God made were very good [n: (Gen. II)]*; but the sinner is a certain evil, which man himself made. And these two contraries are in one and the same at one and the same time, because this, that [1058C] he is man, is good, that he is a sinner, evil.

The Bridegroom, then, addresses the bride more severely, as one wandering and uncertain and less confident, and, showing a certain thing with no mean between, from this one proposition in which he says: *If thou know not thyself, O fairest among women, go forth, and go away after the steps of the flocks,* he teaches that one of the two must necessarily be chosen, namely, that either, taking up by knowledge of herself the good of justice, she cleave specially to God with contemplative charity, or, falling by ignorance of herself into the evil of injustice, she depart from it, by loving the world, through the privative action. for the evil of injustice deprives man of the good of justice.

*46* The Bridegroom, therefore, says to the bride, that is, Christ to each faithful soul, that if she has sunk herself into such blindness of heart that she neither will nor can [1058D] recognize how gracious God has endowed her above others with beauty of mind for considering and understanding the purpose of the divine goodness in her, she is to go forth from the secret place of her mind and go away by the broad and spacious way of erring peoples, where she may nourish carnal motions according to the doctrine of masters who teach contrary things, and who will not have the tabernacle of the Lord, in which are the golden urn having the manna, and the rod of Aaron, and the tables of the testament, because in it are preached and venerated the divinity and humanity of Christ, and the priestly dignity, and the sublimity of the divine commandments.

For heretics or schismatics have many tents, on account of the various doctrines by which they seduce their followers, and they think their assembly [1059A] to be a church, when they are a synagogue of Satan. But there is no place of salvation outside the catholic Church, nor are other churches to be spoken of besides this one, which, although it is divided into many particular churches, yet because of the unity of the faith is one holy universal Church. Whence also in this same Canticle, when the Bridegroom had said that *there are sixty queens, and eighty concubines, and of young maidens there is no number,* he added: *One also is my dove, my perfect one, she is the one of her mother, the chosen of her that bore her [var: Vulg. Ct 6:8 *Una est columba mea … electa genetrici suae*, "one is my dove … chosen to her that bore her"] [n: (Cant. VI)]*. Hence it is that the first Shepherd himself of the same Church, not yet having attained perfect knowledge, when, the glory of the Lord having been seen on the mountain, he said: *Lord, it is good for us to be here; let us make here three tabernacles, one for thee, one for Moses, and [1059B] one for Elias [n: (Matth. XVII)]*, was not heard, nor was such counsel of his approved [sic: *approba um*], because neither in the law nor in prophecy was any Church to be set up apart, but that of the New Testament, in which the law and the prophets are summed up, was alone and one throughout the whole world the holy and universal Church to be received and venerated. For the Gospel is nothing other than the law and the prophets announce as future by things and by words; the Gospel represents it as already done. Whence Ezechiel, in the vision of the glory of God: *The appearance,* he says, *of the wheels and the works, as if it were a wheel in the midst of a wheel [n: (Ezech. I)]*. The wheel in the midst of a wheel, namely, is the New Testament within the Old Testament, because what the Old Testament prefigured, this the New Testament [1059C] displayed; and the exposition of the Old Testament is the New Testament. For the Old and New Testament are one, and hold themselves a judge, because what that one represents in figure this one represents in truth; and what that one shows as to be done, this one shows as already done, so that they are one tabernacle of God.

*47* The Church of God, therefore, neither ought nor can be ignorant of herself, she who is adorned with so great a beauty [sic: *pulchritudinem*] of mysteries and sacraments, whose whole praise is the most seemly variety itself of venerable ordinances.

Further, singly, any soul you please ought not to be ignorant of herself, lest, while she does not at all weigh in herself the gifts of her Creator, by flowing away through unlawful things she become estranged from him. But if she considers any good things in herself through his grace, [1059D] she ought to attend to them gratefully and seemly, and to attend to them with zeal for good action, lest she be rebuked for the buried talent, but rather be presented with the reward of eternal recompense for the talent multiplied. Accordingly you too, O most holy [sic: *santissimae*] sisters, who are gathered together in the house of discipline, be not ignorant of yourselves, but rather look diligently about you, and attend to what or of what kind your bridegroom has made you: how he has set you apart from other men like wheat from the chaff, how he has fitted you specially to himself by the beauty of a singular way of life, with how manifold an adornment of virtues he has decked you, when, with pursuits alternating on this side and that, now by chastity, now by continence, now by obedience, now by humility, now by the frequency of prayers and vigils, you make a spectacle delightful to God and his saints.

[1060A]When, then, you behold yourselves such through the grace of God, do not cast your pearl before swine, that they trample it, that is, do not go out from that contemplative and most safe secret chamber of your Bridegroom, and go away after the din of worldly business, where you may fulfil carnal desires according to the doctrine of impious men, from whose tents you ought to flee, lest the earth of worldly conversation swallow you up; but rather, when you have rightly recognized yourselves, strive in every way to render thanks to the divine condescension, whose gift you are, and to advance day by day from good things to better, so that from this present tabernacle you may be able to pass over to that heavenly one, saying with the Psalmist: *How lovely are thy tabernacles, O Lord of hosts; my soul longeth and fainteth for [1060B] the courts of the Lord [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*.

VERSE 9.-- *To my company of horsemen, in Pharao's chariots, have I likened thee, my love.*

After the Lord has rebuked the Church in general, or specially the soul devoted to him, because in some adversity she doubted of his help, by whose grace she had merited the benefits of many good things, since, proved by adversities, she ought rather to have been stronger, he himself begins to show that she ought to recognize herself, so that the good of the grace of God which she does not know in herself may, by his showing, become manifest to her *48* and from this she may stand more constant in all adversities. He says, then: *To my company of horsemen, in Pharao's chariots, have I likened thee, my love.*



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

[1060C]First it must be said what that company of horsemen of the Lord was, and what the chariots of Pharao; then what is the signification of these two opposites, and also what the proportion of the things signifying and the things signified. The company of horsemen of the Lord was that army of the Israelite [sic: *Israelitci*] people, once accepted by God, which he led out of Egypt with a mighty hand and a lofty arm, Egypt having been struck with ten plagues; the terrible showing of whose tenth plague, and the sacrifice of the Paschal lamb at evening, was the swift deliverance of that people. For so says Scripture: that when the Lord was about to strike Egypt with this last plague, namely the death of all the first-born, he commands the children of Israel that by their families they should sacrifice a lamb at evening, whose blood they should put upon both posts of the house and upon the lintels [1060D] of the houses in which they ate it, so that, this sign being seen, the smiting angel should pass over, no one in that house being harmed. And when they had done this, and in that night the Lord struck every first-born in the land of Egypt, yet every house being safe which had been sprinkled with the blood of the lamb, all that company of horsemen of the Lord went out from the land of Egypt; and Pharao, when he pursued it with his chariots and his horsemen, was drowned in the Red Sea, that company of horsemen having crossed the same sea with dry foot [n: (Exod. XII-XV)].

If we apply the eyes of the mind, it is a most beautiful thing to consider what of mystery is contained in that going out from Egypt, the sacrifice of the evening lamb, the sprinkling of blood on the posts and the lintels of the houses, the pursuit of Pharao and his drowning, [1061A] the deliverance of the people also, and at last the bringing into the land of promise; and how far the likening of all these pertains to the universal Church. At the same time also it is pleasing to begin how the Lord went before that people by a pillar of cloud in the day, in the night by a pillar of fire, and bore before them that rod which by divine command stood forth as the worker of so many signs.

After the likeness, then, of this company of horsemen, which went before in figure, is set in order, or is being set in order, that wonderful company of horsemen of the Lord in which there have served and serve, first indeed the apostles and martyrs, then the priests and the orders of divers professions, then their successors, clothed in the armour of God, that is, with the breastplate of justice, the shield of faith, and the sword of the spirit, which is the word of God [n: (Ephes. VI)]. What then is this company [sic: *equitus*] [1061B] of horsemen? Surely the holy universal Church, in which whatever then went before in the thing only is now daily performed in the sacrament and in the thing of the sacrament. For what is the sacrifice of the lamb at evening but the passion of Christ at the end of the ages; the blood of which Lamb is put upon both posts when the sacrament of Christ's passion is both taken by the mouth unto redemption and is gathered with an intent mind unto *49* imitation. To put the blood of the lamb upon the lintels of the houses too is both to direct the intention of the mind, in which we dwell by thought, to the imitation of the Lord's passion, and to fortify with the sign of that passion the front of the body in which we dwell by living.

That ancient company [sic: *antiquitatus*] of the Lord, then, freed by the sacrifice [1061C] of the lamb from Egyptian slavery, the Red Sea having been crossed and the enemies drowned, after the law had been given and received in the desert, entered the land of promise. But this new company of horsemen of the Lord, after the passion of Christ, the yoke of diabolical damnation having been shaken off, the enemies of the soul, that is, vices and sins, having been drowned through the washing of baptism, has received through faith and the observance of the Gospel the land of the promised eternal inheritance. This his company of horsemen the Lord goes before, in the day by the pillar of cloud, in the night by the pillar of fire: who shows himself mild and gentle, with the lenity of mercy, to the saints, who are understood by the day; but to sinners, who are taken for the night, he shows himself terrible with the terror of judgment.

[1061D]Truly wonderful and glorious according to the mystery was the carrying of the rod in that company of horsemen, by which God is recorded to have shown among his people the prodigies and signs of his power. Could not the wood of the Lord's passion, which is the only hope and the sum of our whole salvation, be prefigured fittingly enough by this rod?

Plainly the Church of the Lord, which by his own voice is likened to this company of horsemen, bears this wonderful sign of the cross venerably before her in all the ordinances of her offices, in the doctrines of all sacraments and mysteries, in the uses of all things to be consecrated [sic: *sacra dandarum*]; nothing will be able to be ratified or holy in them except what has been confirmed by the foretaste of this sign.

[1062A]Of this Church, namely, which not unfittingly is also called the company of horsemen of the Lord, the suffering Christ is as it were the standard-bearer, who with this victorious sign of his death goes before her continually, against those chief horsemen and chariots of Pharao, and against all the onsets of his depraved suggestion and the incursions of his wicked contrivance. For this chief Pharao, as we say, who is the head of the wicked and of which head all the wicked are members, is opposed and contrary to that chief leader and author of this company of horsemen, Christ the Lord, who is the head of all the elect and of which head all the elect are members; and therefore these two companies of horsemen, namely of God and of the devil, always fight against each other, as being citizens of two cities contrary to each other.

[1062B]The city of God is the congregation of the faithful which stretches from Abel the just to the last elect, who have *not here a lasting city, but* seek *one to come* [n: (Hebr. XIII)]. Further, the city of the devil is the synagogue of the unfaithful, which from Cain the reprobate will be bounded at the *50* last of the reprobate, who, like Cain, who first built a city, seek and love earthly things, but make little of heavenly ones. And they are called cities by that mode of speech in which that which contains is put for that which is contained; not that they are contained in buildings, but because they are local and circumscribed: the one by the receptacle of blessedness, the other of misery; the one and the same thing, whether blessedness or misery, being disposed on both sides with a manifold disparity of retributions, [1062C] according to the diverse quality of merits.

But this city, which is taken for the present Church [sic: *Eccelsia*], is mingled of good and bad, but it itself is also separated by the certainty of the future sifting, so that two cities are therefore spoken of in this one, because the part of the good belongs to that receptacle of blessedness which is interpreted the heavenly Jerusalem, that is, *vision of peace*; but the part of the bad to that receptacle of misery which is named Babylon, that is, *confusion*.

That city indeed which is the congregation of the just is the company of horsemen and the chariots of God, of which it is said: *The chariot of God is manifold with ten thousands, thousands of them that rejoice in them [n: (Psal. XLVII)]*; in which chariots are also those of whom it is said to God by the prophet: *who mountest upon thy horses; and thy company of horsemen is health* [1062D] [var: Vulg. Hab 3:8 *qui ascendes super equos tuos, et quadrigae tuae salvatio*, "thy chariots are salvation"] [n: (Habac. III)]. For his horses are the saints, over whom he himself presides, who, the first seal having been opened, that is, the mystery of the incarnation having been revealed, went out on a white horse, that is, in a body shining and immaculate, as being conceived and born of the Virgin not in human wise but by the power of the Holy Spirit; and he was the leader of this his company of horsemen in time, by word and example, being to be its rewarder, after the warfare is completed, without time, with the reward of an unspeakable [sic: *inedicibilis*] good and a share in his eternal kingdom. Assuredly the glory of this procession is celebrated in the whole world to the praise of that leader who goes before: how, the same leader sounding the trumpet of the Holy Spirit, these horses, set in the chariot of God, went boldly to meet those armed with depravity and unfaithfulness, so that their sound went forth into all the earth; at the sound of whose [1063A] preaching they run together daily from all the ends of the world, and, coming to the end and joining themselves to this company of horsemen, cry out with the prophet: *From the ends of the earth we have heard praises, the glory of the just one [n: (Isa. XXIV)]*.

But on the contrary, from the opposite side, that city which is the synagogue of the unfaithful. They are the chariots and the company of horsemen of Pharao, that is, of the devil, under whose chariot are those horses red, black, white and dappled: red indeed with the blood of the saints whom they have slain, black with the deformity of sins, white with feigned faith and working, dappled with the diversity of doctrines and sects; over all of whom presides that one who, the *51* fourth seal having been opened, that is, the Lord's passion being at hand, went out on a pale horse, namely with the sign of persecution or of death, to fight through those who were dead to God against those who lived [1063B] to God, as is also added there, that *hell followed him, and power was given him over the four parts of the earth to kill and to slay with the sword, and with famine, and with death, and with the beasts of the earth [n: (Apoc. VI)]*. For hell is rightly compared to his company of horsemen because of the insatiable cruelty which after the Lord's passion raged through the four parts of the world with the four kinds of afflictions named above, namely the sword, famine, death, the beasts of the earth; in whose wretched company of horsemen the first and better known followers of the present leader were: in the passion indeed of that leader who came above on the white horse. Herod and Pilate, and after them many emperors of the Romans, slaying that most holy company of horsemen of the glorious leader by divers kinds of death through all the parts of the world; after whom, last [1063C] of all, the greater Antichrist: against him madness will be stirred, because in him principally will preside that rider of the pale horse, to seduce those who dwell on the earth and even, if it can be, the elect [n: (Matth. XXIV)]. For of this name [cj: *nomine*; read *homine*, "man"] exalting itself *above all that is called God, or that is worshipped [n: (II Thessal. II)]*, it is to be known that, as the incarnate Truth in his preaching chose for his company of horsemen the poor, the unlettered and the simple, so on the contrary he, to preach his falsehood, will choose the cunning and the double and those having the wisdom of this world. Whence it is said through Isaias: *Woe to the land, the cymbal of wings, which is beyond the rivers of Ethiopia, which sendeth ambassadors by sea, and in vessels of bulrush upon the waters [n: (Isa. XVIII)]*.

[1063D]The land, namely, to which *woe* is said, is principally that damned man who is called the cymbal of wings, because those who through pride fly up into the height of thought sound forth that same perverse man by preaching. Which land is rightly declared to be *beyond the rivers of Ethiopia.* For Ethiopia nourishes a black people, and this world, because it brings forth every man a sinner, gives birth as it were to a black people, Ethiopia; but the land to which *Woe* is said is declared to be *beyond the rivers of Ethiopia [sic: Aethiopia]*, because that damned man is wicked with such perversity that he surpasses the sins of all sinners. *Which sendeth ambassadors by sea,* because he scatters his preachers into the ages; of whom it is rightly added in what manner they are sent, when it is said, *in vessels of bulrush upon the waters.* [1064A] For of bulrush is paper made. For what is designated by bulrush but worldly knowledge? The vessels of bulrush, then, are the hearts [sic: *corde*] of worldly doctors. *In vessels* of bulrush, then, to send *ambassadors upon the waters* is to put one's preaching in the senses of the carnally wise and to call the peoples who flow down into guilt.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Under what prince, then, and with what cohort this company of horsemen is set in order, Ezechiel *52* the prophet intimates when he says: *There is Assur and all his multitude, and round about him his graves [n: (Ezech. XXXII)]* For what is expressed by the name of Assur, the proud king, but him of whom it is written: *And he is king over all the children of pride? [n: (Job XLI.)]* Where *however Assur* is, there is *all his multitude,* because surely the whole body of the reprobate cleaves to its head when, binding itself by his perverse laws, [1064B] it shows itself in his warfare. *And round about him his graves:* graves cover the corpses of the dead; what then are his graves but the hearts of the perverse, which receive him, dead from the blessedness of eternal life, to be buried in themselves through carnal desires. *Round about him,* then, *are his graves,* because those in whose minds he now buries himself through desires, he afterwards joins to himself as a tomb through torments.

But what the fruit of such a company of horsemen is, the same prophet announces a little after, saying: *Who go down to hell with their weapons [n: (Ezech. XXXII)]*. The weapons of sinners are the members of the body with which they carry out the evil desires that they conceive in the mind; [1064C] whence it is said through Paul: *Do not yield your members as weapons of iniquity unto sin.* To go down to hell with their weapons, then [sic: *ego*], is to endure the torments of eternal judgment with those very members also with which they fulfilled the desires of pleasure.

By the opposition, then, of this Pharaonic company of horsemen, the Lord's: that company of horsemen is recognized, because if he never was good whom the depravity of the wicked did not exercise, deservedly will they be judged to belong to this company of horsemen of the Lord, or to this city of the Lord, who, cleaving constantly to their leader, fight against that company of horsemen opposed to them, or the contrary city, with patience and the spirit of fortitude, adorning themselves throughout with such comeliness of humility, patience, long-suffering and the other virtues that as the bride of their Lord both universally [1064D] the whole Church and singly any holy soul whatever may fittingly be proclaimed

Of which universal Church, namely, John in the Apocalypse: *I saw,* he says, *the holy city Jerusalem coming down out of heaven from God, prepared as a bride adorned for her husband [n: (Apoc. XXI)]*. The Lord, then, says to this his bride that she deservedly ought not to fear this city of the devil, or company of horsemen, which is opposed and contrary to her, because he has likened her *to* his *company of horsemen in Pharao's chariots:* so that, as that one of old, freed from Egypt after the sacrifice of the lamb, after the crossing of the Red Sea, after the drowning of the enemies, a hymn of praise having been celebrated for the victory, after the law had been given and kept in the desert, entered the land of promise; so she, to be freed out of the Egypt of this world, [1065A] after the grace of baptism or of compunction, after the receiving of the body and blood of the Lord, after the mortification of vices and the observance of the divine law. Triumphed over *53* at last the whole company of horsemen of diabolical oppression, with thanksgiving for the largess of so great a gift bestowed on her, she was to enter from the solitude of this life into the inheritance of the eternal promise.

And not beside the purpose, for stirring up the minds of spiritual warriors, to consider in such a company of horsemen what the proportion or comparison is between the truth and the figure, and then between the fight of each and the cause of the fight, between victor and vanquished. For according to the likeness which we demand between the signifying and the significative there can indeed be some comparison, but according to the dignity of the thing [1065B] none at all. Now this is the proposition among the things we have set down: as truth stands to the figure, so let the victor stand to the vanquished, so also the cause of the fight to the fight, so that it is as it were the proportion of a hundred to one, namely a hundredfold. For this is the promise of the Lord in the Gospel, that *he who shall leave house, or father, or mother, or children, or lands for my name's sake shall receive a hundredfold [n: (Matth. XIX)]*: not that he shall receive the very things which he left multiplied a hundredfold, « but that in comparison and in their own merit they are such as if a hundred were compared with a small number. » [n: (HIERONYM.)] Although a hundred is also wont to be taken for perfection, so that, according to what we have said, truth to figure, the cause of the fight to the fight, victor to vanquished, are of such dignity that between the perfection of the better part and the [1065C] imperfection of the other there is no comparison at all. For the fight is the life of man upon earth; the cause of the fight is the eternal and unchangeable life, for which in the present life one must fight and labour with the whole strength of the soul. The vanquished: the devil and his company of horsemen; the victor: he himself, triumpher over the devil and his company of horsemen. The figure is the thing apart from the sacrament, the truth the thing with the sacrament. For that circumcision of the ancient God, and the crossing through the Red Sea, whether the food of manna or the giving of the law, whether the flight and victory of those fighting [sic: *pugnativum*] carnally, were the figure of those things which are now done in the sacrament of baptism and of the body and blood of the Lord and in the observance of the Gospel precepts, and of the war also of the Lord's warriors fighting [1065D] spiritually against the enemies of the soul.

Let us compare, then, each company of horsemen of the Lord, the signifying and the signified, that which is according to the spirit, and let us say that the proportion between them is that which is between flesh and spirit, between corruption and incorruption, between temporality and eternity; and the truth is so much greater in proportion than the figure as spirit than flesh, incorruption than corruption, eternity than temporality. But on either side, that is, in figure and in truth, the fight and the cause of the fight, the victor and the vanquished, are far unlike. For in that one, that is, in the figure, there was fighting carnally for a carnal, corruptible and temporal thing; in this one, that is, in the truth, there was fighting *54* spiritually for a spiritual, incorruptible and eternal thing. There the carnal victor [1066A] overcame the carnal vanquished, but here the spiritual overcomes the spiritual; there by strength of body, here by strength of mind.

The proportion, then, of truth to figure, in comparison and in its own merit, can be as it were that of a hundred, to one, that is, a hundredfold: whether because of its perfection, which is signified by a hundred, or because a hundred, carried over from the left hand to the right, expresses the crown of blessedness, which is the strongest reward in this company of horsemen of the Lord's warriors.

The fight and the cause of the fight having been set forth in the contrary intention of this two-formed company of horsemen, it must be considered with what force and fortitude each side is armed, so that from an affection of this kind a place of argument may be taken, if the question arises who is to be assigned to the Lord's army, who to the adversary's. [1066B] Now we have said that the fight is the life of each man, as it is written in Job [n: (cap. VII)]: *The warfare of man is his life*; but life itself is constituted according to the affection with which the soul is affected, according to which the whole force and fortitude of those who fight consists.

Let us suppose, then, that that force with which the soul is affected in the company of horsemen of the Lord fighting is the love of God: for every one who labours and fights for justice and truth labours and fights for that thing which he loves; but justice and truth is God; therefore he loves God. Further: he who labours and fights for eternal life to be gained, loves, for a thing which he desires to have; but eternal life is God himself; therefore he loves God: and because he loves him he desires to have him, and therefore labours and fights for him.

[1066C]Wherefore, if we ask what it is to love God, we know it from a definition of this kind: *To love God is to labour and fight with the whole soul for justice and truth and eternal life to be gained.* The definition is equal to the thing which it defines; for it is converted, since to labour and fight with the whole soul for justice and truth and eternal life to be gained is to love God.

Let the place, then, be taken from the definition, so that we may prove him to be in the company of horsemen of the Lord whose soul through grace is affected with such love of God that for justice and truth and eternal life to be gained he does not shrink even from dying. For it is a maximal proposition: to whatever the definition is present, that too is present which is defined. For it is defined, that is, *to love God is for justice [1066D] and truth and eternal life to be gained to labour and fight,* and there is present also that which has been defined, namely to love God. The love of God and the company of horsemen, then, are joined inseparably. of the Lord, since it is agreed that no one serves as a soldier to God but he who is proved *55* to love him, nor is it agreed that any loves God but he who is proved to serve him as a soldier.

But contrary to this company of horsemen of the Lord is the company of horsemen of the devil, in which that force by which the soul of one serving on this side is affected is envy, which also cleaves inseparably to its subject. For on this contrary side there is always envy, and conversely where envy is, there is this contrary side. Let a place of argument, then, be taken here also from the definition, if it is asked who deservedly is to be reckoned on this side. [1067A] For to envy is to be tormented by another's happiness, and conversely to be tormented by another's happiness is to envy, so that the definition is equal to the thing which it defines. Such an argument, then, is proposed, that it be said that every one who envies does not love his neighbour, because he would not wish another's good to be; but not to love one's neighbour, this is not to love God. And since it is agreed that to love God is to be in his company of horsemen, but not to love one's neighbour, as has been said, is not to love God. To envy, then, is to be on the contrary side, that is, in the company of horsemen of the devil; they are therefore contrary to each other, as the companies of horsemen, so both the affection of that company of horsemen and the cause divine [cj: *divina*; read *diversa*, "diverse"]: for the affection on the one side is of love, on the other of envy; the cause for which there is fighting is on the one side eternal life, on the other the transitory.

But neither side can so pass over into the other [1068A] that at one and the same time they could be one and the same together: for it does not follow that he who wastes away with the disease of envy could be in the company of horsemen of the Lord, and that he who is impelled by the love of God should be reckoned in the company of horsemen of the devil; but he who fights for the transitory life wishes also to drag his neighbour with him, for whom he does not desire eternal goods: *For by the envy of the devil death entered into the world [n: (Sap. II)]*; but he who fights for eternal life wishes to lead his neighbour with him, for whom, as one who truly loves, he desires eternal goods as for himself, so that he fulfils that which is said: *He that heareth, let him say: Come [n: (Apoc. XXII)]*. For he who, hearing and understanding something of the everlasting goods, communicates the same to his neighbour also by teaching and exhorting, shows that he truly loves him whom he invites with himself to understand and to merit those very goods.

*[1067] 56* FIGURE. [1067] [ed: Here Migne prints a diagram across both columns, which the digitization omits. Its labels, from the plate: down both outer sides, JOINED INSEPARABLY; an upper arch, CONTRARY, over ETERNAL LIFE, and a lower arch, CONTRARY, under THE LIFE OF MAN; across the top, LOVE · CAUSE · OF THE FIGHT · ENVY; across the bottom, THE COMPANY OF HORSEMEN OF THE LORD · FIGHT · THE COMPANY OF HORSEMEN OF THE DEVIL; on the left, FROM GOD and CONSENTS THROUGH GRACE, with SPIRIT; on the right, FROM THE DEVIL and CONSENTS THROUGH GUILT, with FLESH; in the centre, PROPORTION over HUNDREDFOLD, crossed by two diagonals, the one lettered THEY FIGHT AGAINST EACH OTHER BY THE INCONSEQUENT, the other, in two parts, THEY FIGHT AGAINST EACH OTHER BY and THE INCONSEQUENT.]

[1069A] *57* Thus, then, four contraries are considered, two goods opposed to two evils. That their reason may appear more evidently, let one arrange them in a square figure thus: so that the upper line holds two facing each other, and the lower line likewise has two facing each other; and so in both lines their contrariety appears at first sight, while on both sides are found those which follow one another: on this side the two goods through grace, above that of love, below that of the company of horsemen of the Lord; on that side the two evils, above that of envy, below that of the company of horsemen of the devil. But crosswise they fight against each other on both sides in such a way that they cannot be together.

VERSE 9.-- *Thy cheeks are beautiful as the turtle-dove's, thy neck as necklaces.*



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

By a still ampler showing of his grace the bridegroom [1069B] leads the bride to the knowledge of herself, while he now proclaims her beauty, whose fortitude he had proclaimed before, so as to intimate the conjunction of this twin virtue to be such that the one cannot be without the other, since the one is the fortification of life that is to be guarded, the other the ornament of it when guarded: for fortitude of spirit is needed to guard that very integrity of life, beauty to adorn that very integrity with good works. « For chastity is nothing without good work, nor is good work anything without chastity. » [n: (GREGORIUS.)]

The virtue of fortitude, then, having been set first, the necessary virtue of beauty is added after, so that it may be shown that no one has done anything bravely in all the pursuits of a dutiful soul if it be established besides that he has not increased beautifully through cleanness [1069C] of mind and body. But this beauty is stretched out with a twin grace, while in general it is described by the one name of beauty, but in particular the beauty of the cheeks is one, that of the neck another; the difference of which is gathered from the quality of the things to which they are compared.

For although in this statement there are two subjects, one predicate, the subjects namely being the cheeks and the neck, and the predicate that they are beautiful, yet in this place the nature of the accident of one predicate cannot be present equally, in one and the same manner, in two subjects: one predicate can indeed, through the grace of God, be present in several subjects, namely so that the cheeks are beautiful and the neck is beautiful, but, as we have said, the beauty of the cheeks is described in one way, [1069D] the beauty of the neck in another, though beauty is nevertheless predicated of both; and therefore in this place the two subjects indeed admit each other, but not what otherwise is one predicate.

But if we wish to gather the diversities of this beauty, the things whose likeness is brought in must be looked into, namely the turtle-dove and the necklaces, so that they may be discerned from their quality, whether natural or material: for the beauty in the turtle-dove is natural, in the necklaces material.

*58* Of the turtle-dove the Physiologi hand down three marks of its nature: one, that it is of such chastity that, having lost its mate, it seeks no other thereafter; another, that instead of song it has a moaning; the third, that, seeking secret [1070A] places, it rejoices in a friendly solitude, building its nest there and rearing its young where there is no injury from adversaries, while its refuges can scarcely or never be found. What is expressed by the turtle-dove, which loves chastity, which moans instead of singing, which seeks secret places, but the beauty of chastity, of compunction, of contemplation. For this wonderful beauty of the cheeks is distinguished in a threefold manner of this kind, according to the threefold nature of this animal; and since there is one subject and three predicates, those three both are in the subject and are predicated of the subject, when through the grace of God the cheeks are beautiful both by the virtue of chastity and of compunction and of contemplation. For these several marks of the cheeks have probable signs of their beauty. For [1070B] the cheeks blooming with the rosy blush of modesty, and with bent head fixing their gaze on the earth, denote the comeliness of chastity; but, moist with a wholesome fountain of tears, they speak the grace of compunction; and pale with the zeal and labour of fastings, vigils and holy meditations, they announce the virtue of contemplation too.

But since, as we set down above, the beauty of the cheeks is one, that of the neck another, we have divided the beauty of the cheeks into three species, namely chastity, compunction and contemplation, so that, these being set as it were as subaltern genera, a further subdivision may be made, and it may be said that chastity is one virginal, another widowly, another conjugal; compunction one reconciliatory, another amatory; contemplation one natural, another intelligible, a third intellectible.

[1070C]For virginal chastity is the everlasting integrity of soul and body; but widowly chastity is a devotion of the soul praiseworthy by the comeliness of continence; further, conjugal chastity is a coupling of soul and body commendable by the grace of faith and modesty. But reconciliatory compunction is when someone, visited by heavenly grace, is pricked to sorrow from the remembrance of his sins, and while he is afflicted through the lamentations of penance is reconciled to God whom he had offended: for first the mind is pricked with fear, afterwards with love. Whence the other part may not unfittingly be named amatory compunction, when the mind is kindled with such love towards God that she who before wept lest she be brought back to punishment afterwards begins to weep most bitterly because she is kept back from the kingdom. The grace of which twofold [1070D] compunction is well described by a figurative narration in the sacred and truthful history, where Axa, the daughter of Caleb, sitting upon an ass, is recorded to have asked of her father the lower watered ground and the upper watered ground [n: (Jud. I)]. *59*



. . . . For Axa sits upon the ass when the soul presides over the irrational motions of its flesh, which, sighing, asks of the Father a watered land, because the grace of tears is to be sought from our Creator with great groaning. From him the soul receives the upper watered ground when it afflicts itself in tears with desire for the heavenly kingdom; but it receives the lower watered ground when, weeping, it is in great dread of the punishments of hell.

But although these are two species of compunction, yet compunction itself is distinguished by four qualities, [1071A] because the soul of a just man is vehemently afflicted in compunction: either when it remembers its own evils, considering where it was; or, fearing the sentence of the judgments of God and seeking secure things, it ponders where it will be; or when, attending skilfully to the evils of the present life, it considers mourning where it is; or when it contemplates by the good of the heavenly fatherland, which, because it does not yet attain, it beholds lamenting where it is not.

Now of the third species, that is, of contemplation or speculation, the first part is that which is called natural, which is occupied about bodies and the knowledge and cognition of them, which the Greeks call physiology, and which declares the natures and passions of bodies. The second part is the intelligible, which of all the heavenly works of the supernal divinity, and [1071B] anything prevails by the sublunar globe with a more blessed mind and a purer substance, and lastly the wonderful disposition of human souls, comprehends by thought and intelligence. Further, the third part is called the intellectible, when the mind is led outside visible things, above itself, to another, invisible thing, which, one and the same through itself, ever abiding in its own divinity, is grasped by no senses ever, but by the mind and the intellect alone: for this is composed, by the searching of true philosophy, for the speculation of God and for the incorporeality and the consideration of the soul; which part the Greeks name theology.

Truly, in these divided parts of the parts it is to be considered that, if they are compared with one another, one is greater in proportion than another according to the more excellent dignity of the altered beauty [1071C]. For virginal chastity is greater in proportion than widowly and conjugal chastity, since to them belongs the merit of this profession alone, *to say the new canticle, and the Lamb whithersoever he shall go* to follow [n: (Apoc. XIV)].

Further, that compunction is more excellent which is generated from love than that which is generated from fear, although by the latter one comes to the former, as the son takes precedence of the servant, because to fear is the part of servants, to love, of sons. And that part of contemplation which is called intellectible is incomparable with the two remaining, since the speculation of these is occupied about the creature, but that of the purified mind, lifted beyond the creature by its eye into the Creator himself, although not perfectly, yet as far as shall be possible [1071D] through his grace, is held suspended; whereby the holy *60* man is separated like the fat from the flesh, seeks or desires nothing outward, but, thirsting for God with burning desire, cries out with the Psalmist: *As the hart longeth for the fountains of waters, so my soul longeth for thee, O God; my soul hath thirsted for the living God, when shall I come and appear before the face of God? [n: (Psal. LXI)]*.

This part the Apostle calls *the third heaven*, into which he says he was *caught up and* heard unspeakable things *which it is not lawful for a man to speak [n: (II Cor. XII)]*.

Now this threefold species of beauty your holy college possesses, as we believe, as familiar things, most beloved sisters, you whom a blessed solitude has set apart [sic: *se posuit*] from all the din of worldly disquiets, so that the further removed you are from the world, [1072A] the more freely you may be able to keep the comeliness of chastity, to seek the grace of compunction, and to stand on the summit of holy contemplation. Indeed your very solitude is a delightful garden of the Lord, where the rose and the lily breathe, the testimony of virginity and of martyrdom; of that martyrdom, namely, which can be without the shedding of blood, and in the mind through the exercises of the virtues; where are the watered grounds of holy contemplation; where the little trees of heavenly contemplation spring up, flower and bear fruit; where all these honest things are so composed that in them are the pleasing, chosen walking-places of the Holy Spirit, who says: *I will dwell in them and walk among them; and they shall be to me for a people and I truly to them for God [n: (Lev. XXVI; II Cor. VI)]* [var: Vulg. 2 Cor 6:16 *et ero illorum Deus, et ipsi erunt mihi populus*, "and I will be their God"]. But with very wary circumspection you must take heed lest [1072B] you lose the privilege of so great a good by any worldliness or empty levity, lest that most sweet and most mighty indweller and walker of yours, provoked by you, flee away, saying *that he hath not where to lay his head [n: (Luc. IX)]*.

But since we said above that the beauty of the cheeks is one thing and that of the neck another, and have already described the beauty of the cheeks according to our small measure, it remains now also to speak of the beauty of the neck, which is also divided into two, this into preaching and incorporation; and first we must speak of preaching, afterwards of incorporation. In the neck is the throat, and through the throat the voice is expressed; food too, when it has been ground by the teeth, is passed through the throat into the belly; by the neck, therefore, the proclaimed saints are not [1072C] absurdly understood, in whom is this twin beauty, namely of preaching and of incorporation. The beauty of preaching, then, is considered in the comeliness of the neck, because in the neck is the voice, and through the voice preaching is done; and as the voice is the mark of the mind and the expression of the silent heart, so the preaching of the saints is a thing to be sought by every man, the honest demonstration of the commonwealth. For I here call that the commonwealth which, for its honesty, can and ought to be taken up publicly, in knowledge, will, action, as is every good whose privation is evil.

*61* For to be chaste, humble, patient, modest, or whatever else is marked by the name of virtue, is manifestly good; and of this good [1072D] the negation, that is, not to be chaste, not humble, not patient, not modest, is consequently evil. But since one or the other of these is consequent, that is, to be or not to be, the simple negation of this simple affirmation which proposes to be chaste, or humble, or patient, the negation which says not to be, is followed by the privative affirmation which says to be unchaste, or proud, or impatient. For it is the same to say not to be patient as it is to say impatient, and that evil which is impatience is the privation of that good which is called patience, and in the same way of the rest. There is, then, a certain commonwealth, the array of the virtues; and the ruler and administrator of this commonwealth is that eternal Emperor from whom all good things proceed, and without [1073A] whom nothing can be called good or be good.

This commonwealth, then, the preaching of the saints teaches and shows, and through it the other part, which we called incorporation, is accomplished; because, as through the throat food is passed into the belly, so through the preaching of the saints some sinner, broken from the grossness of his depravity, is incorporated into the Church by conversion.

But since the beauty of the neck is twin, as we have said, namely preaching and incorporation, each is distinguished by subdivision in a threefold manner; because one preaching is done by word, another by example, another by word and example; and of the incorporated, one by conversion, another by satisfaction, another by conversion and satisfaction together.

[1073B]Preaching is done by word alone, because there are some in the Church who have this by the gift of God, that they are able to edify others by the word of exhortation, but, their infirmity standing in the way, they are not so able to give examples of virtues. It is done also by example alone, because there are very many who do not have the gift of discoursing in the Church, and even if they perceive something, cannot bring it forth openly, yet they shine with such proofs of spiritual works that by wonderful examples of virtues they provoke the minds of those who wonder at them to their imitation; and what those speak with words, these speak with deeds, and these philosophize better by keeping silence than those by declaiming. Of which twin quality of preaching the Apostle says: *For to one is given the word of wisdom, to another the word of knowledge [n: (I Cor XII)]*; because there are some, as we have said, who are wise through the understanding of reason in the word [1073C] of the divine commandments to be observed, but through the slowness of their wit are not eloquent in bringing it forth; and others, again, pour out mightily with an abundance of eloquence the very word which they comprehend, to be sown, of a capacious wit.

Likewise preaching is done in both ways, *62* that is, by word and example, when holy doctors have it also by divine grace, that what they preach with the mouth they show in deeds, and what they teach is to be avoided they also indicate in their own deeds is not to be done; in whose person the Psalmist, speaking to God, when he had first said, *and I spoke of thy testimonies before kings, and I was not ashamed,* added in what follows: *And I lifted up my hands to thy commandments, which I loved, and I was exercised in thy [1073D] justifications [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*: as if he said openly: The law of thy commandments, which I pronounced by preaching openly and confidently, I proved also by the execution of works, so that my hearers might both hear in my words and see in my deeds the things which they ought to do for their own profit and salvation.

Truly this third predicament of this beauty is necessary for the doctors of the Church, because the preaching of those is frequently despised whose life is known to be alien from the good of their preaching itself. And they sin greatly before the eyes of almighty God who, the grace of preaching having been granted them, acquire no gains of souls because of their silence. Whence in the Old Testament it is commanded that the priest, entering the tabernacle, should have little bells [1074A] set in the hem of his vestments, lest he die [sic: *nemoriatur*] [n: (Exod. XXVIII)], because it is a great peril for the doctor if he enters the Church of God without the sound of preaching; and the vestments of the priest are the works of justice, as the Psalmist says: *Let thy priests be clothed with justice [n: (Psal. CXXXI)]*, to which vestments the little bells cling, so that the very works of the priest also may cry out the way of life together with the sound of the tongue.

The other part of the beauty in the neck, which is incorporation, is done by conversion, because there are some who, converted from heathendom to God, are incorporated into the Church through faith and the grace of baptism, as we read of Cornelius, who, the merits of good works intervening through the grace of God, and the gift of the Holy Spirit running before, was shown worthy to be joined to the body of the Church [1074B] [n: (Act. X)].

For when, as though an undeserving man, they were being repelled by those who had believed from the circumcision, the Holy Spirit fell upon him as a needful advocate, whose condescension it pleased to show that God willed this man too to be incorporated into his Church through faith and baptism.

Others also are incorporated into the Church through satisfaction, who, while at first indeed they are of the body of the Church through the unity of faith, yet, their faults requiring it, are sometimes cut off from the body of the Church by a peremptory sentence; but, recovering their senses through satisfaction, they are joined again by a reconciling sentence to the body from which they had been cut off. Whence the Apostle too, writing to the Corinthians, commands that he who had perpetrated the crime [1074C] be delivered *to Satan for the destruction of the flesh, that* *63* *he may be saved in the day of the Lord [n: (I Cor. V)]* [var: Vulg. 1 Cor 5:5 *ut spiritus salvus sit*, "that the spirit may be saved"]: and afterwards he orders him, amended through satisfaction, to be incorporated again into the Church, lest he be swallowed up by overmuch sorrow.
