Some, again, are incorporated through conversion and satisfaction together, who both receive the faith by believing and, confessing their crimes, are joined to the body of the Church through penance and baptism. Hence it is that Peter, preaching to the slayers of Christ: *Repent,* he says, *and be converted, that your sins may be blotted out [n: (Act. III)]*, showing that through the sending before of conversion and satisfaction the remission of sins follows, which is done in the Catholic Church alone, because [1074D] that very remission is plainly incorporation into the Church. But although in the former modes, which we set down above separately, saying that incorporation is done now through conversion, now through satisfaction, there can in both be both conversion and satisfaction, yet on account of the greater and more evident showing of the one part or the other we have distinguished them. For although any saint is adorned not with one but with many virtues, yet a virtue is predicated of him the more, the greater in proportion it is recognized to be in him. Whence the Apostle too, when he was describing the divisions of graces, said: *For to one is given the word of wisdom, to another the word of knowledge, to another faith, to another the grace of healings: to another prophecy, to another the discerning of spirits, to another kinds of tongues, to another the interpretation of speeches [n: (II Cor. XII)]*; not that in this he who has had one has any of the other graces [1075A] whatsoever, but because he is said to have that grace the more which is known to be stronger and more abundant in him. And in Cornelius we read of no satisfaction for his past life; but because it was said to him by the angel that his prayers and alms had been heard, and he is therefore bidden to send after Peter, so that, formed unto the faith by his doctrine, he might become a member of the Church through conversion [n: (Act. X)]. But he who has been converted to the faith, but for some fault is separated from the Church by excommunication, has need of satisfaction in order to merit being received back, although satisfaction itself also can be called a conversion from the former error.

Now since we have distinguished beauty in a twofold manner, and have subdivided those very manners into others, it remains [1075B] now to say why this beauty is compared to necklaces.

A necklace is a breast ornament of matrons, named from *muniendo*, because it fortifies their breast, lest any adulterer injure in any way the modesty of their chastity. And the necklace is not any sort whatever, but that which the hand of a careful craftsman laboriously fashions: it is composed of gold and gems in such a way that a somewhat larger gem is set in the middle *64*, and other little gems, standing at equal distances from it, encircle it.

And what is expressed by the gold but wisdom, what by the gems but the virtues? The necklace, then, is composed of gold and gems, because out of the understanding of wisdom and the comeliness of the virtues is made a certain spiritual and necessary ornament of the soul, which so [1075C] fortifies the secret places of our breast that the ancient enemy dares not put forth any hand of his malice against it.

And certain openings are made in the crafted ornament itself, in which the gems are to be enclosed. And what are these openings but our outward senses, which are as it were certain instruments of our acts? For the lowering of the eyes and the honest or disciplined composure of the countenance is a most beautiful gem, shining with the virtue of humility; the ear obedient to the precepts of God and of prelates bears the gem of obedience; further, in the nostrils glows the gem of holy discretion; the mouth shows a gem radiant with a threefold virtue, namely of abstinence, of silence, and of speech honestly and usefully uttered [1075D] and the hands conspicuously display a gem sprinkled with a twofold brilliance, that is, of continence and of munificence.

Among these, set in the middle place, the greater and more splendid gem of holy charity shines chiefly in the breast, which so supplies the comeliness of this ornament [sic: *ornament*] that nothing of beauty is proved to be had without it: for without [sic: *sive*] charity no virtue can either be or be called [a virtue], because *on two precepts*, namely of God and of the neighbour, *dependeth the whole law and the prophets [n: (Matth. XXII)]*.

Beautifully, then, the neck of holy Church, or of the faithful soul, is compared to necklaces consisting of the material of gold and gems, because through the preaching and the unanimity of the saints we ought so to compose our senses out of the [1076A] brightness of wisdom and the holiness of the virtues that we may establish for ourselves a certain ornament of the soul in which that heavenly bridegroom may delight. But because the page of both Testaments is necessary to the preachers of the same Church, from which they may take the order and manner of their preaching, there is added, in the person of the bridegroom himself, or of those through whom, by his inspiration, the scripture of the Church was set forth, of whom some were at the same time at which Solomon spoke these things, some were to be afterwards.

VERSE 10-- *We will make thee golden murenulae, vermiculated with silver.*

The *murenula* is a certain kind of ornament, like a little chain woven of gold and picked out with little rods of silver in the manner of little worms; named *murenula* for [1076B] this reason, that like the moray it is pliant and flexible, or coiled. For the moray *65*, when it is caught, bends and coils itself in different directions. By the golden *murenula* vermiculated with silver, then, we understand Holy Scripture, whose inner sense, on account of its eminence and dignity, is compared to gold, while the utterance by which that sense is expressed is signified by silver on account of its clearness and brilliance. For Holy Scripture is golden, because it shines with the brightness of wisdom; silver, because it gleams with the clearness of utterance. But because this same Scripture is divided into two, that is, into the Old and the New Testament, it is therefore said in the plural: *We will make thee golden murenulae, vermiculated with silver,* so that both Testaments may be shown to be both golden with the brightness of the inner sense and [1076C] silver with the lustre of eloquence.

These two Testaments are called *murenulae* because they can be bent into different modes of exposition, coiled about with many meanings, only so that they sound nothing contrary to the faith. And although, as we have said, they are golden by the valuation of surpassing knowledge, yet, being marked out by the clearness of utterance, they are recorded as vermiculated with silver, because by the brightness of that very knowledge they thunder now humbly, now moderately, now sublimely; and now they unfold history, now allegory, now morality, not simply but manifoldly. Whence it is also said in Ezekiel that as the living creatures went, *the wheels also went together, following them, and with those that blew they blew, and with those lifted up they were lifted up together [n: (Ezech. I)]* [var: Vulg. Ez 1:21 *cum stantibus stabant*, "with those that stood they stood"]: because, doubtless, [1076D] the Holy Scriptures stand or are moved according to the sense of him who searches and understands them; they stand, namely, when they are understood simply according to the letter; but they are moved when they are expounded according to the spiritual sense, whether allegorically or morally, and the more subtly they are considered, the higher they are lifted up together with the sense of him who understands.

Since, therefore, they are bent by the preachers of the Church not uniformly but with a beautiful variety in the modes of diverse senses, now allegorically, as we have said, now morally, and these things are brought forth according to the capacity of the hearers, now humbly, now sublimely, rightly are golden *murenulae* vermiculated with silver promised to the Church by the voice of the bridegroom, which hang as it were from her neck when they adorn the knowledge and the utterance of the holy [1077A] preachers.

Now it is not without some reason to recapitulate briefly the things above, and to consider how these lower things are continued on to those; above, when the bride had said: *I am black, but beautiful, O daughters of Jerusalem,* she did not recognize that same beauty of the perfect: whence also she complains that so great a heat of temptations affected her that even her brothers fought against him. But the bridegroom reproves this faintheartedness of hers, teaching that she had not rightly attended to the grace of beauty *66* conferred on her, she who had indeed said that she was beautiful, but had not perfectly recognized the quantity and quality of that beauty; wherefore it could happen that when she began to disagree she would go out from the fellowship of her bridegroom and follow the various errors of the world. Whence the bridegroom, [1077B] with his accustomed kindness bringing her back to herself, strengthens her by enumerating the parts of the beauty granted her, so that she, recognizing herself, may perfectly acknowledge how and how greatly she is beautiful, and from this may cleave more ardently to the bridegroom [sic: *sponsio*].

Now that beauty, that we may so define it, is the habit of a well-constituted mind, whose parts are fortitude, beauty, the knowledge of the holy Scriptures, which the bridegroom himself has already enumerated: fortitude, indeed, in the place where he said: *To my company of horsemen in the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee, my love:* beauty, not uniform but as we distinguished above, when he says: *Thy cheeks are beautiful as the turtle-dove's, thy neck as necklaces:* and the knowledge of the Scriptures where he says: *We will make [1077C] thee golden murenulae, vermiculated with silver.*

Now in a maximal and principal proposition, what is in the parts must necessarily be in the whole: taking, then, an argument from the parts, he proves that she is truly beautiful, because if the species of beauty, that is, fortitude, beauty, the knowledge of the holy Scriptures, are the habit of a well-constituted mind, the genus, which is beauty, must necessarily be the same; and accordingly, since she has said that she is beautiful, she ought not to fear adversities, she whom the grace of the bridegroom has made such. But if she does not recognize that she is such through grace, she will not belong to the fellowship of the bridegroom, since the cause of ignorance brings this about, that, going out of doors through sloth and negligence, she is compelled to stray from the companionship of the bridegroom. And it is a topic [1077D] from the efficient cause it may be proved: that every one who has not understood the good of the grace conferred on him is, through ignorance, negligent of that very good in himself, and the negligent is not near to God: the bride therefore, not knowing herself, withdraws from the fellowship of the bridegroom and of necessity seeks outward things. Brought back, then, to herself, she shows by what she adds how she has recognized herself and profitably attended to the grace granted her, saying: *While the king was at his reclining, my nard gave forth its odour.*

VERSE 11.-- *While the king was at his reclining, my nard gave forth its odour.*

The reclining of the king is the dispensation of all the temporal actions of Christ, because he deigned to humble himself from that ineffable sublimity of his divinity, and for human salvation to incline himself to the *67* infirmity of [1078A] humanity: first indeed by being born into the womb of the Virgin, then by dying upon the cross, by lying in the sepulchre and by descending to the lower regions; afterwards, raising himself from that very inclining of his, by rising again and by ascending to the heavens.

In the time of this dispensation the Church, growing up chiefly unto the increase of the virtues, was also taught by the precept and example of Christ to share in his sufferings through patience, when she had advanced into so great a charity towards him that she did not prefer even life itself to his love, saying: *While the king was at his reclining, my nard gave forth its odour.*

Nard is an aromatic shrub, with a heavy, thick, short, black and brittle root, and a small and dense leaf, whose tops spread out into ears; and therefore the makers of perfumes [1078B] celebrate the spikes and the leaves of the nard for their twofold endowment. Whence it is related in the Gospel that a woman brought, for the anointing of the Lord, *an ointment of precious spikenard [n: (Joan. XII)]*.

And what is signified by the nard but the virtue of good work? And the root of good work is charity; because, as from the root is born all the virtue and vigour of plants, so from charity alone is generated the virtue of every good work.



Now it has been said that in the root of the nard there are diverse qualities of nature, which we can refer to the manifold virtues of charity. *For charity is grace* [cj: *gratia*; read *gravis*, "heavy"], because it outweighs all virtues; which the Apostle calls *the more excellent way*, saying: *Yet I show unto you a more excellent way [n: (I Cor. XII)]*. [1078C] And it is thick, as being of a livelier sap according to the inner vigour of the mind, which gives to the other virtues the effect of holiness, since without it every virtue remains as it were dry and arid: whose fatness the Psalmist considering, who declared that he watched unto God with thirsting desire: *As with marrow,* he says, *and fatness let my soul be filled [n: (Psal. LXII)]*. It is short, because it is written of it that *an abbreviated and consummated word shall the Lord make upon the earth [n: (Rom. IX)]* [var: Vulg. Rom 9:28 *verbum enim consummans, et abbrevians … verbum breviatum faciet Dominus super terram*, "a word finishing and cutting short … a shortened word shall the Lord make upon the earth"]; the consummated and abbreviated word is charity, because in it consists the perfection of all the virtues, and this one saying is the conclusion of this holy universe: *This word of charity is consummated, because love is the fulness of the law [n: (Rom. XIIII)]*, of which [1078D] the Psalmist says: *I have seen an end of all consummation [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*, not an end which makes that which is, not to be, but which makes that which is not, to be, and brings it to perfection: it is abbreviated, because the precepts of the whole law are renewed in this one: *Thou shalt love the Lord thy God with thy whole heart, and thy neighbour as thyself; on these two precepts dependeth the whole law and the prophets [n: (Matth. XXI)]*. How short a word, consisting of three syllables and seven letters, but containing the whole fulness of holiness: for *God* is *charity [n: (I Joan. IV)]* *68*, who is triune in the distinction of persons, one in the equality of majesty, and sevenfold is the grace of the Holy Spirit, through whom charity itself is poured forth into the hearts of the saints. If, then, *God is charity,* and nothing of good cannot [cj: *esse non potest*; read *esse potest*, "nothing good can be without God"] be without [1079A] God, he who is convicted of not having charity, which is God, is without doubt concluded not to be good.

The root of the nard is also said to be black, because charity, which is, as we have said, the root of all virtues, willingly bears for God and for the salvation of the brethren the blackness of all persecutions and adversities, because, as it is written: *Charity is patient [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*. And it is brittle on account of the exceeding tenderness of brotherly compassion, because it is compelled through the bowels of mercy to be troubled with the troubled, to be weak with the weak, to weep with those who weep, to grieve with those who grieve, to be sad with the sad; for it is written: *Charity is patient, is kind [n: (ibid.)]*: for charity abounds in such sweetness of kindness that it thinks every need of its neighbour its own, it is of a small [1079B] and dense leaf because charity always shows humility, while it abounds in a copious density of all the virtues. It spreads out into ears, because the holy mind, growing up with the increase of divine love, is lifted up into heavenly things by the keenness and subtlety of understanding.

These seven natural things, then, in the root of the nard can, not absurdly, be taken as these praises which we have set down of the charity proceeding from the sevenfold grace; because, as from the little root of the nard, with the adding of leaves and spikes, is made the confection of a most precious ointment, so from charity, which is the root of all good things, and from the diverse and most choice species of the virtues, the Holy Spirit being the craftsman, is made the confection of holiness; with which holy Church, or any holy soul whatever, as it were anointed, sends forth from herself a most sweet [1079C] odour by the repute and fame of good action; of which odour the Apostle says *that we are the good odour of Christ unto God in every place [n: (II Cor. II)]*, and a certain wise man: A good fame, he says, attacks the bones [var: Vulg. Prov 15:30 *fama bona impinguat ossa*, "a good name maketh the bones fat"]: because every virtue of the soul is enervated unless it is filled with the fatness of a compound of this kind.

Such an odour, then, the Church, or the holy soul, says that she gives from this pistic nard, that is, faithful, wherein that King and bridegroom [d: and bridegroom] heavenly may be delighted *at his reclining,* that is, in that unutterable secret of the Father's bosom. For this is his reclining: to be in the bosom of the Father through the same essence and through the same power of divinity; his reclining is also, as we set down above, to incline himself to the nature of human infirmity through the wonderful order of the consummated [1079D] dispensation. His reclining is also to dwell in the thought of the holy mind, through the most bountiful condescension of his manifold grace.

The odour of this precious pistic nard *69* that woman of the Gospel who is written to have anointed the Lord showed forth in deed and in sacrament: in deed according to the history, in sacrament according to tropology; because to anoint the feet of the Lord was to consider the loftiness of his divinity by the uplifting of the mind, albeit through a glass and in a dark manner. To anoint the head and the feet of the Lord, therefore, is to believe and to preach his divinity and his humanity; whence also of the power of that same anointing with which Mary anointed the Lord it is written that *the house was filled with the odour of the ointment [n: (Joan. XII)]*. For the house [1080A] is the Church, which is filled with the odour of this anointing, because she believes and confesses Christ God and man, and by the rightness of this faith and confession is spread through the whole breadth of the world. Through this house, that is, the whole body of the Church, John the Evangelist sprinkles the sweetness of this odour from the head and from the feet of the Lord: from the head indeed when he says: *In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God:* and from the feet when he says: *The Word was made flesh and dwelt in us [n: (Joan. I)]*.

The anointing of the head and feet of the Lord can also be understood in this way: that he who follows the footsteps of Christ through the pursuit of the active life should lie prostrate at his feet by living well, and so, advancing from day to day to better things, [1080B] through the virtue of the contemplative life should raise himself up, as it were, to the head of Christ, by cleaving to God continually. The anointing of the feet, then, is the operative virtue; further, the anointing of the head is the contemplative virtue. For the offering of good action and the exercise of holy meditation is of a most sweet odour, in which that heavenly King and Bridegroom delights at his reclining, waiting for and desiring those fragrant with such an odour, to place them with himself in the eternal reclining-place of that reclining of his own.

But that holy Church, or the faithful soul, may be able to come to that eternal reclining of his, in this reclining of his temporal dispensation, in which he willed to recline for her salvation by being born and by dying, by following the examples of his humility, patience and obedience, let her prepare for him in her mind a reclining redolent [1080C] with the odour of the virtues, so that afterwards he may lead her, as bridegroom the bride, the immaculate the immaculate, into the bridal chamber, or reclining, of his kingdom; so that, as he now rests in her temporally through the diversity of graces, so she hereafter may rest in him eternally through everlasting glory. Whence by what follows the same bride shows that this is the sentence fixed beforehand by the merciful Bridegroom, that he who shares in his sufferings through patience shall also partake of his eternal kingdom through consolation.

VERSE 12.-- *A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me, he shall abide between my breasts.*

*70* By the myrrh, namely, is expressed the bitterness of the passion, by the cluster the sweetness of the resurrection. While, [1080D] she says, my bridegroom and my king, under whose rule it is mine both to will and to be able the good, had reclined in the passion of the cross and, made obedient unto death, was already laid in the sepulchre, I, who before complained that I was black because not only strangers but my brothers also fought against me, as if compounded as a certain most precious nard out of his most ardent charity, and also out of the example of his humility, patience and obedience, have sprinkled the odour of good repute through the whole world, ready to succumb for his love not by one death only, but by a thousand kinds of deaths; so that *to me my beloved* is not myrrh alone, but a bundle of myrrh, since for the one kind of death which he underwent for me, I desire to gather a bundle of many and diverse deaths.

[1081A]What is this bundle? Perils of rivers, perils of robbers, perils from the Gentiles, perils in the city, perils in the wilderness, perils in the sea, perils among false brethren; labour and hardships, many watchings, hunger and thirst, many fastings, cold and nakedness: mockings and stripes, chains and prisons, stoning, being sawn asunder, lastly slaughter by the sword [n: (II Cor. XI; Hebr. XI)]. Behold *the bundle of myrrh.* But since martyrdom is twofold, one in the body, the other in the mind, let us divide this bundle and suppose one to be the bundle of myrrh, that is, of bodily suffering, and another the bundle of mental myrrh; and the bundle of bodily suffering we have enumerated above; further, the bundle of mental myrrh, that is, of suffering, is virginity of the flesh, renunciation of the world and of all [1081B] worldly pleasure, or the labour of manifold continence. The cause of this twin bundle *is my beloved to me,* says the Church, or the holy soul, because for it, for his name and love, I have put the shoulders of mind and body under the bearing of this bundle: of the mind indeed by the mortification of vices, and of the body by the endurance of diverse sufferings. Why this? Because the beloved himself also, through himself too, was made *to me a bundle of myrrh*, when, taking upon himself for me in his own body scourges, spittings, blows, mockings, the draught of gall, the cross, the piercings of the hands, feet and side, and lastly death itself, he gathered as it were a bundle of manifold sufferings; and *a bundle, my beloved to me,* being made such, *shall abide between my breasts,* because all these signs of his passion I daily [1081C] recall in mind, and while I have the memory of them, I too gather more ardently for love of him the bundle of my own sufferings.

In the situation of the body the breast is placed between the breasts set on either side, and therefore by the commemoration of the bundle of myrrh between the breasts we rightly *71* understand that memory of the Lord's passion which ought to be in the breast.

Or let us suppose the breasts to be the two Testaments, namely the Old and the New, and consider that in these too the beloved abides by the commemoration of his passion, the former Testament foretelling that very passion as to come, the following one presenting it as already done. *Between my breasts,* she says, *he shall abide,* because in the two [1081D] Testaments, set before me for the knowledge of him and for my salvation, I gather the bundle of his myrrh: foreshown in the Old Testament through figures, and in the New shown forth to the whole world through the saving sap of eternal redemption. For the sap of myrrh is said to heal many infirmities, and the sap of this myrrh of the Lord has driven out every sickness of the soul, and has established the medicine of perpetual health in the whole world.

Or *he shall abide between my breasts,* that is, between the two peoples established in my body out of the Jews and the Gentiles, who receive this bundle of the Lord's myrrh by faith, follow it by their manners, and make it in the sacrament by the daily commemoration of him, recall it in mind, and with the mouth take their own perpetual.

[1082A]Or *between my breasts*, that is, between the two thieves, between whom *my beloved*, hanging, composes the bundle of his passion; who prefigured the same two peoples, one believing, the other crucifying; one chosen, the other reprobate. And they were my breasts, the former on account of the natural and the written law, the latter on account of the natural alone, both thus having the nourishment of reason, so that they are inexcusable. And the latter indeed, through my beloved abiding in the midst and holding out the bundle of his myrrh, took on over and above a new law enlightening the natural; but the former, by the hidden but just judgment of God, after the law was given, lost even the natural, on account of unbelief.



Whence also to the beloved himself, hanging on the cross, it offered myrrhed [1082B] wine, the bitterness of which was poured over upon itself, when it said: *His blood be upon us and upon our sons [n: (Matth. XXVII)]*.

VERSE 13.-- *A cluster of Cyprus is my beloved to me, in the vineyards of Engaddi.*

And because it willed [cj: *voluit*; read *noluit*, "was unwilling"] to take up the healing of this, it went without the sweetness of the cluster, and therefore it neither died together with the beloved to sin, nor rose together with him to justice. But to me, who in the other people, coming to the faith, receive, venerate and adore the sacraments of the passion, he has been made *a cluster of Cyprus*, because when he himself rose from death, he quickened me too with the hope and sweetness of the resurrection to come.

For he is the cluster carried from the land abiding with milk and honey, by the two spies signifying those very [1082C] peoples; who, begotten of Mary his mother, flowing with the sweetness and fatness of the Holy Spirit, *72* was hung upon the cross for the salvation of both peoples. But as it is the custom of those who carry that one goes before and the other follows, so the former people was that of the Jews, known to God through the observance of the law, and the following people that of the Christians, taken up through the religion of the new law. And as he who goes before does not see that which he carries, and puts it behind him by a certain turning away of the back, while he who follows always has it before his face, always guards it with his gaze, and enjoys a certain nearness of body: so the Jewish people, carrying Christ in the law, does not understand him, but as it were turning away despises him. But the Christian people understands him, carries him on the forehead [1082D] and in the mind, and guards him by worshipping and by obeying his commandments.

That people abhors and despises this cluster hanging on the cross on account of the curse of the law; this people receives the saving testament of his blood, pressed out in the winepress of the cross, drinking with spiritual sweetness the unique remedy of body and soul; and in hope, the desirable and incomparable joy to come after the general resurrection of the same twin substance, namely of body and soul.

In this people, then, for I am this people, the bride of God, the Church of God, *a cluster of Cyprus is my beloved to me,* because he who was *a bundle of myrrh* in the bitterness of the passion will be a cluster in the sweetness of the resurrection; and he for whose sake I have gathered a bundle of many sufferings, [1083A] both in body and in mind, will be to me a cluster in the eternal refreshment of the soul and in the most sweet recompense of this labour.

Whence he is rightly called *a cluster of Cyprus*. For Cyprus is interpreted *sadness* or *mourning*; he is called, then, *the cluster of Cyprus*, because he himself is the true and sweet consolation of all my mourning and sadness; and as the cluster of Cyprus surpasses the clusters of all lands in size, so my beloved, *the Father of mercies, and the God of all consolation [n: (II Cor. I)]*, surpasses in his dignity and grace all things that are in heaven and earth.

But where has the beloved been made a cluster of Cyprus? *In the vineyards of Engaddi.* Engaldi is said to be a very large village in Judaea, beside the Dead Sea, where there are vineyards, not indeed of wine but of balsam, little trees which are therefore [1083B] called vineyards, because they are propped up in the manner of vineyards. And from balsam [sic: *basalmo*] mixed with the liquor of the olive the chrism is made, by which certain greater institutions of ecclesiastical consecrations are performed; for in it the laver of baptism is consecrated, and the priestly, its own imperial, dignity is confirmed. A certain special holiness is assigned to churches, or altars, and to sacramental vessels. And typically, in the anointing with a liquor of this kind are signified the gifts of spiritual charisms, by which the hearts of the faithful are made fit by the grace of the Holy Spirit for the divine ministries.

And Engaddi is interpreted *the fountain of the kid*. *73* And who is the fountain of the kid but baptism, or the grace of tears? [1083C] Now the kids are the sinners who are to be placed on the left, and all the just are to be set on the right. The vineyards of Engaddi, then, that is, of the fountain of the kid, are the spiritual gifts which are granted only to those who have been washed from the filth of sins by holy baptism, or through the tears of penance: for the fountain of the kid is baptism, by which sinners, that is, kids, are washed and become just, that is, sheep of Christ; or, if after baptism they have fallen into sin, they are restored to their former state through the grace of tears, as through a second baptism.

*A cluster,* she says, *of Cyprus is my beloved to me in the vineyards of Engaddi,* because he is indeed to me the food and drink of consolation and salvation in those who through the grace of baptism have been made worthy to receive the knowledge of the divine sacraments [1083D] and the abundance of spiritual gifts; or who through the fountain of tears merit the absolution of sins, and, dead to sins, rise again to the life of eternal salvation, so that if, according to the Apostle, they are *made planted together* *in the likeness of the death of Christ,* they may become *likewise also of his resurrection [n: (Rom. VI)]*.

The order of these four predicaments is certainly to be noted, namely *the odour of the nard, the bundle of myrrh, the cluster of Cyprus, the vineyard of Engaddi.* For in the same order in which they follow one another here in the series of the words, they are related as fulfilled in the Lord Jesus Christ: when before the passion he was anointed by Mary with precious spikenard, *and the house was filled with the odour of the ointment [n: (Joan. XII)]*: and in the passion he was given myrrhed wine to drink [n: (Marc. XV)], and after the passion, anointed with myrrh and other kinds of spices, [1084A] he was laid in the sepulchre [n: (Joan. XIX)]; whence, rising again after three days [n: (Marc. XVI)], he offered to believers the sweetness of the spiritual cluster in the principal joy of the resurrection: into whom, a little afterwards, he poured the grace of the Holy Spirit, which has all virtue, like balsam *in the vineyards of Engaddi*, giving to it power, through water and the spirit [n: (Joan. XX)] or the imposition of the hand, to give remission of sins and to make of kids the sheep of Christ.

But the things which in this manner, according to the history, went before in themselves in the head can also be shown in figure in his members, according to morality, in like order: so that, the odour of the virtues going before, the mortification of vices should consequently follow; because the affliction of the body and the contempt of worldly glory are the instruments [1084B] of this mortification. Are not hunger, thirst, nakedness, watching and continual prayers the instruments or the matter of this mortification? [n: (Rom. VIII.)] Now it is a principal and maximal proposition that where the instrument is lacking the cause of the instrument also is lacking; and where the matter is not, that also which is made from the matter is not; and that therefore it is consequent that where virtues are lacking, vices rule; because upon the steadfastness of the virtues going before there follows the fall of the vices. *74* Whence the Apostle, describing two things adverse to one another, namely the flesh and the spirit, first says: *Walk in the spirit,* and then, *and you shall not fulfil the desires of the flesh [n: (Gal. V)]*, to show that the flesh is subjected by the strength of the spirit, and that unless virtue goes before, vice cannot give way.

[1084C]In this order, then, in imitation of the head, that is, of Christ, his members, that is, the saints, offer first nard, then myrrh, because they ought first to have the works of holiness, by whose virtue the works of the flesh may be mortified. But because the same Apostle says: *If by the spirit you mortify the deeds of the flesh, you shall live [n: (Rom. VIII)]*, in a fitting order, doubtless, spiritual resurrection follows spiritual mortification, so that those who have died together with Christ by dying to sins may rise again together with him by living. to justice, saying with the same Apostle: If we die together, we shall also live together [n: (II Tim. II)]. For as there is a twin death, namely of the soul and of the body, so there is also a twin resurrection of each, so that those who now through justification rise from the death of the soul, in the resurrection to come [1084D] may rise through incorruption from the death of body and soul. Whence John says in the Apocalypse: *Blessed is the saint that hath part in the first resurrection, in these the second death hath not power [n: (Apoc. XX)]* [var: Vulg. Apoc 20:6 *Beatus, et sanctus, qui habet partem*, "blessed and holy is he that hath part"]. This resurrection indeed is signified by the name of the cluster, so that, because for a time they have suffered a certain bitterness by spiritually mortifying the flesh, afterwards they may perceive eternally the ineffable sweetness of joy, here indeed by resting spiritually from the corruption of the mind, but there both from the corruption of the mind and from that of the body.

Truly this order also pleased the disposing grace as fitting and seemly: that, with the nard of holy repute being fragrant, and by the myrrh of inmost mortification, those already existing there as a living victim, and through the cluster [sic: *hotrum*] spiritually [1085A] inebriating, gladdened by the newness of the soul rising from the death of sin, made superior to the flesh through the spirit, it should show worthy of the coming of the Holy Spirit, through the balsams of diverse graces which Engaddi spiritually brought forth, that is, the fountain of the kid, namely the baptism of the twofold laver, both of washing and of compunction, by which the kids, that is, sinners, are washed and are made sheep of Christ.

Therefore, after the bridegroom rebuked the bride for her ignorance of herself, on account of which she would have received a deserved repulse from the fellowship of the bridegroom, the bride, by his wholesome chiding, at length recognizing herself, she who before had distrusted that she could persevere by herself, now with full faith learned not to fail in tribulations, even unto death, for his love; and the bridegroom, congratulating her on such [1085B] progress of hers and now judging her fair, flatters her with a proclamation of praises of this kind, so that by this she may be provoked to greater works of virtues.

* 75* VERSE 14.-- *Behold, thou art fair, my love, behold, thou art fair, thine eyes are doves'.*

Because, he says, considering thyself at first, thou didst call thyself black and dusky on account of the various heats of temptations, and, being still of little faith, didst cry out for my help, which nevertheless he had not said to thee; but afterwards, recognizing thyself as I showed thee, thou didst advance in such strength of spirit that thou didst not hesitate to offer me not only the myrrh of the passion but a bundle of myrrh: *behold*, I declare to thee that so *fair art thou*, and because fair, therefore *my love*; and conversely, because *my love*, therefore *fair*; and not [1085C] once *fair*, but again *fair*: for repetition is confirmation, because thou art so perfectly fair that thou pleasest me with a doubled beauty, namely of faith and of working, or of soul and of body.

Now in a beautiful order, between two predicates of beauty set on either side, one subject is constructed in the middle place, because the middle itself takes its being from the two; so that if one and of the two, doubly, of the beauty disposed on both sides, is lacking, namely, so that there be faith without works, or works without faith; or chastity of body without chastity of mind, or of mind without chastity of body, no soul is joined to God by the bond of spiritual friendship.

For since it is written: Faith, if it have not works, [1085D] dead in itself [n: (Jacob. II)], and since again that soul is called foolish by the Lord which carries lamps without oil, being about to go forth to meet the bridegroom [n: (Matth. XXV)], it is manifest that these parts of beauty, doubled on both sides, are constitutive of this most holy friendship. For it is not of small estimation before the judgment of almighty God to be perfected by so great a grace of God that he deigns him worthy of his friendship; and to be named, for the special gift of the virtues, the friend of God is certainly a great proclamation. Whence the Lord to the apostles: *Now I will not call you servants,* he says, *but friends, because all things whatsoever I have heard of my Father, I have made known to you [n: (Joan. XV)]*. For the sign of this friendship is the revelation of the Father's secret, and that which is specially conferred on those who love him and keep faith with this friendship, [1086A] the excellence of the contemplative dignity.



But since above, companions are spoken of by the bride in the place where she says: *Lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions,* while now by his own voice the bride is called his love, it must be considered what difference there is between a friend and a companion. A companion is one who is associated with us for doing something together with us; but a friend is one who, like in the practice of life, is joined to us by a devout and faithful love: the companions of the bridegroom, then, are those who seem as it were to be associated with him through some good works, but he proves of things with what intention they did it, when, being desirous of human praise, they show *76* themselves to be very alien from the love of him whom they seemed to imitate not simply and devoutly but for their own temporal advantage. But his friends are those who for the sole hope of eternal goods [1086B] are faithfully joined to him, and, while they are separated from him by no prosperity and no adversity, show how greatly they burn with the love of him before whom they care to set nothing at all. On account of such a quality of life the Church, or any holy soul which follows the footsteps of Christ, when this beauty is proclaimed of her, deserves to be called by the name of his love; but because that beauty shines forth from the speculation of the mind as from a kind of face, he adds the cause why he called her fair, saying: *thine eyes are doves'.*

The eyes of the Church are the holy doctors, because, as the eyes are leaders of the way for us, so the holy doctors, showing us the way of life, go before us by word and example as with a double eye.

Morally, too, any soul whatever, that is, in the head of the inner [1086C] man, has two eyes, understanding and discretion. For it understands and sees what it must do, then discerns how that action ought to be ordered, so that with the one eye it may look at the quality of its act, with the other at the manner. For he sees well whither he ought to tend who both considers the quality of the life to be held, and, by now tightening and now relaxing that quality according to place and time, forms the strong and the weak; yet so that that very tightening or relaxing is set within a fixed and fitting limit. The cause, then, why he says *fair* is this, namely that her *eyes* are *doves'*; which comparison is rightly made on account of the sevenfold virtue of the dove's nature and simplicity.

For the dove lacks gall, harms no one, picks the better [1086D] grains, nourishes the young of others, nests in the clefts of the rock, has a moan for a song, is accustomed to sit above the waters so that it may foresee the coming of the hawk. On account of these seven marks of its nature the bride is compared to the dove; so that if she refers these things with a dove's eye to the moral form, she may worthily be called fair, while she strives to have by grace what the dove has by nature.

It lacks gall; which the bride of Christ also ought spiritually to lack, so that, according to the Apostle: *Let all bitterness, and anger, and clamour, and blasphemy be put away from* her *with all malice [n: (Ephes. IV)]*. It harms no one; the bride of Christ too ought to hurt nobody, by word nor by deed; but, according to the Apostle, [1087A] to have *peace with all men* [n: (Rom. XII)]. It picks the better grains; so too one who loves Christ ought to gather from Holy Scripture the better sentences, by which he may know how both to order his own life honestly and usefully and to form the life of his neighbours after his own example by teaching and acting. It nourishes the young of others; the Church too gathers into her bosom, as if her own sons, those who are not of her *77* body, such as heretics and perverse Christians, and is busy to join them to her body by admonishing, by reproving, by drawing them with benefits; and this any soul which is truly the bride of Christ ought to do, that she love and cherish strangers and those contrary to her, as her own household, for Christ's sake, so that she may always conquer evil into good [n: (ibid.)]. It nests in the clefts of the rock, that it may escape the bites of serpents; so [1087B] too the Church, or the soul joined to God, ought to seek refuge in the passion of Christ, *for Christ is the rock [n: (I Cor. X)]*, whose passion's clefts are the wounds, the strongest defences against the snares of the malignant spirits. It has a moan for a song; and in this temporal life, to one who loves God, moaning ought to be for a song, that is, in place of worldly place and gladness, *that sadness which is according to God, which worketh penance steadfast unto salvation [n: (II Cor. VII)]*: for it is written that *blessed are they that mourn, for they shall be comforted [n: (Matth. V)]*, and on the contrary: *Woe to you that laugh now, for you shall weep [n: (Luc. VI)]*. It is accustomed to sit above the waters so that it may foresee the coming of the hawk; so too the faithful soul ought to dwell in mind above the streams of the Holy Scriptures, [1087C] that from them it may understand and learn how it may be able to beware of the snares of the ancient enemy.

The bride, then, is proclaimed fair on account of these seven parts of dovelike simplicity; which, because she exercises them with the simple eye of the mind as with a dove's eye, she so pleases the eyes of the bridegroom that he declares her fair not once but twice, as though confirming it, and intimates in these parts the twin love, namely of God and of the neighbour. For not to be angry, to hope in the sacraments of the passion of Christ, to groan [sic: *genere*] for one's sins or for the delay of the heavenly kingdom, to bend the mind to sacred Scripture, is the sign of divine love: further, to harm no one, to receive and cherish strangers as if one's own, is the proof of brotherly charity. But because [1087D] *the Holy Spirit, through whom the charity of God* is poured forth *in our hearts*, is the same charity [n: (Rom. V)], consisting in the twin part of love, for John says: *God is charity [n: (I Joan. IV)]*; let us consider in these seven parts of innocence the sevenfold grace of the Holy Spirit himself, namely the spirit of wisdom and of understanding, the spirit of counsel and of fortitude, the spirit of knowledge and of piety, the spirit of the fear of the Lord [n: (Isa. XI)]. For it is of wisdom to repress the motions of the mind while it wisely considers that *the anger of man worketh not the justice of God [n: (Jac. I)]*; hence Solomon: *The fool,* he says, *immediately sheweth his anger, but he that dissembleth an injury is shrewd [n: (Prov. XII)]*; and again: *A stone is heavy, and sand weighty, but the anger of a man is heavier than both; Anger hath no mercy, nor fury when it breaketh forth* [var: Vulg. Prov 27:3 *ira stulti*, "the anger of a fool"] [1088A] [n: (Prov. XXVII)]. Whence a certain one of the Gentiles says: *He who will not moderate his anger*
*Will wish undone what his resentment and his mind have urged,*
*While he hastens by force the penalties of his unavenged hatred.*
[n: (HORATIUS, Epist. I, II, 59.)] *78* And to show that it is of wisdom to know how to rule the mind, he adds: *Anger is a brief madness, rule the mind: which, unless it obeys,*
*Commands: check it with reins, check thou it with a chain.*
[n: (HORATIUS, Epist. I, II, 62.)]

By the spirit of wisdom, then, the disordered motion of the mind is to be repressed, because through this evils are wisely avoided, which for the most part are born from it so grievous that they are scarcely or never laid to rest.

*The spirit of understanding also is [n: (Isa. IX)]*, to harm neither oneself [1088B] nor others, and through the discretion of reason to understand that it is just *that what one would not have done to oneself one should not do to another [n: (Matth. VII)]* But because every injury of hurting is done in two ways by him who inflicts it, namely by word and by action, and is received in two ways by him who suffers it, that is, in mind and in body, a twofold remedy of understanding is also to be applied, that he may weigh that both the integrity of brotherly charity is corrupted and the severity of divine goodness is provoked, which is to harm both oneself and others. For that is true innocence by which one harms neither oneself nor others, because *he that loveth iniquity hateth his own soul [n: (Psal. X)]*, and no one sins against another without first sinning in himself. Great indeed is the gift of this understanding, by which one establishes for oneself such a manner of living that one pleases both God and men, and that this is [1088C] the sign of true innocence, to love God and the neighbour, which is the perfecting of all holiness.

Further, by the spirit of counsel, anyone having zeal for a holy intention gathers the better and more useful sentences of the holy Scriptures, from whose sense, as from a certain help, taking counsel for himself, he may find a certain state of conversation, or one befitting quality; and this, collected out of diverse sentences, may become the counsel of a life to be well ordered.

But the spirit of fortitude greatly strengthens the mind that trusts in the power of the passion of Christ, when by the seal of that passion it is both fortified outwardly in the body and made firm inwardly in the mind against every onset of diabolical assault. For by this spirit of fortitude Moses warred down the Egyptian enemies *(Exod. [1088D] VII-XIV)*, when he laid them low by manifold plague through the rod and delivered the people of God from their bondage, intimating that he himself, *the Lord strong and mighty in battle [n: (Psal. XXV)]*, was to succour the whole world by the wood of his cross, the universal enemy, that is, the devil, being laid low by the power of his passion. By this spirit of fortitude also Gideon conquered the enemy with three hundred [n: (Jos. VIII)] bearing before them the form of the cross in the character of this number, which is marked by the letter TAU: and in Ezekiel [n: (cap. IX)] no one is struck who is found sealed with this letter, as with the form of the cross.

But the spirit of knowledge is, to commit to tenacious memory the series of the holy Scriptures, the things by which he may pour forth the streams of sacred doctrine, which [1089A] he may know how to divide with such discretion that both the simple may have that by which they are instructed, and the learned that by which they are exercised.

*79* But to offer equal charity to all, so as to love enemies equally with friends for God's sake, and, according to the Apostle: To become all things to all, that we may gain all [n: (I Cor. IX)], is assuredly the spirit of piety; by which spirit certainly he is led, whoever strives not slothfully to succour his neighbour in every need, whether of body or of soul, according to his power and beyond his power.

Further, to groan in the twofold manner of compunction, that is, either by the remembrance of sins or by the inmost desire of the soul thirsting for God, is from the spirit of the fear of the Lord, by which one fears either to be damned for sins or to be deprived of the inheritance of the loving Father.




But since there are four fears, one servile, the other [1089B] worldly; the third initial, the fourth filial, the two last are referred to the spirit of the fear of the Lord, of which the one indeed, that is, initial fear, is not without charity, I do not say perfect, but inchoate. Whence John in his Epistle, when he said: *Perfect charity casteth out fear [n: (I Joan. IV)]* showed that, although not yet perfect, charity nevertheless is in initial fear. But in the other, which is filial, consisting in love alone, there is perfect charity, of which the Psalmist says: *The fear of the Lord is holy, enduring for ever and ever [n: (Psal. XVIII)]*. Initial fear is that of which it is said: *The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom [n: (Eccle. I)]*, when a man begins to be wise and to taste God, where fear and the spirit accompany one another, [1089C] as it is read, fear is without hope. And the more good will grows, that is, charity, so much the more does fear almost decrease, so that from this initial fear, by which one fears to be punished, which is of the imperfect, one may at some time advance to filial fear, by which one fears not to be punished but to be separated, which is of the perfect. But the two fears which we set down in the preceding, that is, the servile and the worldly: they do not pertain to the spirit of the fear of the Lord, because they are separated from chaste fear; for servile fear is to cease from evil for the punishment threatening from man, the evil will nevertheless being retained. Worldly fear is to cease from good on account of the punishment which is feared from man: and these fears, namely the servile and the worldly, Christ forbids, saying: *Fear ye not them that kill the body, but have nothing that they may do to the soul* [1089D] [n: (Matth. XX)]. Afterwards he adds concerning initial fear, saying: *But rather fear him who can destroy both body and soul in hell [n: (ibid.)]*, which he would not command unless charity were in it, even if not yet perfect.

And it is to be noted that sometimes in Holy Scripture this fear is called servile, because it is akin to the servile, since both there and here one ceases from evil by fear of punishment. But that fear makes one cease from evil in deed and not in will; this one both in deed and in will. In the spirit, then, of the twofold fear of the Lord, that is, initial or filial, every holy soul has groaning for song, because in the joy of the present life she groans forth a mournful mind, either because she fears to be damned for sin, or because she fears *80* to be separated from God. Therefore, as we have said, on account of the sevenfold [1090A] quality of virtue and of dovelike innocence, the bride is recorded by the bridegroom to have the eyes of doves, because she shows forth in action what, by the grace of the Holy Spirit, judging it useful, honest, she sees with right intention. But because she understands that every good, if she has any, she has not from herself but from God, she ascribes the whole grace of the beauty conferred on her to her Creator and Redeemer himself, saying: *Behold, thou art fair,* etc.

VERSE 15.-- *Behold, thou art fair, my beloved, and comely. Our bed is flowery.*

*Thou,* she says, *O beloved,* essentially *art fair and comely,* I, whatever of beauty I have, have accidentally, by participation in thee, according to the measure of grace bestowed on me by thee, from thee; because thou, as Creator, art capable of being received through indwelling grace, I, as [1090B] creature, am capable of receiving, participating in that grace as thou thyself hast granted. And this indeed according to the power of thy divinity; but according to the humanity, according to the outward man, thou wast *beautiful in form above the sons of men [n: (Psal. XLIV)]*; but according to the inward man, to thee alone, the only-begotten, *God giveth not the spirit by measure [n: (Joan. III)]*, while those who partake of thee receive him by measure, one indeed thus, another thus.

But all beauty and comeliness they have from thee, who truly and naturally *art fair and comely,* because whatever of meekness, whatever of piety, whatever of justice, whatever of patience, of humility, whatever finally of virtue or holiness ever could be or can be, they look upon and receive in thee, the singularly [1090C] holy man, as in the highest exemplar.

Having set forth this saying, then, *behold, thou art fair and comely,* she pursues some parts of that very beauty and comeliness, while she adds: *Our bed is flowery,* etc.

Of which parts indeed the first, that is, *our bed is flowery,* can be referred both to the head and to the members of the head, but the two remaining to the members alone, which nevertheless depend on the head itself by participation in its beauty and comeliness. But first, concerning the first part, it must be said how it will be sought both as the head and toward the members of the head: toward the head indeed, either according to the good pleasure of the divinity ruling all things, or according to the sacrament of the humanity [1090D] redeeming the world; but toward the members according to the participation, as we have said, of divine grace dwelling in the mind of each holy man and working in it the gifts of its condescension.

By the name of the bed rest is designated in two ways, that is, either bodily or spiritual. For as after labour the body seeks rest, so the mind too, wearied by certain labours of perturbations, desires rest; but in this place by the bed spiritual rest ought *81* to be understood, because we have said it is to be referred both to the head and to the members of piety.

Nevertheless, we must first see concerning the rest of the head, which in no way ought to be believed necessary or advantageous to the substance of him who is subject to no accident, and *with whom there is not any change, nor [1091A] shadow of vicissitude [n: (Jacob. I)]*, but the rest of the head is the salvation of the body. For just as God says that he labours when he bears with the depravities of the wicked, so too he says that he rests when he delights in the virtues of the good. Of his labour indeed, by which he bears with those still living perversely, he says through the prophet: *I have laboured, sustaining [n: (Jerem. VI)]*: for the Lord does not labour in sustaining, whose power of dignity no fatigue touches, but, speaking in the words of humanity, he calls his very patience towards us labour. Further, of the rest by which he delights in the good he says: *Upon whom shall I rest, but upon the humble and the quiet and him that trembleth at my words? [var: Vulg. Is 66:2 *ad quem autem respiciam, nisi ad pauperculum, et contritum spiritu*, "to whom shall I look, but to the poor little one and the contrite of spirit"] [n: (Isa. LXVI)]*; as if he should say in other words: He prepares rest for me in himself who in humility and quiet keeps the rule [1091B] of my precepts. The rest of God, then, is our advantage, just as, on the contrary, his labour is our loss.

To a member of this head, then, that is, to some faithful and holy man, the signification of this bed, which we have called rest, is fitted; because just as, by the opposite, for the reprobate the rest and delight of this world is in the future a perpetual labour and fatigue of the soul, so for the elect the labour and fatigue of this world is in the future the eternal rest and consolation of the soul. For the bed of a holy man is the silence and the cultivation of the contemplative life, which the more remote it is from the world, the nearer it is to God; and the more he labours in the observance of the divine precepts, the greater rest he has in the contemplation of the divinity as it were they are in one bed. God through grace inspiring, governing [1091C] and preserving, man through grace inspiring, governing and preserving, willing, acting and perfecting; God participable by grace, man participating in grace.

The members of this head therefore say, *our bed is flowery,* because, since with the aforesaid they are as it were in one rest, participable and participating, that is, head, member, unfading buds of virtues sprout, so that deservedly that bed is called flowery, that is, composed with the varied comeliness of good works. Thus the bride speaks to the bridegroom: the *bed* is not thine, but *ours, flowery,* because to adhere to God through honesty of life flowering with holiness, in thought, will, action, is as it were to rest with him in one bridal chamber; otherwise the soul profits nothing without grace resting [1091D] with it.

Further, according to the sacrament of the humanity, the bed of the beloved is the womb of the blessed Virgin Mary, because it is wonderfully flowery *82* both with the beauty of virginity and with the inestimable and ineffable operation, through the Holy Spirit, of a singular nativity. But *our,* she says, *bed,* because thine indeed it is by the election of the incarnation, mine by faith in that same incarnation; thine on account of the manifold gifts of the proportion of thy grace dwelling in the mind of the Virgin and Mother, mine on account of the example of her magnificent holiness, to be imitated according to my ability, and the excellence of the novelty of novelties, to be adored without example.

The bed of the beloved is also the sepulchre, in which after the passion he was laid away, which is therefore flowery because from [1092A] this burial the ensigns of the glorious resurrection flowered forth. This *bed* too is not only thine, she says, but *ours,* because thou didst rise from this bed, that is, from the sepulchre, and gavest me the hope of rising again, the certainty of which hope is to me as it were a bed in which I rest secure, wholly, through the zeal of thy grace working with me, because I do not doubt of the future joy of the resurrection, and of the everlasting reward of transitory labour.

VERSE 16.-- *The beams of our houses are of cedar our panelled ceilings of cypress.*

But the beauty and comeliness of the following parts, by which the beloved is proclaimed fair and comely by his beloved, which are: *The beams of our houses of cedar, the panelled ceilings of cypress,* look specially to his members, whose virtues nevertheless [1092B] and graces are ascribed to his beauty and comeliness, whose is all that is called good and honest.

First, however, we must look into whose houses these beams and panelled ceilings are: according to universality, the great house of this beloved, that is, of our Lord Jesus Christ, is the holy universal Church; but according to particularity, the single churches which are contained within this universality are also called his houses. And the beams and panelled ceilings of these houses are the angels, that is, the rulers and doctors of the several Churches, who have been in time and place for protection and adornment by words and examples, who also, spreading far and wide the odour of their holiness and doctrine, have protected and adorned the universal Church itself as well, [1092C] protect and adorn; will protect and adorn, whose memory is in benediction for ever and ever.

But these *beams* are said to be *of cedar,* the *panelled ceilings,* however, *of cypress.* Now the cedar has three memorable things in its nature: height, odour, incorruptibility; which indeed rightly befit the holy doctors, because they are raised up by the contemplation of the Divinity, are spread round about by the odour of doctrine and of good action, by no rottenness of unbelief or depravity *83* are corrupted. There is also a fourth distinction of the cedar's nature, that by its odour it is said to put serpents to flight, showing indeed this virtue of the doctors whom it designates, that by their doctrine they are able to repel evil thoughts creeping secretly in hearts [1092D] and to put to flight, together with the thoughts themselves, the ancient serpent who suggests them, that is, the devil.

The cypress too has in its nature two of the graces written above, namely of odour and of incorruption; which also befit the doctors on account of the odour of life and doctrine and the incorruption of faith.

The beams and panelled ceilings may, however, not unfittingly be understood differently, so that we take the beams in the Church as the prelates, and the panelled ceilings as their subjects, because the beams support the panelled ceilings, as the prelates their subjects, while they provide them with necessaries, so that, as by the conjunction of beams and panelled ceilings the house is clothed with beautiful comeliness, so by the religious unanimity of prelates and subjects the Church of God may be ordered with fitting and reasonable disposition.




[1093A]And well are both, as cedar and cypress, described as odoriferous and incorruptible, because both the prelates are raised aloft by the good odour and incorruption of life, doctrine and faith, and the subjects are daily provoked by their erudition to the same graces of the virtues. Whence also the cypress, which is set in the part of the subjects, has this by nature, that, planed by the hand of the craftsman, it presents a comely appearance, because this is proper to good subjects, that when they have been touched by the hand of holy admonition or rebuke by their prelates, they do not recoil insolently, but submit themselves obedient to their admonitions with a cheerful countenance and a patient mind.

But that we may look at all these things generally in the universal Church too, which is as it were a certain commonwealth of the bridegroom and the bride, [1093B] these ensigns of the adornment of the house of the Lord are daily venerated and proclaimed, it contains principally the head, that is, Christ in his deeds and precepts, and after him his members, among whom, above all, eminent and of singular merit, is the blessed Virgin Mary, who in the signification of the flowery bed is held most beautiful: then the apostles or apostolic men and their hearers and followers, set in the very house of the Lord as beams and panelled ceilings, by whose life, erudition, institution, authority in all things this whole commonwealth of God is venerably confirmed and adorned, so that to those who look rightly nothing more honest, nothing more pleasant, nothing surely sweeter can be esteemed in the pilgrimage of this life. For this is as it were a certain way and concourse of future felicity [1093C] and a contemplation, far off, in faith, hope and charity, of that great and incomparable commonwealth.

But that this glorious state of the nobly instituted Church has stood at the nod of him *84* who said: *Without me you can do nothing [n: (Joan. XV)]*, the same bridegroom and ruler of it himself confesses, truth confirming truth, not that he needs our attestation, but that he may instruct us unto greater faith, and that he may teach no one to presume of his own justice in any good.




## CHAPTER TWO.

VERSE 1.-- *I am the flower of the field and the lily of the valleys.*

Rightly, he says, O beloved, dost thou judge, who ascribest all thy beauty to me, because *I* in truth am [1093D] *the flower of the field,* that is, the honour and beauty and adornment of the whole universal Church, spread through the whole world, or most joyful field, and diverse in species of holiness and grace, germinating and flowering from my gift. *I* also *am the lily of the valleys,* that is, the cleansing, refreshment and medicine of the humble, or of those cast down by some sorrow and affliction of this world for the sake of my name.

*85* For the nature of the lily has in it three delightful things: whiteness, odour, medicine against burning; for whoever in this valley of tears, with humbled spirit and contrite heart, bewail their excesses, recognizing themselves infirm and always imploring my aid, without doubt shall obtain cleansing and whiteness of soul, and then [1094A] shall be refreshed by the odour of good conversation, I inspiring and helping, and against all the incentives of the flesh and against the burning of affliction and of the world as it were setting on fire, shall be cooled by the opposing of my love and of the consolation of hope.

Another too, O beloved, *I am the flower of the field,* that one, namely, of whom Isaiah says: *There shall come forth a rod out of the root of Jesse, and a flower shall rise up out of his root [n: (Isa. XI)]*. For thou didst say above that our bed is flowery, namely the womb of my Mother, from whom I willed to be incarnate; but I say that it is flowery for this reason, because I am its flower, from which flower many other flowers proceed, that is, spiritual grace, which the prophet mentioned enumerates. *And there shall rest,* he says, *upon him,* without doubt upon the flower, *the spirit of the Lord, the spirit [1094B] of wisdom and of understanding, the spirit of counsel and of fortitude, the spirit of knowledge and of piety, and the spirit of the fear of the Lord shall fill him [n: (ibid.)]*. I am the flower, then, of the field, namely of that earth of which it is said: *Truth is sprung out of the earth [n: (Psal. LXXXIV)]*; which is therefore called a field, because it is not rough, nor tortuous, a village, but level and even, as being levelled by the power of the Most High after the manner of a plain from every unevenness of morals; which brought me forth, the flower of flowers, that is, the Holy of holies, without the seed of man, just as a field without seed produces the shoots of diverse flowers and herbs.

*The lily* too am I *of the valleys,* that is, the whiteness and odour and salvation of those who imitate the humility of my mother, and not of those who glory in the merit of chastity; for those who glory, and contain themselves for human favour only, [1094C] have not progress, the lily of virginal whiteness, and, by inward purity, the sign; but rather they are unclean with the blackness of vainglory before the sight of my purity, and therefore I neither am nor can be called their lily: but of those who imitate me and her, I am the lily by the gift of grace, she is the lily by example, so that they too may become a lily by imitation. Whence follows: *as the lily among thorns.*

VERSE 2.-- *As the lily among thorns, so is my love among the daughters.*

As I, he says, among the Jews, whom I expected to make grapes, but they made thorns [n: (Isa. V)], shone with humility, meekness, patience and the manifold grace of the virtues, while they, standing like a thorn against [1094D] me, pricked me with many stings of words and of manners, so thou, O my Church, my bride, or any soul whatever that loves me, you ought to be among the daughters *86* of this world, that is, souls thorny with malice and unbelief, in humility and patience, so that, when thou hast imitated me in this manner, thou mayest be proved truly my love, and let such imitation of me be the proof of true friendship.

Or as my mother according to the flesh, Mary, who is a lily by the whiteness of virginity and the odour of holiness, went her way with humility and patience among the daughters of the Synagogue, who like thorns pricked her, putting upon her the stain of infamy, because they did not believe in the virtue and power of God. Because she recognized through the spirit the dispensation of God, and felt fulfilled in herself what she had formerly heard through the prophet and afterwards through the angel. [1095A] So also, by her example, whatever soul is a friend to me through faith and love ought, among the daughters whom the corruption of malice and impiety has begotten, to keep humility, patience and honesty, and on account of the hope of future remuneration never to fall away from them.

Or let us say simply that the soul which wishes to show itself worthy of the friendship of God ought always to stand among depraved men *as the lily among [sic: liliuminter] thorns,* so that, as *the lily among thorns,* being set there, does not lose the dignity of its whiteness and odour, so she, among depraved manners, may not lose the integrity of faith and patience, or of any holiness granted her, but rather may learn by tolerating that, the more she is afflicted by their perversity, so much the more are the merits of greater virtues heaped up for her; [1095B] for, as blessed Gregory says, whoever does not tolerate the wicked, he himself through his intolerance is witness to thee that he is not good. Hence blessed Job speaks of himself, saying: *A brother* am I *of dragons and a companion of ostriches [n: (Job XXX)]*; and the Lord to Ezekiel: *The sons of man are unbelievers and subverters with thee, thou dwellest with scorpions [var: Vulg. Ez 2:6 *fili hominis … increduli et subversores sunt tecum*, "O son of man … unbelievers and subverters are with thee"] [n: (Ezech. II)]*. Peter also approves the life of Lot, saying: *And he delivered just Lot, oppressed by the injury and the luxurious conversation of the wicked; for in sight and hearing he was just: dwelling among them who from day to day vexed the soul of the just man with wicked works [n: (II Petr. II)]*. Hence John writes to the Church of Pergamum, saying: *I know where thou dwellest, where the seat of Satan is, and thou hast my name, and hast not denied my faith [n: (Apoc. II)]*; Paul also [1095C] praises and strengthens his disciples, saying: *In the midst of a depraved and perverse nation, among whom you shine as lights in the world. Holding the word of life [n: (Philip. II)]*.

By these authorities it is sufficiently established how great a virtue it is to tolerate the depraved manners of men, since from the greater malice of the reprobate greater gains of merits grow for the elect, because it is written: *The more Pharaoh oppressed the people of God, the more it was multiplied [n: (Exod. I)]*.

But who the daughters are among whom the Church or holy soul is said to dwell *as the lily among thorns* is gathered from the comparison itself, because as for those which are compared to thorns, it is evident *87* that they are taken in the adverse part; and they are therefore called daughters, because [1095D] they are generated from the wicked seed of perverse doctrine, or from an evil conception of the mind. But the Church, or any faithful soul whatever, which through faith and operation is the bride of Christ, that she may be able to be as the lily among thorns, comes to meet him who rules and protects her with gracious praise through another comparison, saying: *As the apple tree among the trees of the woods,* etc.

VERSE 3.-- *As the apple tree among the trees of the woods, so is my beloved among the sons. Under the shadow of him whom I had desired I sat, and his fruit was sweet to my throat.*

My beloved, she says, has compared me to the lily among thorns, keeping its virtue, which, although he has granted it me by his gift, yet he taught me by his own example that I should become this. For *as the apple tree,* fruit-bearing, having in itself three useful things by which it refreshes, [1096A] this being fruit, odour and shade, *among the trees of the woods* too, which certainly are unfruitful, is held incomparable, *so my beloved,* by the fruit and odour of holiness and of justice and of doctrine, and by the shade of his protection, with which he refreshes, teaches and defends those who believe in him, *among the sons,* that is, the Jews, stood forth; who at first indeed were sons, and now wish to be, but are not, because of them God said: *I have nourished and exalted sons, but they have despised me [n: (Isa. I)]*; and elsewhere: *The strange sons have lied to me, the strange sons have grown old and have halted from their paths [n: (Psal. XVII)]*: who boast themselves to be sons of Abraham, but are not, because they have not the works [n: (Joan. VIII)]; and therefore they are unfruitful and useless trees, and on this account to be cut down and cast into the fire. For among such [1096B] trees, caught by eternal burning, he hung on the tree of the cross, from which he poured out for me the sweetest fruit of his body and blood, with which he daily refreshes those who believe in him: but they, like wild trees, not only brought forth no fruit of good work, but even poured out the most bitter sap of their malice and impiety, crying: *Crucify, crucify [n: (Joan. XIX.)]*, and: *His blood be upon us and upon our sons [n: (Matth. XVII)]*.

Under the shade of this tree, then, that is, the refreshment and consoling defence of the New Testament, which I was desiring now for a long time in the ancient fathers, who sighed with great desire for the times of softening and refreshing grace, now at length after the advent, passion, resurrection and ascension of Christ [1096C] I sat, with faith unshaken and stable. For the Law, with a certain heat of inclemency and of incurable punishment, when it said: *If anyone shall have done this or that, let him die the death [n: (Exod. XIX)]*, had long afflicted me; but grace moved such heat with the shade of mercy and the remedy of penance, under which desirable shade I did not walk, did not stand, but rather sat and rested, because *88* I received this decree of piety, confirmed by the authority of my Lord himself, to be held immutably.

This tree indeed is the tree of life, whose shade and divine protection, which its branches pour out, branches of honour and grace, which namely are the oppositions of diverse virtues against the heats of worldly temptations: as, against pride, [1096D] the opposition of humility is necessary; against anger, of patience; against lust, of chastity and continence; against contumacy, of obedience; against avarice, of largesse and mercy; against strifes and contentions, of peace and concord; against envy and hatred, of love; and that we may comprehend it briefly, against all the delights and temptations of this world surely there must be opposed the remembrance of the future misery of the wicked and the glory of the just.

The outpourings of such shades indeed protect the soul from the heat of carnal suggestions and delights, under which, because I desired them, *I sat,* that is, I rested, since indeed I found in them the grateful and wholesome refreshment of protecting grace.

*And his fruit,* without doubt that of that apple tree, [1097A] that is, of Christ, my bridegroom and Lord, *sweet to my throat,* because the utility and suavity of his precepts is sweet and pleasing to the palate of my heart and to the capacity of my mind. Or the fruit of this apple tree hanging on the tree of the cross is sweet to my throat, because the receiving of the body and blood of Christ pours sweetness and health into my breast, and it is plainly the perfect refection of my body and soul [sic: *ani mae*].

Otherwise also the sons among whom the bridegroom is said to dwell *as the apple tree among the trees of the woods* are taken to be the elect, who are called sons of God by name, on account of the observance of his commandments, for which they shall obtain from their heavenly Father himself the inheritance of eternal life; among whom Christ dwells *like the apple tree among the trees of the woods,* because as the apple tree [1097B] excels all the trees of the woods in beauty and in the odour and sweetness of its fruits, so Christ goes before all the saints in the comeliness of virtues and the sweetness of doctrine, as it is written of him: *Beautiful in form above the sons of men, grace is poured abroad in thy lips; therefore God, thy God, hath anointed thee with the oil of gladness above thy fellows [n: (Psal. XLIV)]*.




## CHAPTER TWO. (cont.)

*Under whose shade* or the Church, because, the branches of that tree, whether of sacraments, or of commandments, or of gifts, being spread through the whole breadth of the world, she is wonderfully protected and consoled from all heat of sadness and perturbation, remaining secure and immovable through the hope of future retribution, *whose fruit is sweet to the throat* of her, because all the mystical things of his utterances, and the venerable institutions of the sacraments, which [1097C] he brought forth from the hidden wisdom of his divinity, are sweet and pleasing to the mind that devoutly embraces and venerates them, *89* on account of the salvation which is conferred on it from thence, quickening and refreshing without end.

But what effect the sweetness and suavity of this fruit has, what more sublime and more perfect thing it brings forth, she shows by what she adds.

VERSE 4.-- *The king hath brought me into the wine-cellar, he hath set in order charity in me.* [var: Vulg. Ct 2:4 *Introduxit me in cellam vinariam*, without *rex*, "the king"]
