The effect of this introduction proceeded from the cause of the fruit, well pleasing and therefore taken up with great zeal of mind, because the more the precepts of God are desired, so much the more fully are they admitted by divine love to ever greater things to be desired. That fruit indeed [1097D] which flowed forth, and still flows forth, from that glorious and most excellent tree, the Lord Jesus Christ, not all receive equally: because one receives this, another that, according to what he savours and loves.

For to this one the virtue of patience savours and pleases more, to that one, of obedience; to this one, of humility, to that one, of continence; this one the assiduity of prayer delights more, that one, of reading, of fasts; and in the whole they procure by divine gift the species of the virtues, of the graces.

But there is a certain as it were singular and as it were separate storehouse, standing in a certain secret place, which is called a cell because of a certain narrowness of its own, in that it admits few into its recesses, where is laid up the sweetness and suavity of that fruit which *maketh glad the heart of man [n: (Psal. CIII)]*, and boils out every humour of vices, [1098A] namely the evangelical precept concerning the utility of charity. Which is rightly called by the name of wine, because as wine, by purging itself, rejects from itself everything pestiferous, and by its natural colour boils out every noxious humour; so the precept of charity puts to flight from the heart every pest of vices, and expels all the passions of the mind, because no one can both have charity and be evil; and therefore the full understanding of this is deservedly called the wine-cellar.

Those, then, who delight to rest under the branches of that saving tree and to gather without loathing the sweetest fruit of holiness, will at some time arrive at this perfection, that they enter the wine-cellar without obstacle, because this way is not narrow for them, for whom the way of worldly pleasure, [1098B] broad and spacious, is utterly closed. For the way of this precept is narrow, because it seems difficult to many and laborious when it is said: *Love your enemies, do good to them that hate you [n: (Matth. V)]*.

But because we read that it has been observed by many saints, this precept is not difficult except to those who have not yet perfectly tasted the sweetness of the fruits, that is, of the divine commandments. This, then, is the wine-cellar, perfection, and, to those freely entering the narrow way, sweetly savouring and on this account perfectible, of the understanding of the divine precept concerning the excellence of charity.

But because charity itself looks in diverse directions, there is necessary in it *90* an order to be attended to, so that one may know in what place each affection is to be put, and which is to be taken as prior, which as posterior. Whence, when she had set before: [1098C] *The king hath brought me into the wine-cellar,* she adds, *and he hath set in order charity in me.* For this is the order of holy love, that first we love God, then our neighbour, lastly even the enemy himself. This order is indeed necessary, because it is impossible that anyone love his neighbour, or, what is more difficult, his enemy, unless he love God, since the cause of the love of these is the love of God, and in this way what was impossible becomes possible. For what is necessary to be, it is impossible not to be, and what is necessary not to be, it is impossible to be. But it is necessary that the love of God be where the love of neighbour is, because no one loves his neighbour for God's sake unless he love God: but it is necessary that the love of God not be where [1098D] the love of neighbour is not, according to the word of John: *He that loveth not his brother whom he saw, God whom he seeth not, how can he love? [n: (I Joan. IV.)]* But it is not always necessary that the love of God not be where the love of the enemy is not, because it can happen that one who loves God has no enemy whom he may love for God's sake: but if he has one, then it is necessary that the love of God not be where the love of the enemy is not, because he proves that he does not love God who is unwilling to love his enemy for God's sake.

This order, then, as has been said, seems to be necessary, when it is determined that love of neighbour is impossible unless the love of God precede, according, however, to that love which is according to God, not that which is according to the world. For the love [1099A] of the world is without the love of God, because that is according to the flesh, this according to the spirit.

But the mode of love according to the flesh is twofold: one which is altogether against God, as is that of the wicked who love one another for the fellowship of iniquity; the other which is not against God, as is that of parents, kinsmen, and friends, to which love, however, the love of God is always to be preferred, and, for God's sake, the love of each neighbour. But the first mode according to the flesh is altogether foreign to the present place, because it is wholly disordered and contrary to God; but the other, which is not against God, is not abhorrent to the present place, if only it be ordered, because if a man is loved more in God than God, then it is disordered.

[1099B]It may, however, not absurdly be taken so that we say that the order of charity is twofold, one in God, the other in the neighbour: for it is written: *God is charity [n: (I Joan. IV)]*: and in this charity which is God, the Church, or any holy soul whatever, ought to learn, know and understand the order, that in the first place she should name the Father as the voice, in the second the Son as the word from the voice, in the third the Holy Spirit as the reason proceeding from the voice and the word. But let her not say it in that sense, as though there were any degree in the Trinity, *91* which it is impious to say, but that she may distinguish in persons, by this order of terms, that charity, that is, God, whom she ought to love with the whole heart, the whole soul, the whole strength, one and the same in substance.

[1099C]But love itself is distinguished by this order, that God be loved first with the whole heart, then with the whole soul, lastly with the whole strength, by believing, with the whole soul by confessing, with the whole strength by working.

But in the love of neighbour charity is ordered through the several precepts of the Law which follow: for not to commit adultery, not to do murder, not to speak false witness, to honour father and mother, etc., following the order of the Decalogue, is charity ordered, each in its own place as well. How also, or with what affection, the diverse persons in the Church ought to be loved, charity is ordered; because in one order ought the faithful friend to be loved, in another order the dearest son, in another order parents, in another order the full brother, in another order [1099D] the spouse, in another order the servant by the master, in another order the master by the servant, in another the stranger, in another the acquaintance, in another the priest, in another the kinsman and neighbour. For to bestow necessaries on the needy, to visit the sick or those committed to prison, to console the needy and those oppressed by unjust judgment, to bury the dead, to correct those who act wickedly, to help brothers in some labour, to instruct the less knowing, or to expend whatever other things are necessary to the utility of neighbours, all these assuredly are orderable offices of holy charity.

Deservedly, then, the holy mind is brought in by her king and bridegroom into the wine-cellar, that is, into the understanding of the divine precepts, when, inebriated by the sweetness of his grace, she understands the diligence of charity to be expended, so manifoldly ordered. Whence, [1100A] when she is affected by so great a strength of this inebriating wine that she remains as it were insensible to every worldly delight, and for the immense desire of charity so ordered is not only stupefied but even languishes, demanding for herself some solace of this languor, she goes on to say: *Stay me up with flowers,* etc.

VERSE 5.-- *Stay me up with flowers, compass me about with apples, because I languish with love.*

What are the flowers but souls bringing forth the beginnings of good work and beginning to be fragrant with heavenly desire? And what are the apples but souls already holding perfection from the beginnings of good works through the nourishment of divine grace, as it were advancing from flowers to fruit? She, then, who languishes with divine love [1100B] seeks to be stayed up with flowers, compassed about with apples, because, since she is not yet able to see as he is him whom she loves so orderly with the whole heart, the whole soul, the whole strength, that is, with faith, confession, operation, yet it is a great consolation to her that she rejoices in the pious advancements of her neighbours, now beginning, now perfected.

*92* For holy Church has in herself certain lesser ones whom she may instruct by word and example, that they may be as flowers catching the first-fruits of holiness. She has also certain greater ones in whom she may be instructed, and by whose doctrines and examples she may be daily edified, who are as apples, pleasing already by the maturity and perfection of good works. Such, then, namely both the lesser and the greater, growing up in this manner with zeal for piety, [1100C] greatly console holy Church, which surely with great gladness of heart sees her sons, small and great, rude and perfect, love this which she herself vehemently loves; and this is a great consolation of her languor, in the exile of this pilgrimage, that they tend with her by desire towards that to which she believes she will at some time arrive by reward.

In this way too any saint whatever who languishes with the love of God can say: *Stay me up with flowers [sic: loribus], compass me about with apples,* when he esteems as a great remedy of such languor the progress of his neighbours, which he sees in the good work of his own, whom he loves in God, these indeed sprouting towards pious pursuits, those bearing fruit in those very pursuits. Did not he who [1100D] languished with such love that he said: *I desire to be dissolved and to be with Christ,* nevertheless desire the utility of his neighbours, saying, *but to remain in the flesh is necessary for your sakes? [n: (Philipp. I.)]* Whose progress to see he meanwhile reckons as a great consolation, when he is perfectly able [cj: *praevalet*; read *non praevalet*, "is not able"] to comprehend the face of God which he so greatly desires to see. Although he greatly desires to be dissolved and to be with Christ, did he who languished with divine love fear that he could not at some time attain what he loved? By no means; but this came about by the dispensation of God, that the delay of this reward might become a greater exercise of virtues, and also an ampler remuneration of labour for the utility of those whom he loved; for he who desired to be dissolved was certainly sure of the reward, as he himself says elsewhere: *We know [1101A] that if our earthly house of this habitation be dissolved, we have a building from God, a house not made with hands, eternal in the heavens [n: (II Cor. V)]*.

Wherefore he bore the pain of this delay more lightly, because he rejoiced that those whom he loved were tending with him to receive the same reward.




## CHAPTER TWO. (cont.)

Whence he sends consolatory things to certain of his disciples, saying words: *I give thanks to my God always for you, in the grace of God which is given you in Christ Jesus, because in all things you are made rich in him, in all utterance and in all knowledge, as the testimony of Christ was confirmed in you, so that nothing is wanting to you in any grace, waiting for the revelation of our Lord Jesus Christ [n: (I Cor. I)]*. Behold, he who desired to be dissolved [1101B] and to be with Christ, how much he rejoiced and gave thanks, that, because he could not yet attain this, yet by the advancements of the sons whom he had begotten in Christ he greatly relieved the inconvenience of this delay.

*93* In another way also the Church desires the utility and progress of her sons through faith and operation, so that she is then said to be stayed up with flowers when she is adorned with the faith of believers, which is the beginning of religion and as it were the first rising flower of holiness. But because *faith without works is dead [n: (Jac. II)]*, she is said to be compassed about with apples when she is said to rejoice in the pious works of those same believers, bearing fruit manifoldly, as in fruits already ripe.

And since these two are so joined to each other that the one [1101C] cannot be useful without the other; for it is written that *without faith it is impossible for anyone to please God [n: (Hebr. XI)]*, but faith, if it have not works, is dead in itself [n: (Jac. II)], great surely is the consolation of the soul languishing in the love of God when she sees her neighbours advance in faith and operation.

But since this bride of Christ, who by that love so magnificently, as we have said, ordered is made weak and languid, is not [cj: *non*; read *non solum*, "not only"] consoled by the progress and utility of her neighbours, but is also lifted up by the reality of present goods and the hope of future ones, she adds, saying: *His left hand under my head.*

VERSE 6.-- *His left hand under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me.*

[1101D]The left hand of Christ, that is, the *sinistra*, are temporal goods, but his right hand eternal goods; but certain temporal goods in this world God grants to his elect for the expectation of future goods, so that while they receive his present gifts they may be prepared more ardently and more securely for desiring the eternal.

But the *left hand* of Christ is *under the head* of her, because she presses down these very temporal goods by the intention of the highest love, so that she may look upon them as lower, and always attend to eternal goods as higher. For under the head, that is, under the mind, not above, ought all these temporal things to be, namely that she may never set them before divine love, but may so use them that, not for pleasure but rather for necessity, she may moderate them by the reasonable equity of the mind [1102A] and may always admire the more, by loving and sighing, those things which ought to be above the mind. For this is *his right hand* which may embrace him, namely those everlasting goods, which, when she shall have obtained him, she may be able to rest in secure blessedness and blessed security.

She says, therefore: *His left hand under my head and his right hand shall embrace me,* because all things that he has ordered in creatures for my utility I shall hold in thought for thanksgiving, but those on account of which these things have been furnished me for a time, as being to abide with me eternally, I shall be zealous to love and to desire with the whole devotion of my mind.

Or, *his left hand under my head,* because that he was born and suffered, that he rose again and ascended into heaven, [1102B] will be in my memory; *and his right hand shall embrace me,* because the memory of these things will animate me to the ensigns of the future resurrection and *94* glory, to be loved and desired.

Or the left hand of God is, in this world, a certain small knowledge of him, but the *right hand* *of him,* after this life, the full and true knowledge of him; but the *left hand under* our *head* is, but the *right hand* *of him shall embrace* us, because *we see now indeed through a glass in a dark manner, but then face to face [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*. For the *left hand* is *under the head* because it is granted to us in this life to think something of God and to see it with the mind, but the *right hand* *of him shall embrace* us, because in the future life he will fill and surround us with so great a vision of himself that what this may be *neither eye* can see, [1102C] *nor ear* hear, *nor into the heart of man* can it ascend [n: (I Cor. II)].

This charity, then, ordered in the bride by the condescension of ordering grace, sets the left hand under her head and embraces her with its right hand, so that, all temporal gifts being put in the second place, she may rest principally in the desire of eternal life; and that in this way the grant of present things may be also the certainty of future things, the consolation of the mind weak and languid in the love of God.

And you, sisters beloved of Christ, who by despising the world languish with the love of God, ought to give such a sign of this kind of love that you may be stayed up with flowers by believing, compassed about with apples by working. Let the left hand of Christ be under your head and his right hand embrace [1102D] you, so that you may always attend to the temporal goods somehow granted you by God as lower, but to the eternal as higher. *For length of days is in his right hand, in his left hand riches and glory [n: (Prov. III)]*; because in this present life he sometimes grants to his elect to foretaste the riches of his goodness, but in the future life to possess them eternally without end. *Delights in the right hand* of him are *even unto the end [n: (Psal. XV)]*, which you can surely obtain, if you so languish in his love that you persevere in him firm and stable even unto the end. His right hand indeed shall embrace you, because not in part as now, but whole and perfect knowledge of himself he will give, and for brief and transitory labour he will grant you rest and life and length of days for ever and ever. Amen.

## BOOK TWO.




## *95* PROEM.

[1103A]

*How,* says the Psalmist, *shall we sing the canticle of the Lord in a strange land? [n: (Psal. CXXXVI.)]* But what is *the canticle of the Lord?* First, to speak simply: *The canticle of the Lord* is whatever we compose, write and say according to the manner of the Church's doctrine or ordinance, in a concordant modulation of senses and words. *The canticle of the Lord* is the whole page of the Old and New Testament, which we sing well when we announce it with right and full faith, setting forth to the praise of the Lord now the series of the things done, now the signification of the things themselves. But a *Canticle* of this kind anyone can sing by way of knowledge, not only in his own land but also *in a strange land;* because a bodily place does not take away [1103B] the power of singing, though it may take away the will and the pleasure, because of some passion of sadness: as with that people set in the grief of captivity, who, when it was said to them, *Sing us a hymn from the canticles of Sion,* answered: *How shall we sing the canticle of the Lord in a strange land?* For, to take it in figure, their not being able to sing *the canticle of the Lord in a strange land* was our not being able to praise the Lord perfectly in the place of our pilgrimage; which is to us as it were a strange land; *Because we have not here a lasting city, but we seek one to come [n: (Hebr. XIII)]*, which is our fatherland and the land of our eternal inheritance. For to tell the works of God, to declare his commandments, to write and expound them, is, as we have said, to sing a certain canticle of the Lord [1103C]: this too is possible in this place of our pilgrimage, since the Psalmist says: *Thy justifications were the subject of my song in the place of my pilgrimage [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*.

But that we may consider something higher, there is also another canticle of the Lord, which it is not possible to sing in a strange land. And which is that? Surely the one which the Holy Spirit modulates by his sevenfold grace, as it were through the distinctions of seven voices; because that voice of the inner man sounds in one way through the spirit of wisdom, in another through the spirit of understanding, in another through the spirit of counsel, and in another through the spirit of fortitude; but in another manner through the spirit of knowledge, and in another through the spirit of piety, and in another again [1103D] through the spirit of the Lord [n: (Isa. XI)].

For by these distributions of graces, as by certain different voices, is constituted the whole modulation, of the canticle *96* of the Lord, of that most sweet canticle, which a life honest, holy, pleasing to God and perfect sings on the strings of the virtues.

For there is no dissonance there between flesh and spirit, because the flesh sings in accord with the spirit so concordantly that they build up the perfect harmony of the spiritual [1104A] canticle, which is, in the place of our pilgrimage, the best kind of divine canticle. But this *canticle* cannot be sung in a strange land, that is, in a body subject to sins; which is then a strange land when, being by sinning in the power of the devil, it is unworthy of the dwelling of Christ: or it is then estranged from us when, doing ill, it struggles against the spirit and the reason of the mind. Strange is the land. Man by always loving earthly things, and on this account estranged from God, and therefore powerless to sing the canticle of the Lord, because *the sensual man perceiveth not the things that are of the Spirit of God [n: (I Cor. II)]*.

But to what purpose are these things? Plainly to this, that I may show you, most holy sisters, that this office which you have enjoined on me is beyond my strength, because while I am not at all [1104B] or but little fit to sing *the Canticle of the Lord,* far less fit shall I be to sing *the Canticle of the Lord of canticles.* For matter of this kind ought to belong to orators declaiming eloquently and wisely, whose office is to speak aptly for persuading, and whose end is to persuade by speech; but since there is in me neither wisdom nor eloquence, in one who lacks the office of speaking aptly, still less assuredly is the end of persuading by speech present. For I should then be writing to you profitably if I were saying the things I say or am about to say so aptly that I could persuade you to honesty of life and profit of soul. For my land is a strange land, resisting the spirit, serving the Lord of sin, and therefore I am not fit to sing in it *the Canticle of the Lord,* how much less [1104C] *the Canticles of canticles.* But as a certain one was, let it be permitted to one who fears to hope, so that I, who for the smallness of my understanding fear to pursue so sublime a matter, may yet hope through your prayers to be helped, out of regard for the Divinity, in carrying through the things I have begun.



## CHAPTER II FOLLOWS.

VERSE 7.-- *I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up nor make the beloved to awake, till she herself please.*

The beloved, therefore, delighted by the sleep of his beloved, under whose head, that is, her mind, he had placed his left hand, that is, temporal profit, but whom with his right, that is, with the love of eternal life, he had embraced, addresses the daughters of Jerusalem, that is, faithful souls, [1104D] in this manner, saying: *I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up nor make the beloved to awake, till she herself please.*

What is this sleep, so sweet and so wholesome, that for its sake such an adjuration of the beloved is made! Is it not that in which the beloved, resting in sweet and desirable quiet, had said above, *his left hand under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me?*

*97* For she had set all temporal things behind her, [1105A] but with that contemplative eye of the desiring soul she was intent on the eternal and unfailing goods, and in this desirable vision of the mind she was delightfully as it were sleeping. For a sleep of this kind cannot come about lightly in a mind already disengaged from all doings and laid down for itself in great quiet in the love of God. Whence the Lord, admonishing through the Psalmist: *Be free,* he says, *and see that I am God [n: (Psal. XLV)]*. These two words are inseparable in this purpose, because no one at all will be able with the eye of inmost contemplation to see the essence of the Godhead unless he has first been careful to be free from every exterior hindrance: having set *be free* first, then, he adds *see,* so as to intimate that one must first be free in mind from all thought of worldly things, so that thence [1105B] it may become possible, the eye being as it were closed upon earthly things, to sleep in the love of the Divinity with a keener and subtler vision.

But since the grace of this sleep does not come and remain equally, as it is established that one is raised up by a greater, another by a lesser power of contemplation (for Mary advanced further in the divine vision than Peter or John, but these further than any other saint): it is certain that all the saints have the holiness of this sleep in diverse manner, manifold because of the disparity of quiet, so that each in his own place, according to the grace of quiet conferred on him, has his special sleep in the contemplative life. Whence the prophet, in the person of every faithful one who sleeps in this manner, says: *I will take my rest, and consider [1105C] in my place [n: (Isa. XVIII)]*: as if he said openly: Because this is the order of sleep, that it is not permitted to sleep before having quiet, I will take my rest, that is, I will take away from me the causes of all earthly care, and then at last, sleeping in mind from these things, I will cleave to divine contemplation in the place of the grace specially conferred on me. While the beloved, then, sleeps in whatever manner or place, the daughters of Jerusalem are adjured by the beloved that they stir her not up nor make her awake, till she herself please.

But what are *the daughters of Jerusalem* but souls tending towards the vision of heavenly peace, yet, the infirmity of the mind still hindering [sic: *praepudiente*], less able to arrive?

And what is it of the beloved to help [cj: *adjuvare*; read *adjurare*, "to adjure"], but to bind by the decretal writ of his [1105D] precepts? Further, what are the precepts themselves, but, like roes and harts of the fields, lifting up to heavenly things and making eager and strong in good work, the doctrines of the Holy Scriptures.

Or by *the roes and the harts* we can understand the Fathers of the Old and New Testament, whose talents, virtues and pursuits are denoted by natural behaviour. For it is said that roes have four notable marks of nature: that when she has been wounded she is healed by tasting *98* dittany; that she discerns wholesome herbs by the keenness of her sight; that she ruminates; that she feeds in mountainous places and always seeks the higher. And this is the life and doctrine of the saints: to heal their excesses by humble [1106A] confession, to discern the better sentences of the Holy Scriptures by the keenness of their understanding, to take the food of the divine commandments by reading and to take it up again by committing it to a retentive memory, to rise up from virtues to virtues by daily progress.

In the hart also three commendable marks of nature are observed: namely, that swallowing a serpent, it drinks from a spring and puts off all the venom together with the hairs of its whole body; that, chased by dogs in the densest woods, it opens a way of flight for itself with its horns; that in changing pastures, forming a column, one setting its head on the back of another, they bear one another up because of the weight of their horns.

These things too it befits the faithful to do and to teach: that he who has been infected by some venom of sin should run back to Christ, the fountain [1106B] of mercy, by the infusion of whose kindness and grace he may be free both of that sin and of the other vices of the soul. If in his mind he is chased by malignant spirits, as by certain dogs, in the woods of worldly affairs, which are full of sins and vices, let him make himself a way of escaping them by a strong repulse of the spirit, or, having renounced all occupations of this kind, let him be careful to be free for God alone in the love of the contemplative life, which is also to open for oneself with the horns a way of salvation. For the horn, though it is born of the flesh, goes beyond the flesh; and he who cleaves to the contemplative life, though he be in the flesh, is yet raised up in mind outside the flesh. Let them be careful to bear one another's infirmities of manners or of bodies with equanimity, and to serve one another with dutiful charity; as [1106C] the Apostle says: *Bear one another's burdens and so you shall fulfil the law of Christ [n: (Gal. VI)]*.

*The daughters of Jerusalem,* then, are adjured by the bridegroom, that is, souls desiring to follow peace are bound, *by the roes and the harts of the fields,* that is, by the precepts and teachings of the holy Fathers, *lest* they stir up *or make to awake* the beloved, that is, lest they disquiet her from the purpose of this inmost quiet and sleep; *till she herself please,* that is, till that inmost indweller of hers inspires her, to whom she is bound by love, so that she may be willing at times to condescend to the infirmity of her neighbours and to provide for their necessary profits. For this willing lies in the disposition of him who provides all things, who by a hidden inspiration makes known to her [1106D] when it is useful and honourable to cleave to contemplation and when to provide for the necessities of neighbours *99* according to place and time.

Finally, the force of this admiration [cj: *admirationis*; read *adjurationis*, "adjuration"] is such, by which the sons of obedience are constrained, that they show their prelates the honour of reverence, because it is written: *Obey those set over you and be subject to them, for they watch as those who will render an account for your souls [n: (Hebr. XIII)]*. Hence it is that they do not dare to rouse them, as vicars of Christ in office, but as spouses [sic: *sponsus*] in the zeal of holy conversation, lest they disturb them, if they compel them by some shamelessness or importunity of their own to be torn away from the secret of inner quiet and more often to be occupied with outward things. The subjects themselves also ought to receive the word of this admiration [cj: *admirationis*; read *adjurationis*, "adjuration"] among one another [1107A], lest they disquiet the pious pursuits of the brethren, whether in reading, or in prayer, or in some spiritual office, so long as the inspiration of the Divinity holds them in it.

But the beloved, ever burning for the quiet of this contemplative sleep, may rejoice that she is confirmed and defended by such an adjuration of the beloved and as it were by right ought not to be disquieted, made more secure she proclaims: *The voice of my beloved.*

VERSE 8.-- *The voice of my beloved, behold he cometh leaping upon the mountains, skipping over the hills.*

*The voice,* she says, of the aforesaid admiration [cj: *admirationis*; read *adjurationis*, "adjuration"] is not the voice of some prophet, not of a lawgiver, not of a teacher, as of one announcing or teaching something of himself; but rather of the *beloved* himself, both through himself, and [1107B] through them establishing the decrees of his mysteries and commandments; because all Scripture is *the voice of my beloved,* uttered by the spirit of his mouth and written in our hearts by the finger of his divinity. And therefore you ought not to hold this voice of small account, daughters of Jerusalem, so as to rouse me for the sake of outward things and compel me to wake more often.

For this too is a certain kind of the voice of the beloved, a voice adjuring and as it were juridical: *Seek first the kingdom of God and his justice and all these things shall be added unto you [n: (Matth. VI)]*, so that this ought to be fair, the beloved himself dictating it, that for those who cleave to God the necessary use of the creature should consequently serve. The beloved himself plainly taught and confirmed it by his own testimony, when he extolled the partner of this my pursuit [1107C] with such praise, *Mary,* he said, *hath chosen the best part, which shall not be taken away from her [n: (Luc. X)]*. This surely is also *the voice of the beloved,* so that I ought rightly to set this pursuit of my sleep before the watchings of all affairs. But what is it that I heard and learned from this voice of my beloved, that is, from Holy Scripture, when through the quiet of the mind I slept in his contemplation? This, assuredly, which I will now tell you, O daughters of Jerusalem, of the power of my beloved himself. *Behold he cometh leaping upon* *100* *the mountains, skipping over the hills. Behold,* she says, *he cometh.* His coming is spoken of not according to the divinity but according to the humanity. For he who according to the divinity is uncircumscribed and not in a place [1107D], how should he be said to come, who, existing immovable and unchangeable, as he remains ever one and the same in himself, so that he is neither changed in place nor borne into diverse states by any variation. But as often as he shall be said to come into the hearts of the saints, this is not according to any movableness of his, but according to their aptitude, by which they apply themselves less or more to his love, that he will be alleged to come through his inspiration. According to the humanity, then, he came, when, made passible for us, he willed to suffer the local translation of human custom: for that he might become visible he came from heaven and assumed human nature, in which he suffered and died and rose again; and he ascended into heaven, all of which he did, to be moved and as it were to come from one thing to another. In this manner of coming, then, [1108A] *the beloved cometh, leaping upon the mountains,* when in the time of the dispensation he shone forth with many and magnificent leaps upon the mountains of the sublime virtues.

For certain mountains are the chief and eminent virtues, upon which this beloved made his leaps when he made certain progressions from virtue to virtue: for God, high above the heavens, came to earth humble by being born; from which mountain of humility he ascended to another mountain, of obedience, *for he was made obedient to God the Father unto death, but the death of the cross [n: (Philip. II)]*; by taking which upon him he made a leap onto the mountain of patience; from which at last he climbed onto the mountain of glory, where by rising again and ascending into heaven he set up the triumph of his victorious death.

[1108B] *This beloved, then, cometh leaping upon the mountains, skipping over the hills;* because whatever of wisdom and understanding, whatever of counsel and fortitude, whatever of knowledge and piety, whatever of divine fear, whatever finally of any honesty, justice and holiness ever could be or can be, shone forth more sublimely and more brightly in this beloved, as in the head, before all his members, that is, the saints, who are called hills. Anyone of understanding can see this in part in the books of the Gospels: how this beloved leaped upon the mountains, that is, upon the virtues, by word and example, skipping over the examples and teachings of all the saints by his singular and incomparable pre-eminence of working and doctrine; *he hath rejoiced [1108C] as a giant to run the way, because from the highest heaven is his going out, and his circuit even to the highest of it [n: (Psal. XVIII)]*.

*101* VERSE 9.-- *My beloved is like a roe, and to a young hart: lo, this one standeth behind our wall, looking through the windows, looking forth through the lattices.*



## CHAPTER II FOLLOWS. (cont.)

Hence he is said to be *like a roe and to a young hart:* for it is natural to the roe always to seek ascents; and while to the saints the distribution of graces is allotted in diverse manner: *Some indeed thus, and others thus,* to the only-begotten alone [sic: *sol*] *God giveth not the spirit by measure, because in him dwelleth all the fulness of the Godhead corporeally [n: (I Cor. VII; Joan. III; Coloss. II)]*. For the saints [1108D] in the pursuit of the virtues now ascend, now do not ascend, because in their merits that grace which they have now works, now rests, so that this may serve them for the keeping of humility, that they do not always have ready to hand the grace of the virtue conferred. But in this beloved the grace of the virtues abides not for a time, nor by intervals, but without intermission, as it is written of him that *the Holy Spirit descended in the form of a dove and remained upon him [n: (Luc. III; Joan. I)]*.

He is likened also to a young hart for swiftness, because, as the young hart, through the lightness of its youthful body, is much swifter than the hart, so this beloved, as it were the young one of the harts, that is, the son of the patriarchs, from whose stock he came forth, is far [1109A] more excellent and more agile in the efficacy of the virtues and the wisdom of his words than even the pursuits and teachings of the ancient Fathers themselves.

Since, then, this beloved by exhorting and by leaping on before calls all to the pursuits of the virtues, and some follow according to their power, some, their frailty standing in the way, despair of being able to follow, some, weighed down by the mass of their sins, distrust that they can rise again to them, [she] takes away from them every occasion of distrusting, holding out to the beloved the ineffable kindness of this beloved, mercifully inviting all to his grace, and adds: *Lo, he himself standeth behind our wall,* etc. There is one general wall, namely that of original sin; but this wall is spread out into many special walls [1109B] of actual sin. For from original sin, as from a certain mass, have come forth many and diverse actual sins, because after they fell through the original sin of the first parent, the kind of this infirmity is poured over into their posterity, namely to be always prone to sins. But this general wall began to be destroyed when the Son of God came through the flesh, by whose blood it was at length removed and scattered, when, *dissolving the enmities between God and man, he reconciled us to God [n: (Eph. II)]*; and lest it be able to be built again, it is destroyed daily in the Church through the grace of baptism.

But the special wall is set up in diverse men in diverse manner, because in one it is the vice of pride, in [1109C] another that of avarice, *102* in this one that of lust, in that one that of unchastity, in some one that of obedience [cj: *obedientiae*; read *inobedientiae*, "of disobedience"], in some other that of impatience, or whatever other vice, which according to the quality, and the manner and the number, of the vice itself makes a wall of whatever kind between man and God also [cj: *quoque*; read *quo*, "by as much as"] the vice is greater or more frequent or more numerous and in man, by so much the heavier and more immovable is the weight in the wall.

But behind this wall, whether the general one of original sin or the special one of actual sin, this beloved stands, because he is ready mercifully to receive whoever, some such wall being removed, have been zealous to draw near to him again by destroying it through penance. For to stand is the part of one who helps, to sit of one who judges. *He standeth behind the wall,* because while he disposes through his grace to come to us [1109D], the wall of sin standing in the way, he is as it were compelled to stand. For at first he stood long so as not to come to remove that wall of original sin, on account of many things, by hindering his coming, by many afflictions and adversities by which those were pursued who desired and awaited his coming, at whose coming they knew that this wall was to be destroyed through his blood and that they were to be reconciled to God.

He stood, then, for this cause and put off coming, *looking* meanwhile *through the windows, looking forth through the lattices.* For what were the windows in the wall but the prophets and other teachers of the divine law, by whose doctrine and preaching God might find as it were certain outlets for looking, through which he might pour out some [1110A] light of his grace, by which the human race, enlightened and admonished, might cease to delay the coming of this beloved, which was to profit its salvation, by the interposing of so great malice as of a certain wall

But the lattices, that is, the front part of the building, were the ancient fathers who went before, namely the patriarchs before the law announcing Christ, through whom he who was to come long after looked forth in part by signs and portents and certain teachings of reasons; admonishing that they should not hinder by the wall of their evils the future times of his so great salvation.

So long, then, did he stand behind our wall, looking forth mercifully and taking counsel by word, law, signs and prodigies, until at last, moved [sic: *per motus*] [1110B] by the affliction of his own, who cried out often: *Come, O Lord, and do not delay, loose the crimes of thy people,* the commotions of all lands being quelled under Caesar Augustus, and a most firm and most true peace being disposed by the ordinance of God, as though the wall of the resisting malice were now shaken, this beloved began to show by being born the face long desired, *103* and thereafter by preaching, dying, rising again, ascending into heaven, and so spreading the law of his Gospel through the whole world, to destroy daily through the grace of baptism the wall of original sin long set against him.

Further, the windows and lattices in the wall of actual sin, through which this beloved is daily looked out upon, [1110C] are the examples and teachings of the Gospels and of the apostles and of the other holy fathers; windows indeed there, where they pour the light of knowledge and understanding into our hearts, that we may be able to see how we may remove the wall of each sin set between us and God; but lattices in that place where, from their writings, through the power of contemplation, *we know in part and we prophesy in part [n: (I Cor. XIII)]* God looking upon us, so that while we attend to the glory or the punishment of the future life, we may labour daily to destroy the wall of sin that separates us from God.

Moreover. The Son of God, coming in the flesh, stood behind the wall of our mortality, looking forth upon us through the windows and lattices of his divinity; who [1110D] also shone out as God from the power of the miracles and yet lay hidden from the nature of human passibility. For he who looks forth through windows and lattices is neither wholly seen nor wholly not seen: so, surely, our Redeemer too, coming in the flesh, showed himself God by the miracles, but hid it by the sufferings. He stood, then, behind the wall of human passibility, because, hidden among men by his humanity, he did not come as God; but he looked forth through the windows and lattices, because by divine compassion he dictated to the whole human race the everlasting gifts of salvation. And because the beloved whom he drew inwardly by the regard of his mercy he instructed outwardly too by the word of preaching, he shows it by adding:

VERSE 10-- *Behold my beloved speaketh to me: Arise, make haste, my love, my dove, my beautiful [sic: formo sa] one, and come.* [1111A]

This speech of the beloved is distinguished in a threefold manner, by the beauty of the address, of the exhortation, and of the cause: of the address indeed, when he says: *my love, my dove, my beautiful one,* but of the exhortation when he says: *Arise, make haste, come;* of the cause when he says: *For winter is now past, the rain is over and gone, the flowers have appeared, the time of pruning is come.* For the beauty of the address is to say: Love, dove, beautiful one; the beauty of the exhortation, *arise, make haste, come;* the beauty of the cause, that the winter and the rain have gone back, that the flowers appear, that the time of pruning is here, that the voice of the turtle has been heard, that the fig tree has put forth her green figs, that the flowering vines have given their fragrance.

[1111B] *104* But the order in this address, exhortation and cause is not disposed without consideration. In the address indeed, when first he says love, then dove, lastly beautiful one; but in the exhortation first *arise,* then *make haste,* lastly *come.* Further, in the cause, in the first place *winter is past,* in the second *the rain is gone,* in the third *the flowers have appeared,* in the fourth *the time of pruning is come,* in the fifth *the voice of the turtle is heard in our land,* in the sixth *the fig tree hath put forth her green figs,* in the seventh *the flowering vines have given their fragrance.* The cause of this exhortation, by which the beloved is admonished to arise, to make haste, to come, is the going back of the winter and the rain, the appearing of the flowers, and the time of pruning; the voice of the turtle and the fig trees putting forth their green figs, and the flowering vines.

[1111C]But it must be seen whence this speech proceeds: she had said above, *the voice of my beloved,* which voice we said is Holy Scripture, for the whole divine page is the voice of God, through which by reading the beloved the leaps of him clothed with the wall of our mortality, and speaking to her through the windows and lattices of miracles and doctrines; for as often as we see or read the Holy Scriptures, God speaks to us or with us. In this manner, then, the beloved, that is, the Church or any faithful soul, perceives the beloved speaking to her, when she hears, reads and understands the order of the divine dispensation, the religion of the sacraments, the profit of the divine precepts.

But the beloved himself, as it were solicitous out of the salvation of his beloved, [1111D] in a speech of this kind takes on [sic: *assumunt*] himself the office of a good orator, so as to draw the hearer in three ways, that is, so as to make her attentive, teachable, well-disposed: for while he calls out not once but three times, saying, *arise, make haste, come,* as if he said, Offer thyself not sluggishly but swiftly and zealously, he makes her attentive, as one about to hear something settled and unwonted; but when he often addresses her with a word of love and of grace, saying: *My love, my dove, my beautiful one,* he renders her well-disposed, so that she may bring forth a greater will to prepare herself and to hear.

Further, he teaches him the cause of this calling distinctly by number, saying: *For winter is now past, the rain is over and gone, the flowers have appeared, the time of pruning is come, the voice of the turtle is heard, [1112A] the flowering vines have given their fragrance,* he makes her teachable, so that she may recognize the time of great salvation and grace, which she ought to receive with great veneration and joy.

And this is the sign of great kindness, by which this beloved often looks toward us through the *windows* and *lattices* of his infinite clemency, *standing behind the wall* of our evils, *set against us as a cloud, that prayer may not pass through [n: (Thren. III)]*, speaking daily and admonishing us to scatter this wall or this cloud with the battering-ram of the virtues and the breath of the Holy Spirit.

What, then, does the beloved speak to the beloved? *Arise,* he says, *make haste, my love* *105* *my dove, my beautiful one, and come:* arise through faith, make haste through hope, [1112B] come through charity: or, arise through intention, make haste through working, come through contemplation: for this is the perfection of thy whole worship and religion, to believe, to hope, to love: for the other three, namely intention, working, contemplation, are constituted from these. For if thou believest rightly, thou also aimest rightly at doing good things; but thou workest for the hope of obtaining the reward, and thou contemplatest the more subtly, the more thou art raised up to God through charity.

And this thou wilt be able to do if thou be a love, that is, if, the error of unbelief being cast away, to be reconciled to me through faith; if thou be a dove, that is, if thou live simply, harm no one and be content with thine own alone, in the hope of the future reward; or if, some frailty [1112C] compelling thee, thou go out of the ark, that is, out of the Church, by doing ill, thou remain not in the world, but return quickly bearing an olive branch in thy mouth, that is, having hope of mercy through confession and the measure of penance.

If thou be beautiful, that is, keeping by the love of God alone, thou set behind thee all the cares of the world, or whatever things can infect the beauty of thy mind, so that, having not the bleared eyes of Lia but the beautiful and fair face of Rachel, thou mayest be able with the eye of the mind purged to gaze into that majesty of heavenly beatitude which is God.

VERSE 11, 12.-- *For winter is now past, the rain is over and gone.*

[1112D] *The flowers have appeared in our land, the time of pruning is come: the voice of the turtle is heard in our land.*



## CHAPTER II FOLLOWS. (cont.)

But why do I so greatly invite thee [sic: *se*] to these things? Because the cold of unbelief and the storm of blindness and ignorance, which had overcast the whole face of the world, have gone back; and *the flowers have appeared,* that is, the beginnings of good works have now begun to spring up, and *the time of pruning is come,* that is, the counsel and rule of cutting away vices and sins are now had in the time of grace. *The voice* also *of the turtle is heard,* that is, the sound of preaching, from which resound the counsels, precepts and examples of chastity, of solitary conversation, of heavenly speculation and of penance, all of which the turtle expresses, chaste and moaning, a lover of solitude and [1113A] nesting on high. For in the very beginning of the Gospel voice the turtle cries out by words and by manners, whether the Lord Jesus Christ himself, the son of virginity, or his mother herself, the blessed Virgin Mary, or his forerunner, blessed John the Baptist. Finally, the Lord himself, born of the Virgin, is the herald of chastity; led into the desert, he consecrates solitude; praying on the mountain, he admonishes that contemplation be pursued; he exhorts to penance, proclaiming as with a moaning voice: *106* *Do penance, for the kingdom of heaven is at hand [n: (Matth. X)]*. The blessed Mother of God also is a singular mirror of chastity, who, solitary [sic: *solidaria*] and set in divine contemplation, is greeted by the angel and taught beforehand that she is to be the mother of Christ, and soon announces the sentence of divine punishment changed by penance [1113B], when she proclaims that *his mercy in generation and generations* is to be [n: (Luc. II)]. But Saint John the Baptist, the son of chastity, a virgin in mind and body, the more he was removed from men, the nearer he stood to God through contemplation; *for he was in [sic: iu] the desert preaching the baptism of penance [n: (Matth. III)]*, and crying with the moaning voice of the turtle: *Bring forth fruits worthy of penance [n: (ibid.)]*.

VERSE 13.-- *The fig tree hath put forth her green figs, the flowering vines have given their fragrance. Arise, make haste, my love, my fair one, and come.* [var: Vulg. Ct 2:13 *Surge, amica mea*, without *propera*, "make haste"]

*The fig tree,* he says, *hath put forth her green figs,* that is, Judaea, hitherto barren, carnal, has begun to understand the ceremonies [1113C] and, by believing *with the heart unto justice, but* by confessing *with the mouth unto salvation [n: (Rom. X)]*, what it savoured carnally, this it now savours spiritually and longs to show by faith and by work.

*The flowering vines also have given their fragrance,* because the gentiles, having heard the word of faith, now begin to spread everywhere the fragrance of good work.

Not without reason, then, is the order of this speech attended to, in which these three, faith, hope and charity; or the other three, intention, working, contemplation, are considered, since these are the consequences of those: that he who believes should hope and love; and no one can hope and love except he who has believed; no one also works unless he first intends what, and how, and when, and why he works; but the subtlety of contemplating [1113D] no one can attain unless he has advanced to it by the sublimity of good works.

But it will not be, as we believe, unfitting to note the sevenfold number of the cause, so that we may show that the effect of the cause itself was always the condescension and good pleasure of the sevenfold Spirit: For the passing of the winter, that is, of unbelief; the passing of the rain, that is, of persecution; the appearing of the flowers, that is, of good works; the cutting away of vices; the proclaiming of the turtle, that is, of the Gospel; the putting forth of the green figs of the fig tree, that is, the revealing of the legal observances into the truth of the mysteries; the sweetly fragrant working of the vines, that is, of the gentiles converted to the faith: what are they but the good pleasure and condescension of the sevenfold Spirit?

For causes of this kind, and what was effected from them, [1114A] or is effected, causes, that is, to believe, to hope, to love, the Spirit of God worked from the beginning and works still daily; because all removal of unbelief and malice, and the bestowal of faith and salvation, stand by the gift of him who provides, composes and orders every *107* state of equity, honesty and profit in holy Church. Whence also the bridegroom, when he speaks to the bride: *The voice of the turtle is heard,* adds, *in our land,* namely so as to intimate that there is no effect of the sacraments, no hope of salvation, outside the Catholic Church: but that every voice of the Gospel preaching, in the observance of the precepts, in the order and reverence of the sacraments, is to be held within holy Church, in which the Holy Spirit himself is given and received. For *in the beginning,* as it is written, [1114B] *the earth was void and empty,* because before the human mind was made fruitful by the divine spirit, it was composed with no flower or bud of the virtues; but after *the spirit of God moved over the waters [n: (Gen. I)]*, that is, over the flowing and unstable hearts of men, while at length the light of truth and the fruit of justice is poured out through the whole world, so that that earth, at first void and empty, became the Church of God, in which he himself might dwell through his grace, and might make it his land, now flowering and fruitful, and showing forth a certain living and rational pattern of honesty and discipline, as it were of a man, through the indwelling grace.

Since, therefore, the beloved has made the beloved solicitous for her own salvation, he admonishes her not to be negligent [1114C] of the salvation of her neighbours too, so as to intimate this charity, which provides for neighbours as for itself with the zeal of kindness, so that she may deservedly be honoured with the address of the grace that calls and justifies, *love, dove, beautiful one.*

For it follows, when the beloved, the former number of the address being repeated, [says] *arise, make haste, my love, my fair one, and come.* Because, he says, thou hast advanced by my grace to this, that thou art able to take care of thyself, by despising the world and by cleaving to my love through the pursuit of the contemplative life, thou oughtest also at times to profit thy neighbours by word and example through the labour of the active life; because, since I have made thee such that thou hast the grace of teaching and of working, not without peril to thyself wilt thou render account for the buried talent. But arise at some time to this labour [1114D] from that most sweet sleep of thy quiet, for the sake of a brother's need, and make haste by teaching and by drawing back thy neighbours from the love of this world, that thou mayest lead them too with thee into that solitary dwelling of thy quiet. And this thou shalt do as my love through faith, my fair one through working, my dove through the infusion of the Holy Spirit.

VERSE 14.-- *My dove in the clefts of the rock, in the hollow of the stone wall, show me thy face; let thy voice sound in my ears, for thy voice is sweet and thy face comely.*

Because I have compared thee to a dove *108.* But the *dove* has her refuges *in the clefts of the rock,* in *the hollow of the stone wall;* what also will be thy refuges [1115A], in which thou mayest set all the hope of thy salvation, so as to flee from the manifold temptation of this world? surely *in the clefts of the rock,* of which it is written: *And the rock was Christ [n: (I Cor. X)]*; for these clefts are the wounds of my passion, which are to every Christian as it were certain refuges in all adversities. For my five wounds are the safe refuges of thy five senses, so that when thou hast held out the sign of my passion in this number, thou mayest be able to persist secure in the integrity of all thy senses. For this integrity of the senses will be necessary to thee as thou teachest and workest, because if thou bring forth the things thou teachest and workest in my name with honesty and profit, reasonably, orderly and moderately, thou wilt be able the more to draw the minds of those who hear or look on [1115B]. For generally an honourable kind of case, if it is brought forth unworthily, gives offence, just as a lowly kind of case too, if it is at times brought forth reasonably, will obtain a share of honour.

In my passion, then, thou hast been made love, fair one, dove, because to this end I laid down my life for thee, I shed my blood, *that* I might present thee to myself a bride *glorious, not having spot, nor wrinkle [n: (Ephes. V)]*. For when, foul of it through thyself, thou didst not suffice for thyself, nor could the whole creation suffice for thee, my passion, to which the whole world is not equal, made satisfaction to God for thee, so that thence, reconciled and joined to me, thou mayest be called *love, fair one, dove.*

[1115C]Or the clefts of the rock are the four Gospels, in which my deeds and words are looked into; or else the pursuits of the virtues, namely of humility, patience, obedience, prayers, vigils, holy readings, and the rest, which are as it were certain approaches for coming to me and for entering the kingdoms of heaven, of all of which thou oughtest to preach to thy neighbours and to show them, because without these they cannot attain salvation.

*My dove in the clefts of the rock, in the hollow of the stone wall.* Christ the rock [n: (I Cor. X)], Christ also the stone wall. The stone wall indeed before the passion, because he was still frail and passible from human infirmity; but the rock after the resurrection, when, all corruption laid aside, he was made impassible from the firmness of his glorified [1115D] body. But the beloved ought to be a *dove in the clefts of the rock, in the hollow of the stone wall,* because while she considers the many marks of powers, of works and of virtues which were in Christ from the strength of the divinity, yet she ought specially to consider that mark of charity which he showed in his flesh, which he delivered to death for her salvation: for he showed the judgment of this great love towards man *109* when for his redeeming he was able to offer nothing other than himself.

The hollow of the stone wall, then, is the charity of Christ dying in the flesh, in which charity, of the Church indeed generally, but specially any holy soul is a dove, because through charity she finds a place of abiding in Christ, where spiritually she may nourish the young of good [1116A] works and escape the snares of the ancient serpent: for it is written that *God is charity, and he who abideth in charity abideth in God, and God in him [n: (I Joan. IV)]*.

The *dove* is also *in the clefts of the rock, in the hollow of the stone wall.* But the dove has a moaning instead of a song; and the faithful soul ought to moan when she recalls the passion of the Redeemer, weighing what God has done for her, when he *who did no sin, neither was guile found in his mouth [n: (I Petr. II)]*, willed to die for sinners, and what man owed, God made man paid. Let her moan also for her excesses, *in the clefts of the rock, in the hollow of the stone wall,* because she has *an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the just: and he is the propitiation for* her *sins* [1116B] [n: (I Joan. II)], who daily confers salvation on every penitent by the sacrament of his passion and the singular example of this charity. For daily our Redeemer renews his clefts for us, that is, the signs of his passion, in the immolation of his body and blood; and he shows the hollow in which we may be protected from the snares of the enemies in the assigning of so great a charity.

The stone wall can also be taken as the doctrine of the apostles, or of apostolic men, because as a stone wall is built of many stones, so the doctrine of the saints is constituted of very many teachings of the virtues. In it there is for every faithful soul a hollow, that is, a keeping of life, because from their sayings and writings she learns how, by fleeing sins and by nourishing the young of good works [1116C], she may escape the snares of malignant spirits.

The beloved, then, is admonished at times to leave the quiet of contemplation and to go out to the labour of the active life for the sake of her neighbours, so that what she herself learned through quiet from the divine precepts and the examples of the saints, this she may bring into the minds of her neighbours by preaching; and as she herself by the sweetness of these was affected as to the assiduity of contemplation, so also she may affect the minds of her neighbours with desire of them through the power of preaching.



## CHAPTER II FOLLOWS. (cont.)

Whence the beloved too, admonishing her, adds: *Show me thy face.* For what is the face of the beloved but a certain image of holiness shining out from fellowship with the beloved? For the more sedulously each one [1116D] cleaves to divine contemplation, the more beautiful the face of the mind shines out from the radiance of holiness. Hence it is that Moses, having conversed forty days with the Lord on the mountain, *100* is written to have brought forth a shining face from fellowship with the speech of the Lord [n: (Exod. XXXIV)]. For he who has tarried on the mountain of heavenly contemplation brings forth a certain face of holiness from the very Spirit of God, with whom he is one; whence the Apostle: *With open,* he says, *face beholding the glory of the Lord, we are transformed into the same image, from glory to glory, as by the Spirit of the Lord [n: (II Cor. III)]*. Those, then, who are led by the Spirit of God are transformed by the Spirit himself into the image of Christ according to the inner man, from glory to glory, so that those who have drawn from him something of glory into the face of the inner man [1117A], by which they may know him more clearly, the nearer they draw to him by contemplative glory, the more glory they may gather to themselves from him, through which they may know him ever more clearly and more truly. Whence also Ezechiel, when he described the vision of the holy living creatures, said: *The likeness of a man in them [n: (Ezech. I)]*. For whatever of justice and meekness, whatever of modesty and humility, whatever of patience and piety, whatever finally of holiness the elect of God ever could have or can have, they drew from that singular man, the Lord Jesus Christ; and, illumined by that principal face of holiness, they too conceived in their own manner a certain face of holiness.

Such, says the beloved, *thy face*, drawn out spiritually from my grace, *O beloved, show me,* that [1117B] is, go forth in public to the praise of my name with a fair appearance of action and speech, so that thy neighbours, seeing *thy works, may glorify* thy *Father who is in heaven [n: (Matth. V)]*, and themselves, from the rightly composed countenance of thy action and speech, may compose the countenance of their own mind and intention too.

*Let thy voice sound in my ears*: the voice of one preaching sounds in the ears of the Lord then, when it accords with my voice, so that what sounds outwardly to the ears of man through the voice, this may speak in the ears of God through faith. And, having set first, *show me thy face,* he fittingly adds, *let thy voice sound in my ears,* to show that *with the heart it is believed unto justice, but with the mouth confession is unto salvation [n: (Rom. X)]*; for by the face [1117C] the knowledge of faith, but by the voice the sound of preaching [can] be expressed.

Further: *Show,* he says, *me thy face, let thy voice sound in my ears,* that is, go forth in public in action and preaching from that angelic dormitory of thy holy solitude with such an intention that thou admit me alone as witness of thy conscience, not so as to expose thyself to human favours, but so as to please my sight alone. *For thy voice is sweet, and thy face comely.* For this reason thou oughtest to go forth, because thy neighbours can attend to this in the beauty of thy manners and the sweetness of thy speeches, whence they may both correct the depravity of their own manners and take hold of the integrity of right faith.

*111* But often also the very showing of the countenance and bearing [1117D] is a certain voice of the mind, and as a certain one says: *Often the countenance, though silent, has a voice and words.*


Whence of her who showed the sadness of her mind by the change of her countenance, after the reproach of barrenness was taken away, it is written that: *Her countenance was no more changed to divers ways [n: (I Reg. I)]*. For the diversity of the countenance showed the diversity of the soul; but the cause of this diversity being taken away, a right and composed countenance too spoke a right and composed soul. But this voice of honesty, set in the countenance and bearing of the outward man, sounds in the ears of God then, when one keeps it unchangeably for meriting the glory of eternal life alone. *Thy voice,* he says, *is sweet and thy face comely: thy voice,* he says, which is the interpreter of the soul and speaks either by manners or by tongue, [1118A] for it preaches and teaches, whether by doing well or by speaking well; this *voice,* he says, which *is sweet, let it sound in my ears,* because that life pleases me greatly which both does and teaches good things, the love of God and of neighbour so acting in it that it both pleases God and edifies the neighbour. *And thy face comely,* because it brings this to pass from thy intention and faith, both of which are known to me alone, that what thou doest or sayest may be worthy of the praise of my approval. With this comeliness, then, and thy beauty set out, proceed prosperously and reign, so that, since thou art joined to me by such a dowry of virtues, thou mayest not unfittingly be called Queen of thy bridegroom who is King, since, with me ruling and instructing, thou knowest both to rule thyself reasonably and to bestow on others the rule of souls.

[1118B]But because heretics often deprave this sweetness and beauty of right faith and right preaching by perverse doctrine, so as to pervert the hearts of the simple, the voice of the beloved makes an apostrophe to the masters of this very faith and preaching when it adds, *catch us the little [sic: purvulas] foxes.*

VERSE 15.-- *Catch us the little foxes that destroy the vines; for our vineyard hath flourished.*

The foxes are the heretics, because of the craftiness and cunning with which they deceive the unwary by the arguments of words; who always seek hiding places in which to teach, and think those things perversely, making conventicles apart, lest the holy and Catholic Church detect their depravity. And they are called little foxes, [1118C] whether because of the feigned humility of the heretics themselves, or because of the subtlety of their most keenly sought-out sentences, by which they force chiefly the minds of those who marvel but understand less to favour their depraved doctrines. *Catch,* he says, *us;* for the heretics are then caught for God when by Catholic doctors, through the assertion of right reason, which they can destroy by no arguments, divine grace going before, they are led back to the bosom of their mother, holy Church, from *112* which they had departed by erring, and confirm by words and examples the faith which before they attacked. *Catch,* he says, *us the little foxes,* as if he said in other words: Convict the subtle and crafty corrupters of right faith by their own sentences, and by probable reason, which they cannot refute at all, compel them to pass over into [1118D] the fellowship [sic: *conturbernium*] of our party: *For I will catch the wise in their own craftiness, and I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the prudence of the prudent I will reject [n: (I Cor. I, III)]*; so that when they have recognized the reason of living or teaching rightly, their own wisdom may displease them, and what they strove before to build up by it they may destroy when the truth is recognized, and may become one with my faithful.

*For our vineyard hath flourished,* that is, either the universal Church or a particular one, or some faithful individual and single person, has taken hold of the beginning of living or believing rightly; and therefore they are to be removed or caught, if any have arisen among them not living or believing rightly, lest they suffocate the tender faith, the works of piety, both by depraved dogma and by depraved examples.

[1119A]Nor is it absurd, as we believe, if we call each faithful soul the vineyard of the Lord, as we do the universal or particular Church, because of the like propagation of the virtues, and the spiritual *wine* which *maketh glad the heart of man [n: (Psal. CIII)]*, which can abound in each faithful individual through the grace of the Holy Spirit, as also in the universal or particular Church. For each one ought with the knife of spiritual exercise to cut back the sacraments [cj: *sacramenta*; read *sarmenta*, "the shoots"] of the vices, to turn over the earth of his heart with the hoe of inmost consideration, and if he has admitted anything earthly or sordid in thought or in deed, to lay it about the root of the heart by keeping it in memory, and to water it by repenting with frequent fountains of tears; to bind the whole intention of his good purpose to Christ, who is the tree of life, [1119B] lest it flow down again to earthly things, and to hope ever in his help and support; or to cleave to the tree of the cross of Christ, bearing about his mortification in his body; and in such a manner, the mind, assiduously tilled and tended by the grace of the Holy Spirit, becomes ever more fruitful, and is called, according to the prophet, the vineyard of Sorec, which sounds "chosen and fair." Whence the Lord's sentence in the Gospel concerning the barren vine and the vine bearing fruit, where he calls himself the vine, and the disciples the branch, but the Father the husbandman: *Every branch in me,* he says, *that beareth not fruit, he will take it away,* the Father, *and every one that beareth fruit he will purge it, that it may bring forth more fruit [n: (Joan. XV)]*. If, then, the Saviour is the vine and we the branches, and God the Father the husbandman, so long as we keep the precepts of God we always [1119C] cultivate, and our tillage is a purging, that we may bring forth more fruit: *For it is not of him that willeth, nor of him that runneth, but of God that showeth mercy [n: (Rom. IX)]*, whose help we always need, that we may arrive at the end of our good purpose.

* 113* VERSE 16, 17.-- *My beloved to me, and I to him, who feedeth among the lilies, till the day break, and the shadows retire.*

Divine love and human love are not constituted in a like manner nor on an equal ground; and though it is said equivocally, yet by the ground of definition it is very diverse, since between affections of this kind, of the flesh and of the spirit, there is no comparison at all. For divine love always has a certain relative, but human [1119D] love not always, because often one who loves is not loved, nor is the consequence the same on both sides. For it follows that a man loving God is loved by God, since to love God is nothing other than to live justly and piously, and it is consequent that God, just and pious, loves the just and pious, when such an affection comes from the gift of his grace: but it does not follow that a man loving is always loved by a man, because, though often, yet not always does carnal affection work this relative; but the affection of the Divinity always works it, according to the sentence of the Saviour, who says: *he that loveth me shall be loved by my Father, and I will love him and will manifest myself to him [n: (Joan. XIV)]*. For the relative of this love is converted, so that because he loves he is loved, [1120A] and because he is loved he loves, the prevenient grace working this in the hearts of the elect, so that they may see by divine love, which also loves its own gifts in each one. Whence here the beloved speaks of her beloved: *my beloved to me and I to him.* For it is proper to relatives to be said reciprocally; for what is predicated of him who is beloved is conversely predicated of her who is beloved: for a *dilectus* is someone's *dilectus*, and a *dilecta* someone's *dilecta*. This relation, then, comes about in this manner, that it is said: *dilectus dilectae*, *dilectus et dilecta*, *dilecti dilecta*: let her say, then, *my beloved to me and I to him;* as if she said: Because the beloved has conferred his grace on me to this end, that I may please him by right faith and working, therefore he is beloved to me; and because, since he has given me free will, I obey the calling grace by keeping his precepts, therefore I am beloved to him: [1120B] for unless he loved me out of mercy, I could not love him out of grace, he would not love me out of mercy. For whatever good lies near me, to will, to be able to do, to perfect, I have wholly from his gift [n: (Rom. IV)].

*Who feedeth among the lilies.* Behold the cause why I am beloved to him: for he feeds, that is, he delights in whiteness, that is, in the cleanness and chastity of mind and body, and in the most sweet fragrance of the virtues; which, since through his grace they breathe out from my love, therefore he has made me worthy of his love.



## CHAPTER II FOLLOWS. (cont.)

*Till the day breathe, and the shadows decline,* that is, until, that true and eternal day of future beatitude growing bright, and the night of this worldly misery and tribulation withdrawing *114.*

[1120C]When *God shall be all in all [n: (I Cor. XV)]* he will give me the full and perfect and unchangeable reward of his vision, where he in me with the beauty of his gifts and I in him with the eternal incorruption of body and soul shall be fed.

But because this mutual love which we have set down always befits any father and son, this is proper to those things which are said relatively, that the very thing that they are, they are said [sic: *dicautur*] to be of others, or in whatever way something to something else. For whatever is recognized in the nature of relation, it is necessary that it be considered together with another: for when father is said, it is necessary that there be a son whose father he is said to be, and in the same way, by conversion, it must be said of the son. If, then, the saints are sons of God, [1120D] through love and the observance of his commandments, it is necessary that God himself be called their father by grace, by creation and by governance, and conversely, if God is father, they are sons. Whence here too it is not absurd to say *my beloved to me* in respect of the father, *and I to him* in respect of the son or daughter; for God himself says in a certain place of his own: I will be to them for a father, and they shall be to me for sons and daughters [n: (II Reg. VII)].

Or let us say it in another way; it seems to have almost the like sense of relation, so that when it is said *my beloved to me,* there is understood: is a helper and protector; *and I to him,* there is understood: am obedient. These too are brought together with each other, so that because he is a helper and protector to me, therefore I am obedient to him, and because I am obedient to him, he bestows his help upon me.

[1121A] *Who feedeth among the lilies,* because he delights in the whiteness of virtues and the cleanness of thoughts and of works, as he says in a certain place: *And I am present to clean thoughts [n: (Prov. VIII)]*; and as it is written: *Blessed are the clean of heart, for they shall see God [n: (Matth. V)]*, so long does he work the effect of his grace in me, until the day of understanding and of spiritual knowledge shine forth, the darkness of ignorance, or of carnal understanding, having been put to flight. For the more the mind is joined to God in advances of the virtues, the higher it is lifted up in the subtlety of divine contemplation.

Or *until the day is,* that is, the light and splendour of good works which has shone forth in my mind through the indwelling of divine grace, may shine again upon the hearts of others by word and example, and remove from them every shadow or [1121B] night of depravity and malice: for the fruit of the light is in all goodness and justice and truth.

*Return: be thou like, my beloved, to a roe, and to a young hart upon the mountains of Bethel.* [var: Vulg. Ct 2:17 *super montes Bether*, "upon the mountains of Bether"]

While the beloved was feeding among the lilies of the soul, shining white and fragrant with virtues, he so refreshes that soul with the sweetness of his charity that, despising earthly things, she was wholly suspended in heavenly things, and yet she could not comprehend the uncircumscribed divinity *115* as it is. Whence, when with the whole effort of her mind she pursues the beloved, who exceeds all sense and as it were in a certain manner flees, because he is incomprehensible and ineffable, she is beaten back by the radiance of so immense a [1121C] brightness, and does not judge that he is to be pursued further, whom she considers can in no way be comprehended. For to this it is written of the holy living creatures that, when a voice came above the firmament, they stood and let down their wings [n: (Ezech. I)]. By the name of firmament, indeed, the supernal powers are designated, which, while other companies of angels were falling from heaven, themselves stood firm in the love of God. But the voice in the mind is a certain sound of understanding; but one voice comes to be beneath the firmament, another from the firmament, and another above the firmament.
