For as holy minds lift themselves up from earthly things to things above, a voice comes to be beneath the firmament when they understand subtly some things of the nature, order, quality of visible things; but a voice comes to be from the firmament when they weigh the ineffable and eternal joys of those supernal [1121D] spirits; but a voice comes to be above the firmament when, in contemplating, they think something of the loftiness of the Divinity, of which, because they can comprehend nothing fully as it is, standing and not able to advance further, they humbly lay down the wings of contemplation, and they are astounded at him whom they understand to be incomprehensible; being astounded, they venerate and are afraid; being afraid, they love; loving, they at some time behold him as he is.

Because, therefore, in the contemplation of the Divinity she who is beloved fails, and cannot long follow him who is beloved as though already flying away, she calls him back and implores that at least he deign in some manner to reveal the sacraments of his humanity, [1122A] saying: *Return, my beloved, be thou like to a roe or to a young hart upon the mountains of Bethel;* as if she said in other words: Since I am not able perfectly to grasp the sublimity of thy divinity, *for we see now through a glass in a dark manner [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*, at least grant me to search out and to understand the truth of thy humanity, the reason of the truth, the utility of the reason; the truth in faith, the reason in the cause, the utility in the end of the cause. For we believe that thou wast made man for this reason, that thou mightest save the human race, whose end is eternal life.

But in the action of a cause of this kind thou wast like to a roe or to a young hart, because, born of the stock of the patriarchs, like a young hart of servants thou didst swiftly dispense the order of human salvation; for [1122B] after thou didst begin to preach, in three years and a half thou didst accomplish the universal good of so celebrated and wonderful a business, and having suffered thou didst rise quickly after three days; and after forty days thou didst ascend the heavens, and such an outrunning and return was as of a roe or of a young hart leaping on the mountains.

*116* But this outrunning or return of thine was upon the mountains of Bethel, that is, of the house of God, which is the Church, whose advance and institution was being carried on in this outrunning and return; upon whose mountains, that is, the apostles and the other holy preachers, like a swift young hart thou didst run together by the ready gift of thy grace, when thou didst teach them by the sudden coming of the Holy Spirit, so that what others can scarcely grasp through long spaces of time, these attained in one moment. [1122C] For by what could thy leap upon these mountains be swifter than to give in one hour the knowledge of all tongues.

Grant, then, that I may understand the sacraments of all these things, that understanding I may be able to meditate on them, that I may not draw back from them in faith and work, in faith by believing, in work by imitating according to my power. For thou art like to a roe or to a young hart in my heart when I turn over these sacraments of thy dispensation, namely how, to institute these things, thou didst run as by certain leaps from heaven into the womb of the virgin, from the womb onto the cross, from the cross into the sepulchre, from the sepulchre again into heaven. But this likeness appears to me *upon the mountains of Bethel,* that is, in the Scriptures which holy Church holds [1122D] and venerates, which Scriptures, namely, are mountains on account of the loftiness of the senses which are contained in them, from the utilities of the divine precepts or from the sacraments and the realities of the sacraments.

But because the whole force of this love between him who is beloved and her who is beloved hangs on faith in the sacraments and the observance of the divine commandments, having let go that intention of seeking by which she wished to comprehend God in his nature, but could by no means, she began to attend to how she might comprehend God through faith and justice. For if, according to the voice of John, *God is charity [n: (I Joan. IV)]*, and to have faith and justice is to love God, assuredly to have this charity is to have God.

[1123A]But what do we call this charity, except all that good which can be conceived in the mind, believed in the heart, confessed with the mouth, exercised in work? truly no one attains all this good except him who perfectly loves God, and no one loves God except him who attains this good, as was set down a little above, *my beloved to me and I to him.*

For this good is a certain way, which leads to better things to be comprehended, of which life the term and close is eternal life. But since there is no small difficulty in finding a good of this kind, while there are very few who have the perfect charity of God, the beloved adds, relating the order of the seeking and the finding of this, which does not come about easily:



## CHAPTER THREE.

*[1123B] 117* VERSE 1.-- *In my bed by nights I sought him whom my soul loveth; I sought him, and found him not.*

By the name of the bed the rest and tranquillity of the mind can be designated; because while through thought we rest from worldly din, we can investigate more subtly the quality and manner of the good to be sought, the end of which good to understand is to find God: but we call it the end, not that by which it ceases to be, but that by which it perfectly begins to be. *In* her *bed,* then, the beloved seeks the beloved, when in the rest of her mind she desires to understand the right rule of goodness and of discipline and of knowledge.

But she seeks by nights, because in the consideration of subtle things the eye of the mind is darkened, so that it does not at once prevail by [1123C] itself to see what it seeks. Whence she by no means finds the beloved whom she seeks, because the very difficulty of understanding makes a certain delay of finding.

VERSE 2.-- *I will rise, and will go about the city: in the streets and the broad ways I will seek him whom my soul loveth: I sought him, and found him not.*

*118* What then is to be done? assuredly, that from that quiet of mind in which she lies alone with her own sense, she rise to study, searching out also the senses, actions and writings of others, so that what she does not prevail [sic: *praevale*] to understand by herself, she may perceive from their sayings and writings.

For this is to go about the city, and to seek the beloved through the streets and the broad ways: to run through the Church and [1123D] to search out the sentences of the holy doctors, to investigate those who walk by the broad and by the narrow, and to consider whether there be in them any traces of holiness, because sometimes secular men have something of good work which is to be imitated, and also to be imitated according to one's power. For the broad way is that of secular men, the narrow that of the saints, because many are they who walk by the broad, but few who walk [sic: *gradiuntu*] by the narrow way [n: (Matth. VII)].

VERSE 3.-- *The watchmen who keep the city found me: Have you seen him whom my soul loveth?*

But the watchmen find her going about the city and the streets and the broad ways, because to the studious mind searching into the documents of the Church, looking into history also, which is as it were the broad way, because it is open and easy to understand, and allegory also, which is as it were the narrow way, because it is [1124A] subtle and difficult to understand, and searching out whether there be in them any things useful and necessary to faith and to working, the holy doctors come to meet her with their sentences and authorities, and show the beloved whom she was seeking to her as she understands and desires to imitate. For they are called watchmen of whom the Lord says in the Gospel: *Blessed are those servants whom, when the Lord shall come, he shall find watching [n: (Luc. XXII)]*. For the holy doctors, set in the body, were watching, and still watch by doctrine and working for their own profit and that of their neighbours; but stripped of the body they watch and will watch by writings and examples, for their own reward indeed, but for the salvation of their neighbours. These watchmen, namely, keep the city, because the holy doctors confirm and protect the holy universal Church by the reasons and authorities of their testimonies and [1124B] examples, and make it impregnable to the enemies of the holy faith.

Such watchmen, then, the beloved, hesitating, questions [sic: *suscitatur*], saying: *Have you seen him whom my soul loveth?* Can you, she says, show and prove in your writings and deeds that you so loved God that assuredly to imitate your life and doctrine is to find the beloved?

VERSE 4.-- *When I had a little passed by them, I found him whom my soul loveth; I held him, and I will not let him go, till I bring him into my mother's house, and into the chamber of her that bore me.*

*119* But because perseverance of the search does not put off the effect of the finding, she adds: *When I had a little [1124C] passed by them, I found him whom my soul loveth:* for when for some while she has passed through the deeds and writings of the holy Fathers by reading or hearing, at length she finds the beloved by understanding and by desiring to imitate.

But because what she has understood here she resolves to observe in faith and work, as the beloved found, she goes on, saying: *I held him, and I will not let him go, till I bring him into my mother's house, and into the chamber of her that bore me.* But what is sought studiously with desire and eagerness is held dearer when found and is held more strongly. Whence the beloved, that is, the holy soul, after, by going about the Church and frequently looking into the sayings and deeds of the holy Fathers, understanding the norm of living or of teaching rightly as God himself, [1124D] found the beloved. Since indeed to live and to teach rightly is to have God, she holds him firmly in memory, and does not let him go, until she insinuates him to others also by teaching and doing, and brings him into their sense through faith and love; not that after this bringing in she ceases to hold him, but that she desires that to others also the participation of this grace, and, by virtue, of it diffused in herself through the Holy Spirit of charity, be conferred everlastingly together with herself. For this word *until* must be understood to have been set for a limit [cj: *pro determinatione*; read *non pro determinatione*, "not for a limit"], but for what is everlasting, as if she said: So long will I commend him to their tenacious memory, pitying him to my sense and to that of others, until he be so made firm in us by faith and action that he cannot fall away any more from our mind.

[1125A]But it is not to be thought that the places of this bringing in are set down so distinctly and differently without reason, that is, *the house of the Mother and the chamber of her that bore.* For since the whole body of the Church, on account of the diversity of senses and of pursuits, is divided into three, that is, into the little, the middling and the great; the house indeed receives all, but the chamber not all, but the great only, and those who attain something of speciality more differently from the others: for every house is a more evident and ample place, into which not only the Lord but also the whole household enters: it is the public place of the universal Church; but the chamber is a certain singular and as it were private place in the Church, to which access and entrance lies open to none but those raised up by ecclesiastical dignity and order, whose profession it is specially to cleave to God. But relative [1125B] names, like father and mother, which are always said to be that which they are of others, are taken in three ways, that is, *120* by nature, by affection, by adoption, of which, the first being passed over, the two remaining ways can be fitted to the mother or her that bore of this place. For holy Church is the mother of every faithful one by affection and adoption, because to him whom she has adopted to herself through faith for a son, living holily and justly, she always lays open her affection. And that we may distinguish something here, let us say that she is also she who bore him, according as she begets him, by forming him to the honesty and utility of a holy life, or by instructing him with the pages of sacred Scripture, and so by bringing him up into a perfect man in virtue and knowledge according to the measure which is pleasing to helping grace.

[1125C]The beloved, therefore, brings the beloved into the house of her mother, when she shows God, whom she acknowledges by faith and work, to the sons of her holy mother the Church, that is, to the little, the middling and the great, both by the examples of the acts of the saints and by the teachings of the Holy Scriptures, which she now brings forth for the letter alone, now interprets for the mystical sense, so that all may attain them, that is, the little, the middling and the great, each according to his capacity; the little indeed to the letter only; the middling both to the letter and to the mystical sense, but in part and not perfectly; the great both to the letter and to the mystical sense, not in part but perfectly, and who can investigate and understand things yet other, and things as it were unsearchable to others. Whence to them [1125D] only is the beloved shown in the chamber of her that bore, because to them the subtlety and discernment of the precepts and a certain secret discussion of the divine sacraments is covered [cj: *operitur*; read *aperitur*, "is opened"] from the secret place of the Church that instructs them.

But after these things the seeking, the finding and the bringing in by the beloved, to be taken in this manner also, so that with respect to the preceding sense they are said in some things otherwise, in some things uniformly. For otherwise by the name of the bed can be understood the secure dissoluteness of carnal pleasure, and dissolute security, where, because it is the night of sin, Christ is not found; wherefore from this useless and wretched quiet let the sinner rise with the eagerness of a ready mind, and, that we may speak uniformly according to the sense above, let him run through holy Church with devout intention, passing through whose watchmen, that is, the doctors, let him learn by reading [1126A] and hearing how he may correct his morals. And let him find in the city of virtues God whom he could not find in the bed of evil security, and let him grow by advancing so long, until he brings him into the house of his mother, which is otherwise to say this world, which is the place and habitation of human offspring, as of a mother procreating universal man. For he openly proclaims to all how great things God has done for him, and, provoking them to his example, exhorts that, carnal pleasure being despised, they forget the house of this their mother by leaving the world.

And when in this way he has brought *121* the beloved into the house of his mother through the active life, at last he advances so far that he brings him through the contemplative life into the chamber, that is, into the secret places of solitude, [1126B] whether of the hermitage or of the monastery, which is uniformly to say of her that bore, that is, of the Church, which begets, nourishes and forms him to the honesty of the monastic life; and her he himself afterwards so surpasses by so great an excellence of virtues, that by his example and doctrine the Church herself advances more than he had before advanced by her instruction; and let this be to bring the beloved into the chamber of her that bore him.

Loving and praising whose study and purpose, the beloved addresses the daughters of Jerusalem, that is, the simple souls in the Church, who are therefore called daughters, not sons, because for infirmity of mind they cannot yet climb up to those things to which all the perfect have attained by manliness of soul. *I adjure you,* he says, *O daughters of Jerusalem,* etc.

[1126C]VERSE 5.-- *I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up nor make the beloved to awake, till she herself please.*

For to those who rest in mind and body in the chamber of monastic conversation, such a cause often arises that it is necessary to lay aside for a while that contemplative eye and to provide in some things for the profit or necessity of neighbours; but because there are some infirm minds which wish this to be done by their superiors too frequently, and demand rather that they be stirred up and awake to exterior things, they are as it were forbidden by certain adjurations, by the sentences and authorities of the Fathers running through the breadths of the holy Scriptures, but rather that they leave it to the judgment of those [1126D] who understand through the spirit of discretion in what place and time they ought to awake to this office.

For the sentences of the holy Scriptures, or the authors themselves of the sentences, are as it were certain roes and harts, because while they propose that earthly things are to be left and heavenly things sought, they show as it were certain leaps from earth to heaven, and while they command men to be fervent and solicitous in good work, they hold out a certain agility of mind to be had by them; for it is written: *Mind the things that are above, not the things that are upon the earth; seek the things that are above, where Christ is sitting at the right hand of God;* and elsewhere: *But our conversation is in the heavens;* and in another place: *Hasten to enter into that rest;* and again: *Hastening and [1127A] making speed unto the meeting of the Lord;* and elsewhere: *So run that you may comprehend;* and the Lord in the Gospel: *Run while you have the light, lest the darkness comprehend you;* [var: Vulg. Jo 12:35 *Ambulate dum lucem habetis*, "Walk whilst you have the light"] and elsewhere: *Lift up your heads, for your redemption is at hand;* and in the Psalm: *Blessed is the man whose help is from thee, he hath disposed ascents in his heart in the vale of tears [n: (Col. III.--Phil. III.--Heb. IV.--Amos. IV.--I Cor. IX.--Joan. XII.--Luc. XXI.--Psal. LXXXIII)]*.



## CHAPTER THREE. (cont.)

Behold the roes and harts of the divine *122* fields, which show that it is more useful to sleep in the chamber of contemplation than to keep watch for exterior business; not that these things ought to be omitted altogether, when the profit or necessity of neighbours demands it, but that the part of Mary is better than that of Martha.

[1127B]With these, then, and other like sentences, as with certain incantations, the beloved adjures the daughters of Jerusalem, lest lightly and without cause they compel their neighbours to come down from inmost quiet and to pass over to exterior actions. Wherefore you, O daughters of Jerusalem, who see the vision of peace, yet so, *if you walk in the Spirit, and have mortified the deeds of the flesh [n: (Rom. VIII)]*, *for the flesh lusteth against the spirit, but the spirit against the flesh [n: (Gal. V)]*, to be free of whose wars is to see the vision of peace, always love the secret of holy contemplation, always be zealous to bring this beloved, that is, the Lord Jesus Christ, into the chamber [1128A] of your mind, so that when the Spirit of divine charity has taken you up, and has taught you to despise all earthly things, you may hear with the prophet *behind* you *the voice of a great commotion: Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his place [n: (Ezech. III)]*.

For you are the place of the glory of God when you receive him in your hearts through the love of holiness, and if you sprinkle forth from yourselves anything of virtue and grace through humility, patience, obedience and chastity, and the other works of piety, it is the glory of God, from whom and through whom you have it, and the same himself is blessed, to whose praise and glory profits all that is seen in you of honesty and discipline.

For this is the voice of a great commotion, because those who are lifted up through the spirit are moved with so great a grace [1128B] of compunction that they afflict themselves with grievous lamentations, either because they are still bound by their sins, or because they are put off so long from the joys of the heavenly life.

You hear also behind you the voice of a great commotion when you attend to the tumults and fleeting joys of this world, but out of love of the eternal joys set them after in your mind, and, forgetting the things that are behind, and stretching yourselves forth to what is to come, you follow on to the prize of the supernal vocation, so that the hearing of this voice may be no small consolation to you, while it is said: *Blessed be the glory of the Lord from his place.*

## *123* BOOK THREE.





## CHAP. III.-- *Continuation.*

[1127C]

We read in the history of the Kings that, when Eliseus the prophet had been questioned by the king of Israel, saying: *Why hath he gathered these three kings, to deliver them into the hands of Moab?* after some things: *Bring me,* he says, *a minstrel; and when the minstrel sang, the hand of the Lord came upon him, and he said: Make the channel of this torrent ditches and ditches [n: (IV Reg. III)]*. It is plain in this matter that the spirit of the prophet is not always present to the prophets, and therefore the prophet, the spirit resting in him, caused a minstrel to be brought, so that, while the minstrel sang, the spirit which was in a certain manner resting in him, granted [n: ( an. concussus?)], might be roused up and might see for him that about which he was being questioned.

But to what end the bringing forward of this example? assuredly [1127D] it looks to your intention, O beloved sisters, and to mine. For because it seemed to your humility that the Spirit of holy love was resting in you, and was as it were idle, you indicate that it is useful and necessary to bring you some minstrel, at whose singing that spirit which had as it were fallen asleep in your mind might be roused, for a double cause, as I think: either that, as David by singing softened the fury of Saul, so, if anything of unevenness should suddenly steal upon your mind, it might be composed by the sweetness of the singer, [1128C] or, if your soul had anything less of fervour in divine love, it might be kindled by a certain inmost force of song.

Since, then, this was your intention, by I know not what spirit you brought my littleness, which knows how to sing nothing of the kind, and enjoined upon me the office of this minstrel. What was I to do? on the one side your affection was urging me on, on the other side the danger to my wit was driving me back. At length, presuming on the help of God and the suffrage of your prayers, I did not refuse to be your minstrel, however unlearned, since I reckoned that with your love my excess would be easy, and because, according to the sentence of the great orator, virtue which charity follows is by no means to be rejected; but what is my virtue? Small, assuredly, and such as [1128D] is of almost no power in the arduous work of so great an office. Yet if charity can do anything in any way, it administers this.

But this indeed was your intention; my intention, however, was to obey those who asked, and to sing something by which your spirit might be roused. But behold, unskilfulness stands in the way of good will, since this ought to be the part of a skilled singer, that he know both how to profit and how to delight, according to that of Flaccus: *He has carried every vote who has mixed the useful with the sweet.*


[1129A] *124* Secular letters report that a certain Orpheus by his song soothed tigers and lions, by way of likeness, namely, because he tamed the bestial and wild manners of men by the reasonable sentence of his speech. Amphion also is said to have moved stones with the sound of the lyre [sic: *testitudinis*] and with bland entreaty to have led them whither he would, because he composed the hard and uncomposed manners of men rightly and by the sweet measure of reason. Great, then, is the skill of a singer, to change the minds of the hearers by the measure of his song. But I do not reckon that the measure of this my begun song has availed to this with your sanctity, not on account of the slowness of your wit, but on account of the density of my ignorance. For he is an unskilled singer who does not know the property of voices and of modes, [1129B] *And the harper*
*Is laughed at, who always strays on the same string,*
[n: (HORAT., De arte poetica, V. 356.)] Whence, after some part of this most excellent canticle had been sung through, I had decided rather to be silent than to uncover further such great follies of mine.

But because sometimes a skilled craftsman, laboriously reshaping a form made from the same material by an unskilled craftsman, makes it as it were another, and a skilled singer restores to a dissonant and as it were discoloured song its proper, very pure and entire colour: I too, encouraged by this, for the sake of your love, will gird myself again with the help of God for those things which I had thought to leave, so that if I [1129C] shall have made some twisted and uncomposed work, some learned and skilled man may find material whereby he may exercise his wit, by acquiring for it a better and more useful, or more lively, form and colour, if yet the work itself shall have escaped the erasure or the deserved flame.

VERSE 6.-- *Who is she that ascendeth through the desert, as a little rod of smoke from the aromatics of myrrh and of frankincense, and of the whole powder of the perfumer?*

Now the beloved has entered the chamber of the beloved, now she has begun to draw a little near to his kiss, because she has flown away from the allurements and cares of secular life, fleeing from which she has removed herself far, and has remained in the solitude of her body and soul. But the solitude of the body is to be quiet in the place assigned to oneself, and not to wander through the exterior senses about the business of this world, either [1129D] in speech or in action. But the solitude of the soul is to be occupied with no exterior things, either in thought or in will.

Which solitude, assuredly, is a great ascent of the mind. And the daughters of Jerusalem marvel at the wonderful ascent of the beloved through the desert, because infirm minds are greatly amazed at and praise the singular life of those who are free for God alone and have no care of worldly honour and advantage. It says, therefore: *Who is she that ascendeth?* But before we describe an ascent of this kind, let us consider what and of what sort is the desert through which this ascent is made. And first let us understand that both ascent and desert are each named equivocally, because by one name they can signify diverse things. For ascent is the ascent of the body and of the heart

[1130A] *125* The desert is the Church of the gentiles; the desert the Synagogue of the Jews, the desert the world, the desert some separated place, the desert the soul taken out from all the cares of the world.

But desert is so called from *deserting,* because it deserts and is deserted; because it both deserts the continent one and is deserted by the continent one: but by grace [cj: *vero gratia*; read *verbi gratia*, "for example"] the world will desert the one who contains himself, because in no manner can it come by affection for itself to the holy soul, which reckons all things of this world as nothing; it is deserted by the continent one, because by no reckoning does the holy soul consent to share in the pleasures or actions of the world, lest, held back by it perhaps, she be able to escape with difficulty or never.

But this continence we name both according to [1130B] the general situation of the body, which belongs to the good and the bad, and according to the situation of the soul, which belongs specially to the good, and specially to the bad, according to the affection of good and of evil.

The situation of the body is the exterior place: the situation of the soul the interior place: and sometimes the exterior place profits or harms the interior place. But we said a little above that the desert is the world, or some place set apart, or the soul exempted from the cares of the world. But to the soul exempted from the cares of the world the world does harm, not according to its substance, but according to the affection of its malice; but the place set apart profits, not according to its nature, but according to the quiet and the clean removal of din from it. According to this, therefore, the exterior both profits and harms the [1130C] interior place. Let us say, then, why each of those things which we set down above is named by the name of desert.

For the Church of the gentiles, so long as she remained in gentility, was a certain desert, because not yet was there in her the worship of any religion and the frequency of sacred laws or sanctions, but as it were the lairs of beasts, on account of the bestial manners of men, in whom reason itself and the natural law were so lulled to sleep that they could not discern what of honesty they owed to their own life, or what of reverence and subjection to the Creator. For of this desert the Psalmist says: *the beautiful places of the desert shall grow fat [n: (Psal. LXIV)]*. For what are the beautiful things of this desert, except the most wholesome and most seemly [1130D] institutions of the sacraments and of the venerable sanctions, which then began to grow fat when, by the teachings and authorities of the holy Fathers, they advanced to greater increments of honesty and reverence? Of which desert the Lord says through Isaiah: *I will set the desert into a pool of water, and the impassable land into streams of waters [n: (Isa. XLI)]*: for he set the desert into a pool of water and the impassable land into streams of waters, when to gentility, which for dryness of mind bore no fruits of good works, he gave the flowings of preaching, and to which, for the roughness of its dryness, the way did not lie open to preachers, he afterwards made streams of doctrine to flow forth. To which also in another place, through the same prophet, he promises again, saying: *The desert shall rejoice, and the impassable and the solitude shall exult, and shall flourish like the lily [n: (Isa. XXXV)]*: *126* [1131A] it shall rejoice in faith, exult in action, flourish in the beauty of virtues, so that like the lily it may be fragrant with the odour of good life, shine with the whiteness of cleanness, avail with the medicine of inmost salvation.

Judaea also is called a desert, from whom the testament and the giving of the law and every right of sacrifices and ceremonies departed, so that, the temple being destroyed and the city made desolate, the whole royal and priestly dignity among them, and every solemn rite, has ceased, but they themselves, dispersed through diverse regions, bear the sign of this desolation. Which the Lord foretold to them in the Gospel as to come, saying: *Your house shall be left to you deserted [n: (Matth. XXIII)]*; and in Jeremiah it is written: *The Lord hath cast down, and hath not spared; all the beautiful things of Jacob he hath destroyed in his fury* [1131B] [n: (Thren. II)].

The world also can be called a desert, not bodily but spiritually, because the more it is heaped with the filth of sins, the more it is made desolate of the presence of divine grace; for it is written: *The land shall be brought down in solitude [n: (Jer. L)]*; the land indeed in solitude, because the mind occupied with earthly cares and pleasures is made desolate of the assembly of the virtues.

A place set apart also is called a desert, because where there is no access of secular business or of forensic actions, that is not unfittingly named a certain solitude. Whence when the Psalmist had set forth beforehand the tribulations of enemies and the troubles of iniquities, and such disturbances of heart that he even [1131C] feared death from them, at length turning back from there: *I said,* he says, *who will give me wings like a dove, and I will fly and be at rest [n: (Psal. LIV)]*. And at last, a wholesome counsel having been found: *Lo,* he says, *I have gone far off flying away, and I abode in the solitude; I waited for him that hath saved me from pusillanimity of spirit and from the storm [n: (ibid.)]* And the Lord, after baptism, being about to fast, was led into the desert, where, being tempted by the devil [n: (Matth. IV)], he triumphed in himself over our temptations, showing that those who love a quiet life are indeed to be wearied by temptations, but to be delivered by the help of God through fastings and prayers.

The soul also removed from all the cares of the world is a desert, because the less it thinks of the things that are of the world, the more it carries a heart solitary from carnal [1131D] desires. Whence the Lord in the Gospel: *When,* he says, *thou shalt pray, enter into the chamber, and having shut the door pray to thy Father [n: (Matth. VI)]*; for praying we enter the chamber when in the secret of our heart we stand alone and without voice lay open to God alone what we ask; we shut the door also when we stop up the entrance of our exterior senses, lest through them anything should be brought into the heart which could hinder our prayers. And this is the solitude of the soul very necessary to each one who seeks God and desires to find him.

But we said above that ascent is both of the body and of the mind, but this is to be considered only of the ascent of the mind, which is not made except through the desert.

*127* For so long as the mind is entangled in the cares and [1132A] tumults of this world, it is by no means able to ascend to the height of the virtues, but first the solitude of body and of mind is to be sought, so that, while the mind is drawn away by no exterior or interior disquiet, it may be lifted up more freely to the study of good work.



## CHAP. III.-- *Continuation.* (cont.)

Holy Church, therefore, which has been gathered from two peoples coming from the foreskin and from the circumcision, ascends through the desert of faithlessness when, the unbelievers of both peoples remaining in faithlessness, she herself among them grew up by faith, hope, charity to wonderful summits of virtues. And the more grievously she was crushed and as it were broken small by their persecuting, the higher did she send up from the fire of the Holy Spirit the smoke of holiness, by the straightness and rigour of life and of doctrine. Whence it is added: *As a little rod of smoke from the aromatics of myrrh [1132B] and of frankincense and of the whole powder of the perfumer.*

She was as a little rod, because she remained straight and rigid in end and in operation: but she was a little rod of smoke, because that very straightness and strength smoked from the fire of divine love. But what was the material in which this divine fire grew hot? assuredly faith, hope and charity, which are signified by the kinds of aromatics, of myrrh and of frankincense and of the whole powder of the perfumer. Now myrrh has a bitter juice, and with it also the bodies of the dead are embalmed lest they putrefy; but frankincense is offered in sacrifice to God; further, from the whole powder of the perfumer a most precious ointment is made. Nine made, when for the preservation of the orthodox integrity of the faith they suffered from the unbelievers and heretics, [1132C] the corrupters of the faith itself, persecutions and deaths many and various, sent up the myrrh of smoking faith as it were into heaven; for through the whole world this smoke of faith and of sufferings ascended, which blinded and put to flight the unbelieving and the persecutors by the bitterness of miracles, but enlightened and confirmed believers with how sweet an odour. But when, fervent in the worship of God and in religion put into deed, they were continually instant in prayers for the state of the Church and for attaining the eternal beatitude which they hoped for, did they not also bring forth the frankincense of smoking and fragrant hope? for unless they had a firm hope of future goods, they would by no means assiduously supplicate God to attain them. But when, strong in faith and hope, they could say with the Apostle: *I am sure that neither life, nor death, nor angels, [1132D] nor principalities, nor powers, nor things present, nor things to come, nor might, nor height, nor depth, nor any creature shall be able to separate us from the charity of God [n: (Rom. VIII)]*. Did they not spread on high the odour of the whole powder of the perfumer? For what is charity but the whole powder of the perfumer? For charity is a universal virtue, because all virtues come forth from its root, and therefore wherever there is any virtue, there is charity.

But who is this perfumer, if not the Holy Spirit, because by his grace he compounds this universal powder? *For the charity of God is poured forth in our hearts by the Holy Spirit who is given to us [n: (Rom. V)]*.

*128* Let us see to what great subtlety the Apostle reduced charity as it were into powder, when, [1133A] enumerating its parts: *Charity,* he says, *is patient, is kind, envieth not, dealeth not perversely, is not puffed up, is not ambitious, seeketh not her own, is not provoked to anger, thinketh no evil, rejoiceth not in iniquity, but rejoiceth with the truth, beareth all things, believeth all things, hopeth all things, endureth all things [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*. Behold into how many parts he divides charity, so that through these determinate parts he may intimate that whatever virtue shall have been the last, and the most fortified [cj: *munitissima*; read *minutissima*, "the smallest"] of the whole body of good, this is the atom of charity. But where all is said, nothing is excepted; all good therefore is charity; therefore, as the whole powder of the perfumer is made from all precious spices, so charity is composed of all the virtues of the soul, so that not unfittingly it is marked [1133B] by the signification of the whole powder of the perfumer; for where any virtue is lacking, charity is not perfect.

Every soul too that is beloved of God ascends through the desert of this world when, having spurned and set after all its cares and business and delights, she always tends to heavenly things by advances of the virtues, and although she be detained in the world by the body, yet she dwells in heavenly things by the mind. Through the desert also she makes ascents of the soul, because the more the soul [cj: *quanto animus*; read *quanto minus animus*, "the less the soul"] is occupied about exterior things, and the more it is deserted by the frequency of useless thoughts, the more is it borne up by the love of divinity and the delight of good acts. And this *as a little rod of smoke from the aromatics of myrrh and of frankincense and of the whole powder of the perfumer,* so that, according to the sense above, she [1133C] too may have the rod of equity, by faith, hope and charity, through the grace of the Holy Spirit. In this vale of tears also let the myrrh of labour and patience smoke in the sight of God, as she weighs: *that the sufferings of this time are not worthy to be compared with the glory to come that shall be revealed in us [n: (Rom. VIII)]*. Let her kindle also the most pure frankincense by the instancy of prayers, delighted by whose sweet odour God may confer on her the grace of his blessing, through which she may grow and be multiplied with an offspring of virtues. And let her be fragrant with the whole powder of the perfumer, so that with a spirit of humility and a contrite mind she may render to God every office of holiness, *for a sacrifice to God is an afflicted spirit [n: (Psal. L)]*: for one is sifted as it were into the finest powder [1133D] when, through the breaking of a heart greatly humbled, one set in the highest virtues is made thin before the eyes of God. For with the finest powder of this breaking and humility, kindled by the fire of the Holy Spirit, David was smoking when he said: *129* *I am a worm and no man [n: (Psal. XXI)]*, and when in another place he called himself a dead dog and a flea [n: (I Reg. XXIV, XXVI)], and in the Psalm Moses is said to have stood *in the breaking in the sight of God, to turn away his wrath [n: (Psal. CV)]*. For with how great contrition of spirit and virtue of compassion was he crushed when, praying for the sin of the people, he said to God: *Either forgive them this trespass, or if thou do not, strike me out of thy book that thou hast written? [n: (Exod. XXXII.)]* Paul also, broken small by the abasement of humility, names himself the least of the apostles [1134A] [n: (II Cor. XV)], but, crushed by the breaking of piety and compassion, wishes to become anathema for his brethren [n: (Rom. IX)].

But this ascent is said to have been *as a little rod of smoke*; for smoke, when it ascends, so fails little by little in ascending that it cannot be seen at all, because so great is the subtlety of the contemplative life in the saints, so great the sublimity of holy conversation through the desert of inmost solitude, that it escapes the minds of those who marvel rather than it is held by consideration to imitate it, so that, while it does not prevail to comprehend by following, it fails in the very consideration of it.

VERSE 7.-- *Behold, threescore valiant ones of the most valiant of Israel surround the bed of Solomon.*

It is not repugnant to reason, as I think, if we take this *bed of Solomon* [1134B] in three ways, either as the universal Church, or the soul of each saint, or the holy Scripture of both testaments; not that he who is uncircumscribed is comprehended by places, but that the granting and preservation of graces and of saving effects is his repose in some one of the aforesaid, saving, guarding and governing.

For the universal Church is the bed of this Solomon, that is, of the peaceable man, our Lord Jesus Christ, because he rests in her through the saving observance of his commandments and the life-giving concorporation of his sacraments, which he displays to her under the species of sacramental things, pacifying those things which are in heaven and on earth, so that there may be as it were one commonwealth of this magnificent emperor, namely one [1134C] Church, both that which rejoices in the fatherland and that which is still a pilgrim on earth. *For the head of the Church is Christ, but she the body of Christ [n: (I Cor. XI)]*; and as the body without the head does not live, nor can do anything; and as the head is not vital without the body; so also Christ confers life on no one without the unity of the body of the Church. Christ and the Church, then, are one, who, since the head is undivided from its body, whoever does not have the peace and unity of the Church does not have Christ either: whence also in the very body of the sacraments, namely in the body and blood of Christ, to the head the body, that is, *130* to Christ the Church, is made consacramental and concorporal, by her own and by his mystery, when the mystery of Christ taken in the truth of his body [1134D] profits no one unless he has taken up the mystery of the Church, namely the ecclesiastical fellowship, in the same sacrament; because, since, as we said, Christ is undivided from his body, that is, the Church, in his sacrament the same Christ is not said to be truly taken unless he be taken whole, concorporated to that same Church by his own and the Church's mystery; for he himself says: *He that eateth my flesh and drinketh my blood, abideth in me and I in him [n: (Joan. VI)]*.

Where, then, does Christ rest more than in the Church, which daily in partaking is concorporated to his body and blood and is made as it were one with him? Whence those who cut themselves off from the unity of the Church by living evilly or by teaching perversely, because they do not abide in the Church, since Christ and the Church are one, do not abide [1135A] in Christ, and therefore, when they partake of his body, they take him indeed in the truth of the substance, but they do not take him to the effect of their salvation. For this is not truly to take him, because he is not taken whole, since indeed, since at the altar the Church, as has been said, is concorporal and consacramental in Christ, he does not take the whole body of Christ, namely the head with the members, who, since he is outside the Church, does not unite himself to Christ and to the Church in the sacrament of both. He takes indeed the sacrament itself according to its essence, which turns out to some for life, to some for their end; but the virtue and the reality of the sacrament he does not take, because, since this is the virtue and the reality of the sacrament, that by loving one another whoever [1135B] partake of that which profits no one to his end, but profits every believer to salvation, be of the unity of the body of the Church, whoever, Christ conforming and uniting them, shall not have been true partakers of so great a fellowship, do not truly take Christ, while they are proved to take him not to their salvation but to judgment: for since Christ himself commands, saying: *If thou offer thy gift at the altar, and there thou remember that thy brother hath anything against thee, leave there thy gift before the altar, and go first to be reconciled to thy brother, and then coming thou shalt offer thy gift [n: (Matth. V)]*; he shows that the gift is not to be accepted where the soul of the offerer has not had the bond of peace and charity. But if this unity, not kept individually among fellow members, repels [1135C] the gifts of those who offer, how much more will he who deserts the unity of the whole body of the Church be able neither to offer a gift nor to partake of what is offered?

But this bed is Solomon's, that is, the *peaceable one's*, who said: *My peace I give unto you, peace I leave unto you [n: (Joan. XIV)]*, who confederates the things which are in heaven and on earth, because, since the Church, the body of Christ, is one, which partly reigns in heaven with Christ *131*, partly is still a pilgrim in the body, whoever withdraws from the unity of this pilgrim part does not merit the fellowship of that supernal part, since indeed, since they are one according to the virtue and reality of the sacrament, that is, of ecclesiastical unity, although they are not yet associated in one place, yet they are so joined in spirit that whoever does not have the one, [1135D] does not have the other. Whence in this bed of this peaceable one, that is, in the Church, in which Christ still rests through the veils of the sacraments, whoever through penance has recovered the lost unity merits to be associated also to that which is on high, when he comes and incorporates in reality who reconciled us to God through his blood.



## CHAP. III.-- *Continuation.* (cont.)

The soul, too, of each saint is the bed of this our true peaceable one, because, since *the soul of the just is said to be the seat of wisdom [n: (Sap. VII)]*, there surely God rests sweetly where, wisely, reasonably and honourably, with wisdom itself governing, the place of that same resting is arranged; where the mind is by grace so coupled to God that it is made one spirit with him, the sacrament of which unity confers the whole effect of salvation, [1136A] as the Saviour himself says: *He that abideth in me, and I in him, the same beareth much fruit, for without me you can do nothing [n: (Joan. XV)]*. For since the mind is powerless for any fruit of goodness and discipline without grace working with it, so great a power of this conjunction is conferred upon it that, since he is almighty to whom it is coupled through unity of spirit, it too is itself made almighty through grace, as he himself says, *all things are possible to him that believeth [n: (Marc. IX)]*. For if the saints joined to God are called gods, he indeed essentially, they by appellation, it does not seem inconsequent that, since they are made one body with the Almighty through the sacrament of unity, they should also be made partakers of his omnipotence in a certain manner of their own, who can say with the Apostle, *he hath given us all things with him [1136B] [n: (Rom. VIII)]*. For to those to whom he has given all things with him, when they do not have [cj: *non habent*; read *nunc habent*, "now have"] the Almighty, there is nothing which in him and through him they cannot do. But such a mind is called determinately the bed of the Saviour, that is, of the peaceable one, because wherever this peaceable one has rested and made his dwelling by the condescension of his grace, all the adversity of carnal affection that fights against the spirit is lulled to sleep.

Further, this bed of this Solomon is also, not absurdly, taken to be Holy Scripture, because in it Christ rests through the teachings of his precepts and the examples of the virtues, where the testament of this peaceable one is preached, which he confirmed in his death, namely the precept of peace and love, when [1136C] he says: *A new commandment I give unto you, that you love one another as I have loved you [n: (Joan. XIII)]*; the fulness of which love consists in a twofold relation, namely of God and of neighbour, on which the whole *law hangeth, and the prophets [n: (Matth. XXII)]*; and because the whole page of each sacrament *132* contains it, and discerns nothing useful or honourable without it, not undeservedly may sacred Scripture be named the bed of this peaceable one. For there Christ is found by one who reads and meditates in it; there he can understand what he ought to believe, what to hope, what to love; and to have this threefold virtue is to have God, to the eternal having of whom he is led and confirmed by faith, hope, charity. There Christ, lying hidden and as it were resting, is recognized and awakened, because in the minds of the reader, [1136D] after he is understood, if the mind is moved by the steps of faith and of good works, Christ is as it were awakened from rest. For in the Gospel it is written that Christ was known in the breaking of bread, he who lay hidden in the form of a stranger [n: (Luc. XXIV)], because Holy Scripture, which is that heavenly and living bread, Christ, is strange to those who do not understand; but when it has been distributed through the breaking of exposition, Christ, who lay hidden in it, is recognized by the mind of one who understands.

But we must see what it is that threescore valiant ones, of the most valiant of Israel, all holding swords and most expert in war, are said to surround the bed of this peaceable one. For many have tried to scatter this bed and to carry off this peaceable one: some by vain [1137A] and irreligious worships, some by perverse morals, not a few by the errors of depraved dogmas; whom these valiant ones most valiantly withstood by deeds, words and writings, holding the swords of the word of God, and most expert in warring down enemies of this kind: first indeed the holy apostles and apostolic men, then the holy martyrs, finally the priests, who were all of the most valiant of those who see God with the mind and draw from him the strength of unconquered fortitude, surrounding and guarding this bed, each in his own time and place; not that they were necessary to this peaceable one, but that he was exceedingly necessary to them, whom they feared to lose through the perversity of the wicked, who laboured in every way to overthrow the Catholic faith.

[1137B]But why is the number of these valiant, or most valiant, ones fixed at the number sixty: for it is not without some reason that, although the multitude of these valiant ones was innumerable, it is comprehended in this finite number: it could indeed be that that real Solomon had more guards of his body, or appointed and designated keepers, each at his own office, of his possessions stored in diverse places; but since God granted him so great a peace that no wars were stirred in his time, it seems a wonder, if it is to be taken according to the letter, why so many valiant ones were deputed to guard his body alone. But since reason demands rather that these things be referred to the spiritual sense, let us see what this number *133* sixty signifies; for sixty is composed of [1137C] six and ten, the one multiplied by the other. Now six, which, when its parts are gathered together, is neither diminished nor extended, is said to signify perfection. But ten, which is composed of five taken twice, suggests the Decalogue of the law, for the keeping or contempt of which, on account of the five senses of the body, by whose office the mind is led either to good or to evil, the whole of mankind, in the order of the ten virgins, the five wise and the foolish, is either rewarded or condemned. So six, which signifies perfection, and ten, which shows the Decalogue, multiplying one another and making up sixty, show that these sixty valiant ones so perfectly fitted their outward senses to keeping the divine precepts [1137D] that they are deservedly reckoned in the lot of the five wise.

Now those who have the science of the art of Arithmetic are wont to say to me that such a number as sixty is superfluous; for, since some numbers are so equalled by the collection of their parts that they take back neither less nor more than the whole body, and are therefore called perfect, while some, because by the composition of their parts they return less than the whole body, are named diminished, those which, when their own parts are gathered together, surpass the whole body by some quantity are called superfluous. But we, who refer this number of these valiant ones to the good part, ought to take this surpassing not as superfluity, but as the increase of spiritual gain, which advances from virtue to virtue, [1138A] so that we may say that these valiant ones, in the talents of the five senses entrusted to them, wrought so magnificently for their own profit and that of their neighbours that, their zeal in good works always taking increase, from valiant they were made most valiant.

Let us see, however, what the parts of this number sixty bring, so that we may be able to know whether they pertain to the perfection of these valiant ones of Christ. Of the number sixty, then, the half part is thirty, the third twenty, the fourth fifteen, the fifth twelve, the sixth ten, the tenth six, the twelfth five, the fifteenth four, the twentieth three, the thirtieth two, the sixtieth one. These parts, gathered together, make up a hundred and eight, surpassing the whole body of sixty [1138B] by forty-eight. And what is the reward of all the perfect, but a recompense of all their labour a hundredfold better, in the future regeneration, when they shall be born again into the immortality of their bodies, to that life of the eighth age which shall never be ended.

Well, then, is the company of these valiant, or most valiant, ones comprehended in the number sixty, whose parts, brought into one, grow *134* to a hundred and eight, so that we may refer this not to superfluity but to the increase of virtues, since by doing and teaching rightly they have gained advancement for themselves and for others, for which they may obtain perfect blessedness, which is designated by the number a hundred, and which will be fulfilled in the eighth age, as we have said, to abide without end.

[1138C]VERSE 8.-- *All holding swords, and most expert in war: every man's sword upon his thigh, because of fears in the night.*

And these sixty valiant ones of the most valiant of Israel, surrounding the bed of Solomon, what do they do? *All,* he says, *holding swords, and most expert in war:* he does not say having, but *holding,* because they could indeed have them in a certain secret store-room of a soul that knows and understands, but not hold them in the public carrying, as it were, of the voice and work of one who preaches. They hold swords, therefore, because they always carry ready the teachings of knowledge and of action, by which they may train those who see and hear them for spiritual contests; but what are [1138D] these swords but the words of God: *For the word of God is true, and effectual, and more piercing than any two-edged sword, and reaching unto the division of the soul, of the joints also and the marrow, and a discerner of thoughts [n: (Hebr. IV)]* [var: Vulg. *Vivus*, "living"]: for such a sword, dividing and separating, God sent into the earth when he came *to set a man at variance against his father, and the daughter against her mother, and the daughter in law against her mother in law, and a man's enemies they of his own household [n: (Matth. X)]*. This sword, surely, of the word of God, by which separation and every kind of discernment is commanded to be made between good and evil, between virtues and vices, the renowned warriors of God ought to use, whose purpose it is to surround the bed of this peaceable one with doctrine and working, whether we refer this bed to the Church or to the proper mind of each saint, [1139A] in which the Godhead is believed to rest by the condescension of his grace; so that in both places they may both defend the Church from the depravity of unbelievers and guard themselves from the suggestion or deception of malignant spirits.

And what are the wars in which such men ought to fight, and in which they are recorded as most expert? Assuredly the great strife and discord of virtues and vices, where at issue is the conflict of two adversaries to one another, namely of the flesh and the spirit, *for the flesh lusteth against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh [n: (Gal. V)]*. And rightly they are proclaimed most expert not in war but, in the plural number, in wars, because one is the war of humility against pride, another of chastity against lust, another of patience against anger, another of obedience against contumacy, another of sobriety [1139B] against luxury, another of avarice against liberality [cj: *avaritiae contra liberalitatem*; read *liberalitatis contra avaritiam*, "of liberality against avarice"], or of whatever virtue struggles against the vice opposed to it; for carrying through which wars happily these sixty *135* valiant ones are recorded as most expert, because in their deeds and writings are the method and counsel for the equipping of these wars, to be brought to a good and honourable end.

Whence also is added: *Every man's sword upon his thigh, because of fears in the night.* For the method of repressing diverse vices is diverse, because pride is repressed in one way, luxury in another, and avarice in another, and single vices demand single contrary remedies, so that against each one it is fought with its contrary as with a different sword. Now the sword is the watchful guard of the mind, of which Scripture commands: [1139C] *With all watchfulness keep thy heart [n: (Prov. IV)]*: *which sword upon the thigh* is set when the suggestion of the delighting flesh is repressed by the watchful guard of the resisting mind.



## CHAP. III.-- *Continuation.* (cont.)

Let us say, by likeness, that in the thigh is also the seed-bed of offspring, from which, conception having been given, man is formed, who, his members being distinguished one by one in the womb, is at length brought forth by being born, and little by little, through the stages of the ages, is led on into the perfect man. And what is the suggestion of the delighting flesh in each man, but a certain conception of a vice suddenly rushing in? Which, if it is not at once pressed down by the sword of the mind stoutly resisting, takes form in the mind through delight, then through consent is as it were born, [1139D] and so, growing step by step through action, it is made through custom a damnable and deadly vice; for in the propagating of each vice these are the parts of its progression, namely suggestion, delight [sic: *delectasio*], consent, action, custom, just as in man too these are the parts of the process of birth, that is, conception, formation, nativity, the drawing-out of the body and the fulness of age. Every man's sword, therefore, ought to be upon his thigh, so that, the head of evil suggestion being cut off [sic: *praecito*] and the seed-bed, as it were, of malice to be conceived being weakened, the whole body of the vice that would be born may be driven off; and why this? *Because of fears,* he says, *in the night.* For what do those who guard a city, or a house, fear by night, but sudden enemies or thieves, but malignant spirits, [1140A] who try to plunder or steal the treasures of our soul? These indeed, through the night of depraved suggestion or delight, with which they wrap the mind of each man so that it cannot discern the peril of the evil itself, rush in suddenly and creep in, and wound the mind with such darts of temptations that at times they cast it down from the state of its rightness. But this they can do only by night, when, the warmth of pious zeal withdrawing, the cold of torpor and negligence has taken hold of the mind; when the sun of justice has set in it on account of sloth and has not shone upon it with the light of his grace; because then they make ready their arrows that they may shoot at the poor man in secret [n: (Psal. X)].

Because of these fears in the night, therefore, that is, the hidden ambushes of temptations, each one ought [1140B] to stand girded with the arms of justice, and to watch all the entrances *136* of his inner house so vigilantly that no place of entering may lie open to the enemy. Zeal of this kind shows who are the valiant or most valiant, who guard, or are able to guard, the bed of the true Solomon by surrounding it, and who are most expert in the girding of a holy purpose, always holding the swords of providence and of ready vigilance, because of the sudden fears in the night of creeping vice.

But where and whence these most valiant ones receive the knowledge and daring of this spiritual warfare, what follows suggests [sic: *insinuaut*]; for it is added: *King Solomon made himself a litter,* etc.

VERSE 9.-- *Solomon made himself a litter of the wood of Libanus.*

[1140C]For whence do they receive it to be valiant, if not from the doctors of holy Church, whose deeds and words they desire to imitate, or from the testimonies and authorities of the holy canonical Scriptures.

Now the litter of this true king Solomon is holy Church, or the whole canonical Scripture of the Church itself, or, in the individual, the soul of any just man; for since "ferculum", a litter, is so called from *ferendo*, bearing, each one of these is surely not absurdly named a litter equivocally: the Church, indeed, on account of the bearing of the divine sacraments which are contained in it; holy Scripture on account of its taking up of the divine utterances, or precepts, whose mysteries and utility it contains; but the soul of the just man [1140D] on account of its perceiving of divine knowledge, through which it is made capable of honourableness and discipline; all which are the delights of spiritual banquets.

And how the several members can adhere to each head of those we have set forth, we shall take care to set out; that is, we shall assign how the silver pillars, the golden reclining-place, the purple ascent, and the midst of these strewed with charity, fit either the Church or canonical Scripture or the soul of the just man.

First, however, it must be considered in what way each of these litters is said to be built of the wood of Libanus: Libanus is interpreted *whitening*. And what is understood by the wood of Libanus, which is recorded to be by nature incorruptible, but the saints and elect, [1141A] who through strength of spirit and the whiteness of worldly life have kept, and keep, an incorrupt holiness in thought, speech and work?

Of the wood of Libanus, therefore, king Solomon established a litter for himself, because Christ, out of the venerable and incorrupt deeds and teachings of his saints, both most becomingly ennobled his Church, and composed his holy Scripture, and instructed the soul of each just man, so that *131* each one of these should be a litter of heavenly banquets and a refreshment of faithful souls.

VERSE 10.-- *The pillars thereof he made of silver, the reclining-place of gold and the ascent of purple: the midst he strewed with charity for the daughters of Jerusalem.*

The silver and the gold are fittingly [1141B] understood in each one on account of the brightness and purity of the utterances, and the majesty of the senses which shine in those same utterances. For if thou refer it to the Church, its pillars are of silver, because the holy patriarchs and prophets, and the apostles too, or apostolic men, in their preaching, or in the exposition of each testament by which they lift up and sustain the Church, set forth what they say or write in faithful and lucid speech.

In which speech the reclining-place is of gold, because their very preaching, or exposition, glows with the precious brightness of its senses; in which anyone who before laboured in not knowing and in seeking, now understanding, may as it were by resting redeem himself, and leans the mind wearied as it were by ignorance upon the banquets of inner refreshment.

[1141C]The ascent of this litter too is of purple, because the highest profession in the peace of the Church is that which, by contempt of the world, mortification of the flesh and the casting away of one's own will, arranges an ascent made purple by the martyrdom of the mind, when the mind offers itself *to God* *a living victim for an odour of sweetness [n: (Ephes. V)]*. For no one can ascend by contemplation to the highest step of divine charity unless he is free from all the cares and desires of the world; because, although the steps of the saints ascending by virtues are many and diverse, as it is written of them: *They shall go from virtue to virtue [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*; yet the highest ascent, as we learn, is where the mind lifts itself above itself by contemplative charity. For in this purple [1141D] ascent there are diverse steps, because one is more eminent than another; for it is more to pray, fast and keep vigil than only to meditate and read; it is more to bear insults and reproaches or persecutions of whatever kind for justice than only to abstain from injustice: yet in all ascents of this kind the labour of body and mind, and the continence of life, is as it were a certain precious purple of the mind.

But *the midst* of this litter king Solomon *strewed with charity for the daughters of Jerusalem,* because in the midst of these so sublime merits in the Church, many minds, as though strewn low by frailty and infirmity, are laid down; because, though they are not able to leave the world perfectly, yet they love and venerate those who despise the world; though they cannot labour for [1142A] God by fasting, keeping vigil and praying, or by mortifying the flesh, yet while they love these things in those who do them, they make them as it were their own, and what they themselves *132* do not have in themselves through infirmity, this they have in others through charity. Such souls certainly belong to this litter, and are refreshed in it in their own manner, because what the saints and the perfect attain in deed and virtue, these attain by charity alone.

Further, if thou look upon the whole body of canonical Scripture, *its pillars of silver, the reclining-place* also *of gold,* are the sentences and authorities of the divine utterances, clear in the brightness of their words, and shining in the singular excellence of their senses, the reading of which is the sweetest refreshment of the soul, since there faith sustains, hope lifts up, charity through contemplation [1142B] makes to recline. Whence also the reclining-place is of gold, because contemplative charity is greater than faith and hope, as gold is more precious than silver. For when any of the faithful has learned from a litter of this kind, by reading or hearing, what he ought to believe, what to hope, then, all transitory things being put behind, in the love of God alone, as in a certain golden reclining-place of contemplation, he is fed and nourished through quiet of mind unto eternal life.

In this litter the ascent is of purple, because therein are recited the passion of the Redeemer, and the glorious triumphs of the martyrs dying for the love of that same Redeemer, in which passions there were several ascents: for as we can see in the very head of the martyrs, first scourged, then smeared with spittle, struck with blows of the hand, [1142C] crowned with thorns, lastly fastened to the cross, he made as it were a certain purple ascent in his passion; following whose footsteps the martyrs had the ascents of manifold labour, because *having had trial of mockeries and stripes, moreover of bands and prisons, they were stoned, they were cut asunder, they were tempted,* at the last *they died in the slaughter of the sword [n: (Hebr. XI)]*, so that through these ascents of passions they might reach the highest step of eternal beatitude. But when pious minds read and hear of this ascent, they busy themselves to imitate it, so that, as of the temporal passions, so also of the eternal consolations they may be made partakers.

The midst also of this litter is strewed with charity, because in the whole divine page charity holds as it were the middle [1142D] place, while all the sentences of the holy Scriptures look to it as to a certain best and sure becoming, as the Apostle says, that *the end of the precept is charity from a pure heart and a good conscience and a faith unfeigned [n: (I Tim. I)]*. And when the Lord showed the two precepts of charity, namely of God and of neighbour, *on these,* he says, *two precepts the whole law hangeth and the prophets [n: (Matth. XXII)]*; but that from which all other things hang seems in a manner to hold the highest and principal place. *The midst* therefore of this litter king Solomon *strewed with charity,* because in the midst of the holy Scriptures he established the primacy of charity.

And why this? *For the daughters of Jerusalem,* that is, for the souls belonging to the heavenly Jerusalem, that is, the vision [1143A] of peace, that they may understand that neither the silver pillars, nor the golden reclining-place, nor *133* the purple ascent, has anything of virtue without the virtue of charity, but that all things, and by charity, ought to proceed as many branches from the one root of a tree. For the Apostle says that *if I speak with the tongues of men and of angels,* which is to have silver pillars, *and have not charity, I am become as sounding brass or a tinkling cymbal; and if I should have prophecy and should know all mysteries and all knowledge,* which is to be a golden reclining-place, *and if I should deliver my body so that I burn,* which is to have a purple ascent, *and have not charity, it profiteth me nothing [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*.

[1143B]Behold how our king Solomon *strewed the midst with charity* in this his litter, when through the whole body of holy Scripture he established charity as in a certain centre, so that all things revolve round it and into it. And as the sun is highest when it is fixed in the centre and goes no further, so holy Scripture, disputing of the virtues, is highest in the proclaiming of charity, and does not go beyond it, to the prejudice of any virtue.



## CHAP. III.-- *Continuation.* (cont.)

But in the third place, if thou consider the soul of any just man, its pillars of silver are the four cardinal virtues, of which all the others are appendages, namely prudence, justice, fortitude, temperance. But how of silver? By the purity of proven faith and by the true preaching of evangelical doctrine, drained of all [1143C] dross of heretical falsity, of which it is written: *The words of the Lord are pure words, silver tried by the fire [n: (Ps. XI)]*. Whence the Lord says of heretics through the prophet: I gave them silver and gold, but they made Baal out of the silver and gold [n: (Ose. II)]; now gold pertains to the mind, silver to utterance: for God grants gold and silver to a holy soul when he grants it to have both a divine sense and wisdom, and to bring forth with beauty of utterance that which conceives the mind [cj: *mentem*; read *mens*, "that which the mind conceives"]. And because heretics deprave this sense and, teaching it depraved, give it utterance, they as it were fabricate idols of falsity out of gold and silver.

He, then, who wishes that his virtues, if he has any, should be pillars [1143D] which sustain the structure of the soul, ought to take pains that they be of silver, because, unless they shine with the purity of faith and doctrine, it is greatly to be feared lest, not being able to bear the structure itself, they sink down through the weakness of their crookedness. But of whom the pillars are of silver, his reclining-place too is of gold, because *out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaketh [n: (Matth. XII)]*; since where wisdom abounds in the sense, there praiseworthy eloquence flows forth outwardly. It is written: *Wisdom resteth in the heart of the just, and prudence in the speech of his mouth [n: (Prov. XIX)]*. For when the mind shines with the brightness of wisdom, it follows that it utters prudently that by which it understands anything. But why is the mind of the just man called a reclining-place? Surely because the Godhead, in a mind which pleases it by the beauty of humility, patience, chastity and [1144A] obedience, is said to recline itself through the gold, the gift of its indwelling grace; *134* whereas, on the contrary, it is said of the minds of the reprobate that *the Son of man hath not where to lay his head [n: (Matth. VIII)]*.

And what is the purple ascent of this litter? The faith and memory of the Lord's passion; to believe which, and to set in it all the hope of one's salvation, and after its form to crucify oneself also to the world, to vices and concupiscences, so that one can say with the Apostle: *The world is crucified to me, and I to the world [n: (Gal. VI)]*, is without doubt to ascend by the purple ascent of the mind.

And how this litter's *midst with charity* is strewed *for the daughters of Jerusalem,* the Apostle [1144B] shows, who says: *I became weak to the weak, that I might gain the weak [n: (I Cor. IX)]*; whence is this but from charity? For since love is twofold, namely of God and of neighbour, and the one hangs from the other, whoever is endowed with the highest virtues ought to have this midst: that, when brotherly necessity or utility has required it, he should come down a little from that height of his rigour, and should provide for the infirmity of his neighbours by condescending to it.

For in this way, unless he love his neighbour, he is by no means proved to love God, since the one love is established from the other: *For he that loveth not his neighbour whom he seeth, how can he love God whom he seeth not? [n: (I Joan. IV.)]* [var: Vulg. *fratrem suum*, "his brother"]

Whether, then, the Church, or holy Scripture, or [1144C] the soul of any just man, be called the litter of this true Solomon, what follows does not resist the members from adhering, not unfittingly, to each head. But if the gaze of the mind be applied diligently, it will not be, as I think, tedious to consider how beautifully the last member of this litter is sealed with the conclusion of charity. For what is there of religion in the Church, what of sacramental reality, what of knowledge or eloquence in the whole divine page, what of holiness and justice in the soul of a saint, which does not set its whole beginning or end in regard to charity? And further, what are the sacraments of the Church, what the utterances of the whole of holy Scripture, or what is it that the soul of the just man ought to believe, to hope and to love, [1144D] but the incarnation, passion, resurrection and ascension of Christ, and the showing forth of all the things he did for human salvation? And what are the holy proclamations of all these, but signs of divine charity? *for on account of his exceeding charity wherewith God loved us, he sent his Son in the likeness of flesh, made a curse for us, that he might redeem us from the curse [n: (Ephes. II)]*. Whether, then, this litter be called the Church, or holy Scripture, or the soul of the just man, everywhere this charity holds the middle place, because from it all things proceed, and to it all things look. For the whole sum of this charity is *God made man,* and this is the whole that the saints teach in the Church through Holy Scripture, namely that we should attend with all our might to the [1145A] *135* greatness of this divine charity and mercy, because through it we have been freed from death and restored to the eternal life which we had lost by sinning.

Whence now, in what follows, the daughters of Sion are admonished to attend diligently to the grace of the divine goodness, not only in the thing but also in the working of the thing itself, and to take notice of the wonderful ordering of their salvation.

VERSE 11.-- *Go forth and see, O daughters of Sion, king Solomon in the diadem wherewith his mother crowned him, in the day of his espousals and in the day of the joy of his heart.*

Sion is said to be *watching, and the daughters* of *Sion,* that is, *of watching,* who are they but the souls of believers, who through the grace of contemplation keenly watch into the [1145B] secrets of the heavenly mysteries? To them it is said that they should go forth, because they ought by no means to be occupied in carnal things who desire to be held by the eye of the mind in spiritual things. *Go forth,* he says, *and see,* for you cannot see unless first you go forth: *Go forth* therefore, that is, go out from the consideration of corporeal and visible things, and fix the gaze of the mind on the glory of the divine magnificence, and on the very cause of the universal redemption, and on the whole incorporeality, altogether, of human salvation. Why? Namely for this, that you may see *king Solomon in the diadem wherewith his mother crowned him:* but with what eye ought you to see? Surely with the inner rather than the outer, because by the faith of corporality you ought to go out to corporality; [1145C] it behoves you to understand spiritually, not carnally, the things which you see or hear: for so it is said *see,* as if it were said, understand.

You, therefore, *daughters of Sion,* who are called not sons, on account of strength, but daughters, on account of infirmity; because you have not yet advanced into the robustness of spiritual understanding, since king Solomon has made a litter for you, at whose banquets you sit daily by looking into the sacraments of the Church, by reading and hearing canonical scripture, by searching out also the sentences of the holy doctors, *go forth* with a higher sense *and see* that same *king Solomon in the diadem wherewith his mother crowned him.*
