What or of what kind is this diadem of king Solomon *wherewith his mother crowned him?* We can indeed [1145D] say, taken singly, the one sign of the passion, namely the crown of thorns with which his mother the Synagogue, who begot him according to the flesh, crowned him in mockery; but it seems better that we take this diadem as the whole sum of the twofold passion, which, as a diadem is composed of many kinds of precious stones, so is composed of many signs of diverse sufferings; which *136* after he had borne it in his body, he was crowned with glory and honour as with a certain diadem, as the Apostle said: *We saw Jesus for the suffering of death crowned with glory and honour, that through the grace of God he might taste death for all [n: (Hebr. II)]*. Whence you, O *daughters of Sion,* do not consider only the outward and contemptible signs of these sufferings, that is, the scourges, the spittings, the blows, the [1146A] crown of thorns, the cross, the burial; but from this consideration *go forth,* and behold with the eyes of the mind what of glory and honour, of remedy and health, has sprung forth thence for the whole world. For this is the diadem which you ought to admire and venerate, and this is the primacy of charity which he established in the midst of his litter; and with such a diadem the excellence of his mercy towards you shines. Was he not crowned with glory and honour, rising again from this corruption of a passible body into impassibility and incorruption, and did he not set the crown of this glory and honour upon his body, which is the Church, when he confirmed to it, with himself, a participation in future incorruption for ever?

Or if we call this diadem, generally, the whole order of the human dispensation, *wherewith his [1146B] mother crowned him,* namely the blessed Virgin Mary, from whose womb he took up that same humanity, it does not seem to be repugnant to reason; for in the composition of this diadem with which Christ was crowned as man there are, as it were, certain ornaments of distinguished gems: the nativity, the circumcision, the baptism, the fast, the temptation, the passion, the resurrection, the ascension; all which are enclosed in the gold of his mercy. For gold is more precious than all metals, *and the tender mercies of God are over all his works [n: (Psal. CXLIV)]*. Out of the most shining gold, therefore, of the infinite mercy of God, and out of the works and signs of that mercy, which we have recounted above, as out of certain gems, is fashioned this diadem of king Solomon, namely of the true peaceable one, [1146C] the Lord Jesus Christ: *Who, reconciling us to God, set at peace all things that are in heaven and on earth [n: (Colos. I)]*. Whence the daughters of Sion, who have the grace of divine contemplation, are admonished that, looking into all these things with the inner sense, they should understand how great a salvation has flowed forth to them thence.

And when was he crowned with such a diadem? *In the day,* he says, *of his espousals, in the day of the joy of his heart.* What was the day of the espousals of Christ, but the time of the incarnation, when he came forth from the Virgin's womb *as a bridegroom coming out of his bride chamber [n: (Psal. XVIII)]*, to couple to himself the Church, for whose redemption he deigned to become incarnate? Or rather the day of espousals was the time of his passion, when, redeemed with the price of his blood, he presented *to himself the Church [1146D] a glorious bride, not having spot, nor wrinkle [n: (Ephes. V)]*. For with this price of inestimable weight he betrothed her; for her he caused the eternal kingdom of his Father to be written down as a dowry; from her he himself also exacts espousal gifts, *137* [d: exacts] namely the merits of faith and the virtues, without which no soul can be coupled to God; some parts of which he enumerates a little later in what follows.

What, then, was the day of the joy of his heart? The time, assuredly, of the resurrection, when, all the sadness of passion and corruption being overcome, he showed himself festive and crowned with glory and honour, with the trophy of a completed redemption. But this affection of heart did not so befall him as that beforehand, doubtful, he did not know what was to come, and then, as if [1147A] with doubt removed, conceived joy in his mind (for no time is ascribed to the Godhead); but a manner of speaking of this kind befits only his humanity, by which he is said to have had a day of joy on account of the glory of the resurrection and the fulfilled purpose and order of redemption; although this too could rather be referred to the Church, which is his body, according to that manner of speaking by which, frequently in holy Scripture, what is said of the head is referred to the body, as, conversely, from the body to the head, on account of the concorporation and unity by which Christ and the Church are made one; for the passion, resurrection and ascension of Christ is the greatest and special joy of the Church, when she remembers that by this wonderful help she has passed from captivity to liberty, from death to life, from corruption to [1147B] incorruption.



## CHAP. III.-- *Continuation.* (cont.)

But since this whole epithalamium of the bridegroom and the bride, which in this place is called, for its excellence, the *Canticle of Canticles;* looks to the wonderful spectacle of this espousal, let us see how beautifully and pleasantly the bridegroom and the bride, alternating with one another, sing the proclamations of holy love. For above the bride set forth the dignity and excellence of the bridegroom who is to be coupled to her spiritually, in the reverence of the bed, in the abundance of the litters, in the splendour and glory of the ornaments, and, what is greater than all these, in the singular prerogative of charity; and she was exhorting the daughter of Sion to go forth to behold and admire the beauty of these nuptials, so that [1147C] they too, striving according to their power to be joined to these nuptials, might, him whom they now saw through a glass in a dark manner, afterwards, the eye of the mind being now wholly purged, see face to face, the king in his beauty.

To whom the bridegroom presently responds with a songful testimony of beauty, and enumerates very many parts of that beauty, offered to him as it were for the espousal gifts of the bride; which nevertheless are of the grace of that same bridegroom who gives them, so that he may show in turn that she who receives is deservedly to be coupled to him, whom he has made glorious by his own gift with so many proclamations of beauties, to whom he has fitted the name also of the bed and of the litter signified before, and whom he has made the cause of the ornaments laid upon her. He says, therefore, *how fair thou art, my love.*



## *138* CHAPTER IV.

[1147D]VERSE 1.-- *How fair thou art, my love! how fair thou art! thy eyes are doves' eyes, besides that which lieth hid within; thy hair is as flocks of goats which have come up from mount Galaad.*

Since by the grace of the Holy Spirit every blessing of the virtues flows forth in the Church's field, fittingly now the bridegroom extols her with sevenfold proclamations of beauties, which he regards in the comeliness and grace of the eyes, the hair, the teeth, the lips, the cheeks, the neck and the breasts; because surely *139* Christ judges the Church worthy of his fellowship, whom, for the abundance of her graces, he commends by the elegance of all the members in her face.

For the face of the Church is her public assembly, in which are considered, ordered and prescribed [1148A] the matters always necessary and useful, by which her members may be informed and set in the comeliness of unity, so that no spot of deformity may appear in them, whether in faith, or in action, or in any constitution of the canonical sanctions. For this public assembly of the Church is distinguished by the varied quality of professions, of dignities and of orders, because one is the profession of monks, another of clerics, another of laymen; and among these one profession is of virgins, another of the continent, another of the married; and of dignities one is imperial, another pontifical, another prefectural, or whatever else can be called by the name of dignity. Further, of orders, one is priestly, another levitical, another sublevitical, or any other ecclesiastical order whatever; all which things are signified by [1148B] those parts described above, laudably placed in the body of the bride by the testimony of the bridegroom. For the eyes of the Church are the watchfulness and prudence of those set over her; the hair, the multitude of subjects; the teeth, the expositors of sacred Scripture; the lips, the elegance of preachers, or of readers reciting Holy Scripture; the cheeks, the modesty of those under discipline; the neck, the sublime eloquence of doctors; the breasts, the diligence of masters instructing the lesser; the distinct variety of all whom in the body of the Church the Psalmist praising together says: *There stood,* he says, *the queen on thy right hand in gilded clothing, surrounded with variety [n: (Psal. XLIV)]*. But of these one by one we must speak more fully in their place.

*How fair thou art,* he says, *my love, how fair thou art!* She whom he calls fair he also names his love, [1148C] to suggest that the one is productive of the other; for she who is adorned with so many kinds of beauties, which he adds below, is deservedly made his love, and she who is proved his love is assuredly shown to be fair: for he would not have made her, as his love, privy to his secret, unless he had coupled her to himself through grace, pleasing him by the beauty of the virtues. And by the repeated praise of beauty he approves the twin virtue of faith and of working, which are so inseparably joined to one another that the one without the other is always proved useless.

And adding the first member of the sevenfold beauty, *thy eyes,* he says, *are doves' [sic: colnmbarum] eyes, besides that which lieth hid within [sic: intrisecus].* As was noted before above, the eyes of the Church are the watchfulness and providence of those set over her [1148D] towards the utility of their subjects; because, as the eyes are certain guides and guardians to the body beneath them ( *for the blind man walketh in darkness and knoweth not whither [sic: ea] he goeth [n: (Joan. XII)]* [var: Vulg. Jo 12:35 *qui ambulant in tenebris, nescit quo vadat*; the wording is that of 1 Jo 2:11, *in tenebris ambulat, et nescit quo eat*]; *140* so prelates by their care and vigilance guard those subject to them, and lead them into the chosen way by preaching and action, set as watchmen in both respects, both for the necessity and utility of souls and of bodies. But these *eyes* are *doves', besides that which lieth hid within,* because innocently and simply they provide for carnal things, and prudently, by administering spiritual things, they foresee and beware the peril of their subjects: for when at times they are occupied in earthly things for the utility of their subjects, they look upon those earthly things not with a raven's eye but with a dove's, because without cupidity and carnal desire they seek and have them simply for sole [1149A] necessity; of whom the prophet says, *and as doves to their windows [n: (Isa. LX)]*; for what are our windows but the outward senses, through which we look out upon these visible things, either by desiring them or by setting little store by them? But the saints are *as doves to their windows,* because whatever they taste in outward things through the bodily senses they set out simply and innocently for their uses; they seize nothing in them with avidity and delight, but regard in them only the common necessity of themselves and of their own.

It is praiseworthy, then, to hold in this way the pursuit of the active life, but far more praiseworthy is that which lieth hid within, to have, namely, the virtue of the contemplative life; for outwardly they dispense outward things honourably, but [1149B] with what intention they do it lies open only to divine knowledge, while very often certain religious men, handling outward things at times, are judged by human eyes, which do not see hearts.

There follows the second part of the sevenfold beauty, in which it is said: *Thy hair is as flocks of goats, which come up from mount Galaad.*

By the hair, as was noted before, the multitude of subjects is designated, who, although they are not able to attain the perfection of their elders, yet adorn the assembly of the Church as hair adorns the head, adhering to their heads, that is, to their elders, by obeying according to their own manner and ability and by keeping the law appointed for them. For each one, in his own place and order, adorns and graces each profession according to the rule fixed for it by the fathers to be [1149C] kept in common, though in diverse manner. For although in the Church universal or particular not all live equally, not all, each set in his own private office, keep the rule equally, and very often exceed it, yet they are covered by the public and common ornament of religion and order; just as, on the contrary, the devotion of one man confers nothing of honour or honourableness on the whole body of the Church, if its whole institution be deformed by irreligiousness. In short, its religious community is its beauty and nobility, as also irreligious unanimity is its baseness and ignobility.

*141* Whence also it is said that these *hairs* are *as flocks of goats,* because in the several professions [1149D] there are certain flocks, multitudes gathered together; flocks indeed as regards the diversities of orders, or as regards particular Churches, but one flock as regards the unity of the faith and the Church universal. And they are flocks of goats, because all the ecclesiastical professions, though differing among themselves on account of the various qualities of orders, yet always look with equal intention to one end, while all, desiring heavenly things, adhere to God each in his own manner.

And goats of what kind? *Which have come up,* he says, *from mount Galaad.* Galaad is said to be *a heap of testimony;* and who is this heap of testimony, but a certain collection and sum of the Holy Scriptures, or of the institutions written down by the holy Fathers for the utility and honourableness of the Church? For what is [1150A] sacred Scripture but a certain heap of testimony, everywhere preaching and testifying of Christ? Of which testimony it is sung in the Psalm: *The testimony of the Lord is faithful, giving wisdom to little ones [n: (Psal. XVIII)]*. For the little ones, that is, the humble, understand the testimony of holy Scripture, and while they obey it by believing and living well they are wise; for the highest philosophy is the best manner of living. The institution of the holy Fathers also is a certain heap of testimony, because, while it is lawfully observed, it is certified to its observers for honourableness of life.

The *hair* of holy Church, therefore, is *as flocks of goats which have come up from mount Galaad,* because the multitudes of subjects advance daily by the testimonies of the holy [1150B] Scriptures and by the institutions [sic: *instititutis*] of those set over them, fixed for them for a common order of living.

VERSE 2.-- *Thy teeth are as flocks of shorn sheep, which have come up from the washing, all with twin offspring, and there is none barren among them.*

In the third place is set, *thy teeth are as flocks of shorn sheep, which have come up from the washing, all with twin offspring, and there is none barren in them.*

The teeth, as was written above, can, not absurdly, be taken as the expositors of sacred Scripture; because, as the teeth grind the food, so the holy expositors in the Holy Scriptures reduce the grossness of the letter to the subtlety of the spiritual sense, so that what could not be understood on the surface may, ground as it were [1150C] by these teeth, pass more easily into the belly of understanding.

But why are these *teeth as flocks of shorn sheep which have come up from the washing?* Namely on account of a mind freed from the business of the world, and washed from the dust of earthly action in the fountain of the divine Spirit. For they cannot advance to the *142* subtlety of so great an understanding unless such cares have been laid aside as certain burdens, and with the whole body of the inner man in the whole of that fountain of the Godhead, of which the Psalmist says to God: *Thou shalt make them drink of the torrent of thy pleasure, for with thee is the fountain of life [n: (Psal. XXXV)]*. And what of utility this washing or drinking confers he suggests when he adds, *and in thy light we shall see him [n: (ibid.)]* [var: Vulg. *lumen*, "light"], as if he said openly: thou pouring into us the light of knowledge, [1150D] we shall understand the obscure things of Holy Scripture.

The teeth, then, of holy Church are *as flocks of shorn sheep which have come up from the washing,* because the handlers of sacred Scripture, the more they have been freed from earthly actions and purged in the washing of divine inspiration, the more strongly are they able to break, and the more finely to grind, the whole literal solidity of the Old and New Testament.

For the allegorical or moral sense, flowing forth fittingly and usefully from the literal surface of the holy Scriptures, is the becoming whiteness and as it were the alluring beauty of these teeth.

Whence also is added: *All with twin offspring, and there is none barren among them.* For what are the twin [1151A] offspring of these, but either the double sense of the holy Scriptures, which are as it were broken by exposition, namely the allegorical and the moral, or the knowledge of the two Testaments, or the showing forth of the twofold charity? For since the sense in the whole body of Holy Scripture is threefold, namely historical, allegorical and moral, wherever the historical is expounded it is resolved into one of these two parts, namely allegory or morality, and very often the understanding of one and the same history is brought back into the senses of both parts, so that sometimes what the history sounds ought to be understood only mystically. Of which threefold sense of Holy Scripture it is said: *Write them threefold in thy heart [n: (Prov. XXII)]*. And they are enriched as it were with the twin offspring of the two testaments, because whatever [1151B] they expound they take up either from the Old or from the New Testament. Further, they are multiplied with the offspring of the twofold charity, because the love of God, whom they savour delightfully in Scripture itself, draws them to the labour of expounding sacred Scripture; the love of neighbour also, for whose utility they willingly take up this occupation for themselves.

In whom there is no barrenness of these twin offspring, because all whom the weight of earthly action does not burden, and whom the most healthful fountain of the divine Spirit washes, can delightfully give themselves to the business of expounding the books with this twin intention, with maternal [cj: *materna*; read *materia*, "matter"] love; looking by intention to allegory and morality, by matter to the Old and New Testament, by love to God and neighbour.

*[1151C] 143* VERSE 3.-- *Thy lips are as a scarlet fillet, and thy speech sweet; as a piece of a pomegranate, so are thy cheeks, besides that which lieth hid within.*



## *138* CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

Of which sevenfold beauty the proclamation is: *thy lips are as a scarlet fillet, and thy speech sweet:* the lips can be called the preachers of the divine utterance, or those who pronounce the utterances themselves elegantly by reading or by singing, and kindle the people of God, by sweetness of sense or of voice, to divine fear and love. Whence also they are compared to a scarlet fillet, because, as a fillet binds the hair together and orders it lest it be scattered, so the holy preachers, or those singing or reading sweetly in the Church, join the listening people by the bond of peace and unanimity, and make them sound together with one another in the harmony of faith [1151D] and love. For the fillet of the subjects is as it were dissonant and discordant from the fillet of the holy preachers, when they neither believe rightly nor work rightly; but when by their preaching they are led through to truth and rightness, they are as it were joined to them in faith and love; while they believe and love the same thing as they themselves, and agree with them in the law of God. And when they hear and attend to those pronouncing the same truth, whether with fair harmony or with an assertion probable to reason, through the grace of compunction they bind themselves the more strongly in the love of God.

Whence also the fillet to which they are compared is said to be *scarlet.* For scarlet is of a fiery colour, and what is charity but fire? For when some, hearing of that [1152A] highest good which God is, are pricked to well-doing for the sake of receiving that good, assuredly they are kindled with the fire of divine charity.

The lips of the Church, then, that is, the holy preachers, or those who dutifully compose the preaching itself, are *as a scarlet fillet,* because by the dignity and sweetness of the very things they preach they set the minds of the hearers alight to the love of eternal life

Whence is added, *thy speech sweet:* for the weak are drawn by gentler precepts and repelled by harsher ones; but under the law sinners were commanded to be punished, and the guilty received retaliation, when they were bidden to give *eye for eye, tooth for tooth [n: (Exod. XXI)]*; but in the time of grace the speech of the Church is sweet, because to sinners confessing their faults [1152B] not punishment but mercy is promised, and the guilty, if they look back, are not fined with retaliation but are absolved by penance. Or else the holy preachers are a scarlet fillet for this reason, that by their preaching they bind in the superfluities of thoughts and works, so that through zeal for holy working they may burn with the fire of divine charity, and through contempt of the world may redden with a certain *144* martyrdom of the mind; for scarlet is both fiery and red, and those who glow with divine charity do not fear to suffer all adverse things for God.

Further, how great a kind of the sevenfold beauty proclaims the modesty of the disciplined in the Church, when it is said, *as a piece of a pomegranate, so are thy cheeks, besides that which lieth hid within.* It is called the "malum punicum" because its kind is from the Punic region, [1152C] carried over from there; and it is also called the "malum granatum", because it contains within itself a multitude of grains; and a piece of pomegranate reddens without and is white within, and gives off more fragrance broken than whole. The cheeks of the Church, then, are said to be *as a piece of a pomegranate,* because the discipline of the religious, who in the house of discipline hold forth the habit of honourableness, displays modesty outwardly in the face with a rosy beauty, which proceeds from the inward diligence of a mind graciously shining white. For the whiteness of chastity which shines in the mind begets the redness of modesty in the face, when the religious soul fears to commit any such thing as would detract from its honourableness or its discipline. Whence, by contrast, to the reprobate mind, which boldly [1152D] does not blush to sin, it is said through the prophet: *The forehead of a harlot woman is made thine, and thou wouldst not blush [n: (Jer. III)]*.

The lowering of the countenance, then, and a glorious composure of the eyes, is the sign of a religious and chaste mind, and therefore those composed honourably in their conduct are fittingly said to be *as a piece of a pomegranate,* who reverently convey outwardly, by habit and conduct, the comeliness of holiness which they bear inwardly in intention and thought. And though the outward bearing, religiously composed, is praiseworthy, yet far more praiseworthy is that inward habit of the mind which is known to God alone, since the holy soul hardly or never suffices outwardly [sic: *est*] to show how greatly it burns within with divine charity.

Whence, when the bridegroom had first said of the bride, *as a [1153A] piece of a pomegranate, so are thy cheeks,* he added, *besides that which lieth hid within.*

For what is it that lieth hid within, but the force of charity which is manifest to God alone, and works outwardly whatever there is of honourableness or holiness.

The disciplined hearts of holy Church, then, are as it were *a piece of a pomegranate,* *besides that which lieth hid within,* because the whiteness of the virtues which they have inwardly through the grace of God, this they display outwardly through the form of patience and modesty; but the very force which contains in itself, by the working of the Holy Spirit, the whole quickening and strengthening efficacy, they leave to the judgment of God alone, while they stand indeed in great hope. But in their very hope they are not without fear. The force of divine charity they indeed [1153B] feel in themselves, but by what scrutiny the things which, out of that charity, they proclaim by conduct and life are weighed before God, they do not know.

* 145* VERSE 4.-- *Thy neck is as the tower of David, which is built with bulwarks; a thousand bucklers hang from it, all the armour of valiant men.*

But in the sixth place of the beauty enumerated this proclamation is set, *thy neck is as the tower of David, which is built with bulwarks, a thousand bucklers hang from it, all the armour of valiant men.* We noted above that by the neck is signified the sublime eloquence of the doctors, which is compared to a tower for this reason, that it is raised aloft by the depth of its words and senses. But this is called *the tower of David,* that is, *strong of hand,* because, namely, whatever of comeliness or [1153C] sublimity the utterances of the doctors contain in themselves, whether by word or by writing, is assuredly of his grace who is spiritually called strong of hand, because it is his *To spare the subject, and to war down the proud.*
[n: (VIRG., Aeneid., IV, 654.)]

As it is written of him, that *he resisteth the proud, but giveth grace to the humble [n: (Jac. IV)]*. For he rests in the heart of the humble, and distributes to them fittingly the gifts of his knowledge, as it shall please him, to one thus, to another thus.

And what are the bulwarks with which this tower is built, but the fortifications of strong and sublime sentences? For by the authorities of the sentences with which the doctors thunder, the Church is fortified and strengthened [1153D] against the dogmas and deeds of the perverse, lest, founded upon the rock of faith, she should be able to fear any contrivances of the contrary and adverse father [cj: *patris*; read *partis*, "party"]. For in this are beheld the counsels and precepts of the virtues, and the wonderful deeds and miracles of the holy Fathers.

Whence also is added, *a thousand bucklers hang from it;* for what is designated by the thousandfold number, which is set for perfection, but universality? For the thousand bucklers are all the instructions of the virtues, which fortify us against the assailing engines of the vices: For if *The breast [sic: petus] seethes with avarice and with wretched desire?*
*There are words and sounds by which thou mayest soothe this pain*
*And lay aside a great part of the disease.*
*Dost thou swell with love of praise? There are sure expiations which,*
*The little book thrice purely read, will be able to restore thee.*
[1154A] *The envious, the wrathful, the sluggard, the drunkard, the lover,*
*No one is so wild that he may not grow gentle,*
*If only he lend a patient ear to cultivation.*
[n: (HORAT., Ep. I, I, 39.)]

For if the glory of this world delights thee, hear the apostle John saying: *Love not the world, nor the things which are in the world; if any man shall have loved the world, the charity of the Father is not in him [n: (I Joan. II)]*: if thou art tempted by the vice of vainglory, hear the Apostle saying: *Let us not be made desirous of vainglory [n: (Gal. VI)]*: if thou art tempted by avarice, hear the same saying: *That God loveth a cheerful giver [n: (II Cor. IX)]*: if thou art tempted by lust, hear him again saying: *Know you not that your bodies are the temple of the Holy Spirit? If any man violate this temple, he shall destroy [1154B] him; for the temple of God is holy, which you are [n: (I Cor. VI)]*. *146* If anger strikes the mind, hear what is said: *Be angry, and sin not;* and that *the anger of man worketh not the justice of God [n: (Ps. IV)]*. If the sin of disobedience corrupts the mind, hear it written *that obedience is better than victims, and to hearken rather than to offer the fat of rams, because it is as the sin of witchcraft to resist, and as the crime of idolatry to refuse to acquiesce [n: (I Reg. XV)]*: if impatience weighs down the mind, attend to what is written, that *the patient man is better than the stormer of cities [n: (Prov. XVI)]*. If pride presses upon the mind, hear what is written, that *God resisteth the proud, but giveth grace to the humble [n: (I Pet. V)]*.

Behold, these are the bucklers which hang from the tower and the bulwarks [1154C] of the most eloquent doctors, whose sentences and authorities are the strongest fortification of the Church, while their precepts and counsels are to every soul, against vices and sins, both instruction and fortification. *A thousand,* then, *bucklers hang from it,* because there is no vice against which a fortification is not found out of all the sayings and writings of the holy Catholic Fathers. But because in the same writings are recited both the valiant deeds of the saints and the precepts of the divine law most constantly kept by them, it is well added, *all the armour of valiant men.* For there thou wilt find how valiantly they fought against vices and sins with the arms of the spiritual warfare, whose armour was the observance of the divine commandments and [1154D] the imitation of the Fathers who went before. *A thousand,* then, *bucklers and all the armour of valiant men hang from it,* because whatever of strength one is pleased to seek against the enemies of the soul, one may find in the most learned sentences and the magnificent deeds of the fathers.

But though the bulwarks and the bucklers have the same office of fortifying, yet they differ in this, that the bulwarks stand immovably, while the bucklers can be held and moved by the hand. And what can the bulwarks be called, but the miracles of the saints? And what the bucklers, but the excellent cultivations of the holy virtues. In Holy Scripture, then, which the utterances of the doctors bring forth and commend, are narrated the wonderful miracles of the saints, which we indeed cannot do and as it were move; but the works of the virtues each of us can exercise [1155A] and, as though holding them in the hand, move by diverse pursuits according to our small measure, if we burn with the love of God.

From the neck of holy Church, then, *a thousand bucklers hang, and all the armour of valiant men,* because in Holy Scripture, which the eloquence of the doctors uncovers and adorns, she finds, in general, against the darts of the vices both protecting safeguards and repelling armaments.

VERSE 5.-- *Thy two breasts are as two fawns of a roe, twins, which feed among the lilies till the day break, and the shadows retire.*



## *138* CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

*147* But the seventh proclamation of the beauty proclaimed is this: *Thy two breasts are as two fawns of a roe, twins, which feed among the lilies till the day break and the shadows retire.* As was shown above, [1155B] by the two breasts can be understood the diligence of the masters who instruct the lesser, the twin teaching of piety and of knowledge: of piety, when they form them in the honourableness of the active and the contemplative life, or in the love of God and of neighbour; of knowledge, when they instruct them in the reading of the Old and the New Testament, or in the historical and allegorical exposition of that same Scripture. Which *two breasts are as two fawns of a roe, twins,* because these instructors of the lesser in the Church are [sic: *pavi*] little in their own eyes, acting and teaching according to the doctrine of their elders, and presuming nothing of their own knowledge, following the holy fathers in life, conduct and doctrine as sons follow mothers, and so tending to the things above, and in these things advancing daily in wit and understanding, and leading with them those whom they teach to the understanding [1155C] and desiring of higher things. Who are well compared not simply to fawns but to twins, because of those same twin teachings of piety and knowledge cleaving inseparably to one another, so that, as though begotten in one moment, neither exists prior in any part of itself. For the Church of Christ has always had nurslings of both lives, that is, of the active and the contemplative; she has always flourished in the love both of God and of neighbour; she has always been bright with the knowledge of both Testaments. And the two testaments are so inseparably joined to one another that the one is significative or expositive of the other; for the Old signifies the New, the New expounds the Old. So too the love of God without the love of neighbour, nor that of neighbour without the love of God, can in any wise exist, [1155D] because the one is probative of the other. For he who loves his neighbour is proved to love God, because he is invited by the love of God to love his neighbour. Neither can he be a lover of the contemplative life who is ignorant of the active life, nor does he hold the active who does not desire the contemplative.

The two, then, doing at one time one and the same thing, because they are convertible by the consequence of subsisting; for it follows that he who understands the Old Testament understands the New, since they are one, this in figure, that in truth; and that he who loves his neighbour loves God, and conversely, which must always be together; but he cannot contemplate God who does not know how to act well. These two, then, existing on both sides in an undivided mode, are called twins, as it were under one generation, [1156A] and are proclaimed in the two breasts of the Church in the seventh and last place of beauty.

Further, the historical and the allegorical exposition are not always so inseparably joined, because very often the pure history alone is to be held, sometimes the allegory alone, when many things can stand according to the letter *148* which can by no means stand according to the spirit, and certain things are understood according to the spirit which in no way are taken according to the letter.

But where they fittingly agree together, the aforesaid likeness of twins is not unfittingly attended to, so that in the two breasts of the Church they are proclaimed undividedly as it were two fawns of a roe, twins. But because every pursuit of piety looks to a certain end, for nothing of good, except for the attaining of eternal life, [1156B] is taught and learned, he adds:

*Which feed among the lilies till the day break and the shadows retire;* for the qualities of all the kinds set down above, which were proclaimed in the beauty of the Church's breasts, feed among the lilies, that is, are nourished in the whiteness and fragrance of the virtues, because no one makes progress in the honourableness of the active and contemplative life, or in the love of God and neighbour, without the page of each testament, except through cleanness of mind and the exercise of good action.

And how long? *till the day break and the shadows retire,* that is, until the day of eternal felicity dawn and the night of this mortality fail, and then that be seen perfectly in truth which here was seen through [1156C] a glass in a dark manner, and what here was held in hope be held there in reality. There those two breasts, which we distinguished above in fourfold wise, will be seen in that eternal day, not in labour but in reward, which the breaking of the day and the retiring of the shadow signify; for the shadow signifies labour, the day reward.

But this word *donec,* "till", is set not for a limit but for the everlasting, that we may understand that we must labour so long in this life by working well, until we lay hold of eternal life by resting happily.

VERSE 6.-- *I will go to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense.*

After the beloved, marked out by an excellent variety, had enumerated the kinds of beauty in the body of the beloved, that is, [1156D] of the Church, he adds the general comeliness of all the members which they ought to have who wish to belong to her body. But this comeliness in a twofold way, that is, by the mortification of the flesh and by the pleading of a devout spirit: for he who walks according to the flesh, and does not by the spirit mortify the deeds of the flesh, is not of that fair body of the Church which has not spot nor wrinkle; *for they that are Christ's have crucified their flesh, with the vices and concupiscences (Gal V)*; the pleading of a devout spirit, too, is born of the most clement breathing of the divine Spirit, because *the Spirit himself asketh for us with unspeakable groanings [n: (Rom. VIII)]*. For "asketh" is said for "maketh to ask", because whose mind [1157A] he fills, he moves *149* to the grace of compunction, so that either from the consideration of his sins, or from the delay of the kingdom that shall profit him, he may groan, groaning may entreat, entreating may at some time receive either pardon of sins or the crown of desired beatitude.

The mortification of the flesh, then, is expressed by myrrh, because of the bitterness of labour and suffering; and the pleading of a devout spirit is understood by frankincense, because of the grief of slight compunction or penance, smoking as it were, or the ardour of charity, from which the pure prayer of the supplicating mind is directed to God. But though both virtues, namely to mortify the flesh and to pray often, are of the highest honourableness and utility in the body [1157B] of holy Church, yet that one is proved the more excellent which is acquired with the greater labour of mind and body.

Wherefore, that their merit may be distinguished, the myrrh is proclaimed by the name of a mountain, the frankincense by that of a hill: he says, then, *I will go to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense,* as if he said more openly in other words: to the minds of those who labour with this intention, that they may mortify themselves to vices and sins, and who study to obtain this by assiduous prayers, I will go through co-operating and conferring grace, that I may prove those minds worthy of my indwelling which the beauty of these virtues delights, since I am ready both to succour those who labour and to assent to those who supplicate.

[1157C]VERSE 7.-- *Thou art all fair, love, and there is not a spot in thee.*

Since, he says, I have above extolled thee with sevenfold proclamations of beauties, and then have graciously regarded in thee the mountain of myrrh and the hill of frankincense, for the subjection of the mortified flesh is indeed a great eminence of mind, whence is born the contemplative pleading of the free spirit towards God, but by the mortification of the flesh vices and sins are so lulled to sleep that there is nothing which resists the spirit, but so great both the purity and the evenness of the mind, that thou canst admit no defilements in thy body which might avail to blot out the grace of thy comeliness.

*Thou art all fair then, my love, and there is not a spot in thee;* a spot, I say, is not in thee, that is, it has no [1157D] being, because even if some one has come upon thee for a little while and by human frailty, it is quickly repelled by compunction of heart and humble confession and daily prayer.

VERSE 8.-- *Come from Libanus, my bride, come from Libanus, come: thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana, from the top of Sanir and Hermon, from the dens of lions, from the mountains [sic: montitibus] of leopards?*

Wherefore, *come from Libanus, my bride, come from Libanus,* that is, because, every concupiscence of the flesh being now conquered and mortified, thou art made white by the cleanness of heart and body; *come from Libanus,* that is, from this thy whitening, for Libanus is interpreted *whitening [sic: canditatio]*; and come not once, but again, because not *150* only must thou please in thought, [1158A] but in deed also; come, I say, from this thy whitening; for thou canst not come unless thou be white; and as my Bride, who art now through this whitening worthy of my embraces, pass over also into the fellowship of my kingdom, that thou mayest henceforth hold the unchangeable state and habit of a whitened soul. Come also a third time, because it profits not to please only in thought and deed, unless thou please also by perseverance in the good work itself, which is the representative certainty of future felicity.

Or else, that thou mayest proclaim the faith of the holy Trinity: come preaching the Father, come preaching the Son, come preaching the Holy Spirit, and, distinguishing the one God in these three persons, establish the holy sacraments of religion, and confirm the whole world in the [1158B] unchangeable truth of this faith.

Or else: come by faith, come by hope, come by charity, that is, all the perfection of good work, take up by faith, lay up by hope, embrace by charity. Or else, the sacrament of my body and blood and the reality of the sacrament: come by faithfully believing, come by reverently handling, come by healthfully receiving, that, incorporated into me through this my remedy of the soul, thou mayest truly be called my bride and love.

But to an incorporation of this kind thou canst not come, except from Libanus, that is, either white by baptism; or, if for fault of thine thou hast been cut off from my body, receiving back thy former whiteness by the fountain of penance.

And all these instructions of truth and faith, [1158C] scatter them through the regions of the whole world, that my praise may be announced through thee even to the ends of the earth; let those who seek, seek my sacraments, recognize them and receive them, and let their conversion become thy reward. For this is what follows, *thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana, from the top of Sanir and Hermon, from the dens of lions, from the mountains of leopards.*

By these three names of mountains are designated three kinds of worldly heights, by whose destruction holy Church has attained perfect peace, and by whose humbling she has merited a perpetual crown.

The first height was in the worshippers of idols, who, when they had known the good of the reason implanted in them and the beauty of visible things, [1158D] the Creator of them all, *glorified him not as God, or gave thanks, but became vain in their thoughts, and their foolish heart was darkened, because they changed the truth of God into a lie, and served the creature rather than the Creator [n: (Rom. I)]*: and thus to despise God was a certain height of pride.

The second height was the persecution of the reprobate, who, gainsaying God by perverse conduct, pursued the heralds of truth and justice with great injuries, afflicting them with many punishments and driving them even to death; and thus to resist God himself in his elect was also a certain height of pride.

*151* Further, the third height was the perverse [1159A] speech or writing of the heretics, by which they tried to destroy the true faith through human reason, and laboured to corrupt the simplicity of Holy Scripture with a certain haughtiness of dialectical discourse; who, while they sought to seem wise, were made fools, desiring through a certain pride of mind only to show off their own knowledge.



## *138* CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

Now Amana is interpreted *night bird,* Sanir *tooth of watches,* Hermon *anathema:* which significations of the names can be fitted first indeed to the very head of all the wicked, of whom it is written: *For he is king over all the children of pride [n: (Job XLI)]*, so that he may be called night bird, and tooth of watches, and anathema. For *a night* indeed *bird,* which by another name is called the owl or the light-shunner, the devil is rightly named, [1159B] who hates the light, that is, good works, and loves sins, which are the works of darkness; and, as it is written: *He sleepeth in the shade, in the secret of the chamber [n: (Job XL)]* [var: Vulg. Job 40:16 *in secreto calami*, "in the covert of the reed"], because the minds round which he flies in tempting, while he subjugates them through consent, he prepares for himself a resting-place as it were in the shade of sin. He also is called *tooth of watches,* because it is written of him that *as a roaring lion he goeth about seeking whom he may devour [n: (I Petr. V)]*, whose watching is never to rest from evil will. He is also not unfittingly called *Anathema,* because he is sequestered and as it were excommunicated from the fellowship of the good spirits; and he hastens to drag others too with him into destruction, that, as he himself, so they also may become anathema from God.

What befits the head, then, can, not unfittingly, [1159C] befit the members also: and we said a little above that by these three names *of mountains* three kinds of worldly heights can be signified, which we placed in three qualities of perverse men, namely in the worshippers of idols, and the persecutors of the saints, and the heretics.

But it must be seen how the interpretations of these names befit such men: the worshippers of idols, namely, can, not absurdly, be called a *night bird,* because of blindness and lightness of mind: blindness, because, as this bird flees the day and wanders rather by night, so they, unwilling to receive the light of truth and faith, embraced rather the darkness of unbelief, so long as they were not freed from this unbelief by the grace of God. Further, because of lightness they are compared to a bird, [1159D] because they wandered in such uncertainty of error that they could grasp nothing true, nothing stable, in their worship. But the persecutors of the saints can be called *tooth of watches* because of the savagery and monstrousness of the persecutions, so that the tooth *152* may be the savagery itself, and the watches the persecutions that have no rest: for in Jeremiah also it is written that, when the Lord had said: *What seest thou, Jeremiah?* he answered: *I see a watching rod; and the Lord: Well,* he says, *hast thou said, for I will watch over my word, to perform it [sic: illua] [n: (Jer. I)]* [var: Vulg. Jr 1:12 *Bene vidisti*, "thou hast seen well"]. As, then, in this place the watching rod is taken for correction, not resting because of the sins of the people until it be fulfilled, so the savagery of the persecutions, watching in the punishment and slaughter of the saints, can be called *tooth of watches.*

[1160A]But the heretics are called *anathema,* that is, separated from God, because, while they are separated from the communion of the Church for the perversity of their faith by the steel of cutting off, that is, of excommunication, assuredly they are separated from the head, that is, from Christ. And the Apostle says: *If any man preach to you other than what you have received from us, let him be anathema [n: (Gal. I)]*; but the heretics preached other than what had been preached by the apostles: therefore they were anathema, and so, anathematized and excommunicated, they went out from the Church.

Therefore the bridegroom cries *Come* to the bride three times, to show that she is to be crowned three times from the head or top of these; *thou shalt be crowned,* he says, *from the head of Amana, from the top of Sanir and Hermon.* And how shalt thou be crowned? [1160B] First *from the head of Amana,* that is, from the worshippers of idols, who, converted at thy preaching, have grown up in faith and action like good trees, whose conversion and progress was assuredly the adornment of thy head. Then also *thou shalt be crowned from the top of Sanir,* that is, from the persecutors of the saints, who, at thy exhortations and at the terrors of miracles, laying aside their savagery, submitted themselves to the yoke of Christ, whose conversion and humbling also is the amplification of thy crown in return for thy labour.

Thirdly *thou shalt be crowned from the top of Hermon,* that is, the proud eloquence of the heretics, who, after they had been changed from the depravity of their false knowledge by thy eloquence of sound doctrine, reasonable and joined with wisdom, [1160C] defended with the strongest assertion of truth the faith which they formerly attacked, whose reformation to the right faith is thy crown and the reward of thy labour.

And since, on the contrary, as *the soul of the just is the seat of God [n: (Sap. VII)]*, so the soul of the perverse is the dwelling of the unclean Spirit, there follows: *From the dens of lions, from the mountains of leopards:* those whom we distinguished under the names of the three mountains could rightly be called dens of lions, because, while the malignant spirits subjected them to themselves through malice, nourishing that malice in them by their own suggestion and the consent of those men, they rested in them as it were in certain dens. For the lions are the malignant spirits, which roar round about to deceive *153* the human race, thirsting for the blood of the soul, that is, desiring to take away [1160D] from the soul the nourishments of the virtues. For as in the blood of a man is his life, so in the nourishments of the virtues the life of the soul is [sic: *es*]

But the mountains of leopards are, over pride, the same hearts of the reprobate, and the leopards are the same malignant spirits, because of their fierceness and the manifold kinds, as it were the varied colours, of their thousandfold frauds. *From the dens,* then, *of lions and the mountains of leopards* the Church is crowned, because she by her preaching makes the minds of those who, brooding over earthly and perverse doings, had been dens of demons, seats of divine grace; and the hearts of those also who, holding forth a proud and as it were singular life or knowledge, brought forth the varied colours of sects and heresies, she makes worthy of the divine indwelling in the spirit of humility; out of [1161A] their conversion and progress, surely, she is heaped with the greater fruit of her holy labour.

VERSE 9.-- *Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, bride, thou hast wounded my heart with one of thy eyes, and with one hair of thy neck.*

Still further the bridegroom, that is, Christ, approves and admires the zeal and progress of the bride, that is, of the Church, or of each faithful soul, and the comeliness and merit of that progress; so that, whereas above he first enumerated seven parts of her beauty, as it were in her outward habit, then set forth the twofold quality of the inward habit, and thirdly offered a threefold crown also for the progress of others, in all which he showed the great good pleasure of his condescension, now he brings in a more eminent, and as it were [1161B] above the rest the greatest, part of beauty, a twofold one, in which the sister and bride, by the greatness of her very dignity, has not only moved the heart of the bridegroom in that same good pleasure of his, but has even wounded it: sister through faith, bride through holy and true love. But what is this twofold part: *One of the eyes and one hair of the neck?* If in each one's sense we consider twin eyes as in one body, we shall place one on the right side, the other on the left. Now the eye of the left side is the wondering at all visible things, wonderfully and orderly disposed; from the consideration of which, though through a glass and in a dark manner, it gathers some magnitude, beauty and power of the Creator, and in [1161C] their administration and government his wisdom and goodness. Further, the eye of the right side is led away above, outside these visible things, when it is loosed from every contagion of this worldly desire, and is fixed upon the very face of the Creator by the gaze of contemplative charity. By this one of the bride's eyes, then, *154* the heart of the bridegroom is wounded, because, while the holy mind so couples to itself the supernal grace through the force of divine charity that it receives the name of Seraphim, that is, of burning, out of its very ardour it brings as it were a certain wound of love to the divinity united to it.

*In one,* however, *hair of the neck* we understand the subtlety of preaching: but since there are many subtleties of preaching, which yet is its one and singular subtlety, as it were one hair of the neck? According to the parts [1161D] of the faith, both in the deity of the Trinity and in the incarnation of Christ. And what is the one hair in the deity of the Trinity? One God in three persons, whose incomprehensible reason of Divinity we are bidden to believe, are not permitted to discuss; and what is the one hair of the neck in the incarnation of Christ? *God made man.* For who can in any wise investigate how the Word is embodied, how the supreme and life-giving Spirit is animated within the womb of a mother, how he who has no beginning comes to be and is conceived?

But in this one hair of the neck, on both sides, the heart of the beloved is wounded, because, while each one believes this, which is not gathered by human reason, he is strongly bound together in the love of almighty God through the merit of faith; *but faith has no merit, to which human reason affords proof.* [1162A] For faith is not against reason, but above reason; and therefore he who will believe nothing except what he comprehends by reason, as the philosophers, has no merit by his faith; but he who believes that which is not contrary to reason, and yet is above reason: whence the Apostle says: *The word is nigh in thy mouth, and in thy heart, this is the word of faith. [n: (Rom. X.)]* Christ too is not against reason, but above reason; *in one,* then, *of the eyes* love, *in one hair of the neck* faith is expressed. But because faith working through love is the foundation of all good things, while without love there is no virtue in faith itself, *for the devils believe and tremble [n: (Jac. II)]*, and for this reason these two cannot be distinguished where they join a mind of perfect virtue to God: by one [1162B] eye, then, of speculative love, and by one hair, that is, by the subtlety of faith, which consists chiefly in two things, namely the mystery of the divinity and the sacrament of the incarnation, the heart of the beloved is wounded, because by the force of a mind abounding in faith and love it is in some manner pierced toward knowing the more; whence not once but again, repeating, he says: *Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, bride, thou hast wounded my heart.* For very commendable before the eyes of almighty God is both faith working through love and love advancing through faith: faith, namely, the foundation of all good. love, building upon it, the grace and adornment of that foundation.



## *138* CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

In another way also there can be understood *155* the one of the eyes and the one hair of the neck, namely sameness and unchangeableness, [1162C] as regards the state of the mind in its purpose of good; so that in the one of the eyes there is always the same and unfailing intention of spiritual actions, and in the one hair of the neck always the same and persevering habit of humility and lowliness of mind; whence it is written: *A holy man continueth in wisdom as the sun, but a fool is changed as the moon [n: (Eccli. XXVII)]*, and of the kine carrying the ark of God it is written that *they went straight on, and walked by one way, and turned not aside to the right hand nor to the left. [n: (I Reg. VI.)]* For what is the ark of God, but the mind of the saint, in which divinity is contained through the gifts of diverse graces; but he who carries it must not turn aside from the way of justice, lest, walking by a perverse way, when divine grace withdraws [1162D] from the mind, he be no longer said to bear the ark of God at all. The one of the eyes, then, is the one way of those who walk rightly before themselves, as is written of the holy living creatures, that *every one went before his face:* the one of the eyes [sic: *soculorum*] is the same countenance of the mind, always *forgetting the things that are behind, and stretching forth to those that are before it [n: (Philip. III)]*. The one hair of the neck is always the same littleness of humility, and a thinness so great that, though the holy man be of great merit with God, he so despises himself among men that he scarcely permits what he is to be seen in him, as one hair, though it be something, is yet scarcely seen and held. The one hair of the neck is the sameness of a mind humbling itself to God through obedience, in the state of whose purpose it so perseveres that it is neither lifted up by prosperity nor takes delight in adversities on the contrary it is said of the reprobate [1163A] mind that *it walked with neck stretched out and nape upraised [n: (Isa. III)]*; and of the Jews who did not humble themselves to God through obedience, *with stiff neck and untameable heart. [n: (Act. VII.)] [var: Vulg. Act 7:51 incircumcisis cordibus et auribus, "uncircumcised in heart and ears"] With one*, then, *of the eyes and with one hair of the neck* the beloved is wounded, because there greatly pleases him the one and the same persevering intention and discernment of spiritual actions, and also the one and ever stable humility of a mind bowed to God through obedience.

VERSE 10.-- *How fair are thy paps, my sister, bride! Thy breasts are fairer than wine, and the odour of thy ointments above all spices.*

It must be diligently considered what this praiseworthy beauty of the paps, or of the breasts, of the bride is, that is, of the Church, or of any holy soul whatever; [1163B] for as in paps or breasts is the nourishment of little ones and of bodies newly begotten, so there can be certain spiritual nourishments by which souls still tender and weak may receive some increases of holiness, either themselves from themselves or others from them: themselves from themselves *156* through exercise, others from them through instruction.

But first it must be seen what these paps, or these breasts, are, and then what their beauty is, thus praised by the bridegroom: I do not think it contrary to reason if we say that these paps or these breasts are the knowledge of truth and the love of the virtues. But since there are two creatures, one spiritual and the other corporal, we believe it is not beside the matter if for each we appoint two qualities of increase, as it were two paps, or two [1163C] breasts. For as there are two creatures, as we have already said, one spiritual and the other corporal, so the good of the creature also is twofold, one of the spiritual, the other of the corporal; for the good of the spiritual creature consists chiefly in the two things which we set down above in the signification of the paps or breasts, that is, in the knowledge of truth and the love of virtue. But the good of the bodily creature is either in itself or outside itself: the good in itself is the integrity of the senses and affections according to the five senses; the good outside itself is the ordered distribution among the creatures: for whatever is corrupted or amended is for man's sake, and whatever is good or evil is good or evil to man. And since all goods, [1163D] whether inward or outward, are the gifts of almighty God, the privation of either is the anger of God and the punishment of man, as, on the contrary, the bestowal of them is the grace of God and the salvation of man: but the knowledge of truth and the love of virtue are to be considered in particular; in each soul they always receive increases, as though from certain paps and breasts of exercises, because the exercises themselves admit of more and less, more if they persist, less if they cease, namely so that they know the truth and love virtue more or less. For these two are so bound together with one another, as two breasts naturally in one body, that he cannot love virtue who has not known the truth, and he who knows the truth cannot but love virtue

[1164A]But if we look at the whole body of the Church universally, as regards itself it does not admit of more and less in knowing truth or loving virtue, she who has an imperfect knowledge of truth and love of virtue. But as regards others whom she believes in Christ, increases of holiness accrue to her daily from the knowledge of truth and the love of virtue.

On both sides, moreover, there are first paps, then breasts; because, as paps are those of girls, who cannot yet have the nourishment of milk but hold the hope of having it, while breasts are those of women, who have abundance of such nourishment, so the Church in those whom she nourishes, or each soul in itself, first has indeed a certain hope through the *157* aptitude of a mind applying itself to good things and always advancing to [1164B] better, and then, now grown up as it were in faith and love, begins to have so full a knowledge of truth and love of virtue that it can nourish all its senses, both inward and outward, and bring them to perfection through the grace of God: and we say here that the outward senses are to be nourished, not that they may be able to do better bodily, but spiritually, lest, badly guarded, they become instruments of sin, as the prophet reproves sinners concerning them, saying: *Death is entered through your windows [n: (Jer. IX)]* [var: Vulg. Jr 9:21 *ascendit mors per fenestras nostras*, "death is come up through our windows"].

And the Church holds out the nourishments of these her breasts in three ways, namely by things, by deeds and by words: by things, as in the sacraments, without which no salvation can come to the soul; by deeds, as in the acts of the saints, by whose examples she is provoked and invited; [1164C] by words, as in the precepts and admonitions of Holy Scripture, by which she is nourished daily to eternal life

We have said, then, what the paps or breasts of the bride are; now it must be said what their beauty is, thus praised by the bridegroom: this beauty is not accidental, such as can be present and absent without the corruption of the subject, but essential, consisting in the property of its own nature: for that the knowledge of truth and the love of virtue are beautiful, what else is it than that they are? For it is beautiful to know the truth and to love virtue, as it is beautiful to be chaste, or just, or humble, or patient, or obedient; and chastity and justice, humility, patience and obedience are a certain beauty, and [1164D] these, as we have said, are not beautiful accidentally in the subjects which partake of them, but essentially in themselves; for if they are, they are beautiful; if they are not beautiful, neither are they, because while they are corrupted they are not beautiful either; because they cease to be what they were. For chastity, or any other virtue, while it is corrupted, how is it beautiful, since that no longer is whose corruption is evil?

Since, then, the good of the soul consists chiefly in two things, by which it is nourished to eternal life as though from breasts, which are the knowledge of truth and the love of virtue, the corruption of knowledge can be called ignorance, of which the Apostle says: *He that is ignorant, shall be ignored [n: (I Cor. XIV)]*, and the corruption of love *covetousness, which is the root of all evils [n: (I Tim. VI)]*. And the more [1165A] this ignorance and covetousness grows, the more the good of the soul is corrupted: for the soul, since it is an incorporeal substance, cannot be corrupted according to its substance, but according to the good in which it partakes, which is the more *158* worn away and diminished in it the more it is itself deformed by sins.

Fair, then, are the paps or breasts of holy Church in general, or of each holy soul in particular, because both the one for her sons and the other in itself shine with so copious an abundance of good senses in the knowledge of truth and love of virtue, that from them flow forth the nourishments of all spiritual goods, and natural goods also, such as reason, wit and memory, flourish the more, and so the whole [1165B] beauty of the soul is formed by partaking of these.

But what is it, that these *breasts* are called *fairer than wine,* when in wine there is riotousness [n: (Ephes. V)], while in these breasts there is the holy and sober delight of the soul, and on this account there can be no right comparison of these at all? Truly it is through the figure tapinosis, which is the lowering of a great thing, that these are called fairer than wine; not that there is any praiseworthy beauty in wine, but because the things aforesaid, namely the knowledge of truth and the love of virtue, are better and fairer in all things to the rational soul, for their utility and sweetness, than all the delights of the world which are signified by wine; since these corrupt the soul by partaking of them, those refresh it. In the same way, finally, [1165C] what follows: *And the odour of thy ointments above all spices:* if we take the odour of the ointments to be the good fame of the virtues, what comparison is there of spiritual and corruptible things? Since indeed there can be both a corruption of the good which is in the spiritual creature, and a corruption of the good which is in the corporal creature; because, as there are two creatures, spiritual and corporal, as we have also already said above, there are also two goods, one in the spiritual, the other in the corporal creature. The good of the spiritual creature is virtue; the good of the corporal creature is the ordered distribution among the creatures: the corruption, then, of the good which comes about in the spiritual creature is, as in the soul, sin and fault, but either punishment or towards punishment. If, then, by the incorruption [1165D] of the spiritual good they unite to God, but by the incorruption of the corporal good they not only do not unite to God, but very often are separated, when through the very delight of it we very often forget ourselves and are led away from the good of our purpose, while by its corruption, when we are afflicted, we are sometimes led back to good things, what comparison can there be in so great an unlikeness.

But in this comparison the argument is from the lesser to the greater, in this manner: If corporal goods, that is, the odour of spices, are of such sweetness, how much more spiritual goods, that is, the odour of the virtues: an incorruptible good incomparable to a corruptible one, as the soul, which is incorruptible, to the corruptible body.

*159* But I call the soul incorruptible according to [1166A] its own substance, which is simple and incorporeal, as the incorruptible good which consists in the soul is so called according to substance, not according to addition. For good can be spoken of in two ways, substantively and adjectivally: for good in itself is not corrupted, but it is said to be corrupted by privation of good, when it withdraws from the soul and is changed into evil, as the soul itself also is not corrupted in itself, but is said to be corrupted by privation of good, because the privation of good is evil.

It is said, then: *The odour of thy ointments above all spices,* as if it were said: The fame and repute of thy virtues is better and more useful to thee than all the chosen things of this world, because these kill, those give life.



## *138* CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

[1166B]But if in another way we wish to refer both parts, namely *the odour of the ointments* and *all spices,* to the spiritual understanding, it must be seen what this special odour of the ointments of the beloved is, and what are all the spices to which that odour is so greatly preferred. For if we take *all spices* to be all the virtues, what will those singular virtues be which are preferred to the whole body of the virtues? It seems to us not absurd if we say that these virtues are faith, hope, charity; for without these virtues all the other virtues are as it were idle; for it is written that *without faith it is impossible to please God [n: (Hebr. XI)]*. But if no one can please God without faith, it is clear that no good works can confer salvation without faith; and he who, believing, works well [sic: *bone*], assuredly does so [1166C] in hope of future goods; but each one loves the things which he hopes to obtain, and whatever good he works, he works through love; because unless he loved, he would not labour to attain them.

Since, then, all good works cannot exist without these, that is, faith, hope, charity, deservedly are these preferred to all the virtues; and so it is said: *The odour of thy ointments above all spices,* as if it were said: Above all virtues there are fragrant in thee faith, hope, charity, which make thee perfectly into a bride and beloved.

But why are these called ointments? Because, as many kinds of herbs, when they have been gathered into one so as to make a certain composition of ointment, [1166D] take the name of the ointment itself from that from which they have the greater virtue, so whatever good works have been compounded with the seasoning of faith, hope and charity are principally called the ointments of the Church, or of each holy soul, because they are not otherwise efficacious unless they have been named by the addition of these, so that it may be said: This ointment is compositive of faith, hope and charity; for example, someone has humility, chastity, patience, obedience, and some other virtues, whose composition can be called a certain spiritual *160* ointment. But it will be altogether inefficacious unless faith, hope and charity be there. Again, there is someone who does not have all the aforesaid virtues, but has others; but neither will he be able to have an approved ointment of these without [1167A] the addition of these three virtues. Whatever virtues, then, or however great, anyone may have, he must never lack these three; if he desires to have sufficiency for his salvation. Since, therefore, there are in the Church some endowed with these virtues, others with those, some with more, some with fewer, yet always cleaving to those, faith, hope and charity, it can rightly be said, not in the singular number the odour of the ointment, but in the plural number *the odour of thy ointments;* but this *odour above all spices,* because the virtues intermingled with these are fragrant and avail; those lacking them, even if they be fragrant, remain inefficacious. There follows: *Thy lips are a dropping honeycomb.*

VERSE 11.-- *Thy lips are a dropping honeycomb, bride; honey and milk are under thy tongue: and the odour of thy garments as the odour of frankincense.*

[1167B]What ought we to understand by the lips of the bride, but the preaching of each Testament? For as in the body the lips are so set that one is above, the other below, so the Old Testament is prior, the New is posterior; and because speech is expressed through the lips, the preaching of these two Testaments is not unfittingly understood by the lips. But what is it that these lips are called *a dropping honeycomb?* for a honeycomb is honey in wax. And what is honey in wax, but Divinity in humanity? And what else does the preaching of each Testament sound but God made man for the redemption of the human race, the one signifying through figure, the other representing in truth the thing signified? And whatever the Church preaches of Christ is both of his divinity and of his humanity [1167C]. But who does not know that honey is sweet, that wax is of honey? And Christ was sweet from his divinity through his miracles and his preaching, and soft and tractable from his humility through the passion of the body: whence also he could be seized and slain, because God, immense, incomprehensible and invisible, through humility showed himself *161* visible and humble and as it were soft and flexible wax: for he was as it were honey in wax, because, bending himself from heaven to earth and ministering the light of his truth, he distilled the sweetness of his mercy and compassion through the whole world. The *lips* of holy Church, then, are called *a honeycomb* that is *dropping,* because the sweetness and grace of all the divine dispensation which was wrought concerning the human race, [1167D] and is now daily wrought in the sacraments and the realities of the sacraments, is known by her preaching. Of which it is further added, *honey and milk under thy tongue,* that is, both in voice and in heart, because, namely, honey is the food of the strong, milk that of infants; and these things in preaching, or the Church, pronounces by speaking or writing, by which both the strong may be strengthened and the weak nourished; and because she confirms her preaching by good works, instructing her sons by word and example, it is added:

*And the odour of thy garments as the odour of frankincense;* that by the garments good works ought to be understood, the authority of Holy Scripture teaches; for it is written: *Blessed is he that keepeth his garments, lest he walk naked, and they see his shame [n: (Apoc. XVI)]*. [1168A] And in the Proverbs of Solomon: *At all times let thy garments be white [n: (Eccle. IX)]*; for to keep one's garments is to have good works, lest one suffer a shameful repulse in the judgment, if one appear naked of the covering of the virtues with which to cover the deformity of one's sins. And to have white garments is not to defile one's works with the filth of vices, because before the eyes of God those works of the virtues are foul which are fouled by the admixture of vices.

And what is the odour of the garments, but the fame and repute of good actions? But this *odour of the garments* is *as the odour of frankincense,* because no good work is of any efficacy unless it be fragrant from the fire of charity. For as frankincense, when kindled, smokes and gives off its fragrance, so the mind of anyone, kindled with the fire of the Holy Spirit, [1168B] smokes from the grace of compunction, either by bewailing its sins or by sighing for the supernal fatherland. And when through the grace of compunction it has recognized its past fault, or in hope of future goods has wished for a short stay in this life, it pursues the more ardently the gains of piety, when it both burns more inwardly through charity and is fragrant outwardly through good repute. For while his neighbours profit by his example, he makes their gains his own also, because in his actions they learn whence they may acquire for themselves the merits of good works.

The bridegroom says, then, to the bride, that is, to the Church or the faithful soul, *162* *The odour of thy garments as the odour of frankincense,* as if he said: The good odour of the virtues pleases me for this reason, that, kindled from the fire of my charity, it [1168C] is fragrant, and so that odour is directed to me as it were a certain prayer, just as frankincense is kindled and offered to me alone for the devotion of prayers.

VERSE 12.-- *A garden enclosed, my sister, bride, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed.*

Through the figure metaphor, which is made in four ways, that is, from animate to animate, from inanimate to inanimate, the bridegroom goes on to proclaim the beauty of the bride, two ways of the aforesaid manner of speech being intermingled, that is, from animate to animate and from inanimate to inanimate: for whereas above he had declared the varied [1168D] beauty of the bride *with the eye and the hair, the paps or breasts and the lips,* which are parts of an animate body, *with the odour* also *of the garments,* which is part of an inanimate body, now through that one mode alone which is from inanimate to animate he enumerates other proclamations also of her beauty, of her very dignity, through the likenesses *of the garden and the fountain and the sendings-forth of the garden itself,* which are described through seven kinds; namely *of Cyprus, spikenard and saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon, myrrh and aloes,* of each of which we must speak.

And why is the Church called *a garden?* Surely because of the shoots of the virtues sprouting in her continually; and as a garden is called "hortus" for this reason, that something always arises ("oriatur") there, so the Church is called a garden because the works of the virtues always arise in her, through the progress of her sons, whom she daily acquires in baptism, and nourishes in faith, and advances in the works of faith. [1169A] But why is *this garden enclosed?* Either because of the invisible virtue and singular dignity of the sacraments, or because of the wonderful depth of the Holy Scriptures, or because of the impregnable firmness of the divine defence. For although from the invisible virtue of the sacraments the salvation of believers daily sprouts, yet it is not open to all: how does the Holy Spirit inwardly work in each sacrament the virtue and the reality of the sacrament? why were the sacraments themselves instituted? how and by whom are the same sacraments to be given and received? what faith, what merit of faith, what order, what cause of order, what finally the reverence of the sacraments themselves, what and how great the fault of irreverence; all which things, because they are so hidden and closed that they are open to no one [1169B] except to those who can say with the Apostle: *But to us* *163* *God hath revealed them by his Spirit [n: (I Cor. II)]*, rightly is the Church, in which these sacraments are contained, called *a garden enclosed;* whence also the officers of these sacraments are not admitted into a garden of this kind [sic: *husjusmodi*] unless they enter through him who says: *By me, if any man enter in, he shall be saved [n: (Joan. X)]*.

But because many obtain the offices of those sacraments through ecclesiastical dignities for a price, although before human eyes they seem to enter this garden, yet before the divine eyes it is altogether closed to them; and though they be called officers of Christ, yet they are not, because they have not entered through the door, [1169C] but have climbed up another way. And what they are, the Saviour teaches, who, when he said that he was the door, adds: *He that entereth not by the door, but climbeth up another way, the same is a thief and a robber [n: (ibid.)]*.

Behold how *enclosed* this *garden* is, that access to it is granted to no one except through the *door,* that is, what is written [cj: *scriptum*; read *Christum*, "Christ"]; and he who has not come to it through him is proclaimed a thief and a robber. Whence Gregory: *Why is it not weighed that the blessing is turned into a curse for him who is promoted to this end, that he become a heretic?
