I do not think that the priesthood subsists there.* To such men, then, this *garden* is truly *enclosed.* Who, although they have the sacraments, yet have not the reality of the sacraments, because they are proved to have these not to salvation but to their own damnation; and therefore what before [1169D] human eyes they seem to be, before the divine eyes they lose.



## *138* CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

Further, on account of the depth of the Holy Scriptures the Church of God is called *a garden enclosed,* because full entry into their sense lies open to no one, except as he leads him in *who hath the key of David, who openeth and no man shutteth, shutteth and no man openeth [n: (Apoc. III)]*. But they who by the grace of God have merited entry into this garden, how many, and how great, and how wholesome kinds of spiritual herbs thinkest thou he will find in it, by which he may be able to drive off the diseases of the soul and to revive the wounded mind? This *garden* is enclosed to Jews and heretics, because they do not understand the sacraments hidden in it, but attend only to the letter; to whom that saying of Isaiah applies: *And the book shall be given to one that knoweth [1170A] letters, and it shall be said to him: Read, and he shall answer [sic: etrespondebit]: I cannot, for it is sealed [n: (Isa. XXIX)]* [var: Vulg. Is 29:12 *nescienti litteras*, "to one that knoweth not letters"; *scienti litteras* and *Non possum, signatus est enim* are the wording of Is 29:11]. This *book,* however, that is, the whole series of Holy Scripture, is contained in this enclosed garden, that is, the Church, *written within and without,* that is, in the literal and the mystical sense, *sealed with seven seals [n: (Apoc. V)]*, that is, with seven sacraments, which the faith of the Church holds, namely the nativity of the Lord, the circumcision, the baptism, the passion, the resurrection, the ascension, the judgment to come.

*Enclosed* therefore is this *garden,* because of the sealed depth of this book, *164* which no one can open, *save the Lamb that was slain, who alone was found worthy to loose this book, and to open the seven seals thereof [n: (Apoc. V)]*, because it belongs to his grace alone to open to human sense the mysteries of Holy Scripture, which [1170B] proclaim the power of those seven sacraments.

But on account of the impregnable firmness of the divine protection the Church can be called *a garden enclosed,* because she who is always fortified by divine defence can by no means be shaken by the onset of malignant spirits or of men; for although they make many and frequent assaults upon her, yet in no wise do they enter into her with any power of their evil will, because she is so enclosed by the hedge of divine protection that they find no means at all of entering into her from the opposing side. Whence, having received great security, she cries out confidently in the Psalm, saying: *The Lord is my light and my salvation, whom [1170C] shall I fear? The Lord is the protector of my life, of whom shall I be afraid? [n: (Psal. XXVI.)] The Lord is my helper, I will not fear what man can do to me; the Lord is my helper, and I will look down upon my enemies [n: (Psal. CXVII)]*.

*A garden* therefore *enclosed* is the Church under divine protection, because God has so strengthened her with his grace that the perverse can neither corrupt her faith, nor take away the institutions of the sacraments, nor cast her down from the state of inmost peace. And if it is granted to them for a time to tempt her by afflicting her outwardly, this temptation turns out for her not to damnation, but to a crown.

Whence also she is called *a garden enclosed* not once, but a second time, because God has enclosed her not only outwardly but also inwardly, while he so sanctifies and guards her [1170D] in all things, that she is kept whole in body and soul unto the day of our Lord Jesus Christ [n: (I Thess. V)]; here indeed temporally, but afterwards eternally.

Not only, however, is the Church called *a garden enclosed,* but also *a fountain sealed,* both on account of the uniformity of baptism and on account of the purity of holy preaching: for as by a fountain the filth of the body is washed away, so by baptism the filth of the soul, that is, sins, are washed; and as by a fountain the weary body is refreshed, so by holy preaching the soul that is failing from God is relieved. Now this *fountain* is said to be *sealed;* but we must see whence, by whom, and why it is sealed.

Whence, then, is it *sealed?* In baptism, in the form, the thing and the intention of the sacrament; in preaching, [1171A] however, in truth, sweetness and utility; for from this the Church is rightly called, in these two, that is, baptism and preaching, *a fountain sealed,* because baptism avails nothing unless there be present both the form of the sacrament, that is, the invocation of the holy Trinity, and the thing of the sacrament, that is, the power and efficacy of the sacrament; the intention also, that is, the will to baptize in the catholic manner. Further, preaching, *165* confers nothing, unless it be true, that it may stir up faith, sweet, that it may draw, useful, that it may inform. For by these parts of the sacrament, as by certain seals, this fountain is shut up, so that its special power and purity and wholesomeness may be intimated.

By whom, then, is it *sealed?* Surely by the Holy Spirit, who has sealed it with the seal of his sevenfold grace, because from the institution and power of the sacraments, and [1171B] from the utility of holy preachings, there flow forth in the Church the watering streams of all spiritual charisms: for there the holy mind receives what, and how, it should savour and understand, by what counsel, by what fortitude it is strengthened, by what knowledge and piety it is adorned, or with what fear of the Lord it is filled. Surely with these spiritual gifts the *fountain* of the Church is so *sealed,* that no one can be a partaker of that fountain who has none of these seven graces.

But why is it *sealed?* Because the sacraments of the Church are to be committed to no one unless he have faith and works; faith, that he may understand and believe that the things which he sees done outwardly and visibly in the sacraments work salvation inwardly and invisibly; works, that in partaking of those sacraments he may show himself worthy. To no one [1171C] also is the office of preaching committed, except to him who is proved to have the grace of this knowledge, as the Apostle says: *How shall they preach unless they be sent [n: (Rom. X)]*? he obtains from God the grace of the office.

VERSE 13.-- *Thy sendings forth are a paradise of pomegranates with the fruits of apples.*

But what do this *garden,* *enclosed and fountain sealed,* kept by so great a divination providence, send forth? *Thy sendings forth,* he says, *are a paradise of pomegranates with the fruits of apples;* for the sendings forth of this garden must needs be wonderful, which is made fruitful by the watering of a fountain so sweetly and wholesomely flowing, and so carefully sealed. For it is established as a paradise by the glorious sendings forth, spiritually coming forth, of diverse [1171D] virtues, or of flowers and herbs, which, although they are enumerated below as very manifold kinds of prayers setting forth the whole range of the virtues, are yet divided into two final causes on account of the twofold quality of the times, namely of persecution and of peace. For every consummation of the saints has either been celebrated in persecution by martyrdom, or accomplished in peace by confession.

Whence well are the *sendings forth, a paradise* of this garden, that is, of holy Church, said to be *of pomegranates with the fruits of apples,* because by the glory either of martyrdom or of confession her holy praises everywhere flourish and give forth fragrance. For what is meant by *pomegranates* but the glorious *166* purple of the martyrs, what by *the fruits of apples,* but the life of the confessors shining white with good deeds? [1172A] O how great are the delights of this paradise, how great the sendings forth of virtues, flowing forth abundantly and happily, where both the strong are nourished and the weak are refreshed! because there those who desire to imitate admire, proclaim and venerate the constancy of the martyrs; there those who cannot be martyrs attend to, approve and emulate the praiseworthy life of the saints living gloriously in peace.

VERSE 14.-- *Cyprus with spikenard, spikenard and saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon with all the trees of Libanus, myrrh and aloes with all the chief ointments.*

Whence there also follows the designated beauty, wholesomeness and utility of this *garden enclosed,* or *fountain sealed,* by seven kinds of aromatic herbs, not only because the grace of the sevenfold [1172B] Spirit effects these sendings forth of her holiness, but also because by this sevenfold number of kinds the totality of the virtues is expressed. For in the whole body of the Church no virtue can be lacking, because, since the head of the Church is Christ, and the body of Christ the Church, whatever of holiness, of honourableness, and of utility ever could be, or can be, it will be permitted to consider in the whole Christ, that is, in head and members.

Let us look, then, at the kinds themselves, by which the totality of the virtues, as we have said, is denoted, and let us know that what we understand in the parts is to be understood in the whole. *Cyprus,* he says, *with spikenard, spikenard and saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon with all the trees of Libanus, myrrh and aloes with all the chief ointments.*

*Cyprus* is an aromatic tree, signifying the blessing of spiritual [1172C] grace, and it is interpreted *mourning* or *sadness. Cyprus* therefore in the Church is any holy soul, like an aromatic tree, which has it from the grace of God that it will despise the passing joys of this world, having that sadness *which is according to God, which worketh penance unto a stable salvation [n: (II Cor. VII)]*; mindful of that saying of the Gospel: *Blessed are they that mourn, for they shall be comforted [n: (Matth. V)]*; and of that apostolic one: *Be wretched and mourn, let your laughter be turned into mourning, and your joy into sorrow [n: (Jac. IV)]*.

*Spikenard* is said to drive off cold, *saffron*, however, to bring coolness to those who burn: what, then, is meant by spikenard but charity, what by saffron but chastity [1172D] is figured? For charity rouses the lukewarm in good work, and makes them burning; chastity tempers the mind and restrains it from the heat of evil concupiscence.

*Sweet cane* is an aromatic tree, short and small, but of hard bark, which, when crushed, is drunk and heals the entrails: further, cinnamon has an ashen colour, and keeps its strength a long time. By the sweet cane, which however, as we have said, is short and small, humility is not unfittingly understood; but *167* by its hard bark, crushed and drunk, the entrails are healed, because while by the virtue [sic: *virtu e*] of humility the elation of the hard and proud flesh is broken, the wound of the soul is healed by the medicine of a contrite and humbled heart. By *cinnamon*, however, which is of the colour of ashes, and keeps its strength a long time, the abject habit of penitents is designated, who can say [1173A] with the Prophet: *I did eat ashes like bread [n: (Psal. CI)]*, and with blessed Job: *Therefore I reprehend myself and do penance in dust and ashes [n: (Job XLII)]*. But this abjection keeps its strength a long time, because that is true penance which so keeps the rectitude of a converted heart that it does not afterwards commit things to be repented of.

*With all the trees of Libanus,* that is, with all the virtues of a life made white by baptism, made solid by right faith, and raised on high by good works. For when he had set forth particularly those virtues which we placed above, by the forenamed kinds of trees, he adds universally, by all the trees of Libanus, all the virtues, that we may understand that the garden of the Lord, that is, the Church, sends forth indeed every virtue from herself, through the varied life of the saints, but that those forenamed are the heads [1173B] of all the virtues, so that the other members of the virtues lie weak without them.

*Myrrh and aloes with all the chief ointments.* He adds yet two kinds of the sevenfold virtues, namely the mortification of the flesh and patience; by myrrh, then, by which dead bodies are kept unharmed from the corruption of worms and from putrefaction, the mortification of the flesh ought to be understood, by which the putrefaction of vices and of carnal pleasures is restrained; by aloes, which has indeed a sweet odour, but a bitter taste, and purges the stomach, we can take patience, which, because on account of the future goods of perennial life it endures the evils of the world, is indeed fragrant from the cause for which it suffers, but bitter from the cause by which [1173C] it suffers: and if any disease of sin weighs down the soul, the bitterness of the very evil which it suffers purges it.



## *138* CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

*With all,* he says, *the chief ointments,* that is, with the precious and approved gifts of spiritual charisms, which the Apostle enumerates, saying: *To one indeed by the Spirit is given the word of wisdom, to another the word of knowledge according to the same Spirit, to another faith in the same Spirit, to another the working of virtues, to another prophecy, to another the discerning of spirits, to another kinds of tongues, to another the interpretation of speeches [n: (I Cor. XII)]*. Behold, these can be called the chief ointments, which are compounded by divine grace, before which, not without reason, are set the two kinds of virtue foreshown above by myrrh and aloes, namely mortification and patience, that we may understand that those only are to be imbued with these spiritual ointments, [1173D] *who have crucified their flesh with the vices and concupiscences [n: (Gal. V)]*, who can say with the Apostle: *And we glory in tribulations [n: (Rom. V)]*.

It is pleasing, then, here to attend and to wonder *168* that the garden of the Lord through these its sendings forth is truly as a most beautiful paradise, that is, a garden of delights, where the eternal salvation of the soul, and all sweetness, flourish, where all beauty and spiritual delight bloom, where perennial life and everlasting rest abide; not that these are conferred here in the present life, but that through those sendings forth of virtues and graces which we spoke of above one attains to these, and that the exercise of those is temporal, the showing forth of these eternal. Whence most rightly this *garden,* or paradise, is recorded to be [1174A] *enclosed,* because by no adversity of malignant spirits is holy Church overcome, she who is strengthened by the grace of the spiritual sendings forth of this garden.

But since, as the Church in general is called the garden of the Lord, so also in particular each holy soul, which flourishes with the bud of the virtues and the beauty of manifold graces, can be called the same, it must yet be considered, since the Church is a certain whole, while one soul is a part of the whole, how much the part differs from the whole. For what is in the part must be in the whole, but it is not converted, so that what is in the whole must also be equally in the part. For the universal Church in its parts, that is, in the singular and individual men of whose collection into one it is constituted, is a certain whole, because while [1174B] many flourish with diverse virtues, and in this diversity all kinds of virtues are contained, the whole indeed contains all in its parts, but the part not all, but some.

Therefore the whole and the part differ in this, that the whole is far more comprehensive than the part, since all things flow together at once in the whole, but in the part not all, but some. Since, then, the holy universal Church in general is called the garden of the Lord, because within her the buds of all the virtues are celebrated; any holy soul also which brings forth some buds of the virtues can be called the garden of the Lord, according to some manner of its own which divine grace has conferred upon it.

VERSE 15.-- *The fountain of gardens; the well of living [1174C] waters, which flow with a rush from Libanus.*

But since whoever is instructed is instructed either by word or by example; and the part has what it has from the whole, while words and examples are universally in the whole, not without reason does it follow that that principal garden of the Lord, that is, the universal Church, should be called *the fountain of gardens* that are particular, that is, of the single souls. For whence is it that they say, that they do, that they teach, except from the doctrines and institutions of the holy universal Church? And whence those doctrines themselves, except from the words, writings and examples of the holy apostles and of apostolic men? Wherefore rightly the Church is called the *fountain* of these *gardens,* that is, of holy souls. *169* Because whatever of holiness or honourableness [1174D] they have, they conceive from the watering of ecclesiastical preaching, and they bring forth all the things which their progress is, the rule and state of ecclesiastical discipline. For a fountain [n: (Gen. II)] goes forth from the paradise of the Lord, that is, from the Church of God, which is divided into four rivers, that is, into the four Gospels, which water the whole face of the earth, that is, every intention of the dry mind, so that, inebriated by their doctrine through the grace of God, it may say with the Psalmist: *The Lord will give goodness, and our earth shall yield her fruit [n: (Psal. LXXXIV)]*: for unless the goodness of God has visited the human heart, it will be able to bear no fruit of good works

But though the same Church, for all these gardens diversely [1175A] bearing fruit on account of the diverse qualities of knowledge and of works, is in common called a *fountain,* because she waters all with the infusion either of knowledge or of pious zeal, yet to some she is singularly called *a well of living waters,* on account of the depth of divine knowledge specially hidden in them, which flows forth from the fountain of the Church itself [sic: *exipsius*]. The fountain indeed flows openly upon the earth, but the water of the well is hidden in the earth, because one is the knowledge which the simple and weak attain for its easiness, another that which the learned and strong scarcely search out for its subtlety. The Church, therefore, is a *fountain* to all the elect, but not to all is she a *well,* because all indeed read or hear and understand the literal surface, but very few look into the depth of the spiritual sense hidden within [1175B].

But this well has living waters, because the Church, while she opens the depth of the divine utterances to those who read or hear, both corrects them from depravity, and kindles them to good works, and thus refreshes, by a vital doctrine as by living waters, those who ought to have died in their sins.

Of what waters, then? *Which flow,* he says, *with a rush from Libanus:* for what is the rush of these waters, but the vehement course of the Holy Scriptures flowing forth with great authority? And what is expressed by Libanus, but the knowledge and virtue of sublime doctors? The waters therefore flow with a rush from Libanus, because the Holy Scriptures, from the knowledge and virtue of the holy [1175C] Fathers, show the great force of their authority. This rush was indeed necessary to the Church, because the course of faith and of sound doctrine would have been hindered by heretics and perverse men, had not the torrent of the eloquence of the holy doctors driven back the opposing machinations of falsehood by the rush of true reason and eloquence.

But because against holy Church, or any holy soul, the adversary has always stood with a twofold battle line, for he tempts her either by threats or by blandishments, that he may cast her down from the rectitude of her state, the bridegroom, wishing to show how much she profits from this temptation, adds in a permissive manner of speech, saying: *Arise, north wind,* etc.

*[1175D] 170* VERSE 16.-- *Arise, north wind; and come, south wind, blow through my garden, and let the spices thereof flow.*

By the north wind, which is a harsh and cold wind, the harshness of threats, which descends from the coldness of a perverse mind, is understood; by the south wind, which is warm, the smoothness of blandishments, which proceeds as it were with a certain sweetness from a deceitful heart, is taken. When therefore the Church or some holy soul is wearied in these two ways, she is trained for greater exercises of the virtues, so that the more strongly she struggles against the enemy, the more gloriously after the victory she is joined to the embraces of her bridegroom. For by this twofold war the constancy of the martyrs was proved, by this also the conversation of the faithful is daily proved and crowned. For what mind is not tempted by the prosperities and adversities of this world? but that mind is crowned as proved, [1176A] which is neither lifted up by prosperity nor cast down by adversity. Whence John is commended by the Lord in the Gospel, because he is recorded not to be a reed shaken by the wind [n: (Matth. XI)]: for a reed, when it is driven by the wind, is bent to whatever side; but he is not a reed shaken by the wind who is bent back to neither side, namely to the right or to the left; because he is neither shaken by terror nor seduced by blandishment. *Arises,* then, the *north wind,* when one is driven by detraction, or by some harshness; *the south wind comes* when one is tempted by favour or by some prosperity.

But these blowing through the garden of the Lord, *the spices thereof flow,* because the more the Church, or a holy soul, is touched by adversities or prosperities, the more, through progress, [1176B] is she filled with the good odour of the virtues. For this quality of life is probative of holiness and justice, so that he may deservedly be called the *garden* of the Lord who is adorned with such virtue of patience, humility and longanimity: but where there has been privation of this good, there cannot be the appellation of spiritual good.

Moreover: By the north wind the devil is often designated, by the south wind the Holy Spirit, because the one binds up in cold, and the other loosens in heat. For on the testimony of the Prophet the devil is related to have said: *I will sit in the mountain of the Testament, in the sides of the north [n: (Isa. XIV)]*. And at the suggestion and breath of the devil iniquity and malice abounded in the world. For on the testimony of the Truth: *Where iniquity hath abounded, the charity [1176C] of many shall grow cold [n: (Matth. XXIV)]* [var: Vulg. *quoniam abundavit*, "because iniquity hath abounded"]. And the prophet asserts of Judaea, saying: *As a cistern maketh its water cold, so hath she made her malice cold [n: (Jer. VI)]*. But by the name of the south wind the Holy Spirit is taken, because by his grace the torpor of the mind is loosened, as the Psalmist testifies: *Turn,* he says, *O Lord, our captivity, as a torrent in the south [n: (Psal. CXXV)]*: *171* for our [sic: *nostrae*] captivity, which through the cold of torpor had remained sluggish for following God, is loosened by the heat of the Holy Spirit, so that it may run into the love of God.

The heavenly Bridegroom, therefore, deigning to visit his bride, that is, the Church or a holy soul, bids the north wind withdraw, and the south wind come, because it is a just progress and a fitting order that, first [1176D] the cold of unbelief and malice having been driven off, by the coming of the Holy Spirit, the mind should receive the heat of faith and of good working. *Arise,* he says, *north wind, and come, south wind, blow through my garden, and the spices thereof shall flow:* as if he should say in other words: Because into the minds designated for me the grace of the Holy Spirit cannot come unless the cold of unbelief has been put to flight, whatever torpor, whatever sloth, thou hast settled in those same minds, whether through ignorance, or through negligence, arise and withdraw and come, O Holy Spirit, who mayest loosen by the heat of thy breath those same minds bound with torpor, that, the vigour of good will and of pious intention having been conceived, they may flow forth with the fruits of holiness and with odours of fruits, spreading far, and wide, drawing many round about to their fellowship.

[1177A]Further, at this voice of the bridegroom, the bride, now as it were animated and in a manner raised up again, rises with a ready mind to the inspiring grace, and because it follows that, the Holy Spirit blowing, she should bring forth the fruit of good working, she shows it, saying: *Let my beloved come into his garden,* etc.



## *172* CHAPTER V.

VERSE 1.-- *Let my beloved come into his garden, and eat the fruit of his apples. Come into my garden, my sister, bride, I have reaped my myrrh with my spices: I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey, I have drunk the wine with my milk: eat and drink, friends, and be inebriated, dearest.*

Well does she say that the beloved comes into his garden, because the cause of his coming is that he is beloved, that his [1177B] is the garden; for unless he were loved he would not come. *If any one love me, he will keep my commandments, and my Father will love him, and we will come to him, and will make our abode with him [n: (Joan. XIV)]* [var: Vulg. *sermonem meum servabit*, "he will keep my word"]. But he who says that he loves God ought to prove it by work, for this is to be his garden: for where diverse flowers of the virtues bloom, and fruits abound, it is not undeservedly called spiritually the garden of the Lord, and to be such a garden is to love God. *Comes,* then, *the beloved into his garden,* when he illumines with the grace of his visitation a mind given to good actions. In which garden *he eats the fruit of his apples,* because he is delighted and fed by the odour and sweetness of the virtues in the mind which his grace has filled. Whence the beloved himself, as if congratulating, answers his beloved: *Come into my [1177C] garden, my sister, bride, I have reaped my myrrh with my spices.* What is taken by myrrh but the mortification of the flesh? But because, as by the delight of the flesh vices are nourished, so by mortification virtues are generated, rightly he puts myrrh first specially, and afterwards subjoins the spices generally: for unless the flesh be subjected to the spirit through the mortification of the vices, the mind does not rise to the virtues, because in its suggestion the spirit does not contradict the flesh; but when the spirit always resists the flesh, *173* to the summits of the virtues, the flesh being now at length subjected, the mind advances: and when through the daily progress of good work it has come to a certain ripeness of perfection, it is as if the time of harvest has now come to it.

[1177D]Her good works, therefore, the beloved reaps, when he gathers her, cut off by the sickle of death from the field of earthly habitation, into the barns of the heavenly kingdom, to be rewarded: this harvest indeed is either the separation of the soul from the flesh, or of the elect from the reprobate: now he who reaps takes up a part, and leaves a part; and in the separation of the soul from the body the soul is taken up and the body is left. Further, the elect, when they are separated from the reprobate, are themselves taken up to life as good corn, as the reprobate, as remaining stubble, are left to the eternal fire. But the beloved reaps myrrh when he says: *Come to me all you that labour and are burdened, and I will make you to rest [n: (Matth. XI)]* [var: Vulg. *reficiam vos*, "I will refresh you"]; he reaps spices when he says: *Come, you blessed of my Father, [1178A] receive the kingdom which is prepared for you from the beginning of the world [n: (Matth. XXV)]* [var: Vulg. *possidete paratum vobis regnum a constitutione mundi*, "possess the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world"]. He reaps myrrh, then, with spices, when he divides from the company of the wicked those who through contempt of the world and affliction of the flesh have advanced to the works of the virtues, and lays them up in the storehouse of his kingdom.

Whom he well calls his myrrh and his spices, because whatever good they have they possess from the gift of his grace: and this harvest is made, as has been said, by the sickle of judicial sentence in this time, both through dissolution and through predestination. Through dissolution indeed, when the soul, as the superior and worthier part, is taken up to heaven, but the body, as the inferior and viler part, is left in the earth. Through predestination, however, because those whom in his foreknowledge he holds as going to persevere [1178B] in good he chooses as corn by predestining them to eternal life; but those going to remain in evil, as stubble fixed in earthly acts, he leaves by predestining them to eternal death. Let the beloved therefore say: *Come into my garden, I have reaped my myrrh with my spices,* because after, by my grace, visiting the Church or the faithful soul, I made it bud with virtues, I fitted specially to myself those dead to the world and given to pious actions, whom, because I separated them from the reprobate by election, I numbered into the fellowship of my kingdom. Whence he presently subjoins: *I have eaten the honeycomb with my honey, I have drunk my wine with my milk.* Yet further the beloved rejoices at the progress of his budding garden, in the pursuits and virtues of the elect wonderfully adorning that same garden. In [1178C] the honeycomb there is honey and wax, the honey indeed sweet, the wax soft and pliant, and a minister of light: further, in wine there is austerity, but in milk whiteness and smoothness. What, then, can be signified by these kinds of things, but the diverse quality of speech in holy preachers, on account of the diverse qualities of minds in hearers? For as the Apostle says to one of them [sic: *corum*], whom he was instructing for preaching: *Reprove, entreat, rebuke [n: (II Tim. IV)]*, the holy doctors *174* temper the force of their preaching, when they consider person, place, time, and profitably weigh where and when, or whom they ought to reprove, entreat, rebuke.

For according to the diversities of minds, there are some who [1178D] willingly receive the word of holy preaching, as soon as they have heard it; and there are some who will neither hear nor receive it; further, there are many who what they will not or cannot receive and hear in this place or time, hear and receive in another place and time. Again, there are some minds which are amended by soft and gentle preaching. But some which are restrained by nothing but hard rebuke, while some are exercised to greater works of the virtues by pious entreaty. On account of these diverse qualities of souls, therefore, the doctors of souls ought to be very discreet in their preaching, because they ought subtly to weigh what, and when, and where, or to whom they preach, lest they seem to throw words away in vain. When, therefore, they correct delinquents whom they see soft and pliant, they ought to reprove them with gentle and [1179A] sweet admonition, according to the saying of the wise man: *A softer answer breaketh wrath [n: (Prov. XV)]* [var: Vulg. *Responsio mollis*, "a mild answer"]: but those whom they see hard and intractable they ought to strike with harsh rebuke, and with the terror of divine punishment; while those given to good acts they ought to entreat, that they may always advance to better.

Since, therefore, prelates ought to instruct their subjects in these three ways, surely when they cherish the good with the sweetness of pious exhortation, and console them by preaching the sweetness of the heavenly kingdom, they are *the honeycomb with honey;* but when they now restrain delinquents with modest speech, now reprove them with hard rebuke, announcing the pains of hell, they are *wine with milk.*

The beloved, therefore, *eats the honeycomb with his honey, drinks wine [1179B] with his milk;* because the rulers of holy Church, whom by his grace he composes for preaching in the manner said above, he makes his members by incorporating them into himself, in whose pursuits and progress he is delighted, and as it were refreshed. And because he has proved them to be of his body, by the conformation and unity by which he has incorporated them into himself, he makes an apostrophe to those who can be instructed by their doctrines and examples, saying:

*Eat and drink, friends, and be inebriated, dearest:* as if he should say openly: Because I have prepared for you the refreshment of souls in the deeds and words of the doctors, whom I have filled with my Spirit, you who ought to be instructed by their doctrines, what you hear and see from them lay up in the belly of memory, and fill your mind [1179C] with such sweetness of the virtues, that you may remain insensible to every motion of the vices: *friends* indeed to me through faith and working, *dearest* through intimate love. For this eating and drinking, the *175* full perception of the divine precepts: whence the Psalmist says of those who are inebriated with this fullness: *We are inebriated with the plenty of thy house [n: (Psal. XXXV)]* [var: Vulg. *Inebriabuntur*, "they shall be inebriated"]. For what is the house of God, but the Church, or the mind of any just man? And what is the plenty of this house, but a certain fullness of the divine precepts and sacraments, with which holy Church, or the soul of some saint, is filled and grows rich? But with this plenty they are filled and inebriated, whoever, from the institution of the Church or the teaching of any saint, [1179D] receive full knowledge in the necessary utility and honourableness of those precepts and sacraments. With this plenty was he filled who said: *Though rude in speech, yet not in knowledge [n: (II Cor. II)]*: the fullness of this plenty the Psalmist desired when he said: *Let my soul be filled as with marrow and fatness [n: (Psal. LXII)]*.

But because the bride had heard the voice of the exhorting bridegroom, and had tasted the sweetness of his precepts with the palate of the heart, and had been inebriated with the fullness of this good by hearing, reading, believing and working. what effect this plenary outpouring had, or what of utility she obtained from it, she declares, saying: *I sleep,* etc.

VERSE 2.-- *I sleep and my heart watcheth. The voice [1180A] of my beloved knocking: Open to me, my sister, my love, my dove, my undefiled; for my head is full of dew and my locks of the drops of the nights.*

The act of this sleeping and watching, if thou look to outward things, can by no means be at once in one and the same, at one and the same time, because no one can at once both sleep and think something by watching, at one and the same moment or time: but if thou look to inward things, and attend to the manner of this sleeping and watching, these two so inseparably cleave to each other that at one and the same time they must either not be, or be together.

For what is this sleep, but a disengagement from all the cares and actions of the world, and as it were a certain [1180B] rest and pause of the mind? And what is the watching of the heart, but a contemplative love tending continually to God with sincerity, or an amatory contemplation? But since it is certain that no one can have the virtue of holy contemplation unless he has striven to be free and disengaged from all the business of the world; and conversely that no one can disengage himself from the business of the world unless he begin to love and desire God with pure and simple intention, it is established that these two, as has been said, so cleave to each other, that at one and the same time, in one and the same, *176* they must either [sic: *ab*] altogether not be, or be together. In this manner, therefore, it is possible to sleep and to watch at once, nor is there any contrariety there, where there is this identity of habit in the affected mind, for to watch in this manner is the same [1180C] as to sleep, and to sleep is the same as to watch; because to cleave to contemplation and to sleep to the world, and to watch to God, and unless he sleeps to the world he does not watch, and unless he watches to God, he does not sleep to the world.

But it can be asked why the bride says: *I sleep, and my heart watcheth,* as if the whole man were said to sleep, and a part of the man, that is, the heart, to watch. To which it must be said that the whole man is said to be body and soul, while a certain part of the whole is the heart; and this part looks to the inner man alone, that is, the soul; but the whole man tends to the outer and at the same time the inner man. Now the whole man ought to sleep when this part, namely the heart, is zealous to watch in contemplation, that is, in body and mind, [1180D] which is, in will and in action, to cease from all exterior things, and as it were to rest, that he may be able more freely to be at leisure for interior things; because, therefore, the bride considered that she could not perfectly cleave to the vision of the heavenly bridegroom unless she had all her senses, interior and exterior, free and disengaged, rightly does she say: *I sleep, and my heart watcheth:* as if she should say powerfully in other words: Because I rest utterly from the din of earthly things, in thought and in work, therefore I watch in mind more subtly in divine contemplation.



## *172* CHAPTER V. (cont.)

But to what precedes it must be joined thus, that because the bridegroom had said of his preachings that he had filled them with such sweetness and authority of sound doctrine that no one ought to doubt that they were incorporated into him, and that therefore to be inebriated in their preaching [1181A] was both very useful and honourable; the bride, as if roused by this admonition, and having greatly profited by the preaching, words and examples of the saints, answers: Since, she says, O beloved, I have obeyed thy admonitions, thou preventing me by inspiring and following me by helping, and the doctrines of thy preachers; I have so applied my mind through faith, hope and charity, that I have not only as it were eaten and drunk, but am wholly inebriated, so my carnal senses have been lulled by this inebriation that behold I now sleep utterly from all the cares and desires of the present life, and in the sole contemplation of the Godhead I watch in heart through intimate love.

I indeed, she says, although taught by the sayings, writings and deeds of the holy Fathers how I ought to advance both in the active [1181B] and in the contemplative life, have yet chosen the better part; since I determined to be at leisure for God alone in the contemplative life with whole heart and body; but because the beloved through his grace *hath fed me with the bread of life and understanding, and given me the water of wholesome wisdom to drink [n: (Eccli. XV)]*, that I may be fit *177* for the office of preaching, he knocks and rouses, and commands that I take care not only of myself alone by resting in contemplation, but also of my neighbours by labouring in preaching. *Open,* he says, *to me,* for *the foxes have holes and the birds of the air nests, but the Son of man hath not where to lay his head [n: (Matth. VIII; Luc. IX)]*: for save a few, by whose faith I have been received, almost the whole world repels me either by unbelief or by depravity of faith, the Jews [1181C] and pagans not believing, the heretics depraving the orthodox faith. Whence my head, placed under the open sky, is drenched with dew, because my Godhead is dishonoured by the perverse opinions of heretics, falling as it were from heaven on account of the elegance of their speech, but warm [cj: *calentibus*; read *carentibus*, "lacking"] with the heat of truth and of right faith. My *locks* also *are full of the drops of the nights,* because the faithful, who ought to cleave to me as to their head through faith and working, are infected by their perverse dogmas, falling in the nights of depraved senses, and are made cold from the love of truth.

Or: *My head is full of dew and my locks of the drops of the nights,* because from the love of God and of neighbour cold [1181D] are the hearts of unbelievers: for by the head God, by the locks neighbours can be understood. *Open* therefore *to me,* because unless thou preach and teach I shall not be able to enter into their hearts: wherefore rise at times from that sweet dormitory of thy inner solitude and take up the office of preaching, that after they, taught by thy preaching, have renounced their former errors, and have prepared for me the lodging of their mind by right faith and working; I may gladly enter and bestow on them the saving gift of my grace.

*Sister,* he says, *mine, my love, my dove, my undefiled:* what means the proving of these four appellations? Does the Holy Spirit attend to nothing in so distinct a placing of these four words? by no means. For if thou apply the eyes of the inner man, [1182A] thou wilt perceive in this number of appellations a reason not to be despised, and the utility of the reason: for the form of spiritual squareness is necessary to every soul wishing to stand on the summit of perfection; for a square, whichever way it is turned, will stand. And this ought to be the constancy of the perfect, that they be neither lifted up by prosperity nor cast down by adversity, but persist always in one manner in the order and place of their holy purpose

But what is this spiritual squareness? Surely faith, hope, charity and working. For *faith,* as the Apostle says, *worketh by love [n: (Gal. V)]*, and, *Faith, if it have not works, is dead in itself [n: (Jac. II)]*; and the proof of charity is working: but he who does not believe does not hope either; and he who does not hope, [1182B] has not what he may love: further, he who does not believe neither hopes nor loves what there is *178* for which he should work. This is the intention of all good work in each one, that what he believes, hopes, and loves, he may at some time merit to obtain. In the measure of a square, however, not one side is greater and another less, but all four sides together are stretched out with equal extent, because also in these same virtues which we have mentioned, namely faith, hope, charity and working, as long as we live in this life, we shall find that they are equal in ourselves. For each one who is engaged in the exercise of the active life believes as much as he hopes; as much as he believes, he works, and loves; he loves as much as he believes, hopes and works; he works as much as he believes, hopes and loves.

[1182C]But let us say that this squareness of the soul also consists in four principal virtues, namely prudence, fortitude, justice, temperance; for these virtues are so in a square that one does not exceed another. For prudence is great; but if it is less brave in dangers, less just in operations, less temperate from pleasures, surely it is less prudent.

Fortitude is great, but if it understands less what goods it should guard, what evils it should resist, if it holds less to the works of justice, and at times is overcome by the domination of injustice; if it tempers the appetite less from pleasures, but is conquered by delight, it is less brave. Justice is great, but if it discerns less than it desires [1182D] between just and unjust works, if it strengthens itself less against adversities, if it tempers the flesh less in delight, it is less just. Temperance is great, but if it understands less whence it should temper itself, if it is less able to endure adversities through fortitude, and casts down the mind in fear, if by its precipitation it sometimes breaks out into works of injustice, it is less temperate.

If, then, the life of the perfect faithful is measured by the square, one side has as much as the single sides; because each one is as much prudent as he is brave, just and temperate; as much brave as just, prudent and temperate; as much just as prudent, brave, temperate; as much temperate as he shall have been prudent, brave and just. [1183A] But because the Doctor of the Gentiles says: *I chastise my body, and bring it into subjection, lest perhaps, when I have preached to others, I myself should become reprobate [n: (I Cor. IX)]*, the preacher ought always to study this, that the right things which he preaches with the mouth he himself also shows in his acts.

There is necessary, therefore, to every preacher the squareness of these virtues which we have set down, namely that he himself have in himself faith, hope, charity and working, and preach them to others to be had; that he also shine with prudence, fortitude, justice, temperance, and train others also to these by teaching.

*179* Since, then, this beloved rouses his beloved, resting securely in contemplation, to the office of preaching, he calls her by the name of fourfold grace, so that by this he may intimate to her that he who is sent to [1183B] preach ought always to be squared by these four virtues. For he calls her *sister* on account of faith, *love* on account of hope, *dove* on account of charity, *undefiled* on account of working. For holy Church, or any holy soul, which has obtained the name of bride and beloved by believing in the Almighty, attains the eternal inheritance, that she may become an heir of God, and a joint heir with Christ, because she merits by grace the sonship of God which he has by nature.

This heavenly Bridegroom, therefore, calls the soul beloved by him, redeemed by the price of his blood, *sister,* because what he is essentially, this she can be called by name; for John says in his Gospel that *as many as received him, he gave them [1183C] power to be made the sons of God, to them that believe in his name, who are born not of bloods, nor of the will of the flesh, nor of the will of man, but of God [n: (Joan. I)]*: of whom the Lord himself also says: *Whosoever shall do the will of my Father who is in heaven, he is my brother and sister and mother [n: (Matth. XII)]*.

He calls her *love,* because while he manifests to her certain secrets of his knowledge, and the immensity of his ineffable goodness, he raises her up into great hope of future beatitude; for it is a sign of true friendship to make someone privy to one's secret; whence the Lord to the disciples, to whom he had revealed the mysteries of the divine will: *Now I will not call you,* he says, *servants, but friends, because all things which I have heard from my Father [1183D] I have made known to you [n: (Joan. XV)]*.

He names her *dove,* because he approves her as simple, innocent and peaceable, endowed also with the love of God and of neighbour, as the dove is a simple and innocent animal, abstaining from all tearing, and sometimes cherishing others' young as her own.

For he who is truly innocent is known to have the charity of God and of neighbour, because true innocence is that which is thus defined: True innocence is that which harms neither itself nor others: for it harms itself when it offends God by acting ill; it harms others when it offers examples of evil action; he, then, who avoids working ill proves that he loves God, because thereby he also keeps himself, whereby he labours lest he lose God; he proves that he loves his neighbour, because thereby he invites him to act well, [1184A] whereby he guards himself lest he offend God. Right, therefore, is the true definition of innocence, which harms neither itself nor others, because through it the love of God and neighbour is expressed, in which the whole fullness of the law consists.

*180* For what the fullness of the law is, is beautifully declared by the fifth kind of definition, which in Greek is called κατα αντιλεχιν, in Latin "ad verbum", word for word, when that which is asked is shown by one word, in this manner: *The fullness of the law, love [n: (Rom. XIII)]*. If, then, the fullness of the law is charity, *but charity dealeth not perversely [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*, surely he who is known to have charity is proved to keep the whole law: and since that charity is poured forth in the hearts of the elect through the Holy Spirit [n: (Rom. V)], it follows that he who possesses the gift of charity confirms himself illumined with the gifts of the Holy [1184B] Spirit. For if no virtue avails without charity, and charity is conferred through the Holy Spirit, surely he who has charity is proved to have the Holy Spirit, in whom the gifts of all the virtues are bestowed. Whence now rightly the bridegroom calls the bride whom he invites to preaching a dove, that he intimates to her that she ought to have charity, which is given through the Holy Spirit, who is asserted to have been seen in the form of a dove.

He calls her *undefiled,* that whatever she shows in working she may remember to do it with pure intention, not that she may receive human praise from it, but that she may please the Godhead alone, because before God those works are foul which have been displayed for the favour of the world.

Or he calls her *undefiled* for this, that he may intimate that cleanness [1184C] of mind and of body is to be loved, or that he may forewarn her to avoid diligently in her preaching every stain of heretical depravity.

Further, on account of the four principal virtues in which the most beautiful squareness of the mind consists, the beloved calls his beloved by these four names, that he may show that she whom he exhorts to rise to preaching ought to be prudent, brave, just and temperate, as being *sister, love, dove and undefiled.*



## *172* CHAPTER V. (cont.)

For he who through the integrity of faith hastens to the inheritance of the supreme Father surely understands prudently what, or of what kind, is that which he believes: but he who, God revealing, knows the mysteries of the divine precepts and sacraments, [1184D] while he bravely endures adversities for God, while he also bravely despises the flattering joys of the world, surely shows what he hopes; but he who, full of the grace of the Holy Spirit, embraces the good of simplicity and innocence, because he is zealous to be just, surely his very justice speaks that he loves God. Further, he who has so ordered for himself his manner of living, that in all his acts he knows how to hold the governance of holy discretion, while he both tempers himself from the pleasures of the world through the pursuits of the virtues, and does not handle the good of justice indiscreetly, shows how clean and right he is in all his working.

*181* Well, therefore, does the bridegroom call the bride, whom he rouses to the office of preaching, *sister, love, [1185A] dove and undefiled,* that he may intimate to her with how squared a mind she ought to stand in all her offices and acts. But she answers him knocking, and refuses to rise from that deliberate leisure of inner quiet, saying: *To be stripped [sic: Exspoliari] of my tunic,* etc.

VERSE 3.-- *I have stripped myself of my tunic, how shall I put it on? I have washed my feet, how shall I defile them?*

The hindrances, she says, of the world, and the cares of the active life, in which I was till now entangled, I have cast from my mind, that I may be able more freely to be at leisure in divine contemplation; how now dost thou exhort me to rise again from this quiet through the office of preaching, and to serve the pursuits of the active life? *I have washed my feet,* that is, the negligences of my works, which I contracted from the dust [1185B] of thoughts and of worldly things, I have washed thoroughly with the water of compunction rising from divine charity; how shall I contract them again from the filth of earthly din? For it is very difficult to converse among the men of this world and to be stained by no contagion of human frailty. For matter applied outwardly to the bodily senses easily infects the beauty of the soul within.

But while I was turning these things over with myself, and did not wholly purpose to depart from my resolve, I will tell, O daughters of Jerusalem, what my beloved did.

VERSE 4.-- *My beloved put his hand through the opening, and my belly trembled at his touch.*

For what is his *hand,* what the *opening,* what his *belly*? Surely you ought to understand the hand of the beloved [1185C] as the power of his omnipotence and of his hidden inspiration. When, however, the mind of man, given to the pleasures of the world, thinks nothing of God, nothing of the salvation of the soul, being as it were shut round and beset by a certain mute, and a wall of perversity, it has now no access or opening through which the light of divine grace may deign to enter.

But because divine power sometimes prevents such men by inspiring, so that they are pricked with compunction for their sins, the wall of the pre-damned mind being as it were broken, an opening and a certain rent is opened, whence may flow the tears of compunction, stirred up by the grief of a penitent and as it were rent heart. Of such a rending the prophet warns: *Rend,* he says, *your hearts and not your [1185D] garments [n: (Joel. II)]*.

And since there are two kinds of compunction, *182* because the mind turned to God is pricked first by fear, afterwards by love, when anyone, dreading the torments of hell, having renounced the desires of the world, bewails the sins which he had committed, it is as if divine grace made an opening and a rent in his heart. But in the progress of good conversation, after he has conceived some security from the divine mercy, he burns with so great love toward God, that he who before wept lest he should be led to punishment, afterwards begins to weep most bitterly because he is delayed from the kingdom.

Then, therefore, through the opening which grief has made in the penitent's heart, the most loving Lord puts in the hand of soothing grace and mercy, and with so very sweet a [1186A] touch refreshes the mind, that, wholly kindled in the love of God, it seeks and desires nothing earthly, sets even all visible things at little, and strives with all its mind to attain only heavenly and eternal things.

At the sweetness of whose touch the belly trembles, because the mind, drinking the sweetness of the contemplative life, is moved by the power of inspiring grace, so that it understands that the love of God cannot be without the love of neighbour, but that this is the fullness of perfect love, that he who loves God for his own sake should also love his neighbour for God's sake. In this manner, therefore, O daughters of Jerusalem, *my beloved put his hand,* that is, the help of his power and grace, *through the opening,* of my pricked heart, intimating and admonishing that, [1186B] because I have made entry for him in my heart by cleaving to his love, I should not hesitate also to prepare an entrance for him into the hearts of my neighbours through the office of preaching, since he himself is always at hand through helping grace, and is wont both to bestow on me what, or how, I should teach, and to open the ears of the minds of the hearers.

Whence also this same sentence which I have referred to myself I can, not absurdly, refer also to my hearers, so that the beloved may be said to put his hand through the opening as often as, by sounding through the voice of preaching at the outer ears of the hearers, by putting the hand of helping grace into the inner ears, he enters. At whose touch my belly trembles, because all the weaker ones of mine, what [1186C] they before did not understand for weakness of wit, understand, and when, hearing the terror of divine judgment, they tremble, they learn to be moved also to the fruit of good work.

VERSE 5.-- *I arose therefore to open to my beloved: my hands dropped myrrh, and my fingers full of the most approved myrrh.*

For since he promised me his help, by which I might obtain the *183* fruit of my labour, it was fitting that I should not flee the office of preaching, but that, as I had opened my heart to him by his grace, so also I should open the hearts of my neighbours to him by the same grace through preaching

*My hands,* however, *distilled myrrh,* because, [1186D] according to the voice of the Doctor of the Gentiles, I chastised my body and subjected it to servitude, lest perhaps while I preached to others I myself should become reprobate [n: (I Cor. IX)]. For my hands are my works, which ought always to distil myrrh, because they ought always to be fragrant of the mortification of the flesh, of abstinence from the vices and of the affluence of the virtues.

*My fingers,* moreover, *full of the most approved myrrh,* because those very works of mine, of the virtues, if there are any, ought indeed to have great governance of discretion, but that discretion itself ought to overflow with charity divine and fraternal. For what is the most approved myrrh, but charity, the most choice of all the virtues? But why myrrh, unless because *love is strong as death, and jealousy hard as hell [sic: inferrus]? [n: (Cant. VIII.)] For no one [1187A] hath greater charity than that a man lay down his life for his friends [n: (Joan. XV)]*.

*Fingers,* then, *mine,* that is, the acts distinguished in one mind, as in one body, by the ten precepts, *are full of myrrh* of charity, because when I love my beloved strongly, I do not refuse to labour even unto death for keeping and preaching his laws.

For the salvation also, or the need, of the brethren I am zealous to have this discretion, that while I desire rather to be suspended in divine contemplation, fraternal charity nevertheless at times compels me to descend, and to gather the myrrh of labour for their utility.

When therefore at the exhortation of my beloved I had now risen, that by preaching the word of life to my neighbours I might open to him their shut hearts also, with my hands [1187B] distilling myrrh, my fingers also full of the most approved myrrh, I girded myself for the office of preaching.

VERSE 6.-- *I opened the bolt of my door to my beloved: but he had turned aside and had gone by. The soul melted when the beloved spoke: I sought and found him not; I called and he answered me not.*

I loosened, I say, for a time the rigour of my purpose, or the enclosure of my silence, and going forth into public, *I opened my mouth and drew in the spirit [n: (Psal. CXVIII)]*. Having cast from myself also every obstacle of the mind, I began more diligently to search out the wonderful testimonies of the beloved, which I might both show in work and declare in speech. But when I wished that he would enter wholly to me, and said to him with Moses: *Lord, show me [1187C] thy face [n: (Exod. XXXIII)]*, *he had now turned aside and had gone by* and had left only his back parts to be seen in some measure.

*184* For what was his turning aside and going by? Surely his turning aside was to lower himself from that sublimity of the heavens to our infirm things by being born and by dying, and because he did it with time, this was his going by: for all those things were in passing, *for Christ rising again now dieth no more, death shall no more have dominion over him [n: (Rom. VI)]*. When therefore I strove to comprehend him as he is, in some measure, and to show him in whatever way to others desiring to hear, he withdrew, turned aside, and went by, because he is incomprehensible, and in this life no one can perfectly grasp that divine [1187D] substance by sense. And when he had gone by, I saw his back parts only, because I could understand something of his humanity, and preach it.

And when I thus preached the sayings and deeds of my beloved, and provoked the minds of the hearers to keep his precepts and to follow his footsteps, a greater force of compunction dissolved me, so that I wholly flowed out in his love, because he who spoke from me outwardly to others through the voice of preaching was speaking also to me inwardly through the grace of inspiration. For as often as we preach him, he himself speaks in us and through us, as he also says in the Gospel: *For it is not you that speak, but the Spirit of your Father that speaketh in you [n: (Matth. X)]*. But where the Spirit speaks to the Father [cj: *Patri*; read *Patris*, "of the Father"], [1188A] the Son also speaks, because those whose substance is one, one also is their operation. And I was taken with such sweetness of his speech, that my soul, hearing him within, not only softened from all hardness, but even melted, and, growing strong by deliberate counsel for the daily exercises of good works, said with the Psalmist: *I will hear what the Lord God will speak in me [n: (Psal. LXXXIV)]*. For he well attends to God speaking in himself, who obeys with ready will the grace that inspires and goes before free will: he also hears God speaking in himself, whoever understands, by its own revealing, what he reads in Holy Scripture. And when by meditating more frequently in it he seeks God, the more subtly he searches him out, the higher is God exalted in his heart; whence the Psalmist: *Man shall come [1188B] to a high heart, and God shall be exalted [n: (Psal. LXIII)]*; and in Ezekiel it is written that when the living creatures were lifted up from the earth, the wheels also were lifted up together beside them [n: (Ezech. I)]: for the more attentively Holy Scripture is attended to, the more sublimely is it raised up into the spiritual sense for him who subtly considers it.



## *172* CHAPTER V. (cont.)

When, therefore, at such speech of the beloved my soul was melting through the fire of charity, I sought him the more vehemently by reading, by meditating, by preaching, and I desired to know what manner of one he was; I sought him by praying, by fasting, by keeping vigil, by macerating the flesh, but in this life I could not find *185* him as he is. *I sought* therefore *him, and found him not,* because I myself could neither know him perfectly by reading. nor show him to others by preaching; *for we see through a [1188C] glass in a dark manner [n: (II Cor. XIII)]*. *I called, and he answered me not:* when I thought him now as it were present to me, although placed in some manner a little far off, and as though I were soon to lay hold of him, and called with some voice of subtle understanding, he answered me not, because, as I went back over the things which I thought I had understood perfectly, he did not open them to the full through the answering voice of his grace. For it is written in the vision of Ezekiel the prophet that *when a voice came above the firmament, the living creatures stood, and let down their wings [n: (Ezech. I.)]* For they had already spread the wings of inmost contemplation, already they had conceived, by flying with the mind, some knowledge, however great, of the Godhead, though yet in a dark manner; but when the voice came not beneath the firmament [1188D] nor from the firmament, but above the firmament, they stood still, not able to go further, and, the wings of the contemplation they had begun being let down, they perceived that to comprehend the incomprehensible was above them. For, deeming him so present that they could comprehend him, they called, but he did not answer them, because what they thought they had understood of God, he did not show by the voice of inner address to be so. *I called* therefore *and he answered me not,* because, while I wished to attempt something above me, I recognized how far beneath it I was, since I could attain no perfect knowledge of the incomprehensible substance.

VERSE 7.-- *The watchmen that go about the city found me: they struck me and wounded me; the watchmen of the walls took away my cloak from me.*

[1189A]What then? I laid down the wings which I had spread in vain beyond my power, and, turned to the writings or sayings of the holy Fathers, I heard: *Advance not the bounds which thy fathers have set [n: (Prov. XXII)]* [var: Vulg. *Ne transgrediaris terminos antiquos*, "pass not beyond the ancient bounds"], who indeed *spoke inspired by the Holy Spirit [n: (II Petr. I)]* and showed the way and manner of believing, or of disputing about God. They, assuredly, are the watchmen of the city, that is, of holy Church, of which it is written: *Glorious things are said of thee, city of God [n: (Psal. LXXXVI)]*, because they guard her from the onset of evil spirits and men by words and examples and by the defences of their prayers.

These, then, *found me,* because they proved me drawing back from my former intention and willing to be [1189B] *186* content with their sayings and writings: *they found,* I say, because they so drew me by the sweetness of their discourses that, every other intention set aside, I would attend to the sole final and supernal cause, namely life eternal, which is God himself; the vision of him is the full and perfect rest and consummation of all our labour. They, then, while I read or heard their writings, *struck me* with the dart of divine love, and not only struck, but also *wounded,* because they so pierced my mind with the compunction of heavenly desire that a fountain of tears flowed from it, as it were a kind of blood of the soul.

*The watchmen of the walls* also *took away my cloak.* Now by the walls in this place the Holy Scriptures can, not unfittingly, be [1189C] understood, because, just as a wall is built of many and diverse stones, so Holy Scripture is made up of many and diverse sentences and teachings of the holy Fathers. And as walls fortify a city, so the Holy Scriptures guard holy Church, because all her fortification or adornment is the knowledge and observance of the Holy Scriptures. Further, the *watchmen* of these *walls* can be understood as all the simple in the Church, of whom it is written: *The simplicity of the just shall direct them [n: (Prov. XI)]*, who, although they have either little or no knowledge of the Scriptures, yet, while they preach by their works the precepts of those same Scriptures that are to be observed, edify their neighbours [1189D] too in no mean degree. These *watchmen,* then, of the spiritual *walls took away my cloak,* because by the example of good works they took from me the hindrances of superfluous thought, which veiled my mind with the enfolding of undiscerning inquiry, and they persuaded me not to be wise in high things, but to consent to the humble [n: (Rom. XII)], and to serve God in simplicity.

VERSE 8.-- *I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my beloved, that you tell him that I languish with love*

Wherefore *I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my beloved, that you announce to him that I languish with love.* But what is my adjuring? Surely to bind by the sacrament of faith and of working; otherwise [1190A] you are not *daughters of Jerusalem,* because you do not belong to the vision of supernal peace, unless you please God by faith and work. *If,* therefore, *you find the beloved,* that is, if you have so far profited by my preaching that by believing rightly and working well you have him, then *I adjure you,* that is, as I have said, I bind you by the sacrament of faith and of working, which to violate is to lose salvation; *that you announce to him,* namely, by showing forth the signs and as it were a certain stamp of my zeal and labour, with which in preaching *187* I have toiled for the sake of your salvation, as the Apostle says to his disciples: *But the signs of my apostleship are you among the gentiles [n: (II Cor. XII)]*.

And what ought you to announce to him? Namely, *that I languish with love,* that is, I am so held by his charity and love that toward every pleasure [1190B] and beauty of the world I remain weak and languid and as it were already without feeling. Do you, therefore, announce by your life and manners how true, right and profitable my preaching has been, so that your progress may become the fruit of my labour also.

At this voice of the beloved obeying the beloved, and rising up to preach, and setting forth the zeal and profit of that same preaching, the *daughters of Jerusalem,* that is, holy souls belonging to the vision of supernal peace, inquiring the causes of so great an adjuration, address the beloved also in this manner: *What manner of one is thy beloved of the beloved,* etc.

VERSE 9.-- *What manner of one is thy beloved of the beloved, O most beautiful among women! what manner of one is thy beloved of the beloved, that thou hast so adjured us?*

[1190C]We attend, they say, to no small force in this adjuration, in which we have learned that the whole sum of our salvation hangs, and therefore we desire to know the power and dignity of this beloved, for whose sake thou hast so adjured us.

*What manner of one is,* then, *the beloved of the beloved,* that is, the Son of the Father, God of God, light of light? For it is a great thing to know him whom thou art at such pains to commend, and of the highest prudence to understand him for love of whom thou languishest so greatly. But since *the beloved of the beloved,* that is, the Son of the substance of the Father, of one essence, of one power, of one will, and of one operation, cannot be perfectly comprehended, *for we know in part and we prophesy in part* [1190D] [n: (I Cor. XIII)], yet whatever that is which can be thought, understood, or said of God, thou, *O most beautiful among women,* that is, among souls that bring forth spiritual sons to God, canst teach, because this thy special prerogative of beauty has merited, that since thou art called the beloved of this beloved, thou canst draw nearer to him with the eye of the mind. For all thy glory and adornment is from within [n: (Psal. XLIV)], and the pure God, to whose purity nothing can be compared, is not discerned except by pure hearts, because *blessed are the clean of heart, for they shall see God [n: (Matth. V)]*.

Beautiful indeed are the other women, that is, the faithful souls which in their own manner and place and time beget spiritual sons to God, but thou art the most beautiful of all, the Church universal, who daily [1191A] beget sons in the spirit through the sacrament of baptism and the teaching of holy preaching. Do thou, therefore, make known to us *what manner of one is the beloved of the beloved,* that *188* is, what is to be understood and believed concerning his divinity, according to human possibility. Or again, *what manner of one is the beloved of the beloved,* that is, how he who was once made beloved to thee by being born and dying for thee is now beloved, by pouring into thee the abundance of his grace and drawing thee into the divine sweetness of his love. Or, how he who formerly, in an earlier time, was beloved in the ancient fathers by showing the majesty of his divinity through figures and dark sayings with very many signs of miracles, is now beloved in the last time by assuming flesh and working the salvation of the whole world. And that [1191B] the same words are repeated is the eager inquiry of a mind striving to know.

Further, the beloved, that she may kindle the souls of those inquiring to greater love and reverence for her beloved, sets forth his quality tropologically, while she sings the singable beauty of his divinity and humanity with ten proclamations, as it were on a psaltery of ten strings [n: (Psal. XCI)]. For the number ten is perfect, and is contained in the sacrament either of the Decalogue or of the four Gospels; which, beginning from one, so arrives at four as to complete the number ten; for if upon unity you add the three following numbers, that is, two, three and four, you will have ten, and so through four [1191C] you will complete the number ten.

Now the psaltery is a musical instrument formed after the fashion of the letter Delta, which in Greek expresses four. Beautifully, then, does the beloved sing the dignity and beauty of the beloved as it were on a psaltery of ten strings, by the testimony of ten proclamations, to show that every sacrament flowing to her from the grace of the beloved sounds forth the Old and the New Testament, the one singing beforehand in figure that it was to be shown, the other now singing that it has been shown in truth. She says, therefore: *My beloved is white and ruddy.*

VERSE 10.-- *My beloved is white and ruddy, chosen out of thousands.*

If we draw near diligently with the ear of the heart, we shall be able to perceive with the inner hearing that the chief matter of such a harmony [1191D] as the beloved intends to measure out on this ten-stringed instrument is the grace, worthy to be preached, of a twin beauty, namely of chastity and of martyrdom, which, first considered in the head itself, is afterwards diffused through his members, now alternately, now with a sweetness intermingled.

*My beloved,* she says, *is white and ruddy:* this twin colour, *189* namely whiteness and ruddiness, appears chiefly indeed and singularly and fully in the head of the Church, that is, Christ, because to the whiteness of his most chaste and most holy flesh, which he took from the most chaste Virgin by the ineffable operation of the Holy Spirit, no whiteness can be compared. And of the wisdom of God the Father, which he himself is, it is written: *Above all the beauty of the stars, [1192A] being compared with the light, she is found before it; for she is the brightness of eternal light, and the unspotted mirror of the majesty of God [n: (Sap. VII)]* [var: Vulg. Sap 7:29 *super omnem dispositionem stellarum*, "above all the order of the stars"].

He is *white,* then, in divinity, because of the unspeakable splendour of the divine nature, by which he *enlighteneth every man that cometh into this world [n: (Joan. I)]*: and *white* also from humanity, because of the assumption of flesh, immaculate, as we have said, and free of all sin. Further, he was *ruddy* specially from the passion, because no charity can be equalled to his charity, since he so loved his own handiwork that he chose to die for it, not disdaining to be made the sin of the flesh and the curse of the cross [n: (Rom. VIII; Gal. III)] Of which singular passion of his he himself speaks in the prophet: *I have trodden the winepress alone, and of the gentiles there is not a man with me [n: (Isa. LXIII)]*.

[1192B]For no passion is compared with this passion, which alone has wrought the salvation of the whole world, alone has led the captive servant out of the servitude of sin into the true *liberty of the glory of the sons of God [n: (Rom. VIII)]*.



## *172* CHAPTER V. (cont.)

Whence, when she had first said, *my beloved is white and ruddy,* she added, *chosen out of thousands:* for the number a thousand, because it is set for perfection, signifies universality. *Chosen,* therefore, *out of thousands* he is said to be, because he alone is set before the whole company of the faithful by the singularity of his virtues and graces: for of him it is written that God *anointed* him *with the oil of exultation above his* partakers [n: (Psal. XLIV)]. And whereas all the elect receive a divided grace, *one indeed after this manner, and another after that [n: (I Cor. VII)]*, to the Only-begotten alone *God giveth not the Holy Spirit by measure (Joan. [1192C] III)*, *and of his fulness all* receive [n: (Joan. I)], *in whom dwelleth all the fulness of the Godhead bodily (Coloss II)*, because *he is the head above all principality, and power, and virtue, and dominion [n: (Ephes. I)]*.

This *beloved,* then, the Lord Jesus Christ, is singularly *white and ruddy* in the personal matter of his own divinity and humanity; *chosen* he is *out of thousands:* but in those thousands themselves, that is, in the whole company of the elect, the whiteness of holiness and the ruddiness of the passion do not shine in one manner nor in equal measure; because in some both, in some not both, but now to this one the whiteness of purity and the ruddiness of martyrdom together, now to that one either whiteness alone or ruddiness alone is allotted, so that in the *190* [1192D] sight of God this one is white and ruddy, that one either wholly white or wholly ruddy; the one goes before with the greater, the other with the lesser, sometimes with an equal share of those virtues. In his body, therefore, that is, the Church, Christ also is white and ruddy, according to the measure which it pleases his kindness and grace to allot to each of the elect.

VERSE 11.-- *His head is as the finest gold. His locks are as the high branches of palm trees, black as a raven.* [var: Vulg. *Caput ejus aurum optimum*, without *sicut*: "his head is the finest gold"]

Whence presently, in what follows, the gifts of his twin beauty are enumerated part by part, now together, now alone, shining forth in the same body of Christ, so that after the singular dignity above all the saints first set forth in the head, there may be added the colours of the diverse [1193A] dignities in his body, advancing in their own manner and place through the grace that unites them and incorporates them to himself. For she says: *His head is as the finest gold.* What is understood by the gold but his divinity? For as gold is more precious than all metals, so the divinity of Christ surpasses all creatures; and whatever glory of its dignity there can be in those creatures is nothing in comparison with the divinity. The *head,* then, *of the beloved,* that is, his divinity, is *as gold,* and not gold absolutely, but the *finest* gold, because the substance of the divinity is good, and not only good, but also the supreme and most general good, and, so to speak, becomes the good of goods, because he himself is the fountain and origin of all good, from whom all good things proceed. Or [1193B] because in Holy Scripture the principal part of the mind is called the head, and Christ is the power and wisdom of God the Father [n: (I Cor. I)], and the Word begotten from the heart of the Father, by which Word and by which wisdom all things were made, and without which nothing is made. The *gold,* then, that is *finest* is that power and wisdom itself, which is *the brightness of the glory of God and the figure of his substance [n: (Hebr. I)]*.

And whatever of beauty and comeliness is diffused into the body of this head flows forth from that divinity and wisdom itself, so that the grace of the locks, of the eyes, of the cheeks, and of the other members, which will presently be numbered in what follows, may worthily be proclaimed of this body.

The first of which is, *his locks are as the high branches of palm trees, [1193C] black as a raven:* the locks of this head can be understood in two ways, namely as the holy prophets and apostles, or as the Holy Scriptures which flowed forth through them by the inspiration of God. For they clung to God as to their head by the sweetness of contemplative charity, and therefore they received from that very power and wisdom of God great subtlety of mind in beholding that divine Word.

*191* Whence they are called *As the high branches of palm trees,* because, since their conversation was in heaven [n: (Philip.
