III)], they were also lifted up in mind by contemplation into heaven, every trouble of earthly delight being overcome and trodden down; the glory of which spiritual triumph is signified by the palm, which is the sign [1193D] of victory. But because for the gaining of this victory they were afflicted with no small evils, whether by bearing adversities from wicked men, or by crucifying in themselves their flesh with its vices and concupiscences [n: (Gal. V)], they are rightly called *black as a raven.* For the despisers of worldly glory were reputed by the lovers of the world as base and obscure, but they were fitted as fine locks to the glory and comeliness of the divine head all the more, the more they were despised by the worldly for the love of the Godhead.

Further, the Holy Scriptures which were brought forth through them can for this reason be called the locks of the divine head, because, as locks spring from the head, so the Holy Scriptures are produced from the divine wisdom; and as those adorn the head, so the Holy Scriptures adorn the [1194A] eminence and principality of the Church of God. And they are said to be *as palms lifted high,* because [sic: *qui a*] the minds of the faithful are lifted up by their admonition or consolation from earthly things to the loving and seeking of heavenly things, and [sic: *ub*] they proclaim the spiritual armour against vices and sins, and promise also the palm of eternal blessedness to those who strive well unto the end. But these *locks* are said to be *black as a raven,* because the Holy Scriptures are wrapped in many obscurities, *for dark is the water in the clouds of the air [n: (Psal. XVII)]*, because the knowledge in the Holy Scriptures is deep. But because this blackness signifies affliction, whether voluntary or compelled, therefore, while some suffering of mind or body is attended to in it, the beloved in this part is proclaimed wine-coloured, that is, ruddy [1194B] only, in his body.

VERSE 12.-- *His eyes are as doves upon the brooks of waters, which are washed with milk, and sit beside the plentiful streams.*

What is understood by the eyes of the beloved but the prelates or angels of the Churches, who are set as it were as watchmen over the house of God, that is, holy Church: whence God says to one of them: *I have made thee a watchman to the house of Israel, and thou shalt hear the word out of my mouth, and shalt tell it them from me [n: (Ezech. III)]*. For the watchman looks out from on high, so that he may announce the coming of enemies the better to be guarded against, the further off it is; whence the Lord says through the prophet to a watchman of this kind: *Let there be a trumpet in thy throat, as an [1194C] eagle upon the house of God [n: (Ose. VIII)]*. For the watchman has a trumpet in his throat when he preaches the word of God openly and publicly, far and wide, and announces with lofty voice and mind what end *192* there is for virtues and vices, who is as an eagle upon the house of God, because, attending to the Godhead with the keenness of subtle understanding, he calls forth those committed to him to the subtlety of the contemplative life, spreading over them the wings of his protection, with which, by zeal for doctrine and by the support of prayers, he may keep them from the snares of the malignant spirits.

But these *eyes* of the beloved are *as doves, which are washed with milk,* because the holy watchmen, attending over the house of God, if they are truly the eyes of God, have works of simplicity and innocence, with chastity of heart and [1194D] of body, by which they nourish both themselves and those subject to them unto life eternal. And whence this? Because they dwell *upon the brooks of waters, and sit beside the plentiful streams.* For this is the natural custom of doves, that, sitting upon the streams of waters, they guard against the coming of hawks: because, then, these watchmen abide *upon the brooks of waters,* that is, the sentences of the Scriptures, therefore they know how to provide both for themselves and for their subjects, by composing or correcting manners, and by guarding against the snares of the ancient enemy. They *sit* also *beside the plentiful streams,* because daily they have before their eyes the teachings of both Testaments, which are in truth not only full but most full of the knowledge of all sacraments, which in the one are set forth through figures and dark sayings, [1195A] and in the other are preached as shown in truth. And in this third member one of the twin colour in the body of the beloved proclaimed above is to be considered, namely whiteness, so that souls washed with the milky colour may bear the ensign of chastity and purity.

VERSE 13.-- *His cheeks are as beds of aromatical spices set by the perfumers. His lips are lilies dropping choice myrrh.*

By the cheeks of the beloved the continent can, not unfittingly, be understood, adorning their purpose by a plurality of diverse virtues, as by a multitude of hairs showing forth manliness of soul, who, while for God they abstain from the pleasure of the eyes or of the ears and of the other senses, of foods also and garments, and of the other delightful things of this world, [1195B] build as many beds of spices as the scents of continence they spread from themselves.

For, for example, the reverent restraint of the eyes, or their turning away, is a certain beautiful bed, in which as many merits of continence shine forth, so many fragrances do the spices breathe. The ear *193* also, and the mouth and the nostrils and the hands can be called certain beds, which because they are perceptive of good or evil things, while certain things, though good in their own nature, very often become evil by the abuse of those who partake of them. As often as the irrational appetites for those things are broken by the love of virtue, those beds breathe with the kinds of spiritual spices with which they are sown, so that each spice breathes according to that which the quality of continence begets, because [1195C] the more it delights by it, the more does the continence of that thing commend it to God.

But because these continences ought to have a fitting state by the virtue of discretion, so that neither natural necessity be turned into vice, nor the rigour of virtue exceed the honest measure, rightly are these *beds* said, *by the perfumers,* to be *set,* because not at random, not without discretion and as it were confusedly, but in order and composedly and with reason are continences of this kind set in their beds, by those, namely, who are proved to have had the knowledge of this setting. For these perfumers are the holy apostles and holy doctors, who in their sayings and writings show forth the kinds of virtues as certain perfumes, out of which [1195D] they compound against vices and sins, as against diseases of the soul, a certain most wholesome antidote. The *cheeks,* then, *of the beloved are as beds of spices,* which are *set* *by the perfumers,* because assuredly those who give out from themselves the diverse odours of continence gather from the sayings and deeds of the holy Fathers whatever of diligence and comeliness they fashion in their manners.

And it is to be considered that in this member too the twin colour of the beauty foretasted above, namely ruddiness and whiteness, shines forth, so that by the outward bearing the inward is denoted, because, while these cheeks are ruddy and white, they give testimony both to the modesty of chastity and to the labour of continence, where it cannot easily be judged which colour is the more eminent, whether ruddiness in martyrdom or whiteness in virginity, because both virginity [1196A] is found in martyrs and itself makes martyrs. For in these cheeks two things are regarded in one, because in the virtue of each one both the merit of continence and chastity shines forth, and for the labour the reward of martyrdom is hoped for.



## *172* CHAPTER V. (cont.)

*His lips are lilies dropping choice myrrh.* Behold the perfumers by whose industry the grace of modesty shines forth in the cheeks, namely the holy preachers, who are therefore designated by the lips, because by their preaching they *194* compound the diverse perfumes of the virtues. They are as *lilies,* because they both give a [sic: *secundam*] precept for integrity and purity of life, and give counsel for the wounds of souls, and they can say with the Apostle: *We are the good odour of Christ unto God in them that are saved [n: (II Cor. II)]*. For the nature of the lily shows forth whiteness [1196B] and odour together, and yields very much healing for the curing of wounds.

They are *lilies dropping choice myrrh,* that is, the best, because, while for the keeping of integrity and purity of heart and body they mortify vices and sins in themselves, and unceasingly preach to others by word and example that they should do the same, they assuredly show how great a charity they have toward God and their neighbour.

For charity can be called choice myrrh, that is, the best and proved, because, where it is true and perfect, it extinguishes in man all the incentives of vices; for as the bodies of the dead are embalmed with myrrh, lest they putrefy, so souls are strengthened by divine charity, lest they incur the putrefaction of vices.

[1196C]And in this member of his body also the beloved is white and ruddy, because in his holy preachers he is called white for the lily of chastity, but through the myrrh of the passion, *For all that will live godly in Christ shall suffer persecution, [n: (II Tim. III)]*, he is proclaimed ruddy.

VERSE 14.-- *His hands are turned and of gold, full of hyacinths. His belly is of ivory, set with sapphires.*

If we look to the head itself, his *hands* are assuredly *turned* and *of gold,* because of him it is written: *How great are thy works, O Lord, thou hast made all things in wisdom [n: (Psal. CIII)]*: for he who works with the lathe works more quickly and more perfectly and more smoothly. But [1196D] who ever worked more quickly and more perfectly or more smoothly than God himself? For very quickly he made what he disposed to make, because he only *spoke, and they were made [n: (Psal. XXXII)]*, and at one and the same time, though in a diverse manner, in a diverse form also, his having spoken was his having made. No one has worked more perfectly than he, because he made not things diminished, or by parts, and such as were yet to be bettered, but made all things perfect, and his having made once was his having made the whole and entire. Further, nothing was smoother than his work, because the nature of things could be retarded by no hindrance, but the course and measure which it once received it kept as a perpetual law: nothing in his works is distorted and unequal, diminished or superfluous, because *he appointed all things in measure, and number, and [1197A] weight [n: (Sap. II)]* [var: Vulg. Sap 11:21 *omnia in mensura, et numero et pondere disposuisti*, "thou hast ordered all things"]. Well, therefore, were *his hands turned,* because he wrought all things with such, as has been said, swiftness, *195* perfection and beauty, that from the works themselves it becomes clear to one who considers them of how great beauty, or of how great majesty, the founder himself is, not that this consideration is a full comprehension of his deity, but that it is a certain beholding of the Creator, setting out from the dignity of the creature and shining through as far as is possible to the human mind.

Whence also the *hands* themselves are called *of gold,* because this, that they are, that they live, that they thrive and flourish, stands by the disposition of the divinity: for by gold, which is more precious than all metals, the divinity is designated, than which there is nothing above.

[1197B]But what is it that they are called *full of hyacinths,* when there are also other stones very suitable too for the adornment of the hands or fingers? All visible things indeed are certain wonderful and beautiful adornments of the divine works, but in these they are more to be wondered at, because they are also the subtler secrets of heavenly wonders; the consideration of which is, in the first place, in the incorporeal creation, namely of the supernal spirits or of holy minds always attending to heavenly things alone, whose conversation is the working of the divine goodness; in the second place, in the incorporeal creation of the superior things, which are certain nourishments of the inferior, whose rational disposition is also the wonderful working of the divine wisdom. For the hyacinth [1197C] is of the colour of the air, and therefore the *hands* of the beloved are called *full of hyacinths,* because the works of the Creator are proclaimed rather in the comeliness and adornment of heavenly things.

Further, if you look to the members of the head, *his hands are turned and of gold and full of hyacinths,* because, intent on good works and always hastening to enter into the rest to come, not slowly nor sluggishly, but swiftly and zealously, they run the way of the commandments; whatever they discern crooked and unequal in their manners they drive out with the turning of right working, and make it smooth, and render it perfect in the eyes of their Maker, yet with him as the craftsman; the cause of which effectiveness is the understanding in them, answering to the gold of wisdom divinely conferred, and always attending with the sweetness of the contemplative life to the felicities of heavenly goods.

[1197D]And they are therefore called his *hands,* because whatever good they do is not of their own virtue but of the grace of God, whose also is this, that they are *turned* by the perfection of manners, that they are *of gold* by the clearness of the understanding, that they are *full of hyacinths* by the sublimity of the angelic conversation.

Truly, in the consideration of this member also a twofold colour, proclaimed with special praise, appears in the body of the beloved, because in this, that the *hands* are *turned,* there is the sign of labour or of passion, by which they have been turned from all superfluity and inequality of manners into a vessel *196* of honour and worthy to be looked upon by the divine eyes. But in that they are *of gold and full of hyacinths,* the shining virtue of the celibate life is designated, by which they glow with the clearness of chastity and modesty; and in this manner in this part also the beloved is sung *white and ruddy.*

[1198A] *His belly is of ivory, set with sapphires:* If we look distinctly upon the person of the beloved himself, we accept not absurdly that his humanity is designated by the name of the belly, because the belly is the frailer part of the body, and that humanity experienced all the frailty of the human body except sin. But what is it that this *belly* is called *of ivory*? For ivory is the bone of the elephant, which animal is held to be most chaste: and what is more chaste than the humanity of Christ, which was assumed from the most chaste Virgin without any work of man, through the sole operation of the Holy Spirit? The *belly,* then, of the beloved is *of ivory,* because, whereas all men are conceived in iniquities and sins, he alone in his humanity was conceived singularly without sin of the most chaste Virgin, as has been said [1198B].

Further, what is it that this belly is recorded to be *set with sapphires*? For the sapphire is of the colour of the sky; and the belly of the beloved is said to be set with sapphires because, although from his earthly humanity he bore hunger, thirst, weariness and the other things which he could suffer according as he was man, yet while suffering he showed heavenly miracles from his divinity by working them.

And if we consider the multitude of the faithful who cleave to that person as members to a head through faith, which for its greater frailty can be compared to a belly: for there are in the Church some given to the bond of marriage, there are some wonderfully continent in the frail sex: but *they that have wives, as though they had none [n: (I Cor. VII)]*, can rightly be called a *belly of ivory* [1198C], because, although outwardly they seem frail, yet they are strong through the comeliness of a holy and chaste life. Whence also of holy married persons it is written that *they were walking in all the commandments and justifications of the Lord without blame [n: (Luc. III)]*: for this is for the belly to be set with sapphires, because amid the cares of worldly things, which the frailty of marriage requires, they shine forth by the devout observance of the heavenly precepts. Further, those wonderfully continent in the frail sex show in this a more praiseworthy keeping of their purpose, because, though they are weak and frail in body, yet they rise bravely by virtue of soul to the merits of holiness. Whence in these also a *belly of ivory* is spoken of, because the virtue [1198D] of chastity is more proclaimed in the frail sex.

And it is recorded *set with sapphires,* because the virtue of chastity is adorned also with the ornaments of the other virtues: for otherwise chastity of body cannot be a virtue, unless *197* it be adorned with chastity of mind, which consists in humility, patience, sobriety and contempt of the worldly life, and the observance of the other heavenly precepts. For, that it may be of the body of this beloved, the *belly of ivory* ought to be *set with sapphires,* because chastity of body profits nothing without good work, nor good work without chastity (GREGORIUS). » Because, then, the frail sex holds the virtue of chastity with a strong mind, the *belly* of the beloved is *of ivory*; but because it also strives after other virtues for heavenly glory, it is *set with sapphires,* because in [1199A] the ivory belly the colour of chastity gleams, in the sapphire, although sky-coloured, yet for the labour of the virtues the colour of the passion is ruddy. Chastity itself also, which outwardly is white in a pure body, inwardly is ruddy through the martyrdom of the mind: for to fight against the natural motion of the flesh is assuredly a certain suffering of the mind.

Truly the singing of the present portion has regard in no small measure to the rule of your profession, most beloved sisters, because, since you are in truth of the body of this beloved, you can be a most comely member of his of this kind. For when, in so frail a little vessel of your body, you shine with the white garment of chastity and modesty, assuredly you can be called and be the *belly* of this beloved *of ivory*; but because together with that chastity you shine also with other adornments of virtues, you assuredly [1199B] gleam *set with sapphires.* But you must take very great heed lest you think you can please God by chastity alone, if you have not other good works, and therefore you ought always to join both chastity to good work and good work to chastity, that you may be able to please God perfectly.

VERSE 15.-- *His legs are pillars of marble, that are set upon bases of gold. His form is as of Libanus, chosen as the cedars.*

In the eighth place, as it were, the attentive beholder of the beloved touches the eighth string of the ten-stringed psaltery, as though rendering that symphony διαπασον, whose property it is to have the same voice in the eighth as in the first. For, whereas above, on the first string, it sounded that the head of the beloved is the finest gold, now she says that the *legs* are set *upon bases [1199C] of gold,* so that the same voice may be low and high, sounding the highest and the lowest golden adornment of the beloved. But it is to be considered what are the legs of this beloved, or why they are compared to pillars of marble built upon bases of gold.

Since God in his nature is immovable and unchangeable, inasmuch as he is not in a place, for he fills and contains all things, while he himself is contained by nothing, who is always wholly everywhere; and since he is in a place who is moved from place to place, but God is not moved, and therefore is not in a place, because immovable, yet he is said to be moved, *198* not in himself, but in those whom he moves by his nod and will through the power that disposes all things, whether according to justice or according to mercy, *for he hath mercy on whom he will, and whom he will he hardeneth* [1199D] [n: (Rom. IX)], of which the one is of mercy, the other of justice. Since, therefore, he goes before all his creation as it were by this twin way of his disposition, namely justice and mercy, justice and mercy can rightly be called his legs: *for all his ways,* as the Psalmist says, *are mercy and truth [n: (Psal. XXIV)]*. But truth and justice are the same: for what is true is just; and what is just is true.



## *172* CHAPTER V. (cont.)

But why are these *legs* called *pillars of marble,* unless because they are straight and strong? For in a pillar there is straightness, in marble hardness; for the justice of God is hidden, yet never unjust. For who can know why he chooses this man mercifully, and [1200A] repels that man judicially? Therefore the Apostle cries: *O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and of the knowledge of God: how incomprehensible are his judgments, and unsearchable his ways! [n: (Rom. II.)]*

Further, what are the bases of gold upon which these pillars, which are understood as those legs, are set? Assuredly, if you weigh it rightly, they are his wisdom and goodness, because all things that he makes he orders and disposes wisely and well: they are *bases,* namely, because they contain and sustain all things, *of gold,* because they shine forth wonderfully in the very effecting of things. The *legs,* then, of the beloved, if you attend to the head itself, are, as has been said, mercy and truth, which, set *upon bases,* that is, the feet of his wisdom and goodness, either stand or walk, according to the measure and quality [1200B] of the things that have been made: for all things that have been made are either eternal or temporal. And they stand in those things which God himself has foreordained for his own to abide eternally according to grace and mercy; they walk in those things which he either confers or takes away temporally, whether mercifully or judicially.

Further, if you look to the members of that head, what do you think are to be called the legs of this beloved? In that ancient people of God, in which alone God was then known, God set chiefly two dignities, by which that people might be fortified and adorned, namely the kingship and the priesthood [sic: *secerdotium*]; outwardly indeed it was armed with royal strength against those who, at the devil's prompting, laboured to destroy the purpose of God, which he held for the salvation of the whole world, and which he showed beforehand to that same people [1200C] in type, so that in their whole commonwealth, with peace and honour, they might run beforehand, in figure, through the business of the divine promise that was faithfully to be fulfilled. Further, inwardly it was instructed by the priestly office, to understand prudently through figure and to handle reverently that same business, so that it might labour toward that purpose of God, one day to be fulfilled, by faith and love, in hope of the salvation to come.

*199* In which dignities, namely, lay so much of the strength of that same people, that afterwards, when God, angered at that same people sinning, threatened many punishments, at last, as it did not correct itself at all, he set forth this final punishment, that, the kingship and the priesthood being taken away and destroyed, all their honour and religion should depart utterly. For it is said thus through Jeremiah [1200D] the prophet: *The Lord hath delivered up to oblivion in Sion the festival and the sabbath; and to reproach, into the indignation of his wrath, the king and the priest [n: (Thren. II)]*. For there to be no king and priest, this was for all the rite of the ceremonies to have failed.

Does not holy Church also in this time, in which that promise is now fulfilled, stand by two dignities of this kind? For the power of royal judgment fights outwardly with the material sword against the unbelieving and incredulous, and the majesty of the priestly office fights inwardly also against the same with the spiritual sword. See, when the Lord, for defending the universal salvation of this new commonwealth which now is, permitted the sword, and heard that two were ready, he answered that it was enough [1201A] [n: (Luc II)], because he established in the Church two peremptory sentences, one by the material sword, the other by the spiritual sword. For outwardly this is brought about by the fear of the kingdom, that the Church may serve God with peace and honour; inwardly she is instructed by the priesthood, that she may know how to serve God with reverence and love. What then? Since these two dignities are to the Church of God, which is to be propagated with all religion, as it were a certain strength and, if I may rightly say so, the strongest support of the whole body of this beloved, can not the kingship and the priesthood rightly be called the legs of that same beloved?

And these, for their strength and eminence, are called *pillars of marble,* since they too are raised up by virtue and power, and in them the whole house of holy Church is sustained. And what are the bases [1201B] of gold upon which these pillars are built? Plainly it is fitting that in that kingship or priesthood, because their cause ought to be that of common utility and comeliness, a great force of reason and virtue of discretion should shine forth, so that in all their dispensation and judgment there may always be reason and discretion. But since these ought to proceed from a great clearness of understanding and wisdom, can they not rightly be called the golden bases of these pillars?

These legs, then, of this beloved are pillars of marble placed upon bases of gold, because in holy Church the kingship and the priesthood flourish by the reason and discretion of dispensation and of judgment.

To the song of this eighth string also that prelude answers, set beside the entry of the ten-stringed song itself, by which the beloved is sung white and [1201C] ruddy, when this part of the kingship so acts, with labour and peril of life, that in it this beloved may be proclaimed ruddy: further, *200* the other part, of the priesthood, may so act in peace and tranquillity [sic: *tranquil itate*] that in it the beloved may be pronounced white, in holiness and chastity, in conversation, in the observance and preaching of the commandments of God. *His form is as of Libanus, chosen as the cedars.* As, she says, mount Libanus is more excellent than the other mountains, in comeliness and beauty, and in the wonderful order of the trees set there, so my beloved is more eminent than all the saints, and far more beautiful, in power of virtues, in wisdom also and knowledge, and in all comeliness of worldly actions, and in all fulness of divine graces. *For God doth not give him the Spirit by [1201D] measure [n: (Joan. III)]*. But *in him dwelleth all the fulness of the Godhead bodily [n: (Coloss. II)]*; and of this his fulness the saints receive *grace for grace [n: (Joan. I)]*; and what he himself has universally, these obtain particularly, and this not always according to each one's will, because that same particle of grace in them is at times ready, and at times lies sluggishly as though asleep, which must be stirred up not without some labour Whence Paul says to Timothy: *For which cause,* he says, *I admonish thee that thou stir up the grace which is in thee by the imposition of my hands [n: (II Tim. I)]*. And it is stirred up in many ways, but most of all by praying and by singing psalms; for example, when the king of Israel said to Elisha: *Why hath God gathered these three kings together, to deliver them into the hands [1202A] of Moab?* he answered: *As the Lord liveth, in whose sight I stand, that unless I reverenced the face of Josaphat king of Juda, I would not indeed have hearkened, nor looked; but now bring me a minstrel: and it came to pass, while the minstrel was playing, the hand of the Lord came upon him,* and the rest. [n: (IV Reg. III.)]

Except, then, this beloved, of whom it is written: *He upon whom thou shalt see the Spirit descending and remaining upon him, he it is that baptizeth in the Spirit [n: (Joan. I)]*: all the other saints receive a divided or particular grace of the Holy Spirit as it pleases the Holy Spirit himself, who breatheth *where* and when *he will [n: (Joan. III)]*. Rightly, therefore, is the *form* of this beloved said to be *as of Libanus,* because he is *beautiful in form above the sons of men [n: (Psal. XLIV)]*, and no form can be compared to his form, no [1202B] virtue to his virtue, no grace to his grace.

*Chosen as the cedars:* why are the cedars chosen before the other trees of Libanus, unless because they have certain special virtues of their nature? For the cedar is incorruptible, it is fragrant, it is hostile to serpents; and who could be without corruption, save only this beloved himself, *who doeth not sin, neither was guile found in his mouth? [n: (I Petr. II.)]* No one was ever able to be so holy, so innocent, that he did not contract something of corruption, since it is written that God found iniquity even in his angels [n: (Job IV)]. For sin is a certain rottenness, a certain corruption, and though in one it is greater, in another less, yet in no one, however very holy, very just, [1202C] can it not be, though it be even the very least; but in this beloved alone no stain at all of rottenness or corruption of this kind, *201* not even the slightest, could exist.

Well, therefore, is he called *chosen as the cedars,* because this one has specially by nature what has been granted to none of the saints by grace. These things indeed are to be said of the head alone.

But if you look to the body, which, joined to the head, is one according to incorporation, because it is incorporated to that head through his grace, fittingly is his form compared to Libanus, when this whole beloved is attended to distinctly in himself and in his body. For what can be considered in the face or form of the Church [1202D] but the presence of the divinity in the performing of the sacraments, in the utterance of the divine oracles, in the disposing and ordering of fitting and reasonable causes? And who is this Libanus, to which the form of this beloved in his whole body is likened? If you attend to the thing itself as it is, Libanus is a mountain of Phoenicia, abounding in cedars both rooted in it and stretched up on high: and as this mountain is adorned with a multitude of cedars, and thereby offers great beauty of itself to those who behold it, so this beloved, adorned in his body with the multitude of his elect who are rooted in him by faith and cleave to him in charity, assuredly offers to the whole world a wonderful spectacle of holiness and comeliness.

[1203A]But if you understand it metaphorically, this Libanus is that of which the prophetic word cries: *Open,* it says, *Libanus, thy gates, and let fire devour thy cedars [n: (Zach. II)]*. For the temple of God, which had been built in that earthly Jerusalem and had boards within round about of the woods of Libanus, of cedar woods, was therefore called by the name of Libanus, because in it was the Holy of holies, which was a certain glory and an eminent beauty not only of that city, but also of the whole people itself, spread through many borders. The form, then, of the beloved is as the form of Libanus, because, as this temple was formerly the head and singular monument of religion to that ancient people of God, so now the assembly of the Church, where Christ is honoured in his body, is a certain head [1203B] and a certain eminence of holiness to all the Christian people, because in it is regarded a certain way and arrival at the sublimity of that highest good and at the eternity of the salvation to come. Still now, to the assembly, as it were to a certain mountain of God, the faithful people runs daily, to look forth in hope upon that land of promise which God will give to his elect, in which is the temple of that supernal Jerusalem, which the Holy of holies himself inhabits, in which the saints are multiplied like the cedars of Libanus, made incorruptible by participation in that eternal beatitude and in the highest good, which is God.

*Chosen as the cedars:* how is this beloved, in his body, that is, the *202* Church, said to be chosen [1203C] as the cedars? If you attend to the natures of the cedar which we recounted above, namely that it is incorruptible, that it is fragrant, that it puts serpents to flight, assuredly you can weigh how great is the utility of the unity of holy Church, and how much it is to be chosen by every soul, and how much it is to be preferred to all other things. For there incorruption of soul is attained, the odour of virtues is perceived, vices and sins, as venomous serpents, are put to flight. What is more excellent than this thing? Rightly, therefore, is Christ in his body said to be *chosen as the cedars,* because in all its action or disposition, canonical or dispensatory, he is the cause of all spiritual effecting.



## *172* CHAPTER V. (cont.)

But in this place the beloved who above, as we have often said, is brought in coloured in two ways, namely white and ruddy, by the beauty of the body alone [1203D] shines in the face of his body, that is, of the Church, which he chose for himself *not having spot nor wrinkle [n: (Ephes. V)]*, which the singing of that part concerning the form of Libanus proves, which is interpreted *whitening [sic: candidutio].*

VERSE 16.-- *His throat is most sweet, and he is all lovely: such is my beloved, and he is my friend, daughters of Jerusalem.*

In the throat is the voice, in the voice is sound, in sound, if the voice is articulate, the sure expression of some thing. What is understood by the *throat* but the sound of Holy Scripture? And we know that the whole of Holy Scripture was established either by the voice of the Lord himself, or by that of the saints, whom God himself used as certain instruments. For what do the Holy Scriptures sound but God speaking either through himself or through his own? And speaking what? [1204A] Assuredly the quality of the promise to come, according to the purpose of him who predestines, calls, justifies and glorifies his elect, the sweetness and suavity of which glorification he grants them to foretaste now through hope that lifts up, and pants after it with all desire, and as it were already in some manner takes a certain trial of that same suavity.

That very trial, namely, can also be called the *throat* of this beloved, because in the mind of every holy soul that foretastes the sweetness of the divine goodness there savours delightfully that suavity which it understands from the preaching of Holy Scripture, of which it is said that *neither hath eye seen, nor ear heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man, what things God hath prepared [1204B] for them that love him [n: (Isa. LIV; I Cor. II)]*. Whence also the Psalmist says to those who profit by the trial of that suavity: *O taste and see that the Lord is sweet! blessed is the man that hopeth in him [n: (Psal. XXXIII)]*. The throat, then, of the beloved is either the voice of Holy Scripture telling forth the suavity of the divine loving-kindness, or the trial of that same loving-kindness sweetly refreshing the soul of each of the faithful. *203* It is a *throat most sweet,* because it utters the memory of so great an abundance of the divine holiness, that to the attentive helper nothing seems to sound sweeter in the ear of the heart than not only to hope for, but also to hold with firm certainty, the everlasting goods of the beatitude to come, and therefore to desire them with the whole soul.

Whence, whereas above the several parts are compared each to some [1204C] thing, as the head to gold, the locks to the high branches of palm trees, the eyes to doves, the cheeks to beds of spices, and the rest, as has been set out above, to things befitting them, this last alone is compared to nothing, because that supreme and most general good, as it is unspeakable, so is it incomparable.

And the proclamations of this beloved having been enumerated part by part, whether in himself or in his body, as though each string of this kind of ten-stringed instrument had been played out, she sets down in some manner the final note of the praise that has been sung, saying: *And he is all lovely:* as if she said: Why is it that I proclaim his beauty and dignity part by part? There is nothing in him that I can except: for *he is all lovely,* whether you attend to the loftiness of his divinity, or regard the lowliness of his humanity [1204D]. For this is the sum of the whole praise, which is sung at the end and expresses in itself a certain colour of the whole song.

For what can be said more briefly and more definitely of Christ, God and man, for the sum of praise, than that it be said, *all lovely!* For if all that is in him, or can be said of him, is lovely, is blessed on every side, assuredly it is concluded that to call him all lovely is the supreme and final measure of praise.

*Such is my beloved, and he is my friend, daughters of Jerusalem.* You have asked of me, she says, what manner of one my beloved was, for whom I so greatly adjured you; behold, I have shown you what manner of one my beloved is, whose bearing I have expressed through each of his members. But what [1205A] is to be said of this bearing? Has this beloved obtained this bearing through disposition or affection, and is he susceptible of intensification and remission? In his own nature, by no means, who is essentially good, wise, fair, holy and just; but in his body, that is, the Church, he passes through disposition or affection into this bearing, receiving more and less, according as it pleases his grace to dispose in each, his member. This quality or this bearing is his nature, because he himself is goodness, wisdom, beauty, holiness and justice.

And he is worthy of my seeking, not that I am necessary to him, but that he is very necessary to me. Why? Because *he is my* *204* *friend, O daughters of Jerusalem;* for the bearing of my inner man [1205B] is formed from him, as from image into image, through participation in the grace dwelling in my heart, by whose illumination are opened to me those secrets of invisible things which are contained in the sublimity and signification of the divine precepts and sacraments, according as understanding and reason instruct and lead me, which are the greatest beauty of that inner bearing of mine. *This my friend* guards me into knowledge of this kind through the grace of humility: for he himself says to the Father: *I confess to thee, Father, because thou hast hid these things from the wise and prudent, and hast revealed them to little ones [n: (Matth. XI)]*: for humility is the guardian of the virtues. And he is called *amicus,* friend, as it were *animi custos,* guardian of the mind; for that I may [1205C] not lose this friend, I ought to have him through humility as the guard of my mind. For if the mind, affected or disposed toward divine knowledge, at some time through co-operating grace receives a bearing, that is, knowledge of this kind abiding in itself, the mind itself becomes as it were one with God by participation in him, so that it too, from fellowship with God, is called God. Whence a certain wise man says to his son: *If God is the mind, as the songs tell us,*
*Let him be chiefly worshipped by thee with a pure mind.*
As if he openly said: Since the mind is God to us, that is, the true and spiritual life, glorify this chiefly by living well. For by the ears, through the power of reason and understanding implanted in it, by discerning rightly and leading its life well, retaining God in itself, it is God [1205D], not indeed essentially, but by appellation, as the Prophet says of the saints: *I have said: You are gods, and all of you the sons of the Most High [n: (Psal. LXXXI)]*, and the power of such an utterance is the covenant of a great friendship; but, attending to the good of this friendship, not the sons but the *daughters of Jerusalem,* that is, souls still weak and not yet able to attain the perfection of the virtues, and this, in the beloved, to have established the great beauty of spirit of this beloved, they too being kindled with the love of holy desire, desire also to enjoy the good of this friendship, by which they may be able to attain [sic: *ad ipisci*] that same beauty of spirit. For they say: *Whither is thy beloved gone,* etc.

VERSE 17.-- *Whither is thy beloved gone, O most beautiful among women! whither is thy beloved turned aside? and we will seek him with thee.*

[1206A]What is it for God, who is not contained by place, and with whom there is no change nor shadow of alteration, to *go away,* or to *turn aside*? [n: (Jac. II.)] God is said in two ways to *go away* or to *turn aside,* namely both by the approach and by the withdrawal of his grace, according to the perfection or the defect of any mind, either drawing near to God, *205* or withdrawing from him. But in this place the going away of this beloved is for someone to be moved by the mercy of his goodness, and his turning aside also is to glide into the mind of anyone by the gift of his grace.

Tell us, they say, *O most beautiful among women,* whither *thy beloved,* by going away and turning aside through his mercy and grace, has set forth to us an example of living well, or of striving more fully in any virtue, [1206B] in any of his faithful. *Tell,* that is, show, either by the reading in which thou daily recitest their life, or by the action by which thou daily numberest them into thy fellowship, and we too, led on by their example, *will seek with thee* this beloved, by praying, fasting, and calling him by other good works, that he may deign to turn aside to us also, visiting us with the gift of his grace. For he himself says: *If you shall seek me with all your heart, I shall be found by you [n: (Jerem. XXIX)]*: for to seek him is not by prayer alone, but also by good action, as also elsewhere he says of those who seek him: *For they seek me from day to day, and desire to know my ways [n: (Isa. XXXVIII)]*. For in saying that they wish to know his ways, he assuredly shows [1206C] that they seek him by good action.

*We will seek,* then, *with thee* the beloved in this manner, *O most beautiful among women.* But of what women? Assuredly either of those who in themselves bring forth to God the fruits of good work, as sons of holiness, by faith and love, or of those who beget others, as sons of light, by word and example unto the day of eternal life. Because, then, we marvel at so great a beauty in thee, *O most beautiful among women,* which thou hast drawn to thyself by seeking and finding thy beloved, therefore we too, desiring to attain some trace of thy so great and so noble beauty, desire to know to whom thy beloved is moved, or to whom he turns aside, through his mercy and grace, so that, seeking him too by thy example, after we have found him, even if not most beautiful, we may yet in some measure [1206D] be accounted beautiful. To these things the Church, or the soul beloved of God, showing the place of the moving or turning aside of this beloved, suggests at the same time also the act in that turning aside of his, saying: *My beloved is gone down into his garden,* etc.



## CHAPTER VI.

* 206* VERSE 1.-- *My beloved is gone down into his garden, to the little bed of spices, that he may feed in the gardens, and gather lilies.*

What indeed was it to seek the beloved, but to ascend to him through the power of contemplation? for since in the hearts of the saints *faith worketh by love [n: (Gal. V)]*, and love always draws the mind to the things above, to work good is therefore to descend [cj: *descendere*; read *ascendere*, "to ascend"] to God in mind. But since all good is from God, without [1207A] whom nothing of good exists, for anyone to be helped by him in good is surely his descending to him; for it is written in Exodus that Moses ascended into the mountain, and the Lord descended to him [n: (Exod. XIX)]. For as much as each one ascends to God in thought, and in action by seeking him, so much does God descend to him, whether by opening his mysteries to him through the understanding, or by conferring the help of his grace through strength of soul. For where anyone ascends to God by free will, in will and in work, there God descends to him through helping grace, by his mercy and condescension

Whence also here the beloved fittingly answered those desiring to seek the beloved, that *my beloved is gone down into his [1207B] garden:* as if she said: Since you desire to know whither my beloved is gone, or whither he has turned aside, behold, I tell thee that *my beloved is gone down into his garden.* What is the garden of the beloved, but either holy Church or any holy soul sprouting with the diverse beauty of the virtues? into which the beloved descends, because to it as it sprouts with good acts he bestows the help of his grace. But after he has gone down into the garden, whither has he turned himself? *to the little bed,* she says, *of spices.* What is the *little bed of spices,* *207* but a mind planted with various kinds of sweetly fragrant virtues, or sprinkled with the repute of diverse graces as with a good odour? for those who can say with the Apostle: *We are the good odour of Christ unto God in every place [n: (II Cor. II)]*, these surely [1207C] in the garden of the Lord, that is, in holy Church, each one in his own manner can be called a *little bed of spices,* because they spread on the minds of their neighbours the odour of good repute through the examples of good works.

But why does she say not "bed" but, by diminution, *little bed,* unless because the holy mind which shines with the sublimity of the virtues ought to be little in its own eyes and greatly to show that *the Lord is high and looketh on the low things and will save the humble of spirit? [n: (Psal. CXII, XXXIII.)]* To this *little bed,* then, the beloved, going down into his garden, turns aside, because to the holy mind, through increases of holiness and knowledge, he shows himself easy and as it were tractable. For every pursuit seems difficult to the beginner, easy to the one grown used to it: and to one entering the way of the commandments of God, or the knowledge of Holy Scripture, [1207D] the gate seems narrow and the entry difficult, but to one who wears it by long exercise, and as it were thrusts back frequent obstacles, the way of holiness and understanding is opened broad and spacious, and therefore easy ingress and egress thereafter lie open.

She adds, however, the cause why he descended: *That he may feed,* she says, *in the gardens, and gather lilies:* he feeds in the gardens because he delights in that odour which is spread from the little bed of spiritual spices, that is, of good works. For he himself says in the Gospel: *My meat is to do the will of my Father [n: (Joan. IV)]*; for the will of the Father is that all men be saved [n: (I Tim. II)]. Further, that men desire salvation and attain to it is his work. And what things the Father works, these same the Son also works, because what [1208A] the Father wills, the Son also wills, and one is the will and operation of both. And he gathers lilies, because he chooses rather those who are white with integrity and cleanness of heart and body, or takes up those perfect in faith and work to be rewarded in eternal beatitude.

VERSE 2.-- *I to my beloved, and my beloved to me, who feedeth among the lilies.*

My beloved, she says, had above commanded me, saying: *Arise, open to me, my sister, my love, my dove, my undefiled,* and I, obeying him, *opened the bolt of my door [n: (Cant. V)]*, by preaching him to all and showing reasonably how great and of what kind he is. But what did my beloved to these things? truly he repays the return of my joyful obedience, chanting to me the praise of his condescension, beatifying not my grace but [1208B] his own in me. For his was it that I willed, that I was able, that I accomplished, and therefore he himself proclaims his gifts in me, that to me and to all obedient to his grace *208* he may show of how great merit before God is a life obedient to holiness and uprightness.

But of what kind is the beloved? *Who feedeth,* she says, *among the lilies,* that is, he delights in clean thought and working, not that he grows into anything by accident, who never receives any increase or loss. Since he remains always unchangeable in one and the same essence and property, but that, by some manner of speech transferred from the creature, the affection of his goodness is expressed toward those [1208C] whom he has deigned to count worthy of his love and grace. For he is said to feed when, in the mind of anyone which he inhabits by his grace, the progress of the virtues is increased.

With what song, then, or in what mode of song, did the voice of this beloved sound to me? with a sweet song surely, a song to be attended to not moderately, in an eight-stringed mode indeed, but on the several strings, because of the force of each sound, notably disposed with its own theorem in this manner.

VERSE 3.-- *Thou art beautiful, my love, sweet and comely as Jerusalem: terrible as an army of a camp set in array.*

Here it is pleasing to behold, as a spectacle, how the modulation of the present song runs through eight parts as through eight voices, so that the property of that symphony which [1208D] musicians call διαπασον may here be attended to, whose last voice is the same as the first, as can be seen also in the disposition of these parts, where, while the first part is set down in this manner, *terrible as an army of a camp set in array,* the last part is nonetheless ended in the same manner, namely *terrible as an army of a camp set in array.*

But the several parts demand several reasons, which are distinguished in this manner. The first is, *thou art beautiful, my love, sweet and comely as Jerusalem, terrible as an army of a camp set in array.*

*Thou art beautiful,* he says, *my love, sweet and comely as Jerusalem: beautiful,* in intention, *sweet* in speech, *comely* in working, and therefore to be joined to me in the inviolable covenant of holy friendship. And how do this [1209A] beauty, or sweetness, or comeliness appear? *As Jerusalem.* And what is Jerusalem interpreted, but *vision of peace?* Now three qualities of this vision are denoted under the name of Jerusalem: one earthly, another spiritual, the third heavenly [sic: *coelestus*]; and the middle one indeed is constituted after the likeness of the extremes, while yet it is itself the surpassing of the first and the preparation of the last. And how is the middle one constituted after the likeness of the extremes? in the mind of each one are the virtues, as the citizens and household of the soul, to which are opposed vices and sins, as *209* the most bitter enemies of that soul. And a likeness is given to it from the vision of earthly peace, that as a city, its enemies conquered and driven out, flourishes in peace, rejoices and prospers, so the mind, vices and sins overcome, rests sweetly in the delight [1209B] of the virtues and grows peaceably in the grace of God, the flesh subject to the spirit, and is raised the more to the contemplation of the Godhead, the less it is assailed by earthly desires.

Further, it receives the other likeness from the vision of peace above, but not wholly comparable, because this is in a riddle, but that in truth, so that as there the soul, free from all misery and labour, beholds God continually with contemplative charity, so here the mind, freed from every assault of the flesh, advances by daily increase to the sweetness of that same contemplative life. Whence this middle one rightly, as has been said, can be the preparation of that last and eternal one, because through this one that one is reached: further, it is the surpassing of the first, because although there is some likeness in them in reason, [1209C] yet there is no comparison in dignity, since that is the momentary reception of worldly peace, this the eternal beholding of the peace above, which here indeed begins but there is perfected.

To which, then, of these three visions of peace is the beauty of the beloved compared? If we say to the first, what comparison will there be of flesh to spirit, of light to darkness, of time to eternity? But if we say to the last, what comparison is there of that which is still held with fear, and of that which, free and secure from all fear, is granted without end? To the middle one, then, it can be compared, so that this beauty, this sweetness, this comeliness are gathered from the vision of inward peace, and these things arise there, advance and abide, where no delights [1209D] of temporal things weary the soul, but, the fight of flesh and Spirit ceasing, according to the Apostle: *The peace of Christ which passeth all understanding keep the hearts and understandings [n: (Philip. IV)]* [var: Vulg. *pax Dei*, "the peace of God"], of one resting in the love of the contemplative life. *Thou art beautiful,* then, *beloved, sweet and comely as Jerusalem,* like the vision of a pacified mind, which no injury of disquiet, no trouble of adversity disturbs in the joyfulness of holy contemplation.

And what effect does the joyfulness of this contemplation impart? surely a great one, because it kindles the whole mind in the charity of God and neighbour, and to the malignant spirits, that is, the enemies of its soul, for she adds, *terrible as an army of a camp set in array;* for the army of a camp, when it is so set in array *210* that, [1210A] from the unanimity and concord of the host disposed together, no access of breaking in can open to the enemy, then surely shows itself terrible to that enemy. But where access of breaking in is granted to the enemy through the disorder of the host, there certainly it seems in no way terrible.

Holy Church, therefore, or the soul beloved of God, when it sets the battle line of spiritual combat against the malignant spirits, must of utmost necessity be found always united and bound close through charity, and never broken through by discord, because, even if it should have whatever goods, if charity be lacking, a place is opened in the line through the evil of discord, whence the enemy may be able to enter to strike. For the ancient enemy fears neither chastity, nor abstinence, nor [1210B] the distribution of earthly things, if they should be without charity, because he himself is not pressed by flesh, so that either he should be undone by its lust, or be urged by its necessity in taking food, or in acquiring the supports of riches.

But he greatly fears true charity, that is, humble love, which the faithful bestow on one another in turn: and he envies their concord exceedingly, because they hold this on earth which he, being unwilling to hold it, lost in heaven.

Well, therefore, is it said *terrible as an army of a camp set in array,* because the malignant spirits fear the multitude of the elect the more, the more they see them united and massed together against themselves through the concord of charity.

[1210C]VERSE 4.-- *Turn away thy eyes from me, for they have made me flee away. Thy hair is as a flock of goats, which have appeared from Galaad.*



## CHAPTER VI. (cont.)

The second and third parts are contained in this verse. He says, therefore, *turn away thy eyes from me, for they have made me flee away;* because, he says, the building of thy mind rises honourably and profitably from the foundation of fraternal charity within Jerusalem, that is, *the vision of peace,* showing its beauty far off, in thought, speech and work; this truly is the summit and the top of that same building, and as it were a certain conclusion, that it be finished in one cubit, namely by setting thy whole intention on the love of God alone: whence thou hast this also, O love, still more abundantly [1210D] from my grace, that thou canst penetrate with the subtlety of a purged mind something of my power and brightness. Yet however much thou strivest, however much thou puttest forward the sharpness of thy soul, thy labour is frustrated, because it is impossible by any wit or reason to define the incomprehensible and uncircumscribed.

Wherefore *turn away thy eyes from me,* that is, do not wish to know perfectly the substance of my Godhead, which is granted to no one in this life, but rather turn back to thyself, and be mindful of human frailty: *211* for thy eyes, that is, the senses of thy mind, fixed too much on me, have themselves made me flee away, because the more thou stretchest toward me, the more thou wilt prove me incomprehensible, according to what is written: *Man shall approach to a deep heart and God shall be exalted* [1211A] [n: (Psal. LXIII)]: for the higher and more subtly thou approachest me by examining the property of my substance, the more sublimely do I fly away, by exceeding all the consideration and subtlety of thy sense. Wherefore *turn away thy eyes from me,* because thy senses are indeed very keen in the contemplation of the mind, by the largess of my grace, but they fail utterly in the consideration of me, as I flee away for the depth of the matter. But since so great is the grace of thy eyes, what too is the grace of the hair?

*Thy hair,* he says, *is as a flock of goats which have appeared from Galaad.* A little above, the praise of the beloved was sung in the same modes, which he therefore now repeats, either to show that she ought to have the same comeliness and beauty of bearing and of soul in the preaching of the word [1211B] of God, for the instruction of the hearers, which she had in the solitude of contemplation for her own profit; or because holy Church, whether universal or particular, is more commendable in her twin order from which she is constituted, namely of those set over and of subjects, in whom all her beauty, grace and honour is considered, from the life and doctrine of the former, and from the obedience and reverence for discipline of the latter.

For as the hairs, springing from the head, cleave to the head, so subjects cleave to the prelates who begot them by the word of life, in faith and morals; as it were going up from Galaad, because, while at their teaching they bear the heap of testimony, that is, of Holy Scripture, they lift their life up to heaven by living well.

[1211C]VERSE 5.-- *Thy teeth are as a flock of sheep which have come up from the washing, all with twin offspring, and there is none barren among them.*

Here the fourth part is touched. What indeed is understood by the *teeth* but the doctors, carrying the grossness of the letter over into the subtlety of the spiritual sense, and white with simplicity, as washed in the washing of holy compunction, whence they themselves also are fruitful with twin charity, or instructed in the double testament, and render those subject to them fruitful with the same charity and the same testament, so instructing them that none among them, that is, those set over or subjects, grows sluggish with barrenness of the virtues.

But the hairs of the particular soul, born from the principal part of the mind as from the *212* head, are the [1211D] subtleties of the thoughts, attending to the heap of divine sentences: further, the *teeth* of that same mind, as it handles and handles again, are the vehement applications with zeal, breaking the hard and strong propositions or oppositions of the Scriptures, and unfolding very many of their wrappings. In these there is no barrenness, because the more zealously and the longer they apply the soul to the Scriptures, the more vehemently are they pricked to the love of God and neighbour, and the more fully do they attain the knowledge of each Testament, or, keeping the precept, they add the counsel over it: for the precept is not to seize what is another's, the counsel to give away one's own.

These surely are the *twin offspring,* by which the soul is made fruitful, namely the love of God and of neighbour, the knowledge [1212A] of the Old and New Testament, the observance of the precept with the adding of the counsel. By the intent and zeal of these, surely, all inward barrenness is driven off, so that deservedly in thought, working, preaching the beloved is pronounced *beautiful, sweet and comely.*

VERSE 6.-- *As the rind of a pomegranate, so are thy cheeks, besides thy hidden things.*

If we refer it universally to the whole Church, her *cheeks* can be called those who live spiritually in her, who, while shining in memorable and wonderful words and deeds, gleam also with stupendous miracles, appear venerable as it were in the face of the Church; for the beauty of cheeks is considered in their redness. But if so great is the glory of her comeliness in those things which appear, what pure glory of hers will there be in those things which do not yet appear? of which it is written: that [1212B] *neither eye hath seen, nor ear heard, what things God hath prepared for those who love him [n: (I Cor. II)]*.

The *cheeks,* then, of the Church are compared *to the rind of a pomegranate,* but her hidden things, because they are ineffable, are therefore also incomparable.

But if we refer it specially to the honour of the holy soul, very often its inward beauty is declared by certain outward signs; for while the modesty of virginal chastity blushes in the face, that is shown outwardly which is held inwardly in the soul. Her *cheeks,* then, are *as the rind of a pomegranate besides her hidden things,* because outwardly indeed she puts forth from bashfulness a certain sign of holiness, but how much that holiness itself flourishes in the hidden place of her mind lies open to him alone [1212C] from whom nothing can be hidden. And this of the cheeks.

* 213* VERSE 7.-- *There are threescore queens and fourscore concubines, and of young maidens there is no number.*

The sixth and seventh parts are described in the present verse. For since in the foregoing he had proclaimed the beauty of the Church by parts, in the prelates and subjects and those living spiritually, who are designated by the hair, teeth and cheeks, now he follows up with renowned praise her comeliness, perfect as a whole, in the unity of faith: for although holy Church in some manner seem diverse by the diversity of merits, yet because of one and the same faith she is not found diverse; for three qualities of those living in her can be considered, because there are in her both the perfect and the less perfect and the imperfect: the perfect indeed [1212D] are those who have left all their things, and have cleaved to God alone with their whole mind; the less perfect, those who have not indeed left all their things, but out of those things which they can have are busy to minister to God in his members; the imperfect, those who in faith indeed cleave to the higher ones, but, as it were in a still tender manner of life, have not yet attained the good of perfect discretion.

Whence here not unfittingly the sum of three numbers is brought in? namely of six, of eight and of ten. For ten, multiplied by six, rises to sixty, while eight, led through ten, makes eighty. Six, moreover, is called perfect by arithmetic law, because when with all its parts, which are three, two, and one, [1213A] for its half part is three, its third two, its sixth one, it is gathered together, it is restored into the prior sum without any increase or decrease. Further, eight is named diminished, because since its half part is four, its fourth two, its eighth one, when these same parts are gathered together seven is made, less, that is, in quantity than the prior sum. And ten, which intimates the Decalogue of the law, because it consists of seven and three, the sevenfold grace of the Holy Spirit and the faith of the holy Trinity confession, which numbers show who are perfect, who imperfect. For the Holy Spirit himself shows in the effects of his gifts who love God perfectly, who less, who as yet have neither the fear nor the love of God.

[1213B]For there are two things which make us wholly subject to God, namely fear and love: his power indeed and his wisdom chiefly *214* strike fear, when we know that he whom we know to exist is both able to punish all things he will, and that nothing is hidden from him; but his kindness [sic: *begninitas*] pertains specially to love, that we may love most him whom we hold most kind. From which also it is certain that he wills to punish impiety, because the more equity pleases him, the more iniquity displeases him, as it is written: *Thou hast loved justice and hated iniquity [n: (Psal. XLIV)]*.

By the number sixty, therefore, which is made by six, perfect, multiplied by ten, the life of the perfect is expressed, who would not perfectly observe the divine precepts [1213C] unless they had first the initial fear, afterwards the filial love that shuts out that very fear. And such souls are well named *queens,* because, divine grace working with them, they know how and are able to rule themselves by reason and understanding, when they discern profit, glory, and honour in the performing of good, and on the other hand loss, misery, and horror in the committing of evil, and therefore they strongly strive after good and strongly turn aside from evil.

But by the number eighty, which is completed by ten led through eight, which is called diminished, the life of the less perfect is denoted, who observe indeed the divine precepts, but do not love God so perfectly that they are able to leave all earthly things. And these souls are designated by the name of *concubines,* because, [1213D] although they are joined to God through good works, yet they have not yet obtained that divine right of which it is said, *He who cleaveth to God is made one Spirit with him [n: (I Cor. VI)]*: for this is the full writing of the spiritual dowry, that those who renounce the world utterly are never separated from the divine fellowship. But of the *young maidens* the *number* is not defined, because infinite is the number of the imperfect, who, set as yet under the first-fruits of faith, need the instruction of their elders for conceiving the fear of God, through which they may at some time advance to his love.

Among whom there are some concerning whom very often the estimate is doubtful, since not all belong to the lot of the elect, [1214A] according to the voice of the Lord which says: *Many are called but few chosen [n: (Matth. XX)]*.

VERSE 8.-- *One is my dove, my perfect one: she is one to her mother, the chosen of her that bore her. The daughters saw her and declared her most blessed: the queens and concubines praised her.* [var: Vulg. *matris suae*, "of her mother"]

But since the qualities of merits in the Church are so diverse, is she therefore said to be diverse? by no means; but as follows, *one is my dove, my perfect one, she is one to her mother, the chosen of her that bore her.* Whence *one?* namely *215* because of the one and the same receiving of faith and of the sacraments, and because of the worship of one God, and a like rite of religion: *for one is the faith,* as the Apostle says, *one baptism, one God and Father of all, who is above all [1214B] in us all [n: (Ephes. IV)]*.



## CHAPTER VI. (cont.)

*One is,* he says, *my dove, my perfect one:* for otherwise she is not my dove unless, bearing in her mouth the sign of peace and mercy, she keep the unity of the spirit in her heart and exercise the virtue of mercy in work: *For mercy and truth have met each other, justice and peace have kissed [n: (Psal. LXXXIV)]*, for she ought always to sing mercy and judgment [n: (Psal. C)], in the power of binding and loosing granted her by me. Nor otherwise will she be *my perfect one* unless she have full knowledge of discretion, that she may know who is justly and worthily to be bound or loosed. *One is* moreover *the dove and one my perfect one,* because outside her there is given no remission of sins, nor distribution of gifts, and therefore those are shut out who do not confess one, [1214C] holy, catholic and universal Church. For in one house the lamb is eaten and its flesh is not carried out, because there is no efficacy of the sacraments except in the one catholic Church.

*She is one to her mother,* that is, to the Jerusalem above, because she who here on earth is one in faith and in the dignity of the sacraments, there in heaven will be one in the beatitude of eternal life: for one is the beatitude of the saints, although they do not enjoy it in one manner, because of the qualities of diverse merits. *The chosen of her that bore her,* that is, of the Church of the ancient Fathers, namely of the patriarchs and prophets, whose faith she imitated and inherited the promise: [1214D] for she is chosen, to whom should be shown that promise of the purpose of God which, made to the Fathers in the first days, was fulfilled in the last days, the same faith justifying her concerning things past and future which justified the ancient Fathers concerning things future only. For faith is as much concerning things present and future as concerning things past; although faith concerning things present is said improperly and not properly: for when the Apostle, defining faith, says: *Faith and the substance of things to be hoped for, the evidence of things not appearing [n: (Hebr. XI)]* [var: Vulg. *Est autem fides sperandarum substantia rerum*, "now faith is the substance of things to be hoped for"], it is surely clear that faith is the evidence of those things which cannot appear, for things which are apparent have not faith, but acknowledgment.

But since we are treating of the Canticles of canticles, does [1215A] any musical speculation of the two numbers set above, namely sixty and eighty, pertain to them? It does not seem at all beside the matter, if, where canticles are treated, we sometimes speculate on the modes of singing. For since the whole frame of our soul and body is joined by musical fitting together, it is plain, and proved by examples, on the authority of divine and human Scripture, that the force of musical harmony has had great power for composing, *216* or for stirring, or for pacifying souls. Ought not some speculation of musical consonance to have power also in this *Canticle of canticles,* which wholly aims at kindling souls to the love of the heavenly bridegroom, for the composing or stirring of the soul? Surely in this place too it will be able to lend a little [1215B] support to the imperfection *of the concubines and young maidens,* so that they may be incited in some way to attaining the perfection which it is agreed they do not yet have.

But since every well-ordered disposition is assigned to the proportions of numbers, it must be seen what consonance consists in the proportionality of these two numbers, sixty and eighty. The relation of eighty to sixty, then, is ἐπιτριτα or sesquitertian, because eighty has within itself the whole of sixty, and its third part, that is, twenty, in which proportion διατέσσερον sounds together in music; it is called διατέσσερον from four, because it resounds with four voices, consisting of two tones and a semitone.

[1215C]If it is asked what is to the point in this place, by the proportion of these numbers, the consideration of this symphony? we answer that there is a reason sufficiently fitting in a consideration of this kind, because the whole universal Church spread through the four parts of the world, which, as has been said, is distinguished by three qualities of those living in her, that is, of the perfect, the less perfect, the imperfect, attains the whole sum of her salvation through the faith and sacraments of the New Testament, which was instituted by the perfection of the four Gospels, so that as it were a certain sweet concord of the διατέσσερον symphony, the consonance of the evangelists, consisting in four books as in four voices, by which the soul of all Christians [sic: *Christianonorum*] is stirred to the worship of holy religion and [1215D] is composed.

*The daughters of Sion saw her and declared her most blessed, the queens and concubines praised her.* Such a Church, he says, as has been said, differing in the quality of merits, but alike in the unity of faith and sacraments, *the daughters of Sion saw,* that is, souls attending by the speculation of the mind, not by carnal vision, and weighing the beauty and honour of the whole Church, in the excellence of spiritual dignities, and the grace of the virtues, and also the necessary profit of that beauty, since there consist in her both the more generous and more wholesome granting of temporal things, as the incomparable and never-ending bestowal of eternal things: accordingly *they declared her most blessed,* because nothing is more blessed than always to have God, [1216A] here temporally by using him in his gifts, there eternally by enjoying him.

*217* *The queens also and concubines praised her,* because to holy souls who know how to rule their inner and outer senses by reason this honour of holy Church and the profit of that honour is very pleasing, and they praise much, that is, they approve, the spiritual insignia of her workings and the pious supports of sacramental things.

VERSE 9.-- *Who is she that cometh forth as the dawn rising, fair as the moon, chosen as the sun, terrible as an army of a camp set in array?*

Whence also these daughters of Sion, or the beloved himself, proclaiming this beauty of hers, not interrogatively as those who do not know, but affirmatively as those who marvel, [1216B] whom indeed they know well, with such a proclamation, as it were with the eighth voice, raise and say:

*Who is she that cometh forth as the dawn rising, fair as the moon, chosen as the sun, terrible as an army of a camp set in array?* To those looking here not in passing but diligently there is offered, in the measure of praise of this kind, a modulation not to be despised of the symphony tasted beforehand, which we have called διατέσσερον, by which is hinted the confirmation of faith, hope, charity, and the sacraments, and the preaching of the Gospel through the four parts of the world, and also the useful and necessary attaining of the four principal virtues. For why should there not be attended to here, as it were, the distinction of four voices, the comparison of four diverse things, namely the coming forth of the dawn, the beauty of the moon, the choosing of the sun, [1216C] the strength of the army set in array. For what more is there in the comeliness of the whole Church? In short, if faith, hope, charity and the sacraments be lacking, if the preaching of the Gospel is not received, if the foundations of the four virtues be not present, from which the buildings of all the other virtues rise, what will be the beauty of the Church, or of each holy soul?

But it must be seen what these likenesses of things intimate, how they fit the things said above, namely faith, hope, charity and the sacraments. The *dawn* is the beginning of the day, further faith is the beginning of holiness and religion as of a certain day, because by the name of day and light holiness is understood, as also by the name of night and darkness sin. The Church, [1216D] then, or any holy soul *cometh forth as the dawn,* because, divine grace calling, she comes first to faith and through the same grace daily takes the increases of that same faith. The *moon,* moreover, always receives light from the sun, and, set in the other part of the hemisphere over against the sun, pours the full light of her orb upon the lands; but when she begins to wane, she returns to the sun, that she may be kindled again by it, and at length with the *218* fullness of her light may aid the world. And whence does holy Church, or any holy soul, receive the light of knowledge, the light also of holiness and justice, and of holy hope, but from that true sun of justice, the Lord Jesus Christ, *who enlighteneth every man that cometh into this world? [n: (Joan. I.)]* But because her beauty differs greatly from the [1217A] beauty of him from whom she receives that she be fair, she is called not absolutely fair, but by comparison *fair* is she called *as the moon:* for as the moon compared to the sun, or shining with the sun, does not shine: yet whatever of light she has she has from the sun, so also the saints compared to the Holy of holies are not, yet whatever of holiness they have, they have from him. And what is the Church but the gathering of the saints? and when, often withdrawing from divine contemplation for the sake of fraternal love, or by the obstacle of their own frailty, they have waned a little from the light of subtle understanding, gathering themselves again to themselves at some time, they run back with their whole mind to him of whom it is written: *Come ye to him and be enlightened [n: (Psal. XXXIII)]*, that, looking back upon him as through a certain opposition of the mind, [1217B] they may receive their fullness, such as it is, from him of whom it is said: *And of his fullness we all have received [n: (Joan. I)]*.

What then do these, coming forth by faith and the works of faith, conceive but hope? For who would not hope for good things to come, whose works are full of light; so that deservedly the Church is called in them *fair as the moon?*

But since faith is *as the dawn coming forth,* that is, pouring forth the first light of religion and of its holiness, through faith certainly one approaches hope, because what we believe we hope that we shall at some time obtain. Whence the Apostle [n: (Rom. V)]: *Access,* he says, *we have through faith into this grace in which we stand, and glory in the hope of the glory of the sons of God;* [var: Vulg. *et gloriamur*, "and we glory"] as if he said in other words: Through faith, which is the beginning and [1217C] as it were a certain entrance of every good work, as the dawn of the day, we approach and advance into the grace of full light, that is, of holiness, according to the measure which grace itself shall have granted us, in the grace of which light we stand, that is, we persevere, because we have hope of being joined through this to the future glory of the sons of God: and at once he adds: *And not only so,* it is understood, we glory in this hope, *but we glory also in tribulations, knowing that tribulation worketh patience, and patience trial, and trial hope, and hope confoundeth not.* And to show the efficient cause of this hope, its certainty, he adds, *because charity is poured forth in our hearts by the holy one who is given to us.* [var: Vulg. *caritas Dei … per Spiritum Sanctum*, "the charity of God … by the Holy Spirit"] For this is the anchor [1217D] of all good intention and working, because, as the same Apostle says: *Charity shall never fall away [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*.

*219* And this is what follows, *chosen as the sun,* namely because of charity, because as the sun is brighter than all the stars, so charity is more excellent than all the virtues, although according to the Godhead it is altogether incomparable.
