And holy Church, in those who burn with perfect charity and shine in working, is called *chosen as the sun,* because, since star differeth from star in brightness [n: (I Cor. XV)], that part of the saints is compared to the sun which is found brighter than the rest in holiness, namely in those who are still weak and tender in faith and work, *she cometh forth as the dawn;* but in those who by the progress of their manner of life have already somewhat [1218A] advanced, she is called *fair as the moon:* further, in those who are perfect, and through the charity which, being perfect, casteth out fear, in of the highest spirits, which are named seraphim, that is, burning, but passing over are joined more nearly to the sun, she is rightly spoken of as *chosen as the sun,* or *chosen as the sun,* that is, predestined unto adoption, that she herself may be this by grace, which he is by nature, the true sun, who *gave power to become sons of God to those who believe in his name [n: (Joan. I)]*.



## CHAPTER VI. (cont.)

But what, someone will say, does this fourth member, as it were the fourth voice of the δια τεσσερον symphony described above, pertain to the sacraments: *terrible as an army of a camp set in array?* Plainly, I say, this too pertains well enough, and this is established from the effects of the sacraments themselves, for what [1218B] is more terrible to the enemies of the soul, that is, to the malignant spirits, than the powers of the sacraments, by which their power is weakened, and all their might and daring put to flight?

For where any sacrament is so performed that the thing of the sacrament also can be present, that is, in the peace and unity of the Church, outside which there is no place for the saving sacraments, there surely the effect of that same sacrament is very *terrible* and unbearable to the evil spirits themselves, whether this be in baptism, or in the Lord's body, or in the distribution of orders, or in the ordering of whatever things are performed by ecclesiastical institutions; for this [1218C] comparison seems to intimate this, by which it is said *terrible as an army of a camp set in array:* because as a well-bound battle line, and packed close in order, suffers no breaking in upon itself, so the Church, bound together by the sacrament of unity, is scattered by no incursion of the malignant spirits. But the heretics who have cut themselves off from this unity, because they are not contained in this battle line, being set outside, are able to repel none of the assaults of those spirits, since indeed the sacraments of the Church which they seem to have, they have not in reality, but only in appearance. Yet those same sacraments, rightly celebrated by them, if the faithful receive them, they receive both *220* the sacrament and the thing of the sacrament, that is, the power working within: while the heretics themselves are proved to take the sacrament indeed, but not the thing of the sacrament, [1218D] and therefore their gathering is not terrible as an army of a camp set in array, because as far as they are concerned the sacraments do not work this, which confer no remedies on them.

VERSE 10.-- *I went down into the garden of nuts to see the fruits of the valleys, and to look if the vineyards had flourished, and the pomegranates budded.* [var: Vulg. *floruisset vinea*, "the vineyard had flourished"]

After the beloved has followed up with gracious praise the beauty of the holy beloved, namely the Church, whose praise however is from him who by his grace presented her to himself *not having spot or wrinkle [n: (Ephes. V)]*, he shows in what follows that, because of the unbelief of the Jewish people, she earned the gift and merit of faith, as the Apostle says: *By their offence salvation to the Gentiles, and their offence the delight [1219A] of the world, and their diminution the riches of the Gentiles [n: (Rom. XI)]* [var: Vulg. *divitiae mundi*, "the riches of the world"]. For he who extolled the one in the manner of admiration, in the same manner presses down the other, because while the one believed and received the word of God, the other would neither believe nor receive that word: whence it is written of her in the Gospel: *And he wondered,* no doubt Jesus, *at their unbelief [n: (Marc. VI)]*. He says therefore, *I went down into the garden of nuts.* What was his going down, but to come down from the height of his Godhead into this world by being born, then to endure all the weak things of humanity? Were not all these things, fulfilled in their order, a certain descent? And where were these descents celebrated? Was it not in Judaea? In short, the Lord himself says: *I was not sent but to the sheep that are lost of the house of Israel [n: (Matth. XV)]*, for this [1219B] is what he says here, *I went down into the garden of nuts;* for the nation of the Jews can, not unfittingly, be called the garden of nuts: for as in a nut the kernel is covered under the rind and shell, so among the Jews the sweetness of spiritual understanding is hidden under the hardness of the literal surface. That nation, then, is called *the garden of nuts,* because although it is called a *garden* because of the profit of the legal precepts and observances, which flourished in their time and profited their observers up to the time of grace, yet all the future sacraments of the Church lay hidden in it under a figure, as under the rind of a nut. Into this garden, therefore, the Lord went down, because first of all he showed the presence of his flesh to the Jewish people by preaching and doing miracles.

[1219C]But why did he go down into this garden? *To see,* he says, *the fruits of the valleys,* that is, to know whether any had made fruit of faith and of injustice [cj: *injustitiae*; read *justitiae*, "of justice"], and, reason showing it, might understand that they cannot be justified by the *221* works of the law, but by the faith which I have set before those who believe through the grace of the New Testament. But I say "to know," not that I have not had this or anything else present to me from eternity, who am everywhere, and to whom all things past and future are present, but, speaking in human fashion, that I might show their works to the world, whether they were to be followed or not. But why do I say *of the valleys?* Because of the lowliness of the letter which they follow carnally, or because of the shadow of the law by which it is surrounded, [1219D] lest they be enlightened by the light of its truth.

*And to look if the vineyards had flourished,* that is, whether they had conceived at least some savour of spiritual understanding, whether they had applied themselves to the faith by some beginning of good work: for the houses of Israel are called vineyards, which are distinguished by twelve tribes; which, although they are called vineyards because of the distinction of the tribes, yet are called one vineyard, because of the one observance of the law; as, although there are now said to be many Churches because of the diversity of places, yet there is said to be one Church because of the unity of faith. And that the whole of that people is called the vineyard of the Lord the Prophet testifies. *The vineyard,* he says, *of the Lord is the house of Israel [n: (Isa. V)]*; of which the Psalmist also says: *Thou hast brought a vineyard out of Egypt [n: (Psal. LXIX)]*; but *when I looked that this vineyard [1220A] should flourish and make grapes, it made wild grapes [n: (Isa. V)]*. for after the Gentiles believed, they remained in unbelief.

*I went down* also *to look if the pomegranates had budded,* that is, that they might not only believe in me, but also have the will of suffering for me: but they would neither believe, nor suffer anything for me, but rather they killed me as I preached to them and showed the way of salvation.

In another way also it does not seem burdensome to expound this particle of the present song by the likeness of the nut, so that the reader may choose what in these he weighs better and more rightly. It does not seem absurd to take the garden of nuts as the whole of the human race, for in the nut three things are considered, rind, shell and kernel; [1220B] and in the human race man consists of flesh, bone and soul; for the matter of the flesh is as the rind, the bony strength is as the shell, further the sweetness of the soul is as the kernel: and as the kernel is covered by rind and shell, so the soul is clothed with flesh and bones. Whence Job, considering that man consists of these three, says: *Thou hast clothed me with skin and flesh, thou hast put me together with bones and sinews [n: (Job X)]*. And to show the third, which quickens and enlivens those two, that is, the soul, he adds: *Thou hast granted me life and mercy [n: (ibid.)]*. Into the garden of nuts, then, Christ went down when, by coming into this world, he took up human nature.

And why? *To see the fruits of the valleys* *222,* that is, to visit the humble having the fruit of justice, [1220C] and to choose them for himself, for his seeing is choosing, so that, coming humble, he might intimate that he takes up the humble and obedient, as it is written: *And he will save the humble of spirit [n: (Psal. XXXIII)]*. He went down also to look *if the vineyards had flourished, and the pomegranates budded,* that is, whether the Churches of the faithful had begun the pursuit of living or teaching rightly, and were striving to persevere in his love even unto death, so that by bestowing his help on them he himself might be both inspirer and fellow worker, as he says, promising them: *Behold, I am with you all days even to the consummation of the world [n: (Matth. XXVIII)]*.

[1220D]VERSE 11.-- *I knew not: my soul troubled me, because of the chariots of Aminadab.*

The people of the Jews puts forward the cause of its ignorance and blindness, that since the law had been given which should justify and save them, and God had confirmed it by many signs and by the great authorities of the holy Fathers, it would be impossible for it to be made void by any novelty, since God is unchangeable, nor does he, in human fashion, say, command and institute anything at one time which at another time he forbids and destroys. For if, they say, that displeases in the future which now pleases, he is changeable, which is most absurd to say, nor can it stand by any reason that God is changeable. But if he himself is unchangeable, his words also and precepts are unchangeable: but because he is wholly unchangeable, whatever [1221A] he has said or established must be fixed and unchangeable, therefore he has laid down no second and new law to destroy the first and old one.

To whom it is answered that what they have proposed must be granted, namely that God is unchangeable, whence in the new law he has established nothing which derogated from the former, but rather that the new law and the old are the same, but that one in the letter, this in the spirit; that one in figure, this in truth. That this is so was shown beforehand of old in the vision of Ezechiel the prophet, where *a wheel in the midst of a wheel [n: (Ezech. X)]* is recorded to have been seen. For the wheel in the midst of the wheel is the New Testament within the Old Testament, because what the Old Testament designated, this the New displayed: God, then, being unchangeable, changed nothing in this, who [1221B] so placed the one under the other that the one should be the signifying, the other the signified.

This, then, that people at first knew not, because it read the signifying, but did not understand the signified: but after, visited by divine grace, it began to know and believe that the letter killeth, but the spirit *223* quickeneth, repenting of its error, it was sore afraid, saying: *I knew not: my soul troubled me because of the chariots of Aminadab.* As if it said: Therefore I could not believe, because I did not yet at all understand the grace of the Gospel [sic: *Evangeiii*], for *my soul troubled me,* because I wondered what it could be, that I should now have to receive the four books of the Gospels who before had received the five books of Moses, as if this change could befall God, that in this way that should not please him which had pleased him before, [1221C] who should found a new law, the old one transferred. But after I understood the wonderful providence of God, who instructed me through carnal things toward spiritual things, so that I might comprehend more easily and more truly not first the spiritual, but that which is animal, afterwards that which is spiritual, having laid aside all doubt I afterwards defended the truth which I had before assailed, not only by believing and teaching that truth, but also by dying for it.

But why do I say *the chariots of Aminadab?* truly because of the four books of the Gospel, in which are preached the four sacraments of that same Aminabad, who is interpreted *the willing one of my people.* And who is this *Aminabad,* but Christ who willingly laid down [1221D] his life, that he might make me for himself a willing people and acceptable to God? And what are his four sacraments but his incarnation, passion, resurrection and ascension? The *chariot,* then, of this *Aminabad* is the book of the four Gospels, containing and conferring the knowledge of the four sacraments aforesaid, the Holy Spirit driving, by whose inspiration chariots of this kind were composed and ordered.

And when the kind Father received that same people which had believed out of the circumcision, returning to him, the black darkness of ignorance shaken off, running to meet it as it were with a consoling and exhorting voice, he calls it to penitence, saying: *Return, return, Sunamitis,* etc.

[1222A]VERSE 12.-- *Return, return, Sunamitis; return, return, that we may behold thee.* [var: Vulg. *Sulamitis*]



## CHAPTER VI. (cont.)

Behold, he says, there runs to meet it the voice of my chariot, which sounds forth the cause and order and necessary profit of my sacraments, namely of my nativity, passion, resurrection and ascension, which voice indeed thou, formerly ignorant, wouldst neither hear nor receive, but now, O *Sunamitis,* that is, captive, who art still held captive because thou hast not yet laid down the bond of original or actual sin through the washing of baptism and the participation of my body and blood, *return,* because thou hast hope of pardon. For thou didst withdraw from me through unbelief and ingratitude, forgetful of the benefits which I wrought toward thee, when I freed thee from the [1222B] bondage of Egypt, when I *224* fed thee with heavenly bread, when I gave thee my law, and chose thee before all nations for myself as a peculiar people. But now *return* to me through the voice of my chariot, through the four Gospels, [the chariot] crying out to thee as through four wheels and four times exhorting thee to penitence, *return, return, return, return.* Return through faith, return through working, return through right intention, return through love: for first thou must believe, then work, because *faith without works is dead [n: (Jac. II)]*; further thy working must proceed from good intention, namely that faith may work through love; for thy working, if it is not from divine love, lies subject to vain [1222C] glory, and therefore lacks reward, because it has not the merit of holiness.

*Return,* moreover, *that we may behold thee,* that is, that we may approve the comeliness of thy soul which thou hast received through faith, and set thee forth as an example for others to follow: for this is thy praiseworthy beauty which pleases our eyes, that, error laid aside, thou follow the truth, and that thou who through error and the shadow of the law didst seem misshapen and dark, through thy return, through the truth and light of the Gospel, be seen beautiful and bright. For as long as thou didst lie hid under the shadow thou wast despicable, but after thou camest forth to the light of truth thou didst appear worthy to be seen, and now *the king hath desired thy comeliness [n: (Psal. XLIV)]* [var: Vulg. *concupiscet*, "shall desire"], that heavenly King namely [1222D] who made thee his bride through the grace of the Holy Spirit, after thou didst return to him by faith, working, right intention, love; these things indeed have been said specially with regard to that ancient people of God, which, the less it at first understood the chariot of our Aminadab, the more it received it with veneration after it understood.

But to return to the moral sense, the signification of this chariot can, not unprofitably, be referred to the whole of the human race, so that we make two parts of the whole, to one of which we give that chariot whose charioteer is the devil. For man, consisting of four elements, and four humours, and also of the increases of four ages, seems to have as it were a certain material chariot [1223A] on the side of the body: further on the side of the soul, since it consists of four affections, and also four virtues by which it is led to good, the force and progress of that soul seem to have a spiritual chariot.

Let us see therefore first the complexions arranged in man by fours according to the body, such few as have occurred to the mind, then the qualities of the affections, designated also in fourfold manner according to the soul, so that from these, as we have said, *225* we may make two chariots, the material namely and the affectual, which both, looking to two chariots [cj: *quadrigas*; read *aurigas*, "charioteers"], namely Christ and the devil, are made subject to one of them, since they cannot *serve two masters [n: (Matth. VI)]*, and either, running rightly and happily through the grace of God, the spiritual one is called the chariot of Christ, [1223B] or, running perversely and unhappily through the privation of that same grace, the carnal one is called the chariot of the devil.

There are, then, as we have said, four elements of which man consists, fire, air, water, earth; which are intermingled with four qualities, namely heat, cold, moisture, dryness. For fire is hot and dry, air hot and moist, water cold and moist, earth cold and dry: and man himself, consisting of these four elements and of the qualities of the elements, while he has heat from fire, breath from air, blood from water, flesh from earth, in four seasons is changed by four humours through the increases of four ages.

[1223C]For spring, which is as it were the first flower and beginning of the year: for then the earth begins to flower and bud, and it is hot and moist; it shows forth childhood, which is hot and moist, because in the child the blood is hot and moist; summer, which is hot and dry, shows youth, which is hot and dry, having red choler which abound in the young, dry and hot. Further autumn, which is cold and dry, announces manhood, which is cold and dry, having melancholy, that is, black choler, which are in men in their prime cold and dry. And winter, which is cold and moist, intimates old age, which is cold and moist, slipping phlegm, which abounds in the old, cold and moist.

For complexions of this kind, when they flourish well and in order in man, [1223D] show the man healthy, but when they wander out of order, they disturb the man and make him sick: but because this fourfold constitution is materially in the body of man, by which man himself is drawn to the love of the flesh and the desires of this world, which he contracted from the propagation of original sin, there are born also from this material constitution four affectual qualities, which draw their force and nature chiefly from four inward members, namely the gall, the spleen, the liver and the heart. In short, man has from the gall that he is irascible, from the spleen that he is risible, from the liver that he is concupiscible, from the heart that he is rational: further from that heart, or mind, or soul, there proceed as it were four conceptions, which [1224A] precede every action, namely thought, intention, will, deliberation. *226* For being about to do something we first think, then we intend why and how we should do it, afterwards we dispose in the will what is to be done, lastly we deliberate to accomplish it. But the very force of reason is spread fourfold: he who has wit, memory, understanding, discretion; for he has through wit to find things unknown and hidden by seeking and investigating, through memory to retain things found, through understanding to dispose things retained prudently, manfully and honourably, through discretion to assign things well disposed modestly to the places fitting them.

The rational soul itself also has four affections implanted in it: that is, fear, hope, joy [1224B] and sorrow, of which the poet says: . . . *These fear, desire, and rejoice, and grieve.*
And a philosopher, to a certain pupil of his whom he desired to relieve from the affections of these passions, says: Drive out joys,
Drive out fear,
And put hope to flight,
Nor let sorrow be present.
And these affections, by those who treat of physics, are said, according to the complexions of the material qualities which we have set down above, to rule more in some, less in others: but [1224C] over all these presides the soul strong in reason, which, when they thrust themselves in out of order, is to compose them and bend them to some measure of honour or profit, the divine Spirit ruling. And for a pursuit or discipline of this kind there must of necessity be applied, and implored from God, the four principal virtues, that is, prudence, fortitude, justice, temperance, which, because in every structure of the spiritual building they are laid as it were as certain corner stones by which the other virtues are enclosed and held, greater gifts of divine grace are considered in them.

Since, then, we have made two chariots, the material namely and the affectual, let us set also as it were two presiding over each, spirit and flesh, and as charioteers God and the devil: for that affectual one [1224D] is moved more or less toward reason and honour just so far as it is more or less driven by that material one, through care of the flesh, to the love of temporal life; and so far as the one presiding over each, namely spirit and flesh, which two are always opposed to one another, shall have been superior or inferior; because *the flesh lusteth against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh [n: (Gal. V)]*. For therefore we say that spirit and flesh preside over each, because there are in them certain parts which tend from the flesh to the spirit, or slip from the spirit to the flesh.

For in that material chariot very often some who are stirred by the natural *227* heat and moisture of the flesh contend with such strength of spirit through the grace of God that they break all that natural impulse of the flesh [1225A] through the fire of divine love and the impulse of the spirit, and at times utterly burn it out, and so grow hot toward God that they remain wholly cold toward the world. On the other hand some of cold nature, as they are cold inwardly toward every action in the love of God, therefore are zealous to labour at no good work. In that affectual chariot also there are those who are angry for temporal inconvenience, and there are those who are angry for justice; there are also those who desire good things, there are those who desire evil things; there are those who laugh and rejoice because of the wretched prosperity of this life, there are also those who rejoice and laugh in adversities because of the hope and security of future beatitude; there are also those who use the good of reason well, and act and savour rightly; there are those who [1225B] use it ill and savour and act perversely. There are nonetheless some who use natural wit for good, there are those who use it for evil; there are those who understand good rightly, there are those who understand it ill; there are those who offer rightly, but do not divide rightly, that is, discern; and there are those who both offer rightly and divide rightly, that is, discern; as on the other hand some neither offer rightly nor divide rightly, that is, discern.

Whence to both these chariots there cleave four species which lead both into the full motions of their own pursuit, namely suggestion, delight, consent, custom. For the soul of each one is led either to good or to evil in these four ways, to good indeed by the suggestion of the Holy Spirit, as it is said of him: *For he himself shall suggest to you [1225C] all things whatsoever I shall have said to you [n: (Joan. XIV)]*; by the delight of the soul, by the consent of the spirit, and by the custom of good itself: but to evil by the suggestion of the devil, by the delight of the flesh the consent of the spirit, by the custom of evil itself.

In the midst, then, of these chariots, which are formed from the matter of the flesh and the affection of the mind, the four principal virtues which we have set down above, namely prudence, fortitude, justice, temperance, must needs be set, so that by a certain setting of them as it were round about, the whole concretion of the body placed within may be ruled and composed to some measure of rational form. For as a certain one says: *Virtue is the mean of vices, and drawn back from both,* [1225D] [n: (HOR., Ep. I, XVIII, 9.)]
that is, removed from either part: for example, to be dull or to be evilly cunning is vicious, but in the midst of these vices, namely dullness and evil cunning, the virtue of prudence is set, by whose governing this inequality may be corrected: fortitude also is set between timidity and daring, so that this may truly be to be brave, *228* to be found neither timid in good nor daring in evil. Likewise it can be seen also in the other virtues, from the consideration of which, set beside themselves, the quality of the vices is understood.

From the whole matter, then, of this kind, carnal namely and spiritual, distinguished fourfold, there is made one certain thing formed of matter, which shows in itself as it were a twin chariot of flesh and spirit, over which presides [1226A] as charioteer either God or the devil: but God ruling, saving and quickening; the devil overturning, damning and killing.




## CHAPTER VI. (cont.)

The name indeed is equivocal, since though in both cases it is called a *chariot*, yet in definition it is different: for to fall away from God through the suggestion of the devil and the delight of the flesh, the consent also of the spirit, and the custom of evil working, is to be the chariot of the devil; and conversely, to advance toward God through the suggestion of God, and the delight of the soul, the consent also of the spirit, and the custom of good working, is to be the chariot of God: and conversely the chariot of God is also the mind made four-square by the four virtues which we set down above, that is, by prudence, justice, temperance, fortitude; to which is opposed [1226B] the chariot of the devil, namely the mind unnerved by the four vices contrary to those very virtues, that is, by folly, iniquity, lust, fear.

The Church therefore cries, or the bridegroom himself through the Church, to the Sunamite: *Return, return, return, return,* so that she who had transgressed in these four ways, by returning to God may offer four fruits of penance also, mortifying these things in herself through the grace of God and the strength of the mind. *Return,* he says, through penance and satisfaction for past evils, *return* through contempt of the world, *return* through mortification of the flesh, *return* through constancy in the virtues. *Return,* moreover, *that we may behold thee,* that is, that after thou hast done penance we may have mercy on thee. For to see is, in God, to have mercy: whence the Psalmist to [1226C] God: *Look,* he says, *upon me and have mercy on me [n: (Psal. LXXXV)]*; and on the contrary the turning away of his face is his wrath, as the same Psalmist says: *Thou turnedst away thy face from me, and I became troubled [n: (Psal. XXIX)]*.

But that he says in the plural, *That we may behold thee,* that is the manner of speech used of God by which he says elsewhere. *Let us make man to our image and likeness [n: (Gen. III)]*, which manner is brought in on account of the plurality of persons in the holy Trinity,




## *229* CHAPTER VII.

VERSE 1.-- *What shalt thou see in the Sulamite, but the choirs of camps? How beautiful are thy steps in shoes, O daughter of the prince! the joints of thy thighs are as necklaces which are made by the hand of a craftsman.*

[1226D]So long as Judaea was held captive by the chain of her error, or any soul whatever by the yoke of sin, no gladness could be seen in her, but misery, because both the former, in the fight between the letter and the spirit, and the latter, in the fight between the flesh and the spirit, was ever inferior in her better part: for the former followed the letter alone, the latter the flesh; but when through penance the former acknowledged and received the spiritual sense, and the latter, the love of the flesh trodden down, began to live according to the spirit, the kindly Father, receiving each mercifully, showed that he would bestow his help upon her, so that, having now obtained true and full liberty, and having as it were won the victory over the part adverse to her, she might, rejoicing in flesh and spirit, pay him devout praises. For this he himself promises through the prophet, saying: [1227A] *Return ye to me, and I will return to you. [n: (Malach. V)]*. Whence also, rejoicing together at their conversion, he says to the Church: *What shalt thou see in the Sulamite, but the choirs of camps?* For in choirs is shown the alternation of melody sounding in concord; but in camps a fellowship of warriors is gathered for defence. What then are *the choirs of camps,* but the praises of warriors? and the sense is: Because this Sulamite has turned to me from the captivity under which she was held through *230* guilt, I will confer on her so great a grace of liberty and security that thou, O beloved, oughtest to see and wonder at nothing else in her than joyful thanksgivings and daily proclamations of praise for the motions of the vices overcome and cast down, and the advances in the virtues granted.

[1227B]Now, the two walls coming from opposite sides being as it were joined into one, namely the Jewish and the gentile people, of which he shows the whole universal Church to consist, he is directed again to her praise, that he may proclaim the so diverse graces of his condescension notably set in one body, and that for this reason thanksgivings are justly and worthily to be paid to him. He says therefore: *How beautiful are thy steps in shoes, O daughter of the prince!*

What is expressed by *steps,* but the courses of actions? what is signified by *shoes,* which are made from the skins of dead animals, but the examples of the fathers who went before and have been withdrawn from this life? for as the feet are protected by shoes, so by the examples of the holy fathers the manners of those who follow them [1227C] are defended.

But who is the *daughter of the prince,* but either the Church, or any holy soul whatever, given over through the grace of adoption to him who is the prince of the kings of the earth? The Church therefore, or any holy soul whatever, has beautiful steps in shoes, because she has beautiful and reverend acts from the deeds and teachings of the holy fathers. Wherefore not unfittingly he adds: *The joining of thy thighs as necklaces which are made by the hand of a craftsman.* [var: Vulg. *Juncturae femorum tuorum*, "the joints of thy thighs"]

By the *joining of the thighs* the concord of the two Testaments is understood, because the Old and the New Testament are so evenly joined that what the one announces in figure, this the other represents in truth. [1227D] But what is it that these testaments are compared to *necklaces*? For a necklace is composed of gold and silver and precious stones, and is placed on the breast of matrons, that it may be to them both for a defence and for an ornament. But each Testament is built from the brightness of the divine sense and the lustre of the utterance, as it were from gold and silver, and also from the predications of the diverse virtues, as it were from certain precious gems. And as a necklace defends and adorns the breast of matrons, so each Testament spiritually defends and adorns the breast of holy Church, or of each holy soul: defends it indeed from the hostility of the vices, adorns it with the beautiful variety of the virtues.

*The joining* therefore *of her thighs, as necklaces,* because [1228A] the concord of the two Testaments, by which holy Church, or any holy soul, is daily instructed, confers on her a great protection and ornament; a protection *231* indeed against the perversities of heretics, an ornament on account of the comeliness and beauty of the senses and utterances, or of the power of the sacraments which are celebrated in her, and the honourableness of the ecclesiastical orders.

But who is this craftsman, by whose hand these necklaces, that is, the Testaments, have been made, but almighty God, by whose will, power, working these things have been honourably composed, profitably set forth, happily received? Wherefore the dignity of these Testaments is greatly to be commended, since they have been established by such an author, whose workmanship is not [1228B] human, but divine, not devised, but willed and of his power, for *all things,* indeed, *whatsoever he would he hath done [n: (Psal. XIII)]*, and ever his willing was also his having done.

Holy Church therefore, or any holy soul whatever, gathers all the comeliness and beauty of her inner man from the consideration of these Testaments, [as to] what her needful profit sets before her to be believed, what to be hoped, what to be loved. But why is her *joining of the thighs* praised by the bridegroom? because the concord of the two Testaments, or of the two peoples, or of the twofold charity, constitutes the greatest and whole rule of living well, and lays up the most certain glory of reward for the rule kept; and well did he say the joining [1228C] not femorum, but *feminum*, because from these is conceived, born and advanced all the honourableness and profit of holy Church, or of any faithful soul.

VERSE 2.-- *Thy navel is like a turned bowl; never wanting cups. Thy belly is like a heap of wheat set about with lilies.*

The navel is the middle point of the belly: what then is the *navel* of the Church, but the holy preachers and teachers, who move in the midst of the people, gathering that same people to themselves by teaching and working? For as all the entrails adhere to the navel, so the people adheres to its preachers, ever looking to their words and examples as to a certain middle point [1228D] set about it, that it may establish for itself in their manners and discourses a certain firm foundation of living well.

But this navel is said to be *like a turned bowl,* because, that they may cut away the superfluous and uneven things of manners, the holy teachers ever turn about the same thing, when for the attaining of that which is ever unchangeable and persists the same in its quality they run, labour, and hasten to draw others with them. Which bowl is said *never to want cups,* because these same preachers ever abound in the *232* sentences of the divine utterances, with which they themselves too are made drunk through the grace of the Holy Spirit, and are zealous sufficiently to refresh the thirsting breasts of the peoples.

In another way also, if we look to the soul of each faithful man, [1229A] holy charity can spiritually be called the navel of the mind, which, set as it were in a certain centre, touches all the virtues as it were through the circumference, because, since no virtue can be without charity, all the virtues, looking toward it, assuredly seem to be as it were appendages of charity.

But how is this called a *turned bowl?* The Apostle says that *the end of the commandment is charity unto justice to every one that believeth [n: (I Tim. I)]*; therefore all perfection consists in charity, because it is turned, retaining in itself nothing of superfluity and of obstructing vice, and therefore perfect and smooth; *for perfect charity casteth out fear, because fear is not in charity [n: (I Joan. IV)]*. For this perfection is the end of the commandment, that is, the consummation, as the prophet says, the word [1229B] consummated which the Lord hath made upon the earth [n: (Isa. X; Rom. IX)].

But how *never wanting cups?* the apostles were said to be full of new wine when they burned with the Holy Spirit, that is, with the fire of divine charity: for they were drunk with that cup of the spiritual chalice of which the Psalmist says: *And my chalice which inebriateth me, how goodly is it! [n: (Psal. XXII.)]* They then who are filled with divine charity, how are they not ever moist and warm with that unfailing cup of the Holy Spirit? On the other hand, that land is said to be parched with thirst which is not sprinkled with the infusion of divine charity: whence the Lord cries to those parched with this thirst, saying: *He that thirsteth, let him come to me and drink, and out of his belly shall flow living waters [n: (Joan. VII)]*. Therefore *this turned bowl never wanteth a cup,* because perfect [1229C] charity never lacks the heavenly dew, and the desire of that highest good. But because there are in the Church prelates and subjects, strong and weak, prudent and simple, after Christ has praised his Church in the virtues of the prelates, he adds also a praiseworthy testimony from the obedience and discipline of the subjects, *thy belly,* saying, *is like a heap of wheat.* For because the belly is a frail and soft part of the body, *by the belly* is designated the frailty of the subjects, who cannot yet attain the perfection of the masters; but because they too belong to the Church, and are of the lot of the elect, whom that highest Householder calls *his wheat,* and commands to be gathered into his barn, it is not unfittingly compared to *a heap of wheat.* Now a heap is broadened below, [1229D] sharpened above, and the whole pile is a gathering of grains as it were embracing and bearing one another.

*233* What then is expressed by *the heap of wheat,* but the multitude of the faithful obeying their prelates, who, although through the infirmity of the flesh and the cares of worldly things they are somewhat pressed down about the broad and spacious way, yet through obedience and humility, and brotherly charity also, are raised up by faith and hope to the things above? for embracing and helping one another, and bearing with the infirmities of manners and of bodies, they fulfil that saying of the Apostle: *Bear ye one another's burdens, and so you shall fulfil the law of Christ [n: (Gal. VI)]*.

[1230A]But this is said to be a *heap of wheat set about with lilies,* because through the odour of right faith and the confession of sins it advances much toward the whiteness of eternal life; for in lilies there is both odour and whiteness and medicine: the odour is in faith and working, the medicine in the confession of sins, the whiteness in the recompense of eternal life.

VERSE 3.-- *Thy two breasts are like two young roes that are twins of the she-goat.*

By the two breasts may not absurdly be understood the two precepts of charity, through which the soul of each faithful man is nourished by the tenderness of inward milk and is brought to the strength of spiritual exercise, so that it can say with the Apostle: *I can do all things in him who strengtheneth me [n: (Philipp. IV)]*; for *love is strong as death [n: (Cant. VIII)]*; and no tribulation separates the saints from charity [1230B] [n: (Rom. VIII)]. For through this they go *from virtue to virtue [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*: whence also rightly the precepts of charity themselves are compared *to two twin young roes of the she-goat,* which by nature ever seeks the heights.

But why *twins?* surely on account of the concord and unity of that twofold charity, namely of God and of the neighbour, because they are so conceived and grow together in the mind of the saints that neither can be prior to the other, but when one loves God, one loves the neighbour also, and when one loves the neighbour, one loves God. For each is also a proof of the other, because, as John says: *He that saith he loveth God and loveth not his brother is a liar [n: (I Joan IV)]*: and therefore these two precepts of charity are as it were two twins of the she-goat, because they are both carried on in one place and time, [1230C] and ever advance to higher things by the daily increase of the virtues.

Or, the *two breasts* are the two Testaments by which holy Church is daily nourished to works of piety, which also are *as two young roes that are twins of the she-goat,* because they are so one, the signifying and the signified, that although they were not of one time in their showing forth, yet they are of one time in their signification, and so great is the majesty of these Testaments that to those disputing of them an ever greater and loftier subtlety presents itself.




## *229* CHAPTER VII. (cont.)

*[1230D] 234* VERSE 4.-- *Thy neck is as a tower of ivory: thy eyes like the fishpools in Hesebon, which are in the gate of the daughter of the multitude. Thy nose is as the tower of Libanus, which looketh toward Damascus.*

Because the voice goes out through the pipe of the neck, and food is ministered to the belly, rightly are the holy preachers designated by the *neck,* who through the office of preaching minister the victuals of doctrine to their fellow servants. But it is to be considered why it is called a *tower,* and why *of ivory. A tower,* first on this account, because the preaching of the saints is lofty on account of the sublimity of its sense; next because from the lofty contemplation of the mind, as from a certain tower, the coming of the enemy is foreseen, so that their snares may be guarded against, and this contemplation of the mind is taken from the teaching of the holy preachers: *of ivory* on account of the whiteness of the wisdom shining within; but the chaste utterance of the eloquence sounding without, as is sung in the Psalm, that *the words [1231A] of the Lord are chaste and the commandment of the Lord is lightsome, enlightening the eyes [n: (Psal. XVIII)]*.

The *neck* therefore of holy Church, or of each holy soul, is *as a tower of ivory,* because its preaching is lofty, and firm, and beautiful, and while it raises the mind to God, it defends it from the snares of enemies, and suffers it to be corrupted by no stain of heretical depravity; on the other hand the Synagogue is called through the prophet *a cloudy tower [n: (Mich. IV)]*, because the carnal knowledge of the Jews is dark and involved, which is indeed a *tower,* on account of the containing of the sublime sacraments which are hidden in it, but *cloudy* on account of the carnal understanding of the letter alone. *Thy eyes like the fishpools in Hesebon, which are in the gate of the daughter of the multitude.*

The *eyes* of holy Church, or rather of each faithful [1231B] soul, may not unfittingly be understood as two considerations in the mind, as it were in the head, directed to right and to left, namely to the smallness and misery of the temporal life, and to the eternity and felicity of the perpetual and unchangeable life.

Which are rightly called *as fishpools in Hesebon,* which is interpreted *the girdle of mourning,* because assuredly from mourning and sorrow for sins, or from desire of the eternal goods, fountains of tears flow forth like fishpools through compunction. These fishpools indeed flow forth from a twofold compunction, which springs from the consideration of these two eyes, because while the mind weighs the punishments to come, and remembers its own evils, it is pricked to penance, as it were watered from one of the two fishpools.

[1231C]But these *fishpools* are *in Hesebon,* because by this twofold compunction it is girded on the one side with the mourning of penance, on the other with that of love, so that it both may not slip back to its former vices, from the remembrance of the evils to come, and may bind itself strongly to the *235* pursuits of the virtues from desire of the goods to come, fulfilling the Lord's precept: *Let your loins be girt [n: (Luc. XII)]*.

*The fishpools* moreover are said to be *in the gate of the daughter of the multitude.* But what is this gate? surely the going out of this life and the entering in of the life to come; whence it is said in the Psalm of the blessed man: *He shall not be confounded when he shall speak to his enemies in the gate [n: (Psal. CXXVI)]*: for there shall be no confusion to the holy man in the going out of this life, [1231D] when the adverse party would withstand and harm him, because in his life he served him in whom the prince of this world found nothing of his own.

But who is the daughter of the multitude, whose that gate is said to be, but the holy soul of each faithful man, the daughter namely of that multitude of which *there was one heart and one soul [n: (Act. IV)]*? Which then indeed on earth was the rude and primitive Church, but it prefigured the unity of that which now in heaven, true and perfect concord, reigns with Christ through ineffable and unchangeable charity.

The daughter of this multitude surely has those fishpools of twofold compunction in her gate, because through penance for past evils, and desire of the goods to come, going out of this [1232A] life confidently and happily she enters the kingdoms of heaven. Beautifully therefore the *eyes* of the Church or of the holy soul are said to be as *fishpools in Hesebon which are in the gate of the daughter of the multitude,* because while from the remembrance of sins, or from love of eternal life, they are moist with the fountain of tears through twofold compunction, they open to themselves the entrance of the heavenly kingdom in the going out of life.

Otherwise the *eyes* of the Church may not unfittingly be called its overseers and teachers, to whom also this likeness can be fitted, that as the *fishpools in Hesebon* gird and fortify the city, so these gird and fortify the Church with the *girdle of mourning,* which Hesebon is interpreted, while by their teaching and preaching, as by a certain saving fountain, they call forth to compunction, to confession, to satisfaction, [1232B] saying that word of the Gospel: *Do penance, for the kingdom of heaven will draw near [n: (Matth. III)]*. For penance can rightly be called the girdle of mourning, because the life of the penitent ought so to be girded and fortified on every side that he both mourns what he has committed, and does not further commit what is to be mourned. For that is true penance by which past sins are so looked upon that future ones are feared, and it has mourning from the past, fear from the future. But because this fear hath punishment, while from the remembrance of the past and fear of the future it draws on mourning of mind, rightly does the penitent bind his life with the girdle of mourning.

But these *fishpools* *in Hesebon* are *in* the *236* *gate of the daughter of the multitude:* but who is the daughter of the multitude, or what is this multitude? Did not the apostles, and their successors, apostolic men, [1232C] beget holy Church? Beholding whose dignity the Psalmist says: *Exceedingly,* he says, *are thy friends honoured, O God, exceedingly is their principality strengthened [n: (Psal. CXXXVIII)]*; considering that they were not few but many, *I will number,* he says, *them, and they shall be multiplied above the sand [n: (ibid.)]* The *daughter of this multitude* therefore is holy Church, whom, as we have said, they begot by their teaching, who has a *gate,* namely the door of discretion, through which she discerns whom or what sort she should repel from her body. For since tares are always mixed with the wheat, the good indeed she incorporates into herself as sweet foods, the bad she tolerates in her body like bad humours, but heretics she utterly estranges from herself as deadly [1232D] poison.

In this gate of discretion, therefore, the overseers or teachers of holy Church stand daily; as certain eyes in the head they know how to discern through the spirit of wisdom who belong, or do not belong, to the body of the Church, which fishes are to be chosen or which rejected on the shore of the last station: in fine, to the faithful they show the doors of life open, for sinners who do penance they open them when closed, but for the impious and those who do not do penance they close them eternally.

Or, *the gate of the daughter of the multitude* is called faith, which is the beginning and as it were a certain entrance to the way of salvation, without which it is impossible to please God [n: (Hebr. XI)], which the holy teachers set forth in the forefront to those seeking the way of life, and desiring to come [1233A] to the heavenly fatherland; because each ought first to believe rightly, and then, believing rightly, to work well.

But because we have said that this *gate* *of the daughter of the multitude* is the grace of discretion, by which the holy overseers and teachers of the Church know how either to admit the penitent or to exclude those who do not do penance, what sort that discretion is or ought to be is added, when it is said: *Thy nose is as the tower of Libanus,* etc.

For by the *nose,* by which we discern odours and stenches, is designated the discretion of the saints, through which they know how to discern the odours of the virtues and the stenches of the vices, that they may understand what they ought to choose and what to reject. Which surely is said to be a *nose as the tower of Libanus,* [1233B] because, namely, the discretion of those set over others ought both always to be fortified by circumspection, and always to stand in the height of life, that is, not to lie in the valley of infirm working. For as a tower is set on a mountain for a watch-post, that enemies who come may be seen from farther off, so the life of the preacher ought always to remain fixed on high, that after the manner of the nostrils it may discern the stenches *237* of the vices, and the odours of the virtues, may foresee from afar the onsets of the malignant spirits, and make the souls committed to it wary through its own prudence. And because discretion itself is said to be the mother of the virtues, it is rightly compared to the tower of Libanus, which sounds *whitening,* inasmuch as through it the beauty of all the virtues is understood to be guarded.

But this tower is said to look *toward Damascus,* [1233C] because the life of the saints is fortified with a strong guard against the temptations of the vices through the lofty consideration of discretion. For Damascus is interpreted *drinking blood,* by which this world is rightly designated, which is daily made drunk with vices and sins; for the prophet shows that sins are designated by blood, saying: *And blood hath touched blood [n: (Ose. IV)]*, that is, sin has come to sin; and the Psalmist: *Deliver me,* he says, *from bloods, O God, God of my salvation [n: (Psal. L)]*.

This tower therefore is directed *toward Damascus,* because the life of the saints, lofty and fortified with virtues, is fortified beforehand against the pleasures of this world by strong and discreet consideration.

VERSE 5.-- *Thy head is like Carmelus: and the locks of thy head as the purple of the king joined to the channels.* [var: Vulg. *vincta canalibus*, "bound in the channels"]

[1233D]By the *head* the principal part of the mind is designated. But Carmelus is interpreted *knowing circumcision* or *soft river.*

Rightly therefore is the holy mind compared to Carmelus, because it is both lofty in divine contemplation, and knows how to cut off from itself the carnality of manners or of thoughts by spiritual circumcision, and, the grace of the divine Spirit pouring it in, is zealous to water the hearts of the hearers with sweet teaching as with a soft river. *And the locks of thy head as the purple of the king, joined to the channels.*

*The locks of the head* may be understood as the thoughts of the mind, which are said to be *as the purple of the king joined to the channels,* because the more they are joined to and intent upon the Scriptures as upon certain channels, the more they are kindled to the imitation of the passion of Christ by mortifying [1234A] their flesh with its vices and concupiscences, or by offering the flesh itself, if need be, to persecutions for Christ, sacrificing to God the precious purple of heart and body in martyrdom.

We can also in another way fit this little portion of the speech to the head itself of the Church, that is, to the Lord Jesus Christ, namely so that in his own person he brings himself in speaking to that same Church, his bride: *Thy head is like Carmelus, and the locks of thy head as the purple of the king joined to the channels.* I, he says, O bride, who am called and am thy head, can be compared to Mount Carmel on account of its height and the interpretation of its name; because since I am the most general good, there is nothing that could be more excellent than my nature according to the divinity, and further according to the humanity [1234B] *238* nothing holier than my life and conversation, and in my teaching true and perfect circumcision of heart is learned, and all my preaching runs down truly as a soft river, that it may refresh the minds of the hearers by its wholesomeness, and form them to the pursuits of good work. *The locks of this thy head* are my apostles, my other saints also, who cleave to me by charity as locks to the head, to whom *purple* well agrees, because as the purple which is nearer the channels is better dyed, so their mind which is made nearer to my words and institutions or those of my prophets is the more kindled by power to the love of me, and in my tabernacle, that is, in my Church, as precious and royal *purple,* namely scarlet twice dyed [n: (Exod. XXVIII)], to shine forth with the love of God and of the neighbour it [1234C] strives.




## *229* CHAPTER VII. (cont.)

Therefore, because *the head of man is God [n: (I Cor. II)]* [var: Vulg. *omnis viri caput Christus est*, "the head of every man is Christ"], if any faithful man desires by virtue of mind to be a *lock* of this his head, it is needful that as a searcher he should cleave more diligently to the sacred *channels* of the Scriptures, because there he finds wherewith he may be dyed, that he may be made the *purple* of his heavenly King through inward love by the mortification of the carnal life. But it is to be noted that the beloved beautifully answers his beloved with a proclamation of praise of this kind in ten parts as it were on ten strings, sounding spiritually on one ten-stringed instrument, which is so fittingly set over against the ten-stringed instrument above, which the bride had sung in praise of the bridegroom, that the one from the higher parts, the other from the lower, impose the measure of a most pleasing melody. For because the bride ought always to ascend to the bridegroom with right intention and perfect [1234D] love, it is just that she should rise from lesser things to greater, and therefore the proclamation of her advancing and as it were growing toward the virtues of the mind is sung by the bridegroom condescendingly; not that this advance, virtue and power belongs to the one receiving, but rather because it is the praise of the bridegroom who gives and bestows on her this virtue and power by his gift.

But the bride, reverently attending to the immense kindness of the bridegroom descending from heaven, and humbling himself to our infirmity for our sake, begins the proclamations of his praise from the highest proclamation of the body, that is, from the head, as it were from the higher string, that she may show that she, set lower, always ought to look back toward the life and teaching of her bridegroom set over against her above, and to ascend [1235A] by imitation to him whom she knows to have descended to her through the tenderness of compassion.

* 239* VERSE 6.-- *How beautiful art thou, and how comely, dearest, in delights!*

After the bridegroom has proclaimed in the bride, part by part, the variety of the members composed from the habit of a mind well [sic: *bone*] and honourably ordered, showing that one certain whole had grown up thence, he calls the whole of the virtues by the name of stature, but declares the abundance of doctrine by the signification of breasts. For what beauty of manners or of utterances is lacking in the whole body of holy Church? for since this one shines with this virtue, that one with that, another with another, some possess more kinds of virtues, some fewer, what of form, of beauty and of comeliness can there be which [1235B] holy Church does not have in her body? And since diverse men are renowned for diverse learning in holy doctrines, what knowledge of truth or of holiness can there be in which holy Church does not abound? For since according to the Apostle *to one may be given the word of wisdom, to another the word of knowledge, to another faith, to another the grace of healings, to another the working of miracles, to another prophecy, to another the discerning of spirits; to another kinds of tongues, to another interpretation of speeches [n: (I Cor. XII)]*, assuredly all the beauty and adornment of works or of speeches shines forth spiritually in the inner stature of holy Church.

Whence she is deservedly pronounced *beautiful and comely,* and *dearest in delights;* because assuredly the abundance of the virtues and the saving teaching of wisdom are great [1235C] delights of the soul; but the more the soul is delighted by these, the more fully is it filled with the sweetness of divine love.

VERSE 7.-- *Thy stature is likened to a palm tree, and thy breasts to clusters of grapes.*

But why is a stature of this kind likened to a palm tree? For the palm is a sign of victory; and what victory is greater and more glorious than to triumph over vices and sins? And whence has a stature of such a kind, wonderful and glorious, grown up, but from the victory of the virtues and the destruction of the vices? Not unfittingly therefore the *stature* of the Church *is likened to a palm,* because all the honourableness and probity of her manners tells of notable victories.

[1235D]Further, her breasts are likened to clusters of grapes, because her doctrine and preaching abounds in the sweet and pleasant savour of wisdom and of spiritual understanding, and so inebriates her hearers that it makes them as it were insensible to the pleasures of this world. And that the bridegroom himself may show that the beauty of this stature advances and grows chiefly and principally from the mortification of the carnal life, he holds out to her the example of his passion and the gain of that same passion: *I said,* saying, *I will go up into the palm tree.*

* 240* VERSE 8.-- *I said: I will go up into the palm tree and take hold of its fruit: and thy breasts shall be as the clusters of the vine, and the odour of thy mouth like the odour of apples.*

As if he should say openly to that same bride of his: Whence [1236A] hast thou to conquer, whence hast thou to advance? Is it of thy own strength and industry that thou canst procure for thyself the glory of so great beauty? If thou considerest rightly, thou wilt by no means ascribe the merit of any good to thy fortitude or prudence. For I *said,* that is, before the times of the world I decreed and determinately disposed, since through the transgression of the sign thou didst fall and didst begin to be powerless, and by sinning didst find an enemy to thyself, whom afterwards thou couldst not prevail against, but as a captive didst lie subject to his dominion, that I would again through the opposing of the tree recover for thee the good that was lost, and snatch thee from the power of the enemy, and from the captivity in which thou wast held, and, liberty being restored, would lay the enemy beneath thee, and would give thee foresight against his snares by guarding thee beforehand, and the strength [1236B] of resisting: therefore *I said: I will go up into the palm tree,* that is, into the cross, upon which, after I have triumphed over the enemy, and have taken hold of the fruit of this my passion, and in sign of victory by rising again have brought it forth, I will bring forth to all who obey me and who have hope in my strength this oracle of consolation: *Have confidence, I have overcome the world [n: (Joan. XVI)]*. Thou therefore oughtest to fight, and because thou art secure of the victory, I having already obtained the victory, ascend the cross with me by mortifying the flesh, that the whole *stature* of thy soul may be likened *to the palm,* and by the title of perseverance may be made over to eternal victory.

But to be venerated and adored by every soul is the ascent of this palm. Why? because great assuredly and [1236C] incomparable is its fruit: for what? has not the whole human race been refreshed and repaired through this fruit? And who or what sort was and is this fruit? Surely the redemption of the human race, the reconciliation of enmity toward God, the daily remission of sins, a firm defence against the snares of the enemy, the going out of worldly misery, and the entering in of the heavenly kingdom. For all the advance of the Church began from the time of the passion and resurrection of Christ, and from then the proclamations of her holiness and the good things of the Holy Spirit flowed forth most abundantly. Whence also it follows: *And thy breasts shall be as the clusters of the vine, and the odour of thy mouth like the odour of apples.* For her preaching and doctrine, after the removal of the shadow through the showing forth of the truth, flowed out sweeter and richer, and [1236D] after the passion and resurrection of Christ it made many so *241* drunk with divine charity that it boiled out of them all the moisture of worldly pleasure by the heat of the Holy Spirit, and kindled them to the love of the heavenly life.

But from the throat the voice goes out, and therefore by the throat the memory of the divine precepts, by the voice the preaching of those same precepts, is not unfittingly understood; for unless they are first held in the mind through memory, they will by no means be brought forth abroad through speech; but the voice of speech of this kind is compared *to the odour of apples,* because as through odour a thing not only present but also absent is perceived, so from the time of the passion and resurrection of Christ, through the preaching of the Church, not only the present [1237A] deeds and sayings of the Lord himself or of his saints, but also the past, have become known and are daily known by reading and hearing. The good or evil things to come also are gathered from these, when both the rewards of the just and the punishments of sinners are weighed from afar as it were from the odour of a certain inward touch. Now this voice is referred not to the odour of flowers but of *apples,* because the preaching of the Church, which now sounds through the whole world, not rude or tender, but ripe and perfect, shows one faith to believers.

VERSE 9.-- *Thy throat like the best wine, worthy for my beloved to drink, and for his lips and his teeth to ruminate.*

The *throat,* moreover, is said to be *like the best wine,* because the memory of the divine precepts inebriates the mind with its sweetness, [1237B] and what it drinks sweetly by meditating within, it pours out profitably by speaking without. *Worthy for my beloved to drink.* Christ in his body, that is, in the Church, which is made one with him through the Spirit, just as he is said to be refreshed with justice, faith and holiness, so also to be given drink; as on the contrary in the unbelieving he is proclaimed to hunger and thirst for justice, faith and holiness. For hanging on the cross when he said: *I thirst [n: (Joan. XIX)]*, he thirsted for the faith and holiness of the unbelieving people, because for its sake he had been wearied as it were by a certain heat of labour, which he had led out of Egypt with many signs and portents, before whose face he had crushed the nations, to whom he had confirmed the covenant of his testament and law, to whom he had assigned the land of promise, the city and the temple, [1237C] for whose salvation at the last he had not disdained to undergo death itself.

When therefore holy Church, or any holy soul whatever, meditates on the law of the Lord day and night, Christ is given drink in its mind with a certain sweet wine of meditation, and its holy meditation is as it were a sweet drinking and worthy of God. Whence when the bridegroom was proclaiming *the throat of the beloved,* that is, the meditation of the commandments *242* of God, as *the best wine,* she, interrupting the voice of him who praised her, and rejoicing at co-operating grace, *worthy,* she says, *for my beloved to drink,* that is, worthy that he himself should further it by his grace, and bring it to effect.

How? *By ruminating with lips and teeth:* for [1237D] what one understands by often meditating with oneself, he ought to spend on others by teaching, to handle by expounding, and to handle again by discussing, which is as it were the property of a clean animal, to ruminate. For in the one body of the Church, by which the whole Christ is understood, the *throat* is the meditation of Holy Scripture, the *lips* the pronouncing of that same Scripture, the *teeth* its explanation, as it were the breaking up of the coarse letter, and its grinding down to the subtlety of spiritual understanding. Wherefore it is said to be *worthy* for the bridegroom himself *to drink* or *to ruminate,* because all the advance of the Church or of any holy soul whatever is of his gift and grace.

VERSE 10.-- *I to my beloved, and his turning is toward me.*

Since, she says, the grace of my beloved [1238A] himself going before, I please him by meditation of the holy Scriptures, therefore I will, and by right I ought, to draw nearer to him by obeying, by applying myself to good acts, and by announcing his precepts to others, so that that may come to pass which is written: *Draw nigh to God and he will draw nigh to you [n: (Jac. IV)]*. For if I shall turn myself to him, then *his turning is toward me,* as he himself said: *Turn ye to me, and I will turn to you [n: (Zach. I)]*: but there is no changeableness in God, so that he may be said to be turned away or turned toward after the manner of men, but the motion of grace coming or withdrawing in man is called a certain turning or turning away of God. *His turning* to us is the mercy of grace calling, justifying and glorifying, through which free will is helped, and the intention of good will is brought [1238B] to the effect of a good purpose. Whence well he says first: *I to my beloved,* and then, *and his turning is toward me,* because it is ours first to take up the intention of good will toward the pursuit of good work, his to bring the work conceived by good intention through co-operating grace even to a good end. Wherefore, because, feeling a ready will in herself, she takes up confidence concerning the help co-operating with her, she forthwith girds herself for the pursuit of working, saying: *Come, my beloved.*

* 243* VERSE 11.-- *Come, my beloved, let us go forth into the field, let us abide in the villages.*




## *229* CHAPTER VII. (cont.)

*Let us go forth,* she says, because after thy turning to me shall have been made through co-operating grace, I can freely and securely go out, both to the [1238C] office of preaching and to the pursuit of good action, so that all my working may both always begin from thee and, begun, be finished through thee: for without thee we can do nothing of good.

But whither shall we go forth first? *into the field,* namely, that is, the nation of the Jews, which is now fit to receive the seed of the word of God, because it has its hearts already ploughed through the written and also the natural law, and therefore can the better understand from the law and the prophets the purpose of the divine goodness by which he decreed to restore the human race. For to them first it behoves *to speak the word of God [n: (Act. XIII)]*, *whose adoption of sons is glory, and the testament and the giving of the law, and the service, and the promises; whose are the fathers, from whom [1238D] Christ according to the flesh, who is God blessed for ever [n: (Rom. IX)]*. *Into* this *field* therefore, to be sprinkled with the seed of the divine word, *let us go forth* through the office of preaching, and *because* first *the words of God were committed to them [n: (Rom. III)]*, let us cast among them also the first seedlings of faith and of the divine sacraments.

Then, going further, *let us abide in the villages,* that is, among the gentiles, who are far removed from the city of God, that is, the Church, among whom, because they are slower to believe on account of the unaccustomed and unheard-of preaching of the faith, it will be necessary to make a stay, and by announcing the word of life more frequently, to bring them back to the elegance of city custom, that is, to the observance of the ecclesiastical law. *Let us abide* therefore among them, thou, O beloved, inspiring through grace, I by preaching [1239A] helping, that, wearing away by frequent instruction their inborn ignorance and the simplicity of a rude mind, we may pour in our civil honourableness and true wisdom, through which they may merit to be enrolled as happy dwellers of that city above.

But when we shall have joined these two walls coming from different sides, namely the Jews and the gentiles, in one faith, there will be not two, but one people, one vineyard of the Lord of Sabaoth, which, cultivated by the work of the Holy Spirit, will bring forth no longer wild grapes, but sweet grapes.

VERSE 12.-- *Let us rise early to the vineyards, let us see if the vineyard hath flourished, if the flowers be bringing forth fruits, if the pomegranates have flourished: there will I give thee my breasts.*

*244* *To* these *vineyards,* moreover, planted from the circumcision and [1239B] the uncircumcision, *let us rise early,* namely in the rising of faith, when the true sun shall have begun to rise in the hearts of believers and to irradiate them with the charity of his wisdom and to kindle them with the heat of his power: *let us see if the vineyard hath flourished,* that is, whether one Christian people, now made, has brought forth the beginnings of good works; *if the flowers be bringing forth fruits,* that is, whether those beginnings are striving to rise to some integrity and perfection; *if the pomegranates have flourished,* that is, whether they burn with so great a love of God that they desire to suffer for him not only in mind but also in body.

*There will I give thee my breasts:* for after we shall have made these two peoples one through the unity of faith, and shall have moved them forward to the pursuit of good work, then to the praise of thy name, O beloved, the sacraments which [1239C] by thy gift we have come to know from the subtlety of the two Testaments we will commit to them also, so that by them they may always be nourished to greater works of the virtues, and be strengthened against the temptations of the world, and be the more kindled to seek the future goods of blessedness [sic: *beatudinis*]. For I will preach confidently, and will unlock the precepts of thy precepts ( *sic* ), since I have hope of bearing fruit, and of attaining the effect of good work. Whence? because *the mandrakes have given their odour.*

VERSE 12.-- *The mandrakes have given their odour in our gates. All apples new and old, my beloved, I have kept for thee.*

That very odour brings me hope of preaching, because I believe that from that odour there consequently comes [1239D] the profit of gathering fruit. But it is to be diligently considered what this *mandrake* is, or what the *odour of this mandrake.* If thou look to the letter, the *mandrake* is an aromatic herb which, formed to the likeness of human members, lacks the head alone, whose power is that, given in drink to those whom wakefulness oppresses, it brings on sleep and rest; further, if barren women use it, it makes them conceive.

But if thou attend to the spiritual sense, the *mandrake* can, not absurdly, be understood as the conversion of the gentiles, which, so long as it was driven on in the depravity of its error, was wearied by a labour not only vain but also destructive; but after, converted, it began to know God, it learned both to be less wakeful to the cares and pleasures of worldly things, nay, more than to sleep, and to rest [1240A] from the depraved labour of this world; and she who before was barren of good work afterwards became fruitful in virtues, the prophecy being fulfilled which says: *Rejoice, thou barren, that bearest not: break forth and cry, thou that travailest not, for many are the children of the desolate, more than of her that hath a husband [n: (Isa. LIV; Gal. IV)]*.

*245* The *mandrake,* however, as we have said, though it has the likeness of a human body, lacks a head, because the gentile people, though it was by nature vigorous in reason for the discerning of good and evil, yet that very force of reason had in it been blunted in this part, by which it was not able to know its Creator, whom, because it did not have in its knowledge, it lacked as it were a head. The Jewish people also can be signified by the *mandrake,* which, while it used reason through the observance of [1240B] the law, yet did not have a head, because it refused to receive Christ; but this *mandrake* afterwards *gave its odour,* because she who before through unbelief had been barren, afterwards, believing, begot many sons to God. *The mandrakes* therefore *have given their odour in our gates,* because the Jewish and the gentile people, thou, O beloved, ascending into heaven, received the sacraments of the faith through thy grace and my preaching. But which are these gates, in which the beloved says that the believing people gave its odour? Surely if thou consider diligently, thou wilt be able to recognize through what gates the soul desiring to find God advances little by little even to the very secret places of the bedchamber: for first there is in man the conceiving of the mind, by which it conceives that whose sum [1240C] it desires to attain, which being conceived, it investigates what or of what sort it is, until it has knowledge of it. And when it is known it embraces it, and so uses it to its own profit that it strives at some time to turn all its wholeness to itself. There is therefore in each one who is to believe a certain gate and as it were a first entrance to God, the first conceiving of faith in the mind, by which he receives the will to believe, then the investigating of that faith, that he may know what or how he ought to believe; after which second gate of investigation there is opened to him a third gate, namely the knowledge of faith; after knowledge follows working, without which faith is judged to be idle; and the contemplation of a clean heart joins to God the one who works well.

[1240D]Those therefore who strive to come to God through these *gates,* at the very entrance of the gates *scatter the odour* of good will; but this odour, passing through the second gate of faith investigated, reaching also the third gate of faith now known, and then running through the fourth gate of working, through the fifth gate at last, of contemplative sweetness, glides in to the very inner secrets of the heavenly bridegroom. When therefore *the mandrakes give their odour in* these *gates,* it is evident that the souls seeking God are now ripening to the fruit of good work. With worthy rejoicing therefore the bride *246* plays with the beloved, saying: *The mandrakes have given their odour in our gates,* because while the Church attends to the advance of her sons, she pays due thanksgivings to God. Whence she girds herself the more for [1241A] preaching, because she hopes that she is making progress in the hearts of those whom she considers to have been turned to God with the whole heart. But this her advance she imputes not to her own labour and industry, but to divine grace, saying: *All apples new and old,* etc., *my beloved, I have kept for thee.* For thy honour, she says, I have kept, O beloved, all the fruit of holiness which I have come to know from the institution of the Old or of the New Testament, or which I have learned from the instruction of the precepts, or which I have gathered from the life and flowers of the Fathers by informing myself. For this is of thy gift, not of my labour, whether that I myself have advanced by living well and teaching, or that others have been able to advance by my example or teaching. Or, to say it in another way, *All apples new and old I have kept for thee,* because all things which in [1241B] the Old Testament are read as done and said mystically, but in the New as shown forth in reality, concerning thy nativity, passion, resurrection, and ascension, I have taught and expounded as written, and to all who read and hear I have shown in them the fruit of faith and of good working.



## *247* CHAPTER VIII.

VERSE 1.-- *Who shall give thee to me for my brother, sucking the breasts of my mother, that I may find thee without, and kiss, and now no one may despise me.*

If we look into the allegorical sense, this voice can be referred to those saints who before the coming of the Lord burned with great desire to see what they knew had long been foretold by the patriarchs and prophets, and what they themselves foresaw in the spirit as to come, namely [1241C] the incarnation of Christ, through which they did not doubt that the universal salvation of the world would come about. Let that ancient people of the saints, then, say in the desiderative mode of speech: *Who shall give thee to me for my brother sucking the breasts of my mother: my brother,* I say, *my* on account of the taking up of human flesh, or the kinship of race, *sucking the breasts of my mother,* that is, born and nourished in the synagogue of the Jews. And why with this desire? *that I may find thee,* she says, *without and kiss,* that is, that I may see thee openly in the flesh and embrace thee, whom in the secret of the Godhead I cannot see: *and now no one may despise me,* nay rather may venerate and love me, on account of the special nobility of so noble an offspring born of my flesh; *I will take hold* moreover [1241D] *and bring into the house of my mother,* because this pleased thy condescension, that in the nation of the Jews alone thou shouldst show the presence of thy flesh and demonstrate the power of thy miracles. *There* then *thou shalt teach me* both by words and by deeds what I ought to understand in figure, what in truth, and that thou thyself art *the end of the precept unto justice to every one that believeth [n: (Rom. X)]* [var: Vulg. Rom 10:4 *Finis enim legis, Christus*, "For the end of the law is Christ"; *finis praecepti* is 1 Tim 1:5].
