But how much shall I profit by the sweetness of thy teaching! *I will give thee a cup of spiced wine,* because thou shalt, as it were, be given to drink with the delight of my faith and working, which I will offer thee in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, and in the virtue of faith, hope and charity. For as spiced wine consists chiefly of three things, that is, of wine, honey and spices, so my mind is seasoned from the teaching of thy words with the faith of the Holy Trinity, or, as [1242A] has been said, with the virtue of faith, hope *248* and charity. *I will give* also *new wine of my pomegranates,* because thy preaching or working will bring forth in me the newness and sweetness of a soul purged from vices, from the fervour of charity and the zeal of manifold virtues.

Further, if we consider the moral sense, we shall wish this voice for every faithful soul which, intent on sound Scripture, builds up itself with knowledge and others with the word of exhortation: for the soul that desires to have the knowledge of Holy Scripture cries to God with all the eagerness of the mind in these words: *Who shall give thee to me for my brother sucking the breasts of my mother?* Why I call thee *brother*, assuredly both on account of the sharing of humanity and on account of the grace of adoption, by which thou namest thyself *brother*, saying: *Go, tell my brethren [1242B] [n: (Matth. XXVIII)]*, and: *Whosoever shall do the will of my Father, he is my brother, sister and mother [n: (Matth. XII)]*. *Who* then *shall give thee to me for my brother,* that thou mayest be with me, and labour with me through co-operating grace, *thee* I say *sucking,* that is, making to suck, *the breasts of my mother.* For this is said in that mode of speech which Scripture also uses in another place: *For he himself asketh for us with unspeakable groanings [n: (Rom. VIII)]*: saying *asketh* for what is making to ask.

But what are *the breasts of my mother,* and of what mother of mine? Assuredly *the breasts of my mother* are the doctrines of holy Church, which from the pages of both Testaments nourish the infancy of my mind and bring it forth into the strength of manly understanding. But why do I so greatly [1242C] desire thee to be with me? *that I may find thee without and kiss:* what is this? For where dost thou lie hidden when thou art not without? Assuredly in the cloud of the day, that is, in the obscurity and subtlety of spiritual understanding; and when I have received it by the illumination of thy fire, that is, of the Holy Spirit, assuredly I find without thee who didst lie hidden within, and when I have found thee, *I kiss,* that is, I embrace thee with all the arms of my mind, because I so delight in the gift of understanding received that it pleases me to say with the Prophet: *How sweet are thy words to my palate, more than honey and the honeycomb to my mouth! [n: (Psal. CXVIII.)]* [var: Vulg. Ps 118:103 *super mel ori meo*, "more than honey to my mouth", without *et favum*]

*Now* indeed, as I thus cleave to thy embraces and wisely speak out the mysteries of Holy Scripture, [1242D] *no one shall despise me,* because, as it is written: *The wise man shall be filled with blessings, and the living shall praise him [n: (Eccli. XXXVII)]* [var: Vulg. Sir 37:27 *videntes*, "they that see him"], and: *The wise man shall inherit honour among the people, and his name shall be living for ever [n: (Ibid.)]*; and again: *The mouth of the prudent is sought in the Church, and they shall think upon his words in their hearts [n: (Eccli. XXI)]*. *No one* therefore *shall despise me,* because they hear that the anointing of thy Spirit teaches me concerning all things [n: (I Joan. II)], nay, falling upon the earth they have adored the Lord, declaring that God is truly in us [n: (I Cor. XIV)].

VERSE 2.-- *I will take hold of thee and bring into the house of my mother. There thou shalt teach me, and I will give thee a cup of spiced wine, and new wine of my pomegranates.*

[1243A] *249* So much, moreover, does this sweetness please the palate of my heart that, having found thee in this way, I would not hold thee alone for my own profit, but rather, *taking hold, I will bring thee into the house of my mother,* namely the household of holy Church, so that what I understand from thy grace I may share with goodwill with my brethren who desire to hear and to learn.

*There* then *thou shalt teach me,* because the more I teach others, the more I learn with them: for conference brings more than reading. *And I will give thee a cup of spiced wine,* because by thus teaching, or conferring, I shall set forth the figure of the law with the truth and grace of the Gospel, or the historical sense seasoned with allegory and morality, and this *cup* I will give thee, because I shall bring all this knowledge to thy honour and grace.

[1243B] *I will give thee* also *new wine of my pomegranates,* because while I preach the patience of the saints and the manifold labour of the virtues, I myself also receive the example of their labour and patience; all of which, however, I ascribe neither to their power nor to mine, but to thy loving-kindness and grace.

Therefore, by so great a presence of my beloved and my brother, and by the sweetness and grace of him abiding with me, snatched away as it were from all the pleasures and cares of this world, I turn to you, O daughters of Jerusalem, and I declare what I hold in me as my purpose, and show with how great charity I cleave to him. What, they say, is this? *His left hand,* she says, *under my head.*

[1243C]VERSE 3.-- *His left hand under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me.*

For all the temporal goods granted me by him I put after, for love of the eternal goods, but the eternal goods themselves I desire with my whole mind, and I purpose to attain to them with all the strength of my soul. For although *in his left hand* are *riches and glory [n: (Prov. III)]*, as regards the usefulness and beauty of the creature by which man is led to the knowledge of his Creator, so that he may not be inexcusable [sic: *inexusabilis*], yet *length* of *days is in his right hand [n: (ibid.)]*, not such as is ended, but such as abides unchangeably in its own blessedness.

*His left hand* then *under my head* will be, but his *right hand* *shall embrace me,* because the memory of temporal things will hold me, yet not for delight in them and [1244A] one perpetual, but the desire of eternal things will surround me wholly, so much that it turns all my senses to the pursuit of them, and compels me to forget the things that are behind, and to stretch my whole self forth into those that are before. [n: (Phil. III)].

*250* But because the bridegroom does these things, who rejoices in the pious pursuits and progress of the soul, and is delighted by the sweetness of her devotion and pure intention, *I adjure you,* he says, *daughters of Jerusalem,* etc.

VERSE 4.-- *I adjure you, daughters of Jerusalem, that you stir not up nor make the beloved to awake, till she herself please.*

Do not, he says, daughters of Jerusalem, lead the beloved away from so profitable and honourable a purpose of hers by any care of passing things, importunately suggesting to her [1244B] your necessity or her own concerning outward things, which do not allow the mind to be free to seek and perfectly to keep the leisure of holy contemplation; but rather leave this to her judgment, who is taught by the anointing of my Spirit [n: (I Joan. II)]; hold this discretion, that she may know when it is profitable now to apply herself to divine things, now to condescend to your infirmity. Or else, the *beloved* that is to be, in no way *stir up, nor make to awake,* by compelling her with threats or promises, because God does not approve forced services; rather leave her to her own will, because it is written: *Voluntarily will I sacrifice to thee [n: (Psal. LIII)]*, and, *They that offer themselves of their own accord to the Lord, are blessed by the Lord [n: (Lev. XXII)]*: *But no one cometh [1244C] to me, unless the Father who hath sent me draw him [n: (Joan. VI)]* [var: Vulg. Jo 6:44 *nemo potest venire ad me*, "no man can come to me"]. For if he is drawn he comes, if he does not come he is not drawn: therefore do not compel her *till she herself please:* for then will the sacrifice of a good work be acceptable to God, if it is supported by the seasoning of a good will.

But you, most beloved sisters, whom this part of the Canticle has held with its melody somewhat long and at some length, attend to this voice, as it were the last, in its laying down, considering how acceptable to God and how sweet it is for the soul to rest in the purpose of holy conversation and to sleep in the divine pursuit of contemplation; and on the contrary how dangerous, and how contrary to God, it is to lead you away from this purpose on any occasion, and to twist you aside to the care of temporal things.



## BOOK FOUR.

[1243]

[1243D] *251* If the zeal of my littleness in this special kind of Canticle had so far profited with your love that, stirred up in your whole spirit, with the cares of temporal things put behind you, you could say with the Apostle: *I will pray with the spirit, I will pray also with the power; I will sing with the spirit, I will sing also with the power [n: (I Cor. XIV)]* [var: Vulg. 1Cor 14:15 *mente*, "with the understanding", both times], my heart would assuredly exult greatly in the Lord, because it would be no small rejoicing of mine to say with the Apostle: *The joy and my crown are you in the Lord [n: (Philip. V)]*. For if [1244D] after the manner of my singing you now already put forth leaves somewhat in the Lord, you rightly praise the canticle, because this assuredly is to praise the canticle: to experience the canticle and its measure. But what is the force of this canticle? According to its name, by which it is called *Canticle of Canticles,* to bear the mind more excellently and more sublimely to the love of its Maker, and perfectly to lay aside from it whatever from the things of the world can stand in the way of contemplation of this kind. If then you have [1245A] experienced it somewhat, let the concord and harmony of flesh and spirit tell you, for which not to be opposed to one another is to sing beautifully and to make melody rightly. If the handmaid serves the free woman, that is, if the flesh consents to reason, there is no better nor sweeter harmony which may please the supreme Godhead; this indeed is to sing *a new canticle* and of this kind is the *praise* that is *in the Church of the saints [n: (Psal. CXLIX)]*. For *the praise of God* is the pious life and conversation of the saints, because they have this such living of theirs from the grace and kindness of God: therefore God is to be praised, not man himself, because this is not the work of man, but the work of God who justifies man himself.

For what is *a new canticle,* but the venerable commandment of the New Testament? What is it to sing *a new canticle* [1245B] but to put off the old man and put on the new? *Put off,* says the Apostle, *the old man, and put on the new, who according to God is created in justice and holiness [n: (Ephes. IV)]*: therefore to be just and holy is assuredly to sing a new canticle, for all perfection of holiness is recognized from the instrument of the evangelical instruction. For whereas the law says: *Thou shalt not kill,* the Gospel teaches that one must not be angry without cause; and whereas the law *252* says that one must not commit adultery, the Gospel teaches that one must not sin even in concupiscence [n: (Exod. XX; Matth. V)]. Such perfection of holiness is surely and assuredly the melody of the new canticle. This new canticle the Church of the saints sings, and by frequent singing of this kind attains to the singing of [1245C] *the Canticle of Canticles.* For what indeed is it to sing the Canticle of Canticles, but to love the Lord with the whole mind, the whole soul, the whole strength? For as it is called the Canticle of Canticles because it surpasses all other canticles in its dignity, so charity can be called the virtue of virtues, because it surpasses all virtues by its excellence.

If then you, O beloved sisters, truly and sincerely love the Lord, assuredly you sing the Canticle of Canticles; and because *praise is in the Church of the saints,* in you certainly will be the praise of God, who, as we believe, are the Church of the saints. Bless therefore the Lord in his works and sing to him *in the canticles of your lips, and with harps [n: (Eccli. XXXIX)]*. For whatever marks of religion and holiness are in you, they are [1245D] assuredly his works, in which, when you bless, when you ascribe all these things not to your own industry but to his grace.

Hence you ought to sing to him *in the canticles of lips and harps,* lest it be said of you: *This people honoureth me with the lips, but their heart is far from me [n: (Isa. XXIX)]*. For what is it to sing *in the canticles of lips and harps?* Is it not when voice and life praise the Lord? for then lips and harp sound well together. Now in the harp are stretched strings made from the entrails of dead animals, which are fitted in the wood in so diverse a manner that they give a diverse sound according to the tension and the diversity of the measures. What then can be designated by the harp, but the mind of the just man? in which strings made from the entrails of dead animals are stretched, because in [1246A] it are presented the mortifications of vices, which it has learned from the sayings and writings, or the deeds, of the holy Fathers withdrawn from this life, by which it so chastises its senses inwardly and outwardly that in the ears of the Lord of Sabaoth it presses down with sweet melody the concord of flesh and Spirit. And a certain diversity of sounds is there in some manner denoted, as long as the mortification of pride is carried out in one way, that of lust in another, of wrath in another, and of avarice in another; rightly, with a certain most beautiful harmony, the mind is seen to sound forth delightfully to the Lord its God *a new canticle,* nay *the Canticle of Canticles:* you sing *in the canticles* then *of lips and harps,* when both by voice and by work you pay to the Lord your God the service of your holiness. If then at the voice of this canticle your mind has been for some time stirred up, as we believe, [1246B] to the love of the heavenly bridegroom, and if you have the ear of the heart ready for a harmony of this kind, your devotion will the more animate us to the things that remain. For no one willingly recounts to an unwilling hearer.

I shall begin, then, to touch the last part, as it were the final string of this Canticle, the Holy Spirit guiding the fingers of the mind, and in it, the Lord helping, I shall lay down the whole *253* harmony of the promised tetrachord.

VERSE 5.-- *Who is she that goeth up from the desert, flowing with delights, leaning upon her beloved? Under the apple tree I raised thee up; there thy mother was corrupted [sic: corruvta], there she that bore thee was violated.*

If I ascribe this voice to the Church wondering at the conversion [1246C] of Judaea, it will not be absurd; because it was assuredly to be wondered at that a people of hard neck and untamable heart, which after the passion of the Lord would not believe him, though many signs attested his Godhead, but rather, after many injuries inflicted upon him, fastened him to the cross, suddenly, when the apostles preached, seized the way of salvation and took up the doctrine of the new law.

And she wonders, and not without cause, because she greatly attends to this, that *she goeth up from the desert,* that *flowing with delights,* that *leaning upon the beloved* she goes up. For the Synagogue was a kind of desert, abandoned by God on account of unbelief, as he threatens concerning her through the prophet, *And there shall be left,* he says, [1246D] *the daughter of Sion as a covert in a vineyard, and as a lodge in a garden of cucumbers, as a city that is laid waste [n: (Baruch VI)]*. And the Lord in the Gospel: *There shall be left,* he says, *to you your house deserted [n: (Matth. XXIII)]*. From this *desert,* then, Judaea, when she believed, *went up* through faith, because from that low-lying and weak understanding of carnal observance she passed over to the spiritual and sublime form of the evangelical instruction. But she could not go up except *flowing with delights,* because she could not attain to that sublimity of new understanding and grace except through the affluence of the virtues and the abundance of holy charity. But she could not flow with these delights of the virtues unless she leaned *upon the beloved* of holy Church, that is, Christ, because without his co-operating grace no [1247A] proclamations of holiness and faith could come to her.

Further, in another way, if thou look to each holy soul, attend carefully to what is above, *where the beloved adjures the daughters of Jerusalem that they stir not up nor make the beloved to awake till she herself please.* For what dost thou think the beloved attained in this her sleep, that the beloved should so earnestly forbid her now to be stirred up and awakened? What, I say, so beautiful, so delightful, and so pleasing to himself did almighty God attend to in this her sleep. Assuredly, if thou considerest rightly, this sweetest beloved regards with the placable countenance of his condescension something great and honourable and worthy of admiration. For he wonders at her ascending, at the place also of the ascent, [1247B] at the bearing of the one ascending and at the support of her ascending. What is this ascent as of one sleeping and resting, but a contemplative lifting up from earthly things to heavenly, from the world to the Lord? But because solitude of mind and body helps this ascent much, for the one is helped by the other, since that of the mind is defended by the solitude of the body, and solitude of the body profits little if the mind is busied about the din *254* of the world the almighty Father with a serene gaze of loving-kindness wonders at the soul going up from the desert, saying: *Who she that goeth up from the desert? Who is she?* he says, « *who hath disposed ascents in her heart, in the vale of tears [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*, » *going up from the desert,* that is, from the solitude of a mind [1247C] despising the din of this world and sleeping in the quiet of the Spirit and choosing only the observance of my commandments? For from this it follows that she conceives such a bearing that she seems *to flow with delights,* because this is what the observance of my commandments confers, that she flows with the delights of the virtues, nor is it anything else to flow with virtues than to keep my precepts. For whatever can be or be called virtue, this is all contained in the catalogue of my precepts. But whence has she this power, namely *to go up, to flow with delights?* Is it from herself? By no means, because every soul is infirm and weak without co-operating grace. But who is this beloved? My Son, I say, of whom I said to you: *This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased (Matth. [1247D] III)*. Because then she was *leaning upon him,* because she followed his footsteps, keeping and, as far as she could, imitating what he showed and taught through the flesh by doing and speaking, therefore she was able to go up in mind, and to flow with the delights of the virtues.

It will not be absurd to understand it in another way also, namely that we take the *desert* as the present life, and these *delights* with which *the beloved flowing goes up* as Holy Scripture. For why should Holy Scripture not be called *delights,* in which so great diversities of understanding for the profit of the soul are found, like diverse dishes of food at one table: for as among diverse dishes one food is more delicate than another, so in Holy Scripture among diverse understandings one sense is more subtle than another: for there, now [1248A] the bare history feeds those who read like a coarser food, now under the texture of the letter the moral allegory refreshes them to the marrow like a more subtle food, now contemplation, lifting them up to higher things, sweetly affects the mind like a singular and choice food. For whoever *flows with delights,* is loosened in a certain relaxation of himself, and is released from the pursuit of labour as if from weariness: because assuredly when the soul has begun to abound inwardly in delights, it by no means any longer consents to brood upon earthly desires, but, taken by the love of its Maker, and free in a sweet captivity, it sighs by fainting to contemplate his beauty, and as it were by growing weary grows strong, because while it can no longer carry sordid works, it hastens through quiet to him whom it loves inwardly. *From the desert,* then, of the present life of the world *the soul flowing with [1248B] delights goes up,* because while it is fed with the mystical understandings of the Holy *255* Scriptures, it is lifted up daily to contemplate the things above.

Yet she is described as *leaning upon the beloved,* because unless she herself cleaves to Holy Scripture by studiously searching it, she by no means lifts the step of her ascent to grasp those sublime things of Spiritual understanding: for the mind intent on Holy Scripture *leans* upon her beloved, because while God is found and recognized in Scripture itself, the mind of the one recognizing and loving leans as it were upon him.

This voice, then, can be that of the Church exulting at the progress of each holy soul and saying: *Who is she that goeth up from the desert, flowing with delights, leaning upon her beloved?* As if she said in other words: [1248C] Who is she that, intent on the meditation of the Holy Scriptures, cleaves with such zeal to my beloved, the Lord Jesus Christ, that by their mystical understandings, as it were flowing with certain delights, she is able to go up through contemplation to the love of eternal life.



## BOOK FOUR. (cont.)

Wherefore he at once adds: *Under the apple tree I raised thee up: there thy mother was corrupted, there she that bore thee was violated.*

After the beloved has approved his beloved, that is, the faithful soul, for the state of virtue and the bearing conferred by his own gift, he suddenly makes an apostrophe to her herself, bringing back to memory the victory of the good state, so that she may grow together into a greater [1248D] grace of divine charity. *Under the apple tree,* he says, *I raised thee up:* as if he said: If thou considerest [sic: *anidmavertis*] what thou wast in the first parent, whence together with the whole human race thou didst contract guilt as it were by descent, not in vain wilt thou cleave to me more zealously, when thou attendest to what manner of one I have now made thee, or how great a grace I have conferred. *Under the apple tree* indeed *I raised thee up,* because, that thou mightest persevere in my grace, I held out before thee the form of thy condition, and that thou mightest learn to be under my power, I bound thee with the law of obedience. For when I said to Adam: *Of every tree of paradise eat, but of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil do not eat [n: (Gen. II)]*; I raised him up as one still lukewarm and ignorant, and admonished him to the duty of using reason well, that he might learn to fit himself to disciplines [sic: *di ciplinas*] and to the pursuit of obedience; but what? [1249A] he transgressed the precept and despised the good of discipline and obedience.

*There* then *thy mother was corrupted, there she that bore thee was violated.* How? Eve was seduced and violated, she persuaded her husband, and both presumed upon the forbidden tree. *Corrupted was* then *and violated under the apple tree thy mother* or *she that bore thee: corrupted* in the incontinence of the outward senses, violated through the consent of spirit and soul, in the guilt of disobedience; for she saw and desired [sic: *concapivit*], she obeyed the persuasion of the serpent and, touching the forbidden fruit, ate, and offered it to her husband to eat [n: (Gen. III)]. *256* This sin therefore, after it was consummated, begot death [n: (Jac. I)], and the penalty of this death, as if proceeding from this root of thy former parents, has been spread into the [1249B] posterity of the whole human race.

Therefore in thy first parents I raised thee up, and called thee forth to the pursuits of the virtues, but thou [sic: *tui*], in thy parents themselves, didst decline rather to vices, and didst not fear to become liable to death.

But when thou hadst been lost in such a way, who restored thee to salvation? who made thee such as thou art now seen to be? Did not I undertake death for thee and redeem thee from that death? Did I not again, *thee under the apple tree,* that is, under the wood of the cross, *raise up,* and lift thee to so great an honourableness and beauty of life?

But that thou mayest keep this honourableness and beauty of life inviolate, thou must needs persist with a strong mind in my charity.

[1249C]VERSE 6.-- *Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a seal upon thy arm: for love is strong as death, emulation hard as hell; its lamps are lamps of fire and of flames.*

Two seals the beloved here proposes to his beloved, one namely to be set upon the heart, the other upon the arm. For what are these two seals, namely of the heart and of the arm, but the keeping of the heart and the keeping of work? For of the *seal,* that is, of the keeping of the heart, it is written: *With all keeping keep thy heart, for from it life proceedeth [n: (Prov. IV)]*: but of the *seal,* that is, of the keeping of work, it is written: *In all works remember thy last things, and thou shalt never sin [n: (Eccle. VII)]*.

[1249D]And well are these two necessary to man in all his life, and if the first keeping has been neglected, the other perishes; for as *a good man out of the good treasure of his heart bringeth forth good,* so *an evil man, out of the evil treasure of his heart bringeth forth evil [n: (Luc. VI)]*. For whatever proceeds outward into work was first conceived in the heart, and therefore if the keeping of the heart is neglected, so that it is not ruled by reason, whatever is shown outwardly in work is judged irrational. If the heart is ordered by reason, what is brought forth from it into work is honourable, because according to the state or quality of the mind, every action of the agent is understood to be either good or evil.

What is it then that the bridegroom says to the bride: *Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a seal upon [1250A] thy arm?* What keeping can be safe and secure without God? *Unless* indeed *the Lord keep the city, he watcheth in vain that keepeth it [n: (Psal. CXXVI)]*: and therefore the Psalmist prays: *Keep me, O Lord, as the apple of the eye; under the shadow of thy wings protect me [n: (Psal. XVI)]*. But because, just as nothing of good work can be begun without him, *257* so neither can it be completed, the same Psalmist says: *Unless the Lord build the house, they labour in vain that build it [n: (Psal. CXXVI)]*: Therefore also it is necessary that *the seal of God upon the arm* be set, lest the building of good work be destroyed.

Rightly then is it said to the bride: *Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a seal upon thy arm,* because she cannot think or do right things [1250B] unless divine grace inspires and governs; nor bring these to the end by persevering, unless the same grace keeps her. For when the works of holiness are shut and as it were sealed, lest they be corrupted by any depravity, then truly *the seal of God upon the heart and arm* of each soul is not unfittingly understood to be *set*.

Or let us say that *the seal of God* is the impression of holiness which is to be set upon the heart and arm of each soul desiring to live holily and justly. For when the life and conversation or the teaching of the Lord Jesus Christ is set before the holy soul, to be imitated or to be observed, rightly *his seal upon the heart and arm* of her is fixed, so that as she has borne *the image of the earthly* she may bear *also the image of the heavenly [n: (I Cor. XV)]*. [1250C] Did not the Lord, when he said to his disciples: *Learn of me, because I am meek and humble of heart [n: (Matth. XI)]*, seem as it were to say: Set *me as a seal upon your heart?* For there he wished to leave as it were a figure of meekness and humility impressed upon their hearts; but when he said: *I have given you an example, that as I have done, so you also may do [n: (Joan. III)]*, did he not seem to say: Set me as *a seal upon your arm?* For there he wished to leave them a certain figure and image of good working and serviceable charity, which they themselves also afterwards might impress upon their hearers by teaching and working. Whence also the Apostle, writing to the Philippians, and wishing to impress the seal or signet of holiness from his own mind [1250D] upon their minds: *Brethren,* he says, *whatsoever things are true, whatsoever modest, whatsoever just, whatsoever holy, whatsoever lovely, whatsoever of good fame, if any virtue, if any praise of discipline, think on these things, which you have both learned, and received, and heard, and seen in me, these things do, and the God of peace shall be with you [n: (Philip. IV)]*.

The bridegroom therefore, that is, Christ, says to his bride, that is, to the faithful soul: *Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a seal upon thy arm;* as if he said to her in other words: Whatever thou readest in my teaching, whatever in my works, lay up in thy mind, and fulfil in acts, that from them thou mayest receive the form and image of holiness, through which *258* thou mayest lay aside every figure of the old man, which he assumed by sinning *under the apple tree [1251A]*. And why? *for love is strong as death.*

Well and fittingly, when the beloved had first said: *Set me as a seal upon thy heart, as a seal upon thy arm,* he straightway adds, *for strong as death* is *love, emulation hard as hell,* because since *God* himself is *charity,* as John says [n: (I Joan. IV)], to set God upon the heart is to have his charity, but to set him upon the *arm* is to work good through the same charity, because, as it is written: *Faith worketh through love [n: (Gal. V)]*. Now charity is the root of all the virtues, because as from one root many branches come forth, so from one charity many virtues are begotten. If then charity alone begets many virtues, and no one nourishes virtues [1251B] in himself but he who loves God, assuredly to have charity is to have God, and therefore, his seal being set upon heart and arm, the working through love is good and honourable.

But what is it that made God be incarnate and die, but charity? *For God so loved the world, as to give his only begotten Son, that whosoever believeth in him may not perish, but may have life everlasting [n: (Joan. III)]*: and so each faithful soul sets God *as a seal upon its heart and arm,* when, in imitation of him, as he laid down his life for it, so it does not hesitate to labour for him, and, if the cause demands, to undergo death also. Whence well is that very *love strong as death* so called, because it kindles the mind that truly loves God with so great a force [1251C] that it does not hesitate to die for him whom it fears to offend by any love of a worldly thing. For whomever charity itself has perfectly possessed, it kills utterly to the love of worldly things. *Strong* then is *love as death,* because as death destroys temporal life, so the love of God in the mind of the just man slays the love of this world, and whomever charity has perfectly imbued within, it renders as it were insensible to all outward things to be carnally desired.

*Hard* also *as hell is emulation,* because the imitation of the works of Christ is as mighty to absolve and justify man from sins as hell is mighty to torment sinners: which *emulation,* however, proceeds from the ardour of charity, which brings so great a force of pain [1251D] from the desire of the supernal [felicity], as hell brings from the remembrance of their evils to the sinners whom it torments.

Whence it is added that *its lamps are lamps of fire and of flames,* that in the hearts of the elect charity itself may be understood to be not simply heat but fire and flame.

VERSE 7.-- *Many waters could not quench charity, nor shall the floods overwhelm it; if a man shall give all the substance of his house for love, he shall despise it as nothing.*

Because so great is its force in the mind of each saint that, as *259* waters cannot quench an immense fire, so the many tribulations of the world cannot take away the fervour of charity from the mind of the saint. [1252A] Whence a certain one who was burning with this fire of charity says: *Who shall separate us from the charity of Christ, tribulation or distress, persecution or famine, or nakedness, or peril, or the sword? But in all these things we overcome because of him that hath loved us. For I am sure that neither life, nor death, nor angels, nor principalities, nor powers, nor virtues, nor dominions, nor any creature shall be able to separate us from the charity which is in Christ Jesus our Lord [n: (Rom. VII)]* [var: Vulg. Rom 8:35–39 sets *persecutio* after *periculum*, reads *neque mors, neque vita*, has no *potestates* or *dominationes* but *instantia, futura, fortitudo, altitudo, profundum*, and reads *a caritate Dei*, "from the love of God"].

Whence rightly it is added that *if a man shall give all the substance of his house for love, he shall despise it as nothing.*



## BOOK FOUR. (cont.)

For why should the holy soul not despise all its substance for the love of God, when it despises its very self [1252B] so far that for the same charity of God it does not fear death? For if it despises for the sake of his love all those things which are not God, what is there that it could set before that same love? And since there is nothing in heaven which it sets before charity, which is God, assuredly it is not a little but nothing if it despises all its substance for its sake. It follows therefore that charity is the highest good, if no good can be set before it.

VERSE 8.-- *Our sister is a little one and hath no breasts; what shall we do to our sister in the day in which she is to be spoken to?*

Above the beloved had commended the eminence of charity, and had shown of what kind or how great a good charity itself is; how it can be reached [1252C] he now purposes to demonstrate: because, since it is of so great excellence, so great dignity, the way is not easy to lay hold of it, and to hold it in the peace of perpetual security. Necessary therefore for obtaining it are constancy or fortitude of mind, knowledge of the Scriptures, and perseverance in good work. Wherefore the beloved says of the beloved, that is, the Church, or any holy soul whatever, which he calls sister on account of faith and good working, that *she is a little one and hath no breasts;* and he gives at once counsel as to what is to be done for this imperfection.

*Our sister,* he says, *is a little one:* whence is she a little one? Because she is still tender in faith, in sense, in word and reason, in strength of mind and in the [1252D] action of a good life. *And hath no breasts.* How *hath she no breasts?* She does not yet have the understanding of Holy Scripture, which is contained in the two Testaments, because although she reads and hears what *260* the letter shows and sounds, she has not yet received the knowledge of spiritual understanding, in which all the force and method of speaking and disputing rightly consists, without which the power neither of teaching nor of preaching can be obtained. Wherefore she ought first *to put away the things that are of a little one [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*, and to tend to perfection by believing and working, that after she has had breasts through the entire knowledge of the two Testaments, then at length she may fittingly take up the office of teaching and preaching.

*What then shall we do,* he says, *to our sister, in the day [1253A] when she is to be spoken to?* Since our sister is still a little one, and therefore cannot speak to [others], yet let her grow daily and desire to attain to perfection; when the time of speaking to her shall have come, so that it may be said to her: *Arise, make haste, my love, show me thy face, let thy voice sound in my ears [n: (Cant. II)]*, what counsel shall we find in the day of discretion now possessed, of understanding also and knowledge, and of an honourable life?

VERSE 9.-- *If she is a wall, let us be built upon it bulwarks of silver: if she is a door, let us join it together with boards of cedar.* [var: Vulg. Ct 8:9 *aedificemus*, "let us build"]

There are two qualities of prelates: one of those who, intent on the office of public preaching, against heretics and all reprobates *themselves as a wall for the house [1253B] of Israel,* that is, the Church, *do oppose [n: (Ezech. XIII)]*. The other of those who, bearing only the office of teacher and cleaving to the contemplative life, kindle their subjects to the pursuit of good action by word and example, to whom they are as it were a door, when they show them the entrance of the heavenly kingdom by teaching rightly and working well.

*What then shall we do to our sister, in the day* such *when she is to be spoken to,* and admonished that she now begin to rise to preaching or teaching? She must have two things necessary for this office, namely a *wall* and a *door,* that she may be constant and learned, resisting heretics and instructing the people of God: for she will have the wall for defence, the door for leading in.

[1253C] *If* then *she is a wall,* what shall we do? *Let us build,* he says, *upon it bulwarks of silver:* for bulwarks are necessary against the ambushes of enemies; but why *of silver?* because *the words of the Lord are silver tried by the fire [n: (Psal. XI)]*. Bulwarks of silver therefore are the fortifications of the divine words, which are to be built in the mind of the preacher through the knowledge of the Holy Scriptures. *If,* he says, *she is a wall, let us build upon this wall bulwarks of silver;* if, he says, she has amid adversity and prosperity a spirit of fortitude *261* and of such constancy that she stands like a wall for the defence of justice, let us give her also the knowledge of the Holy Scriptures, from whose sentences, as from certain bulwarks, [1253D] she may be able to fight against the depravities of heretics or of perverse men and to defend the reason of truth.

Further, *if she is a door,* and studiously keeps watch to this end, that she may instruct her hearers with the word of life, and lead them into a good manner of living, what shall we do? *Let us join,* he says, *that door together with boards of cedar;* for a painting is in place of reading to those who do not know letters. What then is the matter of this painting which is to be placed on this door, but the life and conversation of the saints? For when any prelate, for the sake of edification, sets before his subjects the examples of the saints, assuredly he paints as it were in their minds what they ought to love, what to fear, or how they ought to order their life in imitation of them.

[1254A]Whence also the painting itself is said [sic: *dieitur*] to be composed *with boards of cedar;* for the cedar is of an incorruptible nature, and therefore *boards of cedar* are rightly understood as the saints, because they are corrupted by no rottenness of vices, nay they fill the Church with a good odour, because *the good odour of Christ* they are *in every place [n: (II Cor. II)]*.

*With boards of cedar then the door is joined together* when any one of the preachers confirms his preaching by the virtues of the saints whom he brings in for example, so that he may open the gate of eternal life to those who imitate, or shut it to those who despise.

Therefore holy Church, or any holy soul whatever, which tends to the love of her bridegroom, while she is still a little one and tender in faith and knowledge, in morals and discipline, if she [1254B] desires to attain that perfection which may bring forth in her the full charity of God, it will be necessary for her to have, with constancy of mind, the bulwarks of the divine words, and to stand with happy perseverance in doctrine also and a good life. For when she has had the breasts of preaching and teaching, so that she can also nourish others and lead them to perfection, she seems as it were bound by debt to share also with her neighbours this which she has from the grace of God, as the Apostle says: *To the Greeks and to the barbarians, to the wise and to the unwise I am a debtor, so, as much as is in me, I am ready to preach the gospel to you also that are at Rome [n: (Rom. I)]*. Whence, at once after the grace of knowledge has been obtained, presenting herself of her own accord at the door of preaching she constantly answers her bridegroom: *I am a wall and my breasts are as a tower.*

*[1254C] 262* VERSE 10.-- *I am a wall, and my breasts are as a tower since I became before him as one finding peace.*

For she shows the cause of the grace conferred upon her, namely the finding of peace, through which she strives to tend constantly by preaching and working to her own profit and that of others; for since *the life of man upon earth* is *a warfare [n: (Job VII)]*, when one is in the fight and suffers the force of adversaries, how should he stand for the defence of others, who is still labouring for his own? It is necessary therefore first to feel peace and quiet in oneself, to obtain inmost security for him, and then first to be a wall for the defence of others; first to know how to instruct, rule and teach oneself, then to extend the breasts of one's doctrine to the profit of others, [1254D] which also is a sign of a certain peace, because when the mind labours on account of ignorance, and struggles with itself to obtain knowledge, once knowledge has been obtained, after it long fought with itself, it is assuredly proved to have gained the fruit of great peace and quiet.

What peace then does she say she has found, and where? Assuredly that peace *which surpasseth all understanding [n: (Philip. IV)]*, which the bridegroom himself left to his lovers, saying: *Peace I leave with you, my peace I give unto you [n: (Joan. XIV)]*; for what peace can be preferred to this peace, which here indeed begins, but there abides eternally? For Christ himself is peace, of whom the Apostle says: *For he is our peace, who [1255A] hath made both one [n: (Ephes. II)]*; whence also she says she found this before him. *Since,* she says, *I became before him as one finding peace:* for there she found peace, where she recognized as present him who is true peace. But how did she find him present and recognize him? and how, he being present, did she find peace? Assuredly in such a way as he himself taught, who says to his own: *Have salt in you, and have peace among you [n: (Marc. IX)]*. For being about to teach concerning having peace, not without cause did he put first, *have salt in you;* for in no way can anyone attain this peace unless he has had a mind seasoned with the salt of wisdom; for as a certain one says. Wisdom is to have been free of folly, because as salt takes away rottenness from flesh, so wisdom takes away folly from the heart. And by the seasoning of wisdom, [1255B] folly having been taken away from the heart, which begets the rottenness of vices, one is quickly drawn together to this peace, by which every fight between flesh and spirit is lulled to sleep, and the flesh desires nothing that is repugnant to the spirit; and so, this peace having been found in this land, one will at some time come to that which is to be taken away by no end. But how is this fight taken away, so that *263* peace may be granted, except before him, except in the presence of him who, consoling his own who are thus to fight, says: *Have confidence, I have overcome the world [n: (Joan. XVI)]*; as if he said: Because I have overcome, through me you also shall overcome?

Rightly then the bride says: *Since I became before him as one finding peace, I am a wall, and my breasts are as a tower;* because since by his grace I have overcome carnal affections, and have been strengthened with spiritual peace, I can [1255C] stand for the salvation of others also, and constantly speak forth the words of exhortation. *My breasts* indeed *are as a tower,* because I have grown so much from the divine grace in the word of exhortation that I can, according to the prophet, ascend a high mountain and lift up my voice with strength that I may preach the gospel to Jerusalem [n: (Isa. XL.)] Indeed *I am a wall,* for defending and keeping the people of God, *and my breasts are as a tower,* that I may be able to look forth from on high, and to announce from afar things to come.

VERSE 11.-- *The peaceable had a vineyard in that which hath peoples: he let it out to keepers, a man bringeth for the fruit thereof a thousand pieces of silver.*

It is greatly to be considered who this peaceable one is, [1255D] or what is the vineyard of the peaceable one, and who the peoples, or who the keepers, or what the fruit of this vineyard.

This peaceable one is the Lord Jesus Christ: for the causes of the peace noted above he is called peaceable, *whose place is made in peace [n: (Psal. LXXV)]*, and who called us in peace, *dissolving the enmities in his flesh, making void the law of commandments in decrees, that he might make the two in himself, into one new man making peace, that he might reconcile both in one body to God, by the cross killing the enmities in himself, and coming he preached peace to them that were afar off and peace to them that were near [n: (Ephes. II)]* [var: Vulg. Eph 2:17 *pacem vobis, qui longe fuistis*, "peace to you that were afar off"].



## BOOK FOUR. (cont.)

With the eye of the inner man illuminated it is to be carefully looked into how fittingly, how truly, and how uniquely this one is peaceable, who such and so great and so long-lasting [1256A] enmities dissolved in his flesh; the wonderful and adorable majesty of the reconciliation that was made is then the more recognized, when the quality and the quantity of the enmity is weighed; for how great an enmity did the first man contract toward God through the evil of disobedience, that he not only lost that felicity of paradise in which he had been placed, but also took upon himself death, the avenger of transgression? But the weight of this enmity grew through the succession of the human offspring by the increase of malice and wickedness, so that the Maker himself, as Scripture says, *touched inwardly with sorrow of heart*, said: *It repenteth me to have made man [n: (Gen. VI)]*, and therefore, sentence of judgment having been given, save Noe, who alone was then saved with his wife and sons, the whole world was consumed by the avenging water.

[1256B]But who would dissolve the weight of so great an enmity? Would *264* an angel? But an angel, equally with man a creature of God, needed the grace of God, and a creature could not free a creature from a necessity of this kind. There came therefore the Maker of the creature himself, needing nothing, almighty, and in every way sufficient to destroy the wall of this enmity, and to establish without any obstacle the eternal covenant of reconciliation and of true peace. And because he could not be seen through the Godhead, he came through the humanity that he might become visible; and at his birth the whole world met him with a sign of temporal peace, announcing and proclaiming the token of that highest and eternal peace which was to be established through him. Whence also at his nativity the angels cried: *Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace to men [1256C] of good will [n: (Luc. II)]*.

Who then is more and truly peaceable than this so great eternal maker of so great a peace? Solomon indeed, who was called from his name *peaceable*, for Solomon is interpreted *peaceable*, had temporal peace, a temporal king, by God's granting, so that he might build a house to God with no one hindering; but by his name, and his work, and his time he went before this true peaceable one, as night goes before day, the shadow before the truth; who, that he might found for himself a house, namely holy Church, laid as it were in the foundation the sign of this wonderful peace, which is to be kept, *Peace,* saying, *I leave with you, my peace I give unto you [n: (Joan. XIV)]*.

To this *peaceable one there was a vineyard,* namely holy Church, which is therefore not unfittingly called a *vineyard,* because, [1256D] if the cultivation or the nature of the vine is attended to, she seems to draw to herself no small profit for her own progress from its likeness. For what indeed is the manner of cultivation that is applied to the vine? In the time of pruning, the useless shoots being cut back, it is as it were cleansed; then, dug about, it is raised up and tied to stakes: further, if thou attend to the quality of its nature, thou wilt see that first it fixes a living root, then, because it is pliant and apt to fall, with its tendrils, as with certain arms, it binds whatever it lays hold of, and by these it raises and lifts itself up. What then is the pruning in the Church or in any soul whatever, but the cutting away of vices from the hearts? and what the digging about, but the bringing back to the mind [1257A] of sins through compunction and confession? For as with a kind of hoe of compunction the mass of earthly works is brought back to memory, so that the mind, unburdened through confession and satisfaction, may be able to bear fruit better in good acts. And what does the minister of this office do who receives the penitent, and imposes on him as he confesses the measure of penance? He raises him with the hand of sure consolation and with the hope of the divine mercy, and sets before him, through the examples of the saints, what *265* purpose of life he should hold henceforth, by which he ought to support himself, as if tied to stakes, lest he fall.

Further, according to the nature of the vine the progress of the Church or of any soul whatever is considered, because after it has been planted and fixed in the root of faith, the layering of humility being pressed down, lest it could be bent back or drawn away by any storms or tempests [1257B] of the world, it embraces all its neighbours with the embraces of charity as with tendrils and coils, and rests in their compunction.

Such, then, *was the vineyard* to the true *peaceable one,* our Lord Jesus Christ; not that it is not so now, or ought not to be in the future, but we ought to take *was* as said in his foreknowledge and predestination, as it is written elsewhere of every creature: *What was made in him was life [n: (Joan. I)]*, because in his foreknowledge all things already were and lived, just as in the mind of some craftsman there is the form or pattern of the thing to be made, whose likeness the hands of the worker imitate, so that he may bring the thing forth into the open.

The *vineyard,* moreover, *was in that* (understood: peace) *which [1257C] hath peoples,* because the Church has been gathered not from the one nation of the Jews, but from the peoples of all nations, into the unity of faith, love and peace, as it is written: *They had one heart and one soul [n: (Act. IV)]*. Further, it is to be noted that he does not say, *in that* peace which the peoples have, but *which hath peoples,* so that the grace of him who calls, justifies, glorifies may be the more attended to than the labour and zeal of him who wills, runs, strives to lay hold, because, as the Apostle says: *It is not of him that willeth nor of him that runneth, but of God that sheweth mercy [n: (Rom. IX)]*. *For he is our peace who hath made both one [n: (Ephes. II)]*, and from it is *the peace which surpasseth all understanding [n: (Phil. IV)]*. When therefore this peace *hath peoples,* no schism, or any dissension, can break into the unity of their faith, but they cleave so firmly [1257D] in that peace that they become a most choice *vineyard* to the true *peaceable one,* our Lord Jesus Christ: for if they themselves had peace without the peace that has them, they would not be held by a firm root, because they would not be planted together in that peace in which in the selfsame they might sleep and rest [n: (Psal. IV)]. Which sleep and rest is given after this life in the selfsame, *because we shall see him as he is [n: (I Joan. III)]*, and *there is no* change there, *nor shadow [sic: obrumbratio] of alteration [n: (Jac. I)]*, but the peace which we shall once have received we shall keep for ever without any change.

But these *peoples* whom this peace *hath,* although in the first place we ought to take them as the universal Church gathered from Jews and Gentiles, yet in the second [1258A] place we can, not unfittingly, take them as the particular Church established within the universal itself through the diverse kinds *266* of professions, which also can be called the *vineyard* of the Lord, and ought to be bound together by the same peace and unanimity. Further, if we descend to individuals, some faithful soul serving God can, not absurdly, be called the *vineyard* of the Lord, whose cultivator is the Holy Spirit through grace, since according to the form set down above, which we said befits the whole, it seems not unfittingly to suit the single one also, namely that this be its zeal: to cut away vices and sins like useless shoots with the hand of pious work through co-operating grace, to bring earthly works back to the mind through compunction and confession, as through a digging about, [1258B] and so, by doing penance and doing good works, to offer fruit acceptable to God.

*He let it out to keepers:* the keepers of this vineyard, that is, of holy Church, were the holy apostles or their successors, apostolic men, whose zeal and diligence it was to instruct her in holiness and justice, and to defend her by word and example from the plague of evil works and of depraved doctrines; not that whatever progress they attained in this keeping was of their own work, but because they were ministers and officers of the divine operation and efficacy, as it is said by the Lord in Numbers to Aaron: *Put ye my name upon the children of Israel, I the Lord will bless them [n: (Num. VI)]* [var: Vulg. Nm 6:27 *Invocabuntque nomen meum super filios Israel, et ego benedicam eis*, "And they shall invoke my name upon the children of Israel, and I will bless them"] : as if he said in other words: Do you, through the ministry delivered to you, pour my grace [1258C] into men by word and work; it will be mine to show the good of that grace in them and to bring it to effect.

Now the word of delivering which is uttered in this pronouncement is to be attended to, not without fear, by all the prelates of the Churches and by their subjects, because it forewarns both those to the virtue of prudence and these to the virtue of obedience, to be wary and careful. For the Apostle speaks to prelates: *Take heed to yourselves and to the whole flock, wherein the Holy Spirit hath placed you bishops, to rule the Church of God, which he hath purchased with his own blood [n: (Act. XX)]*; further, to subjects: *Obey,* he says, *your prelates and be subject to them. For they watch as being to render an account for your souls, that they may do this with joy and not with grief, [1258D] for this is not expedient for you [n: (Hebr. XIII)]*. Therefore it ought to make you, that is, the prelates, attentive with solicitude, providence, and these, that is, the subjects, with humility, patience, on account of the word of this delivering, in which it is said: *He let it out to keepers.*

If then God has delivered his Church to keepers, it is greatly to be feared lest, if the keepers neglect the keeping, and those to be kept despise the keepers, or resist and disparage the keeping, they gravely offend him who placed the keepers and established the keeping. These keepers indeed God calls watchmen, as he himself speaks by the prophet: *I have made thee a watchman to the house of Israel; the things which thou seest, thou shalt announce to them from me* [1259A] [n: (Ezech. III)]; *267* *the things which thou seest,* he says, *thou shalt announce to them from me,* that is, what thou understandest with the mental eye, or beholdest with the bodily eye, as needing to be corrected or amended, *thou shalt announce to them,* by exhortation, counsel and precept, *from me,* that is, according to the reason and manner which I shall have shown thee beforehand by inspiring. And in the delivering of this keeping God set before the keepers themselves, or watchmen, the danger, and the manner of escaping the danger, saying: *If, when I say to the wicked: Thou shalt die the death, and thou declare it not to him, he indeed shall die in his iniquity, but his blood I will require at thy hand,* behold the danger. But the manner of escaping this danger follows in what he says: *But if thou declare it to the wicked, that he may be converted from his iniquity, and he hear thee not, he indeed shall die in [1259B] his iniquity, but thou hast delivered thy soul (ibid)*. To the keepers therefore, that is, to the prelates in the Church, danger is set before them for negligence, security for vigilance, whence greater care and solicitude ought to hold them toward the office entrusted to them.

Further, the danger of private persons or subjects is not less, nay it is graver, for contempt and disobedience; because while prelates mostly sin from infirmity and negligence, these offend from pride and rashness, and the contempt and irreverence of those is the contempt or irreverence of God. So he himself says: *He that heareth you heareth me, and he that despiseth you despiseth me [n: (Luc. X)]*; and to the prophet, whom he says he gave as a watchman to the house [1259C] of Israel: *Therefore* he says, *they do not hear thee, because it is an exasperating house [n: (Ezech. III)]*. An evil and fearful appellation, that the house of the undisciplined and disobedient should be named *an exasperating house*, and in another place they are called *of stiff neck and uncircumcised heart*, because they resist the Holy Spirit [n: (Act. VII)]. For they who resist the word of discipline and honourableness resist the word of God. *The words,* moreover, *of God are spirit and life [n: (Joan. VI)]*, and therefore they resist the Holy Spirit. Whence the Prophet, because he saw that to resist the word of God is damnable, asked to be taught goodness and discipline by God, because he had believed his commandments and been obedient [n: (Psal. CXVIII)].



## BOOK FOUR. (cont.)

But the divine word commanded in the sacred law concerning the stubborn and wanton and disobedient son, that his parents, having seized him, [1259D] should bring him *to the elders of the city and to the gate of judgment [n: (Deut. XXI)]*, namely lest they hasten their own sentence, but that they bring in wise men as witness; for they ought to know that the first fault of our race, for which we all pay, is disobedience, and that the justice of God lays up no pardon for the stubborn and wanton, but, as it is written: *God resisteth the proud, but giveth grace to the humble [n: (I Petr. V)]*.

For such are always to be examined at the gate of judgment, so that, if they remain in their stubbornness or rashness and will not obey, they be cast out from the fellowship of the brethren; but if they repent and become humble and quiet, *268* they be received again. This is also the gate of judgment in the city of God, that is, holy Church: that the stubborn and rebellious, if they will not be corrected, be cut off with the sword [1260A] of excommunication, like putrid members, from the body of the Church, of whom it is written: *They went out from us, but they were not of us [n: (I Joan. II)]*; but that the penitent, and those putting an end to their malice, be restored like healed and holy members into the body of the Church. Therefore the other sins are indeed to be tolerated in the house of God, but the severity of wise fathers overwhelms stubborn and wanton sons, that it may take away the evil from its midst, with the authority of the law of the Lord which we have mentioned; nay, by the very example of God, who expelled the disobedient first men from paradise [n: (Gen. III)].

The word *delivering*, therefore, which is pronounced of the lord of the vineyard, is to be thought on and weighed not negligently but diligently and with fear, because on either side [1260B] neglect or contempt, whether by prelates or by subjects, looks to the judgment of him who delivers.

*A man bringeth for the fruit thereof a thousand pieces of silver.* The diligent and studious inspector of this chapter ought to attend who, or of what kind, or how great is the *fruit* of this vineyard, which is valued at so great a price that for the obtaining of it not ten, or twenty, or thirty, but determinately a *thousand* are the *pieces of silver* to be brought; and that he who, lacking these, could purchase this fruit, is not any powerful and rich man, whether official or private, but ought absolutely to be called, and to be, a *man*.

What, then, is this *fruit*? Surely that which is painted as it were with three colours of beauty, namely power, wisdom, goodness: power [1260C] indeed, than which nothing is more efficacious; wisdom, than which nothing is more reasonable; goodness, than which nothing is sweeter. For power is constrained by no law of impossibility or infirmity, wisdom admits nothing which lacks the virtue of reason, goodness abounds with so great a multitude of sweetness that there is nothing above it. Moreover, the touch and use of such a fruit affords an efficacious and most wholesome experience of health to the whole man. But where is this found among creatures and visible things? Is it in angels? But it is written that *in his angels also* he *found* iniquity [n: (Job IV)]. For a creature can be subject to corruption and mutability; but that which cannot must be immutable and incorruptible.

Further, we have said that the three beauties of this fruit [1260D] are such that they can receive nothing above themselves; yet something can be below, which from affection for them or participation in them may be said in some manner to be powerful, wise and good, *269* but not substantially power, wisdom, goodness. For that which is receivable and participable is not the same as that which receives and participates; for the receiving and participating thing is such when it neither receives nor participates, and therefore it is subject to mutability and can be corrupted: but the receivable and participable, abiding ever immutably in its own essence, can never be corrupted; but no creature is receivable and participable; it is therefore God. When therefore we ask what the *fruit* of this vineyard is, and attend to all the circumstances of its virtue and beauty. [1261A] beyond doubt we conclude at the last that it is God. But by reception and participation the rational creature attains this, that at some time it may pass into incorruption and immutability, not by nature but by grace; and thus, from man working and God co-operating, this fruit is as it were born and grows, which is the reward of all who pertain to the kingdom of God, and is called true beatitude. But true beatitude is God himself, whom to have and whom to enjoy eternally is truly to be blessed. This *fruit*, therefore, is God.

Further, what shall we say of the quality of such a fruit, since it is written of it that *eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man? [n: (I Cor. II.)]* Nevertheless above we distinguished the same fruit by three colours, which pertains rather to quality, [1261B] not that the difference of the colours makes a different fruit, but that this diversity so inseparably coheres in one substance that it in no way takes away any virtue and efficacy of its property. For just as, for example, three colours come together in some one subject, and one has the name so that it is called red, another white, the third green, yet one and the same is the substance which contains them; so in this fruit, though power is not one thing, wisdom another, and goodness another, yet the fruit is one and the same, and has everywhere one and the same virtue and efficacy, namely to be able, to be wise, to quicken, which nevertheless bring it about that there is nothing good which is lacking to its fullness.

[1261C]But what is to be said of the quantity of this fruit, since it is of such immensity that it is circumscribed by no place, enclosed by no boundary? Yet it seems to have certain extents, not according to itself, but according to the participant: but nothing can be a participant of this thing save the rational creature. Further, the rational *270* creature is in one kind spiritual, as the angel and the soul, in the other corporeal, as the elements and the things which are composed of them. But since this is not the place to dispute about the dimensions of a corporeal thing, which are constituted by line, surface, or solid body, while this fruit, of whose quantity we began to speak, is not corporeal but spiritual; *for God is a spirit [n: (Joan. IV)]*, of whose quantity, because he is incomprehensible and uncircumscribed, [1261D] no one can either think or speak; let us nevertheless now see what are the extents which it can have, not according to itself, but according to the participant. For whatever of goodness, honourableness, holiness, discipline or knowledge the participating soul can have, it takes from the participable God, as it were the vigour of grace flowing forth into it; and through this the participant seems to have something longer or shorter, broader or narrower, higher or lower, according as it pleases him to distribute who gives grace *to every one* according to *the measure of faith [n: (Rom. XII)]*. Whence the Apostle, speaking to those whom he was exhorting to receive such dimensions: *In charity,* he says, *rooted and founded, that you may be able to comprehend with all the saints what is the length, [1262A] breadth, height and depth [n: (Ephes. II)]* [var: Vulg. *latitudo, et longitudo, et sublimitas*, "breadth, and length, and height"].

But since this fruit of which we speak is born and grows from the root of charity, and comes to maturity as it were to perfection, not as regards God who is to be grasped, who is always perfect, but as regards the capable mind of man, which is subject to increase and loss, so that by advancing in this manner it may at some time comprehend God, with whom in its own manner it may become perfect and unchangeable, let us first see concerning our Lord Jesus Christ himself how, according to his humanity, he received in himself from the root of charity all these dimensions, namely length, breadth, height and depth: for who had greater charity than he, who laid down his life for his sheep? [n: (Joan. XV.)]

[1262B]But if we set the eyes of the mind on the kind and quality of his passion, we shall see all these dimensions venerably contained, and every Christian soul manfully promoted by this example. For in the figure of the cross are to be noted these four dimensions, namely length, height, breadth and depth, which from the root of Christ's charity are stretched out into the whole world, signifying and admonishing every soul which has been redeemed by his passion that, if it wishes to attain the aforesaid fruit, that is, eternal beatitude, it must reach it by the dimensions of the same charity, according to the measure and nature granted to it.

*271* For charity ought to be broad, because it ought to be extended even [1262C] to the love of enemies, as Christ hanging on the cross prayed for his enemies, saying: *Father, forgive them, because they know not what they do [n: (Luc. XXIII)]*. It ought to be long, because not for an hour but to the end of life, as it is written of Christ: *Having loved his own, he loved them unto the end [n: (Joan. XIII)]*; for charity ought to have such great perseverance that it fails for no tribulation or distress. It ought to be high, because of the final cause, namely that it attend for what it acts or suffers, that is, for eternal beatitude, which is this fruit of which we speak, for the sake of which it is admonished always to tend upward and to have its conversation in heaven; it ought to have depth, because it ought never to be without fear, but always to attend [1262D] that whatever it attains from breadth, length and height, it has not from its own merits but from the mercy of God; and therefore, because it knows not the hidden and deep judgment of God, why he should choose one and reject another, and the end is always uncertain while *many* are called, *few* are chosen [sic: *cliguntur*] [n: (Matth. XX)], it ought never to be without fear.

Since therefore *in Christ dwelleth all the fullness of the Godhead corporeally [n: (Coloss. II)]*, and to him *God doth not give the spirit by measure [n: (Joan. III)]*, while *all* the saints receive *of his fullness*, *grace for grace [n: (Joan. I)]*, they seem to reach to the extents of these dimensions, each in his own manner or measure, according as he gives to them who has in full and can give of his fullness as much as he will.

[1263A]Further, since charity, set like a centre in the middle, touches all the virtues as it were through the circumference, for every virtue descends from the root of charity; and since every motion has six species, for there is before and behind, up and down, to the right, to the left; to whatever part the mind of man directs its intention for attaining such a fruit, it always receives the origin of every good motion from the vigour of charity. And since man himself, called the lesser world, consists after the likeness of the greater world of four elements, he is also measured by four parts, by which he is wholly contained, if he has been established for preserving the likeness of God, namely prudence, justice, temperance, fortitude.

These four parts being stretched out from the centre of the mind, that is, [1263B] from charity, at an equal distance, whatever of virtue can be in the soul, thou, as with a circle, both touchest by carrying round and enclosest.

This, therefore, can in some manner be the measure or dimension which this fruit, participating, can have, not according to itself, as has been said, but according to the participant, so that when at some time it has been attained by the participant as it is, *272* it may take away from then on all passibility or mutability of dimension in growing, and confer perfectly eternal impassibility, immutability, immortality.



## BOOK FOUR. (cont.)

This fruit, therefore, is true beatitude, and true beatitude is God, whose quality and immensity is incomprehensible. But since we now know what is the fruit of the aforesaid vineyard, let us now see concerning the [1263C] one who offers, who he is, since he is said to be a *man*, and could not offer unless he were a *man*. For *man* is so called from virtue, and he who ought to offer for so great a fruit must shine with the beauty and grace of the virtues, because an oblation will not be accepted which no brightness of the virtues commends. For in the matter of what is given and received the form ought to be this: that there be in the giver such composure of bearing, and such grace of honourableness and discipline, that it bring forth in the receiver good pleasure and cheerfulness of countenance, and render the gift more acceptable. And if mortals affect this good of discipline and honourableness among themselves, and men approve it, what do we think the omnipotent and immortal God wills and seeks from us, whose nature is goodness, holiness, singular [1263D] honourableness and discipline? Therefore he who offers to such a Lord ought to be *clothed* with *strength* and girded with virtue [n: (Psal. XCII)]: with strength of mind, that is, for restraining and repelling vices and sins, with virtue of spirit for doing and exercising good works. For that oblation is not right which the darkening of guilt repels, since the Lord said even to Cain: *If thou offer rightly, but divide not rightly, thou hast sinned [n: (Gen. IV)]* [var: Vulg. *nonne si bene egeris, recipies*, "if thou do well, shalt thou not receive?"]. He offered rightly indeed, who gave the fruits of the earth to God, but he did not divide rightly, who kept his heart in evil and stained his oblation with the plague of envy: for he ought to have separated the good from the evil, because there is no participation of light with darkness, nor can there be together in the sight of God [1264A] truth and falsehood, equity and malice. He offered rightly indeed, because he offered to God, to whom he ought; but he did not divide rightly, because what was better he rightly denied to God, what was viler he gave: for he is not dear enough to God, nay, he is unworthy of God, who offers his goods to him indeed, but keeps himself for himself.

Such a man, therefore, the Prophet describes in the first psalm, where he represents the bearing and purpose of such a man; and since that psalm is known to those who understand, whoever desires to take on this man, let him meditate on it continually and exercise it in deed: in another place also he mentions this same man briefly, saying: *Blessed is the man who feareth the Lord, he desireth exceedingly in his commandments [n: (Psal. CXI)]* [var: Vulg. *volet nimis*, "he shall delight exceedingly"], and the rest; for this is what makes this man, and blessed, since it belongs to virtue [1264B] to fear God, and to consider and keep his commandments; but to beatitude, that for this, *his seed shall be mighty upon earth, and his justice remaineth for ever and ever (ibid)*.

*273* The offerer having been described, whom it befits to be a man, it is now to be seen what the man brings, and ought to bring, for such a fruit. *A thousand,* he says, *pieces of silver.* The number thousand is put for perfection, and silver designates the pure and bright sense of chaste words, as it is written: *The words of the Lord are chaste words, silver tried by the fire [n: (Psal. XI)]*. But what can we call a *piece of silver* formed and inscribed from this silver? If we look diligently, what is it that forms or inscribes in the mind of man the image of God, the image of holiness and justice, [1264C] save *the commandment of the Lord, lightsome, enlightening the eyes [n: (Psal. XVIII)]*, the eyes, namely, of the inner man? And where do we hear the commandment of the Lord, and whence do we know it, save from Holy Scripture, which is the speech of the Lord? This Scripture, therefore, well understood, forms, and makes as it were a kind of inscription in the mind of man, so that when from the reading of Holy Scripture he has perfectly taken on charity *from a pure heart and a good conscience, and an unfeigned faith [n: (II Tim. II)]*, he receives the coin of a certain piece of silver, inscribed in this manner: *to the image and likeness of God,* for this form the Maker of things himself appointed to be impressed on man in creating him, when he said: *Let us make man to our image and likeness [n: (Gen. I)]*, [1264D] which is to say: Let us make man living, rational, like to God: for the condition of man excels all living things by a twofold honour, when it is said *to our image and likeness; to the image,* namely, that he may be rational, to *our likeness,* that he may be upright by following the justice of God. The one of these, namely rationality, he cannot lack; the other, that is, the likeness of the divine uprightness or justice, he cannot attain save through grace: but the faculty of rationality, which man cannot lack, is the instrument, or as it were a kind of eye, for seeking the likeness of God. He is therefore inexcusable when he has not come to the likeness of God, [1265A] because, namely, he would not use a good instrument for that for which it was given him.

But when God first made man, he made him not only rational, but also endowed with the honour of his likeness, *For God,* says Scripture, *made man upright, but he entangled himself with many questions [n: (Eccle. VII)]*. But now, seduced, he has lost indeed the honour of the likeness of God, but the faculty of reason has remained in him, through which he may be able to hear him who calls him back and to follow him as leader and teacher to his restoration. Such silver, therefore, that divine and highest money-changer inscribed in the soul of man, when he made it rational and a partaker of his holiness.

*274* Wherefore man is a debtor to God, so that, since God [1265B] marked him with this twofold honour, he should understand his debt, namely that, being capable of reason, with reason itself as leader, he should always advance to the likeness of God through holiness, lest, if he understand not, he should be compared *to senseless beasts, and* become *like to them [n: (Psal. XLVIII)]*. Finally, that man owes this debt to God the Lord shows in the Gospel, since, when he was tempted by the Scribes and Pharisees saying: *Whether it be lawful to give tribute to Caesar or not,* he answered: *Show me the coin of the tribute;* and when they had shown it, he said: *Whose is the image and inscription?* And when they answered: *Caesar's: Render,* he says, *the things that are Caesar's to Caesar, and the things that are God's to God [n: (Matth. XXII)]*.

By this sentence of his, therefore, the Lord shows, and by showing teaches, that just as it is just that to a [1265C] mortal man the things which are his, and which have been given by him, be rendered in tributary subjection, so it is just, nay most just, that to the omnipotent and immortal God the things which are his, and which have been granted by him to man, be brought with the highest devotion and humble subjection. Now reason and understanding have been granted to man by God, which he ought so to use that by living well he may advance to the likeness of God, and receive in his soul and mind, from the brightness of holiness, this inscription of the piece of silver: *to the image and likeness of God.*

Such a piece of silver, therefore, one or two or three or more, according to the measure of God's gift, some faithful man offers, that he may purchase that fruit, [1265D] that is, eternal life, which works through love, tends and advances to the likeness of God. But he *brings a thousand pieces of silver,* who by the brightness of perfect holiness has attained the image and likeness of God.

Or else the thousandfold unity signifies one faith, one baptism, one God who worketh all in all; and he who desires that *fruit*, that is, eternal life, must hold and teach the true and right faith, must have and worship one God and one man, must adorn with faith and works the name of Christianity which he received in baptism, must read, teach, embrace and follow the true, catholic, and one Holy Scripture.
