When, therefore, the beloved says these things of herself to the grace and glory [1266A] of the beloved, he at once answers and says: *The vineyard,* etc.

VERSE 12.-- *My vineyard is before me. A thousand are thy peaceable ones, and two hundred for them that keep the fruit thereof.* [var: Vulg. *fructus*, "the fruits"]

*My vineyard,* he says, *is before me,* that is, I know and approve my *275* vineyard, from this, that I find in it the grace of my planting; if I did not find it, it could not be before me, nor could it be called my vineyard. For of you who can be called and be my vineyard I spoke by the prophet, that *I will look upon you and make you grow; you shall be multiplied, and I will establish my covenant with you [n: (Levit. XXVI)]*: for those who are so before me that they are worthy of my presence and sight, shining with the brightness of good works, I acknowledge and keep; because *the eyes of the Lord are upon the just [n: (Psal. XXXIII)]*, just as on the contrary, [1266B] those who set me behind them by despising, them too I set behind me by reprobating, and therefore by not bestowing the help of my grace.

*A thousand are thy peaceable ones, and two hundred for them that keep the fruits thereof;* from the opposite of that vineyard which was turned into perversity, of which it is said that *I looked that it should bring forth the grape, but it brought forth wild grapes [n: (Isa. V)]*, the sense of this chapter will better appear. For the Lord says by Osee of her, under the figure of a harlot: *And I bought her to me for fifteen pieces of silver, and for a core of barley, and for half a core of barley [n: (Ose. III)]*: now a most beautiful sacrament is hidden in these numbers; here *in a thousand pieces of silver,* there *in fifteen pieces of silver, and a core of barley and half a core of barley;* the sum of which is this, that this one is both a virgin and joined to a husband, that one [1266C] neither a virgin nor joined to a husband; for the thousandfold unity signifies the virginity of this one, but her conjunction or nuptials the hundredfold two. Arithmeticians or abacists know better what is said, and by what reason unity is pronounced a virgin they have in use in their treatises, namely that multiplied into itself it creates nothing, because once one is one, which befalls no other number: for the other numbers, led into themselves, create others, as twice two four, and the third [cj: *tertia*; read *ter tria*, "thrice three"], nine: further, *the hundredfold two designates her nuptials,* and this is most happy, because this beloved who is of faith is both always joined to a husband and always remains a virgin, always brings forth, yet always remains a virgin.



## BOOK FOUR. (cont.)

[1266D]On the contrary, it designates the widowhood and at the same time the corruption of that one, that her price neither remained in unity nor came to two, when it is said: *I bought her to me for fifteen pieces of silver and a core of barley and half a core of barley.* If thou attend to the pieces of silver, there are neither ten pieces of silver, which would be the tenfold unity, nor twenty, which would be the tenfold two, but there are *fifteen,* in which number, namely, it is more than unity, less than two: likewise there is neither one core of barley only, nor are there two cores, but a core and a half of barley, more than the monad, less than two. And so this too is most unhappy for her, that she is neither a virgin nor has a husband. Therefore *a thousand are thy peaceable* pieces of silver, *276* that is, all the discourses harmonizing in one faith; for this number is a thousandfold [1267A] unity, and they are thine, making or acquiring peace, that is, the remission of sins; since to have one Lord, one faith, one baptism is to be in the catholic Church, where the remission of sins is given through peace, that is, Christ, *who is our peace, who hath made both one [n: (Ephes. II)]*. For Holy Scripture, which sounds forth and teaches these three things, contains in itself the whole sum of this peace, because whatever there is of law, of God, of faith, of the religion of Christianity, and all the sacraments of the Church are constituted from these, and he who has them as he ought to have them attains that peace which feels the wars of the vices; and when by this peace he has advanced to an abundance of virtues, the remission of sins having been given him, he will reach that perpetual and never-to-be-ended peace, where there will be no labour, no pain, [1267B] nothing at all which could oppose him.

These three, therefore, are the instruments of this peace, since every soul tending toward it must always consider in whom it believes, what it believes, how it believes. For if thou ask whom, or in whom, thou oughtest to believe, God is set before thee; but if thou askest what to believe, the sacraments of the Church are shown thee; further, if thou ask how thou oughtest to believe, the religion of Christianity is prescribed to thee. *For faith without works* is idle, nay, *is dead [n: (Jac. II)]*, and: *Without faith it is impossible to please God [n: (Hebr. XI)]*. Accordingly, as we have said, on account of the unity of these three things, which constitute and acquire the true and eternal peace, there may rightly indeed be said to be for this vineyard a *thousand* who are *peaceable.*

[1267C]But *two hundred for them that keep the fruits thereof,* why are they said to be? First indeed it is to be seen who are these *keepers of the fruit,* then what this number, the hundredfold two, given to them, means, of which we spoke a little above. As keepers of this *fruit* we can name in the first place those who preside in the Church of God by word and work, and who, lest this fruit be snatched from the minds of the faithful by heretics and other perverse men, strive to withstand with tongue and hand, that is, with doctrine and working, never to be barren, but always to beget an offspring of virtues in themselves and in others. But in the second place all Christians generally ought to be keepers of this *fruit*, because the Christian religion requires this of them, that they lose not eternal life by living evilly, but, being always one Spirit with God, [1267D] be heaped from this spiritual conjunction with the fruitfulness of good works, and so at length in a virginal wedlock, in the heavenly nuptials which the hundredfold two here signifies, may enjoy God for ever. The dowry of which wedlock will be *277* the double stole, namely the beatitude of body and soul, which the hundredfold two can also signify, because the hundred, too, on the right hand, expresses the crown of the same beatitude.

VERSE 13. *Thou that dwellest in the gardens, the friends hearken: make me hear thy voice.*

After many and diverse colloquies of the beloved and his beloved, beautifully and laudably uttered, the same [1268A] beloved, laying down as it were the sum and final voice of this epithalamium, as though he now instructs [cj: *erudit*; read *eruditae*, "to one now instructed"], and proclaims to her fully taught out of the fellowship of his discourse, and announces that she should come forth as an example to others in her form and beauty which she has taken on from this instruction. *Thou that dwellest,* he says, *in the gardens.* What are the gardens, if not the pleasantness of the Scriptures or the delight of the virtues? for nothing flowers more delightfully or smells more sweetly than the region about the streams and flowing waters or shoots of the Holy Scriptures, and the beautiful, delightful and wholesome variety of the virtues. For on both sides the discipline is laudable and heavenly, here in speaking, there in doing. But what is it to dwell in these *gardens*, if not to rest in frequent meditation of the holy law and exercise of the virtues? For he whose will is in the law of the Lord, and who meditates on his law day and night, dwells simply at home with Jacob [n: (Gen. XXV)], that [1268B] is, abides in the tranquillity of his mind with secure stability, admitting nothing varied, nothing changeable into the sameness of faith or morals. But on the contrary he does not dwell in these gardens who, intent with Esau on the hunting of worldly things [n: (Gen. XXV)], wanders about the din of the world through the woods of thoughts, keeping neither the unity of faith nor the integrity of morals.

Therefore, O beloved, *thou that dwellest in* these *gardens* simply and wholesomely, *make me hear thy voice.* What voice? The voice of pious discourse, the voice of virtue, the voice of one teaching well and rightly, the voice of one living justly and piously. For as true and right preaching is a great voice, so also a pious and holy way of life is a great voice. Humility is a great voice, obedience is a great voice, chastity [1268C] is a great voice, patience is a great voice, charity is a great and resounding voice; and with as many virtues as the holy soul shines, with so many great voices, as it were, it cries to God.

And why do I wish *to hear thy voice*? Namely that I may hear thee crying out by word and work, as it is written: *The children that shall be born and shall rise up, let them hear and tell it to their children, that they may put their hope in God and not forget the works of God, and may seek his commandments [n: (Psal. LXXVII)]* [var: Vulg. *et narrabunt filiis suis*, "and shall declare to their children", without *audiant*]. And what is my *hearing*? Surely to approve, to accept, and to judge worthy of my help, *278* for when I hear I approve, when I hearken I help. But do thou *make me hear thy voice.* How wilt thou do it? By living honourably and piously, by teaching true and pious things. For reason and understanding, [1268D] memory and wit I have granted thee; that thou mayest be inexcusable, and not say that thou neither knowest nor art able, I have taken away from thee every necessity of impossibility, so that thou mayest have and do as is due: but I wish to be a debtor to thee, so that when thou hast done what thou oughtest by right to do, I may be overcome when I am judged [n: (Psal. L)]; by thy judgment, I say, I wish to be overcome, because I am greatly delighted if thou usest my gift well, and therefore it is thine to bring it about that I wish *to hear thy voice:* and yet thy doing is not thine, but mine, because that thou art willing and able is the gift of my grace.

*Make me,* he says, *hear thy voice.* Why? Because *the friends hearken:* whose friends? Both mine and thine: mine through charity, because they love me by keeping [1269A] my word; thine, because they love thee by wishing thee salvation. For there are friends whom thou seest not with bodily eyes, who rejoice over thy progress and salvation, or over thy conversion, of whom it is written that: *There is joy to the angels of God upon one sinner doing penance [n: (Luc. XV)]*. *These indeed are ministering spirits in all the earth for them who receive the inheritance of salvation [n: (Hebr. I)]* [var: Vulg. *in ministerium missi*, "sent to minister", for *in omnem terram*]: for these are solicitous and act for the salvation of men, as it is said by one of these invisible friends to one of the visible friends, that *at that time shall Michael rise up, who standeth for the children of thy people [n: (Dan. XII)]*, and presently, *and none is my helper in all these things but Michael your prince [n: (Dan. X)]*.

Therefore these *friends hearken,* because they are greatly delighted [1269B] *to hear thy voice* crying to me, whether by praying, or by psalmody, or by preaching, or by wishing. There are also other friends, whom thou hast before thine eyes, who are likewise delighted to hear such a fourfold symphony of concords, like a beautiful διατέσσαρον, and therefore they give ear and hearken, and long to hear thee singing and saying: *But I, like an olive tree, have borne fruit in the house of the Lord, I have hoped in the mercy of my God, and I will wait for thy name before the face of thy saints [n: (Psal. LI)]* [var: Vulg. *sicut oliva fructifera in domo Dei*, "like a fruitful olive tree in the house of God"]. These, I say, holy and such friends, hearken, in the optative and desiderative mood, that thou mayest become such as they themselves are, as one of them wished to become *anathema* for his brethren [n: (Rom. IX)], whose salvation he desired; and because [1269C] he himself was already dwelling in the gardens of the virtues, of wisdom also and of knowledge, he was solicitous so to temper his voice in my ears that he might please both God and men, and might show by life and doctrine what befitted the salvation of men. Whence this voice of his is sounding from the gardens of wisdom and virtue, and audible to me: *I chastise my body and bring it into servitude,* I subject it, *lest perhaps, while I have preached to others, I myself become a castaway [n: (I Cor. IX)]*. In this *279* way, while the flesh is so subjected to the spirit that it desires nothing below itself, seeks only what is above itself, this is the voice which sounds delightfully in my ears; nay, this is the *Canticle of canticles*, than which mortal and passible man can sing nothing sweeter in this mortal life; by which voice and by which canticle he is ever caught up [1269D] to take hold of that ineffable sweetness which *eye hath not seen, nor ear heard [n: (I Cor. II)]*, the attaining of which will make man immortal and impassible. While the angels and all my saints hearken to this voice and this canticle and desire to hear it, they, the ones (that is, the angels) having attained, and the others (that is, men) holy through faith, hope and charity not doubting that they will attain, the same glory, long greatly that thou also, who hast not yet attained, mayest attain it with them, and for this they unceasingly make supplication to me. What, at length, does the beloved answer to this voice? *Flee away, O my beloved,* etc.

VERSE 14.-- *Flee away, O my beloved, and be like to the roe, and to the young hart upon the mountains of spices.*

If we look attentively, great is this voice of one humble [1270A] of heart, whether we refer it to the whole Church, or to any soul devoted to God: for why should not holy Church, or any holy soul, use this voice toward God in the present life? For one of the saints says: *We know in part and we prophesy in part; but when that which is perfect is come, that which is in part shall be done away [n: (I Cor. XIII)]*. What did he perceive, who pronounced the imperfection of knowing and prophesying in this present life? for that which is in part is not perfect; but he says this imperfection is to be done away when in the future life that which is perfect shall come; and such imperfection, if thou consider rightly, is surely the flight of this beloved.



## BOOK FOUR. (cont.)

Further, whatever is done in the sacraments of the Church [1270B] contains great and profound mysteries, all of which proclaim the power, virtue and grace of this beloved; but who is there who is able to dispute of these to the full? whence the beloved, astonished before such greatness, knows in part and prophesies in part, but because she is not able to comprehend to the full, she fails, and cries out to the beloved to flee, not from neglect but from failing: for "flee", in the discussion of the subtle matters of the sacraments, is the flight of this beloved. For when we cannot fully set forth or discuss their force and efficacy, this beloved flees us: likewise when we direct our gaze on the face of sacred Scripture, but cannot see it fully through and enter the secrets of its treasures, assuredly [1270C] this beloved flees us.

*280* But let us not rashly hold him back as he flees; rather, whatever we cannot comprehend in a sacrament or in the subtlety of the holy Scriptures, let us burn with fire, that is, reserve to the Holy Spirit, and lest by discussing too much we fall into a heretical sense, let us suffer the beloved to flee. For according to the example of blessed Jacob, who wrestled with the angel, we indeed are admonished to wrestle with the word of God, but after, the blessing having been given, we are lamed by the touch of the sinew, let us permit the word of God to flee. For what is it to wrestle with the word of God, if not untiringly to search out the sense of sacred Scripture? What, then, is it for the victor to ask and receive a blessing from the vanquished, if not to seek and [1270D] find the understanding of that sense? But the victor, the blessing received from the vanquished, limps with his thigh withering, because he who by God's inspiring has understood sacred Scripture well, every pleasure of the flesh being quenched, advances in the active and contemplative life, and conquers with great gain indeed to the spirit, but with great loss to his flesh. Lastly, he limps who conquered, because he who was conquered touched the sinew of his thigh, and it withered; how does he limp? Namely, resting one foot on the earth, holding the other up from the earth, that is, not wholly abandoning the active life, for the care of those subject to him, and cleaving with all his strength to the contemplative for its sweetness.

This lame man leaps or ascends like a hart, and scarcely deigns to touch the earth, for *his conversation is in heaven [n: (Philip. III)]*: yet *in part,* for [1271A] with one foot only is he now meanwhile suspended there, until *that which is perfect* shall come. And therefore, because now he cannot comprehend perfectly, he rightly says: *Flee away, O my beloved.* When therefore, speaking with the Lord on the mountain, we perceive with the mind the sense of the word of God, let us attend to that saying of the Apostle: *Be not highminded, but fear [n: (Rom. XI)]*, lest while we incautiously pursue the fleeing beloved we slip into the pit of perverse dogma. Again, we say in this manner that the beloved flees, because he is always incomprehensible; for this is to be granted to his majesty, that he cannot be comprehended, and that he is always desired and desirable, and that it is hidden from us, as long as we are in this body, *how great* is *the multitude of his sweetness, which he hath hidden for them that fear him [n: (Psal. XXX)]*.

[1271B]Let us therefore say to him: *Flee, flee,* being cautious and provident for ourselves, lest ever while we live we think that we have comprehended, following that we may comprehend. Or let any faithful and wise soul say in this manner to God with a humble and devoted mind: *Lord, I am not* worthy *that thou shouldst enter under my roof [n: (Matth. VIII)]*, I am not worthy that thou shouldst show in me some frequent miracle of thy presence or visitation. *Flee away,* she says, *O my beloved,* I seek not glory in the present life, but I desire grace; I seek not the brightness of miracles, but I long for the remission of sins. *Flee away,* I say, *O my beloved,* and *281* make such leaps like the leaps *of the roe* or *of the young hart,* not upon me, but *upon the mountains of spices,* upon the lofty merits of the saints and of the perfect. It is not mine to treat or dispute of [1271C] the secret of thy divinity, of the highest teachings of thy precepts, of the deep mysteries of thy sacraments, but rather of those who can be called lofty mountains for the eminence of their wisdom and virtues, who, touched by thy Holy Spirit, send forth as smoke thy good odour, so that they can say with the Apostle: *We are the good odour of Christ [1272A] unto God in every place [n: (II Cor. II)]* [var: Vulg. *Deo in iis qui salvi fiunt*, "unto God in them that are saved"], and therefore they are rightly called *mountains of spices.*

And what wonder if I, conscious of my infirmity, say to thee to flee, when the great and learned mountains themselves are astonished in the consideration of thy very works, or precepts, or even sacraments, and sometimes suffer thee to flee? For one of these mountains, who had been stretched up even to the third heaven, and had heard *things which it is not granted to man to utter*, [n: (II Cor. XII)], fearing to pursue thee, the beloved, as thou fleest, *O the depth,* he says, *of the riches of the wisdom and of the knowledge of God! how incomprehensible are his judgments, and how unsearchable his ways! For who hath known the mind of the Lord, or who hath been his counsellor? or who hath first given to him, and recompense shall be made him? for of him and [1272B] by him and in him are all things; to him* be honour and *glory for ever and ever [n: (Rom. XI)]*.

Therefore, *O my beloved, flee away,* not that thou mayest forsake me wholly, but that what I cannot attain here, since thou art incomprehensible, thou mayest grant me in the life to come, in true and eternal beatitude without end. Be like *to the roe, and to the young hart upon the mountains of spices.* That is, transcend even the understanding of the perfect saints by the incomprehensible knowledge of thy divinity; or make such leaps in their hearts that they may advance *from virtue to virtue* [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)], because as often as the elect advance from good things to better, from virtues to virtue, God makes as it were certain leaps in their minds, so that by these steps or leaps little by little they may come to that highest and perfect [1272C] state *in which the God of gods is seen in Sion [n: (Psal. LXXXIII)]*, where they may unceasingly say and sing in the ears of the Lord of Sabaoth, not simply a canticle, but the Canticle of canticles, that is, to know and to perceive that highest and best good for ever and ever. Amen.



## *282* IN PLACE OF AN EPILOGUE, THE AUTHOR'S PARAENESIS To the sacred virgins for whom he wrote and to whom he inscribed these commentaries.

[1271]

[1271D]Behold, beloved sisters, as we were able we have sung to you, and as much as that highest master of ours has granted, under whose discipline are all who seek the knowledge of singing rightly and well. But since the knowledge of singing well and rightly consists of a threefold instrument, namely *heart, mouth* and *work,* I am convicted of not being a skilled singer. With the mouth indeed I can sing, but if the other two are lacking, every form of my singing will be empty before the Lord: for to sing with the heart and with work can please God, even if one sing not with the mouth; but to sing with the mouth only, and not [1272D] with the heart and with work, it is impossible to be able to please God. Further, he who wishes to have the perfect knowledge of singing, and to know its highest dignity, must always have the faculty of these three. The Apostle, showing that he knew and had it: *I will sing,* he says, *with the spirit, I will sing also with the mind [n: (I Cor. XIV)]*. For to sing with the spirit is both to utter spiritual words from memory, which is to sing with the mouth, and to proceed to the works conceived in the spirit, which is to sing with work; to sing with the mind is to perceive with the mind the living sense of the words, which is to sing with the heart.

[1273A]The instrument, then, having been given for attaining the knowledge of singing rightly and well, there is need that we should also know what is first to be known in singing, and what canticle, that we may come to the canticle of canticles. For first we must learn and sing that new canticle to the singing of which the Holy Spirit exhorts us in the Psalm: *Sing ye,* he says, *to the Lord a new canticle, his praise in the Church of the saints [n: (Psal. CXLIX)]*. If thou consider the quality of relatives, where *new* is said, it follows that there is also an old. But let us first see what the old canticle is, that from the knowledge of it we may better understand the *new*.

That old canticle the old man Adam sang and taught, but it is greatly opposed and contrary to the new canticle which was heard afterwards, and distant from it by that distance [1273B] which is between the mortifying and the vivifying. And what is that canticle? Surely that by which that old man cried out against his Maker, and clamoured against justice and piety, namely pride, disobedience and the excusing of his sin. For it was just that he should be subject to his maker and ruler, and obey; but it was wretched that after his fault he did not acknowledge himself, *283* nor presume upon the piety of God, but, a despiser of his fault, an exile from paradise, he transmitted its punishment to all his posterity. The more many sing the doleful tragedy of this canticle, by imitating his presumption and negligence, the greater the vengeance of the imposed death they pay.

But opposed to this canticle is that *new [1273C] canticle,* the canticle of peace and gladness, the canticle of life and justice, the canticle of virtue and salvation, which that new man sang before us, and not simply man, but God and man; for by such a precentor, who said: *Learn of me, because I am meek and humble of heart, and you shall find rest to your souls [n: (Matth. XI)]*, we are admonished to sing the new canticle of usefulness [cj: *utilitatis*; read *humilitatis*, "of humility"]. Further, by the example of the Lord himself, of whom the Apostle says: Christ *became obedient* to God *the Father unto death [n: (Philip. II)]*, we are admonished to sing the canticle of sweet obedience; at the voice also of the same new precentor, who says: *Do penance, for the kingdom of heaven will draw near [n: (Matth. IV)]*, we are admonished not to incline *our heart to evil words, to make excuses [1273D] in sins [n: (Psal. CXLVI)]*; because this is the most grievous, nay the most wretched voice of the old impenitent man, the voice, I say, of the old canticle full of sorrow; but rather, the manner of such a canticle being converted and changed into the new, let us sing together to God a melody of the contrite spirit, laborious indeed as regards the body but wholesome as regards the soul, in the genus of four modes, that is, compunction, confession, satisfaction, perseverance; and on the four finals of these modes, namely thought, speech, action and custom, let us daily make our cadence.

For of both canticles, that is, of the old and of the new, these are the finals, thought, speech, action, and custom, and according to the form of the preceding mode [1274A] they take their cadence; either the elegiac as regards the old, or the sweet and musical as regards the new. For the musical cadence is that which is made by a reasonable conjunction, disposition, and tenor of voices or tones, and this the new canticle takes more, nay always, on account of the reasonable motion of the mind singing well and beautifully, and always tending to a good end by thinking, speaking, acting, being accustomed. On the contrary, the elegiac, this is a sad and wretched cadence, is that which is made by an irrational and unequal motion of the mind; and because the form of such singing is confused, it generates an unpleasant [sic: *in suavem*] and harsh quality of cadence.

There is therefore between such two canticles that distance which is between life and death; because the old [1274B] kills, but the new gives life, and this by a likeness; for certain sea beasts are spoken of, which are called sirens, which, while they allure and draw sailors by the sweetness of their song, *284* suddenly sink the incautious and negligent: and in this manner, while all carnal men in the sea of this world, sailing as it were, are held by the sweetness of the carnal life, by the delight of sin, suddenly, neglecting the salvation of their soul, and unwilling to be converted through penance, they are snatched by unclean spirits to eternal death. And this is the mode of the old canticle, since, beginning from the spirit of pride and disobedience, it makes its last cadence in the abyss of eternal damnation with the most grievous and most wretched sound of an impenitent heart.

Further, on the other side, there is that likeness which is [1274C] shown us from divine and human Scripture. From the divine, where it is written that David with his harp calmed the fury of Saul [n: (I Reg. XVI)]: from the human, where it is written that Orpheus by his song soothed tigers and lions; and that Amphion by the sound of the lyre [sic: *testitudinis*] moved stones; which signifies that this is the power of the new canticle, that it calms the bestial minds of men raging against their own salvation, and composes the hard hearts of mortals, by a reasonable motion of words and senses, to a good manner of living and a reasonable honourableness of morals. And this is the mode of the new canticle, which, beginning from the spirit of humility and obedience, makes its last cadence in the joy of eternal beatitude with the highest and most joyful sound of an innocent or penitent heart.

[1274D]As therefore life and death are contrary to each other, so the old and the new canticle are contrary to each other, according to the qualities of the persons by whose authority each canticle takes its beginning and end. For the author of the old canticle is the devil, but the author of the new canticle is Christ. Wherefore, despising the author of death, let us follow the author of life, and, continually attending to his delightful and most sweet harmony of holiness, let us pass by with deaf ear the foolish voices of the sirens, that is, the foolish persuasions of malignant spirits.

But the workshops and dwellings in which the instruments of this new and wholesome canticle are prepared and kept, and in which we may hear and learn the canticle itself, and by the custom and exercise of singing [1275A] may come to that Canticle of canticles whose sweetness and delightfulness will never fail, are the beloved tabernacles of the Lord, of which once that diviner, who said that he was a hearer of the words of God, and one beholding the vision of God, who falleth, and so hath his eyes open, was about to inquire:

*How* beloved, he says, *are thy tabernacles, O Jacob, and thy tents, O Israel; as woody valleys, as watered gardens beside the rivers; as tabernacles which the Lord hath pitched, as cedars by many waters [n: (Num. XXIV)]*. Now the tabernacles of the true Israel and spiritual Jacob, sojourning or serving as a soldier in the present age, are the religious, and those well ordered according to the *285* discipline of Christ, namely the orders of the married, the continent, widows and virgins, according to [1275B] either sex; these tabernacles plainly are each beautiful in its own purpose.

*How beautiful the tabernacles, how beautiful the tents? as woody valleys, as watered gardens beside the rivers.* This likeness is clear from other places of Scripture: for in Isaias the Lord, promising to the gentiles, once forsaken, the merits of spiritual virtues like trees to a desert: *I will give,* he says, *in the wilderness the cedar and the thorn, the myrtle and the olive tree: I will set in the desert the fir tree, the elm and the box tree together, that they may see, and know, and consider, and understand together [n: (Isa. XLI)]*. Therefore *the tabernacles are beautiful as woody valleys. Valleys* on account of humility, *woody* on account of the beauty of diverse virtues.

[1275C]For in these tabernacles of God, that is, in holy professions, there are cedars, that is, saints incorrupt and incorruptible in faith and work, and fragrant with the odour of the virtues: there are *thorns,* pricking others by word and example and provoking them to good works: there is the *myrtle,* tempering the tribulations of neighbours through compassion by the word of consolation: there are *olives,* overflowing with acts of mercy and piety; there are *firs,* rising ever on high by virtue for contemplation: there are *elms,* who, though secular and less fruitful, yet sustain and help others who live well with their means: there is the *box,* who, though not rising to the heights of the virtues, nor bearing fruit, yet live always by the faith of their parents: [1275D] these tabernacles of the Lord are therefore *as woody valleys,* because they draw this from a humble heart, and from lowly action also and bearing, that they grow green and flower with the fullness of such great virtues.

They are also *as watered gardens beside the rivers,* as it is said elsewhere: *And thou shalt be like a watered garden, and like a fountain of waters whose waters shall not fail [n: (Isa. LVIII)]*. And in the same canticles: *A garden enclosed, a fountain sealed, the fountain of gardens, the well of living waters, which run with a strong stream from Libanus [n: (Cant. IV)]*. Behold *the tabernacles beautiful as watered gardens beside the rivers.* Because from the moisture of the Holy Spirit they are always green and flowering in speech and action, and in the assiduous meditation of the divine precepts.

They are also *cedars* by the waters; which the Psalmist [1276A] shows, who, when he had said: *Watering the hills from his upper rooms,* etc., continued at once: *The plains,* he says, *shall be filled, and the cedars of Libanus which he hath planted [n: (Psal CIII)]* [var: Vulg. *Saturabuntur ligna campi*, "the trees of the field shall be filled"]. Therefore *the tabernacles are beautiful as cedars by the waters,* that is, beside the waters, namely, in which the sparrows make their nests [n: (Psal. CIII)], by whose abundance, when they are rich men, the need of the poor is supplied.

Therefore these tabernacles are *286* as *tabernacles which the Lord hath pitched,* because all this their beauty, adornment and ordering is the work of the omnipotent God, who by his grace places, advances, adorns and sustains them.

*Water shall flow out of his bucket, and his seed shall be in many waters [n: (Num. XXIV)]*. What is *his bucket*? Nay, what is *his water*? Surely *of the true Jacob,* that is, *the supplanter,* [1276B] or of the true Israel, that is, *the mind seeing the Lord.* Some of whom dwell in these tabernacles, that is, professions. Whose *bucket* is Christ, from whom water is drawn, that is, Holy Scripture. For through him Holy Scripture is opened, nay by him thirst is aroused, that this water may be desired; by him the sense is opened, that the Scriptures may be understood. *Water* therefore *shall flow out of his bucket,* that is, the true understanding of the Scriptures shall stream from his Christ. What next? *And his seed shall be in many waters.* What is *water out of his bucket,* this is the multitude of waters from his seed. For *his seed, the seed of his father Abraham [n: (Gen. XVII)]*, namely of him who truly is *the lofty Father,* or *the Father of many nations,* Christ.



## *282* IN PLACE OF AN EPILOGUE, THE AUTHOR'S PARAENESIS To the sacred virgins for whom he wrote and to whom he inscribed these commentaries. (cont.)

This *seed* of his *shall be in many waters,* that is, this [1276C] seed pours forth many waters. By drinking we have experienced that his *seed* truly is *in many waters.* *He that believeth in me,* he says, *out of his belly shall flow rivers of living water [n: (Joan. VII)]*. Again: *He that shall drink of the water that I will give him shall not thirst for ever, but the water that I will give him shall become in him a fountain of water springing up into life everlasting [n: (Joan. IV)]*. This, I say, we have experienced by drinking of his grace. For behold, from this bucket, which is Christ himself, we have received many gifts of the Holy Spirit in the one Church, we possess in mind many and most abundant things of the Scriptures.

In these tabernacles of the Lord, therefore, that new canticle ever resounds which the dwellers of these tabernacles sing with the seven differences of voices, [1276D] the Holy Spirit ruling the song with his sevenfold grace, namely wisdom and understanding, counsel and fortitude, knowledge and piety and the fear of the Lord. Further, from these seven differences of voices diverse beatitudes come about, as it were eight species of modes; namely humility, meekness, mourning, or three [cj: *tria*; read *tristitia*, "sorrow"] according to the Lord, peace, mercy, cleanness, the desire of justice, patience.

And this song, or praise of God, is not save in the Church of the saints, because that they are saints, and that they know and are able to do that whereby they are saints, is the gift of the grace of God alone. And such, going out daily from the Egypt of this world, by contempt of their own will holding the harps of God, that is, the mortification of the flesh, sing not only *a new canticle,* but also [1277A] *the canticle of canticles,* because in this manner they advance to that ineffable charity of God, which is the virtue of virtues, and from which proceeds, and in which is contained, the fullness of all the virtues.

You therefore, beloved sisters, since you are the tabernacle of God, or dwell in the tabernacles of God, sing together always this new canticle with humility, chastity, patience, obedience, mortification of the flesh, love of God and of neighbour. Let the justifications [1278A] of the Lord be songs to you in the place of your pilgrimage, [n: (Psal. CXVIII)], that at some time, going out from the Egypt of this age and coming to the land of promise, and eternally [sic: *aeterna liter*] enjoying the good things of that land, which *eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, neither hath it entered into the heart of man [n: (I Cor. II, Isa. LXIV)]*, you may sing with all sweetness and delightfulness, without intermission, not only the new canticle, but also the canticle of canticles, for ever and ever. Amen.
